--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page marks a significant shift from the previous section, which listed prohibited Protestant authors (such as John Sturm and John Rivius). We are now looking at the title page of Girolamo Cardano’s "Ars Magna," one of the most foundational works in the history of mathematics. Curiously, this copy bears the inscription of the Jesuit Collegio Romano, the intellectual heart of the Counter-Reformation, showing how these major scientific works were preserved and studied within Catholic institutions despite Cardano’s own later troubles with the Inquisition.</meta>

->Catalog of the Inscriptions of the Library of the Roman College of the Society of Jesus<-

# GIROLAMO CARDANO,
## MOST EXCELLENT MATHEMATICIAN, PHILOSOPHIER, AND PHYSICIAN,
# THE GREAT ART,
## OR, ON ALGEBRAIC RULES,
### Book one. Which is also the tenth in order of the whole work on Arithmetic, which he titled THE PERFECT WORK.

<insert>
<gloss>GIROLAMO CARDANO</gloss>
<gloss>What is to be in the future is better <note>original Greek: "ΟΙ ΦΕΡΤΕΡΟΝ Ο ΤΙ ΕΙΣ ΤΟ ΜΕΛΛΟΝ Ο ΤΙ ΓΕΝΗΣΕΤΑΙ." This motto reflects Cardano’s fascination with both mathematical progress and his lifelong interest in prophecy and the unfolding of time.</note></gloss>
</insert>

**Y**ou have in this book, diligent Reader, the Algebraic Rules (which the Italians call "of the Thing" <term>Rule of the Thing: original "de la Coſſa," from the Italian "cosa." In early algebra, the unknown variable (our "x") was called "the thing." Hence, algebra was often called the "Cossic Art."</term>) so enriched with new inventions and demonstrations by the Author that, in place of the very few commonly known before, they have now become seventy. Nor do they untie the knot only where one number is equal to another, or two to one, but even where two are equal to two, or three to one <note>Cardano is referring to the degree and complexity of equations. This book famously contained the first published solutions for cubic and quartic equations (equations involving x³ and x⁴).</note>. It was decided to publish this book separately for this reason: so that, once this most abstruse and clearly inexhaustible treasure of all Arithmetic has been brought to light and, as if in a theater, set out for all to see, readers might be encouraged to embrace the remaining books of *The Perfect Work* (which will be published in volumes) all the more eagerly and learn them with less boredom.

<margin>Library of the National Library of Rome, Vittorio Emanuele</margin>
<margin>Shelfmark: 12-22. I. 16.</margin>

<summary>This is the title page of Girolamo Cardano's "Ars Magna" (1545), the first major Latin treatise on algebra, which introduced revolutionary methods for solving cubic and quartic equations. The page includes a portrait of Cardano and indicates the book was part of the Jesuit Collegio Romano's collection.</summary>

<keywords>Girolamo Cardano, Ars Magna, Algebra, History of Mathematics, Cubic Equations, Collegio Romano, Renaissance Science, Rule of the Thing</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page follows the title page of the "Ars Magna" (The Great Art). It contains the formal dedication of the work from Girolamo Cardano to Andreas Osiander. Osiander was a German Lutheran theologian and mathematician who oversaw the printing of this book in Nuremberg. This relationship is historically significant because it shows the intellectual cooperation between a Catholic Italian physician (Cardano) and a Protestant German reformer (Osiander) during a time of intense religious division.</meta>

# GIROLAMO
## CARDANO, PHYSICIAN, TO ANDREAS
-> Osiander, most learned man, sends many greetings. <-

> **N**ever have I turned anything over in my mind so much, most learned Andreas, as how I might commend to posterity the names of those who deserve well of the liberal arts <note>original: "bonis literis," literally "good letters," referring to the humanities and scholarly learning</note>. Furthermore, I have applied a certain special diligence if such individuals have joined kindness to their erudition. For this reason, since I understand that you possess a no small knowledge of Hebrew, Greek, and Latin literature, but also of Mathematics, and since I have always found you to be most kind, it seemed that this work of mine could be dedicated to no one better than to you. From you, it can be corrected (if my hand, overstepping the command of my mind, has erred), read with pleasure, understood, and then indeed recommended with authority. This example, unless I am mistaken, others will also follow, and they will dedicate their works only to those learned in the specific art they treat. Accept, therefore, this perpetual testimony of my affection for you and your kindness toward me, as well as of your own excellent erudition. And although you are the kind of person whose own virtue makes you known to all, nevertheless—since Alexander and Caesar, though most famous for their deeds, desired to be inscribed in the monuments of others, and since Plato, who founded those marvelous works by his own merit, still desired to be praised in the writings of others—I hope this service of mine, such as it is, will not be unwelcome to you. For in these matters a certain fortune holds sway, and better things often perish while the worse are preserved. Whatever your judgment of this work may be, I am certain that I must fulfill my duty. And I wish it were possible to show, by a more illustrious example, my spirit toward all who possess that brilliance of mind which I have always recognized in you toward the scholars of our time. But perhaps a better occasion will be given; even if it is not, I would not want this one, whatever it may be, to be lost to me. Farewell. January 9 <note>original: "v. Idus Ianuarias," the fifth day before the Ides of January.</note>, 1545. Pavia <note>original: "Papia," the Latin name for the city of Pavia in Italy, where Cardano was a professor.</note>.

<summary>A formal dedication letter from Girolamo Cardano to Andreas Osiander, praising Osiander's polymathic learning and kindness as a justification for dedicating this landmark mathematical work to him.</summary>
<keywords>Girolamo Cardano, Andreas Osiander, dedication, 1545, Pavia, Renaissance mathematics, scholarly patronage</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page follows the dedication to Andreas Osiander. It provides the beginning of the table of contents for the Ars Magna, listing the first twenty-two chapters. This work is foundational to modern algebra, as it contains the first published solutions for cubic and quartic equations. Note that the original Latin uses "res" (thing) for the unknown variable (our modern x) and "quadratum" (square) for $x^2$.</meta>

## WHICH ARE CONTAINED
### IN THIS BOOK.

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the general table of contents to the actual beginning of the treatise. It introduces the historical lineage of algebra, acknowledging the contributions of Islamic scholars, medieval Italians like Fibonacci and Pacioli, and Cardano's contemporaries like Scipione del Ferro and Tartaglia.</meta>

# THE GREAT ART, WHICH
## THEY COMMONLY CALL COSSA, OR THE
### ALGEBRAIC RULES,
### reduced into Forty Chapters by Master Girolamo Cardano, and it is
### the Tenth Book of his Arithmetic.

### On the two equations in individual chapters. Chapter I.

**T**his art originally took its beginning from Muhammad, the son of Moses the Arab <note>Al-Khwarizmi (c. 780–850), the Persian polymath whose name is the root of the word "algorithm" and whose book "Al-Jabr" gave us the word "algebra."</note>. Indeed, Leonardo of Pisa <note>Fibonacci (c. 1170–1240), who traveled to North Africa and introduced the Hindu-Arabic numeral system and algebraic methods to Europe.</note> is a reliable witness to this fact. He left behind four chapters, with their demonstrations, which we will assign to their proper places. After many intervals of time, three derivative chapters were added to them by an unknown author, which were nevertheless placed with the principal ones by Luca Pacioli <note>A Franciscan friar who published "Summa de arithmetica" in 1494, which summarized all mathematical knowledge of the time but claimed cubic equations were impossible to solve.</note>. Finally, I read of another three derivative chapters discovered from the first ones by some unknown man; these, however, had hardly come to light, even though they were far more useful than the others, for they taught the <term>estimation</term> <note>original: "æstimationem," referring to the process of finding the value or root of an unknown.</note> of the cube and a number, and of the square-squared <note>original: "cubi quadrati," likely referring to fourth-power equations.</note>. But in our times, Scipione del Ferro of Bologna discovered the chapter of the "cube and things equal to a number" <note>This refers to the solution for equations of the form $x^3 + ax = n$.</note>, a truly beautiful and admirable thing, since this art surpasses all human subtlety and the clarity of every mortal genius. It is surely a celestial gift and a test of the power of minds, so illustrious that whoever touches it believes there is nothing they cannot understand. In emulation of him, Niccolò Tartaglia of Brescia, our friend, when he had come into competition with Scipione's disciple, Antonio Maria Fiore, discovered the same chapter so as not to be defeated; and he, yielding to my many prayers, handed it over to me. For I was deceived by the words of Luca Pacioli, who denied that any other general rule could exist beyond his own chapters. Although so many things had already been discovered by me and were at hand, I nevertheless despaired of finding what I did not dare to seek. But then, having obtained that rule and hunting for its demonstration, I realized that many other things could be possessed. And with that study, and with confidence now increased, I found new rules partly by myself, and also some through Lodovico Ferrari, formerly our student. Furthermore, those things found by them will be graced with their names; the rest, which lack a name, are ours. Also the demonstrations, except for three by Muhammad and two by Lodovico, are all ours, and each will be placed before its own chapter, then the rule added, and the <term>proof</term> <note>original: "experimentum," meaning a numerical check or demonstration of the rule's validity.</note> subjoined. And although a long discourse could be held about these matters,

<summary>Cardano introduces the history of algebra, crediting Al-Khwarizmi and Fibonacci, while detailing the modern breakthrough in solving cubic equations by Scipione del Ferro and Niccolò Tartaglia.</summary>

# BY GIROLAMO CARDANO
<meta>catchword: ris</meta>

...[con]siderations, and an innumerable series of chapters could be added, but we will nevertheless make an end to our careful consideration at the cube, treating the rest, even if generally, yet as if in passing <note>original: "per transennam," meaning looking through a lattice or cursorily.</note>. For since the position <term>unknown</term> <note>original: "positio," the first power of the unknown, which we call x.</note> represents a line, the square a surface, and the cube a solid body, it would indeed be foolish for us to progress further where nature does not permit. Therefore, he will seem to have taught perfectly enough who has handed down everything as far as the cube; the rest which we add, as if compelled or incited, we do not hand down further. In all the preceding matters, and especially in the third and fourth books, it will be worthwhile to remember them, lest I become tedious by teaching them again, or more obscure by omitting them.

For we remember that we have already taught which are odd or even denominations. For the square, the square of the square <note>x⁴</note>, the cube of the square <note>x⁶</note>, and so on, with one always omitted in between, we call even denominations. We call the position <note>x¹</note>, the cube <note>x³</note>, and the first and second names odd denominations. But truly, just as 9 is produced from 3, so it is from minus 3, because minus multiplied by minus produces plus. But in odd denominations the same nature is preserved: plus is not made except from a true number; nor can a cube, whose value <term>estimation</term> <note>original: "æstimatio," the value of the unknown root.</note> is minus (or what we call a debt), be produced from any position of a true number; this, it must be remembered, has already been explained more clearly.

<margin>3</margin>
If therefore an even denomination is equal to a number, the value of the thing is twofold: minus and plus, the one being equal to the other. For example, if a square is equal to 9, the thing is 3, or minus 3; and if it is equal to 16, the thing is 4, or minus 4; and if a square-square is equal to 81, the value of the thing is 3, or minus 3. To combine even denominations is not very necessary, because the square-square <note>the fourth power</note> belongs to derivative chapters; but if you carefully observe what I write, you will satisfy your wish with this rule. For when a square and a square-square are equal to a number, the ratio will be the same as in the simple case—namely, a double equation, one plus and the other minus, and equal to each other. For example, 1 square-square plus 3 squares equals 28, the position is worth 2 or minus 2. But truly, if a square-square and a number are equal to squares, we will indeed demonstrate in the eighth chapter that there are two values of the thing in true numbers, and it will have just as many by minus, each corresponding equal to the other. For example, if I say 1 square-square plus 12 equals 7 squares, the value of the position is either 2, or minus 2, or the root <note>original: "Rz," the symbol for radix.</note> of 3, or minus the root of 3, and thus there are four equations <note>meaning four possible roots/solutions</note>. But if it should lack a true value, it will also lack that which is by minus; for example, 1 square-square plus 12 equals 6 squares; because it cannot have a true equation, it will also lack a fictitious one <note>Cardano uses "fictitious" (ficta) to refer to negative roots/estimations.</note>—for so we call that which is of debt or less. But truly, if a square-square is equal to a number and squares, there is always one true value of the thing, and another equal to it which is fictitious or by minus. For example, if 1 square-square is equal to 2 squares plus 8, the value of the thing is 2, or minus 2. The same reasoning applies to the others...

<summary>Cardano explains that algebraic powers correspond to physical dimensions (line, surface, solid) and argues that going beyond the cube is "against nature." He also defines how even powers (like squares) result in both positive and negative solutions, which he calls "true" and "fictitious" values.</summary>
<keywords>even denominations, odd denominations, square-square, fictitious roots, negative numbers, estimation, dimension of the cube, Ars Magna</keywords>

# ON ARITHMETIC, BOOK X
**A 4**

...[when they are] equal in all their denominations among themselves, and are joined with a number, how this may be done through depression <term>depression</term> <note>original: "depressionem," the algebraic process of reducing the degree of an equation, such as dividing $x^3$ by $x$ to get $x^2$.</note>, we have fully taught in the fourth book.

<margin>4</margin>
But for odd denominations <note>powers of the unknown like $x^1, x^3, x^5$.</note>, only one true estimation <term>estimation</term> <note>original: "æstimatio," meaning the value of the root or solution for $x$.</note> exists, and none is feigned <term>feigned</term> <note>original: "ficta," Cardano’s term for negative roots, which were considered "false" or "fictitious" at the time because you cannot have a negative physical length.</note>, when they are compared to a number alone. For instance, if two things <note>original: "res," the unknown $x$.</note> are equal to 16, the estimation of the thing is 8. If two cubes are equal to 16, the estimation of the thing is 2. However, the number to which the denominations are compared in this chapter is always assumed to be true <note>positive</note>, not feigned; for what would be so foolish as to weaken the foundation itself? Nevertheless, the opposite reasoning must be followed in opposite cases. The logic is therefore the same where many denominations are compared to a number; even if there were a thousand of them, there will be one true estimation of the thing, and none feigned. For example, 1 cube plus 6 positions <note>original: "positionibus," another term for the unknown $x$.</note> is equal to 20; the estimation of the thing is none other than 2, neither true nor feigned.

<margin>5</margin>
When, however, two denominations are compared with a number, either both are odd, and the comparison is made to the extreme or to the middle (for the case made to the number has already been discussed in the preceding rule), or one is odd and the other even (for we spoke generally about both being even in the third rule). If, therefore, the extreme denomination—namely the cube—is compared with the number and the middle denomination—that is, the positions—see whether by multiplying two-thirds of the number of Things by the root of one-third of that same number, the resulting number is equal to, greater than, or less than the proposed number. If it results in the proposed number precisely, the estimation of the thing is twofold: one is true, namely the root itself which was multiplied. 

**Example:** A cube plus 16 is equal to 12 positions <note>$x^3 + 16 = 12x$</note>. By multiplying 8 (which is $\frac{2}{3}$ of 12, the number of things) by 2 (the root of 4, which is $\frac{1}{3}$ of the number of things), the result is 16, the proposed number of the equation. Therefore, the estimation is 2 (the root of 4). And there is another feigned estimation, which corresponds to the true one of a cube equal to those same things and the same number. As in the example: if a cube is equal to 12 things plus the number 16 <note>$x^3 = 12x + 16$</note>, the true estimation is 4. Therefore, if a cube plus 16 is equal to 12 positions, the estimation of the thing is minus 4 <note>original: "m: 4"</note>. For 12 things are minus 48, and the cube of minus 4 is minus 64; when 16 is added to this, it becomes minus 48. 

But if the product of $\frac{2}{3}$ of the number of things multiplied by the root of one-third of that same number exceeds the proposed number of the equation, then the chapter will have three equations: two true ones and a third feigned one. 

**Example:** 1 cube plus 9 is equal to 12 things <note>$x^3 + 9 = 12x$</note>. One true equation <note>root</note> is 3; another is the root of $5 \frac{1}{4}$ minus $1 \frac{1}{2}$ <note>$\sqrt{5.25} - 1.5$</note>. The third feigned one is always the sum of these two, and it corresponds to the true estimation of a cube equal to those same things and that same number; it is the root of $5 \frac{1}{4}$ plus $1 \frac{1}{2}$, and thus the remaining feigned one, which we spoke of in the other example, is the sum of the two true ones. But because the true ones are equal to each other in that case, the feigned one is always double the true one. It is clear, therefore, that the false or feigned equations of the chapter "cube and number equal to things" correspond to the true equations of the chapter "cube equal...

# THE WORKS OF GIROLAMO CARDANO

...[will be] equal to things and a number, where the things and number are the same. But truly, when from such a multiplication of the root of a third part of the number of things, into two-thirds of the same number, there results less than the proposed number, then there will be no true equation <note>original: "æquatio uera," a positive solution</note> but only a feigned one <term>feigned</term> <note>original: "ficta," meaning a negative solution, which Cardano considered "fictitious" even as he used them.</note>. This feigned solution is equal to the true solution of the chapter on the cube equal to just as many things and the same number. For example, 1 cube plus 21 is equal to 2 things <note>$x^3 + 21 = 2x$</note>; although it lacks a true equation, the feigned one is nonetheless minus 3, and this is the true estimation of the cube equal to two things and the number twenty-one <note>$x^3 = 2x + 21$</note>.

<margin>6</margin> From these things, it is not difficult to hunt out how many equations the chapter on the **cube equal to things and a number** <note>$x^3 = ax + n$</note> may have. If, therefore, from two-thirds of the number of things multiplied by the root of a third part of that same number, the proposed number is produced, the chapter has two equations: a true one equal to the feigned one of the preceding rule, and a feigned one equal to the true one. Thus, the true one is double the feigned one, because in that case the feigned was double the true. For example, if 1 cube is equal to 12 things and the number 16 <note>$x^3 = 12x + 16$</note>, the true equation is 4, and the feigned is minus 2; because if 1 cube plus 16 is equal to 12 positions <note>$x^3 + 16 = 12x$</note>, the true estimation is 2, and the feigned is minus 4.

But if from the said multiplication there should come forth more than the number of the equation, the true estimation will be one, corresponding to the false one of the preceding rule, and the false one will be double, each responding to the true one of the preceding rule. For example, if a cube is equated to 12 positions plus 9 <note>$x^3 = 12x + 9$</note>, both false estimations are the root of $5 \frac{1}{4}$ minus $1 \frac{1}{2}$ <note>$\sqrt{5.25} - 1.5 \approx 0.791$</note> and 3, and the true one is the root of $5 \frac{1}{4}$ plus $1 \frac{1}{2}$ <note>$\sqrt{5.25} + 1.5 \approx 3.791$</note>. Thus you see how the false respond to the true, and the true to the false in turn; moreover, the true is composed of both false ones, for from the root of $5 \frac{1}{4}$ minus $1 \frac{1}{2}$ and 3, comes the root of $5 \frac{1}{4}$ plus $1 \frac{1}{2}$ <note>Note that Cardano is observing the relationship between roots: $x_1 + x_2 + x_3 = 0$ for $x^3 + px + q = 0$.</note>. But if from such a product there results less than the number of the equation, the estimation is only one and true, just as in the preceding rule there is only one and feigned—such as if a cube is equal to two things and the number 21, the equation is 3, just as in the cube plus 21 equal to two things, the feigned estimation is minus 3.

<margin>7</margin> However, in the chapters in which a number and both even and odd denominations are equated to each other—either the even is the extreme <note>the highest power, like $x^2$ in $x^2 + x = n$</note>, such as when the square and the position and the number are equated to each other; or the extreme denomination is odd, such as when the cube and square are equated to a number—if therefore the square is equated to positions and a number <note>$x^2 = ax + n$</note>, it will have two equations: one true, equal to the feigned one of the chapter of the square and the same things being equal to the same number, and another feigned one, equal to the true one of that other chapter. 

**Example:** If a square and 4 positions are equal to 21 <note>$x^2 + 4x = 21$</note>, the true estimation is 3, and the feigned is minus 7. And if the square is equal to 4 positions and 21 <note>$x^2 = 4x + 21$</note>, the true estimation is 7, and the feigned is minus 3. Therefore, once the true ones are found, the feigned ones are mutually found, just as in the preceding rule, but in a different way: for here the extremes are compared to extremes, while there the middle terms are compared to the extremes. For there, the chapter of the cube and number equal to things is compared to the chapter of the cube equal to things and a number; here, the chapter of the square and things equal to a number is compared to the chapter of the square equal to things and a number.

He uses specific numerical examples to show how roots can be derived by comparing different algebraic structures.</summary>

# ON ARITHMETIC BOOK X.
<sig>B</sig>
<meta>catchword: pituli,</meta>

<margin>8</margin>
But when the square and the number are equal to things <note>Modern form: $x^2 + n = ax$</note>, and the case is possible, then there are two true solutions <note>original: "duæ ſolutiones ueræ," meaning two positive roots</note>, as by saying the square plus 12 is equal to 7 positions <note>$x^2 + 12 = 7x$</note>, the position can be 4 or even 3. For it is verified in both, except when the number is equal to the square of half the number of roots <note>i.e., when $n = (a/2)^2$, resulting in a single repeated root</note>, for then there is only one equation, namely half of the number of those roots. In this chapter, however, there can never be a feigned solution <term>feigned solution</term> <note>original: "solutio ficta," Cardano's term for a negative root</note>, nor an equation through "minus." But where there is a solution through a true number, it is double; where it lacks a true solution, it cannot be solved any further by a feigned equation.

<margin>8</margin>
If indeed an equation is sought in the chapters of the cube, squares, and the number, then if the cube is equal to the squares and the number <note>$x^3 = ax^2 + n$</note>, then there is only one true solution, just as if I say, the cube is equal to three squares plus 16 <note>$x^3 = 3x^2 + 16$</note>, the thing is worth 4, and no other can be found.

<margin>NOTE</margin>
**NOTE WELL.** In all chapters in which there is only one solution, the equation is easier to find and more elegant, such as in the chapter of the cube and things equal to a number <note>$x^3 + ax = n$</note>, and the cube equal to the square and the number <note>$x^3 = ax^2 + n$</note>, and in the chapter of the cube equal to things and a number <note>$x^3 = ax + n$</note>, where that product of $\frac{2}{3}$ of the number <gloss>of things</gloss> into the square root of a third part <gloss>of the things</gloss> is less than the number. I say the same where the cube with the number is equal to things <note>$x^3 + n = ax$</note>, and only a feigned equation can be had; the others, however, in which the estimation <term>estimation</term> <note>original: "æstimatio," the value of the unknown or the root</note> of the thing is manifold, are more difficult and confused.

If therefore the cube and the square are equal to a number <note>$x^3 + ax^2 = n$</note>, then the estimation of the thing is only one through "plus" <note>a positive root</note>, where from $\frac{1}{3}$ of the number of the square into the square of two-thirds of the same number, there is produced less than the number of the equation. And this same estimation is the feigned one of the corresponding chapter of the cube and the number equal to squares under the same quantity. Example: The cube and three squares are equal to 20 <note>$x^3 + 3x^2 = 20$</note>; then, because from 1 (the third part of the number of squares) into 4 (the square of two-thirds) it becomes less than 20, I say that there is only one equation, and the thing is worth 2; and this is the estimation through "minus" <note>the negative root</note> of the cube plus 20 being equal to three squares <note>$x^3 + 20 = 3x^2$</note>. But where such a number can be produced from that multiplication, there will be one true estimation and two feigned ones, and the true will correspond to the feigned of the other chapter, and again the feigned to the true ones. Example: if I say, the cube and 11 squares are equal to 72 <note>$x^3 + 11x^2 = 72$</note>, the thing is the square root of 40 minus 4 <note>$\sqrt{40} - 4$</note> for the true estimation, but for the feigned it is 3 minus <note>$-3$</note> or the square root of 40 plus 4 minus <note>$-(\sqrt{40} + 4)$</note>. And if the cube with 72 is equal to 11 squares <note>$x^3 + 72 = 11x^2$</note>, the true estimations are 3 or the square root of 40 plus 4, and the feigned is the square root of 40 minus 4 minus <note>$-(\sqrt{40} - 4)$</note>. Therefore, in seeking the feigned solution, we always seek the true one and the corresponding one of the other chapter.

# GIROLAMO CARDANO

[of the] chapters, they subsequently have only one solution in the rest, just as the chapter of the **cube equal to things and a number** <note>original: "cubi æqualis rebus & numero," modern form: $x^3 = ax + n$</note> in the lower part, and the chapter of the **cube and squares equal to a number** <note>$x^3 + ax^2 = n$</note>, and the chapter of the **cube and number equal to squares or things** <note>$x^3 + n = ax^2$ or $x^3 + n = ax$</note>. For in one part they have three equations, and in the other subsequently only one. Similarly, the chapter of the **square-square and number equal to the square** <note>$x^4 + n = ax^2$</note> has four equations in one part, and subsequently none in the other. Some indeed have two throughout, such as the chapter of the **square and things equal to a number** <note>$x^2 + ax = n$</note>, or the chapter of the **square equal to things and a number** <note>$x^2 = ax + n$</note>. Those which have only one are such as the chapter of the **cube and things equal to a number** <note>$x^3 + ax = n$</note>, and the chapter of the **square and number equal to things** <note>$x^2 + n = ax$</note>, which has two equations in one part, and subsequently none in the other.

<margin>Note well.</margin>
And you should know that the equations of the chapters of the **cube and squares equal to a number**, and likewise the **cube and number equal to squares**, are related such that the difference between the true and feigned solutions <term>feigned solutions</term> <note>original: "æquationum uerarum & fictarum," meaning positive and negative roots. Cardano calls negative roots "feigned."</note> is always the number of the squares. For example, if the **cube and 72 are equal to 11 squares** <note>$x^3 + 72 = 11x^2$</note>, the feigned solution is the root of 40 minus 4 <note>$\sqrt{40} - 4$</note>, and the true solutions are the root of 40 plus 4 <note>$\sqrt{40} + 4$</note> and 3. The difference between the root of 40 minus 4 and [the sum of] 7 plus the root of 40 is 11, the number of the squares <note>Cardano is observing that the sum of the roots equals the coefficient of the $x^2$ term, accounting for the sign of the "feigned" root: $(\sqrt{40} + 4) + 3 - (-(\sqrt{40} - 4)) = 11$ is not his logic here; rather he is looking at the magnitude of the difference.</note>. And it is the same if the cube and 11 squares were equal to the number 72.

<margin>9</margin>
In these chapters, however, which consist of a double denomination, one odd and one even, plus a number <note>Referring to equations with terms like $x^3$ (odd power) and $x^2$ (even power)</note>, if the **cube and things are equal to squares and a number** <note>$x^3 + ax = bx^2 + n$</note>, the solutions can be three, and all of them true, and none feigned. This is because, as has been said, when a "minus" is led to a solid <note>i.e., when a negative number is cubed</note>, it becomes minus, and thus a minus would be equal to a plus, which cannot be.

Where indeed the **cube, square, and thing are equal to a number** <note>$x^3 + ax^2 + bx = n$</note>, then there will also be three solutions: one plus and two minus. This occurs if, under the same denominations, the squares can be equal to the things, the number, and the cube; and the true solutions here are the feigned ones in that example. Thus, if **1 cube plus 6 squares and 3 things is equal to 18** <note>$x^3 + 6x^2 + 3x = 18$</note>, then the true valuation of the thing is found from its own chapter. Then it has the feigned valuations of the chapter **1 cube plus 3 things plus 18 equal to 6 squares** <note>$x^3 + 3x + 18 = 6x^2$</note>. One of these is 3, another is the root of $8\frac{1}{4}$ plus $1\frac{1}{2}$. Therefore, minus 3, or minus (root of $8\frac{1}{4}$ plus $1\frac{1}{2}$), is the feigned valuation of **1 cube plus 6 squares plus 3 positions equal to 18**. And with this, there is also a third true solution.

From this are obtained the three solutions of the chapter of the **cube, things, and number equal to squares**, where the solution is possible. This is known from its own chapters. Two of these, therefore, are true and equal, as was said, to the solutions of the chapter of the same number of squares, things, and the cube equal to the same number. As in the said example, the third [solution] corresponds to the true one of the other chapter and is feigned. Therefore, the solution of the chapter **1 cube plus 6 squares plus 3 [things]**...

<page-num>6</page-num>
# ON ARITHMETIC, BOOK 10

Subsequently, the true solution is, by [the rule of] the chapter, **1 cube plus 3 things plus 18 equal to 6 squares**. <note>original: "1 cub^i p: 3 rebus p: 18 æqualiũ 6 quadratis," modern form: $x^3 + 3x + 18 = 6x^2$</note> But when the number of squares is too small to be equal to the cube, things, and number, then in the chapter of the **cube, squares, and things equal to a number**, there is one true solution and no feigned one. <note>Cardano uses the term "feigned" (ficta) to refer to what we now call negative roots.</note> However, in the chapter of **squares equal to the cube, things, and number**, there is one feigned solution and no true one; for example, if we say **1 cube plus 1 square plus 2 things are equal to 16**, the true value of the thing is 2. And this is the feigned solution for the **cube, two things, and 16 equal to 1 square**. It is clear, therefore, that the chapters of the **cube, squares, and things equal to a number** and the **cube, things, and number equal to squares** correspond to each other.

<margin>10</margin>
Similarly, the chapter of the **cube equal to squares, things, and number** corresponds to the chapter of the **cube, squares, and number equal to things**. Therefore, where the things are very few, there is one feigned solution, which corresponds to the true solution of the other chapter of the **cube equal to the same number of squares, things, and number**. For example: if a **cube is equal to 2 squares, 1 position, and the number 6**, the thing is worth 3, and no more or less. <note>In modern terms: $x^3 = 2x^2 + x + 6$. If $x=3$, then $27 = 18 + 3 + 6$.</note> This is because if the cube, 2 squares, and the number 6 were equal to one position, there could be no true solution, but there would be a feigned one of minus 3, which was the true one in the other chapter. But if there are so many things that the chapter of the **cube, squares, and number equal to things** can have a true solution, then the true solution will be double, and there will be one feigned one, corresponding to the two feigned and one true solution of the other chapter. For example: if the **cube, 3 squares, and the number 6 are equal to 20 things**, there will be two true solutions, namely **3** and the **root of 11 minus 3**, and one feigned one, namely the **root of 11 plus 3, minus**. <note>Cardano expresses the negative root $-(3 + \sqrt{11})$ as "$\sqrt{11}$ plus 3, minus."</note> Therefore, the true solution for the **cube equal to 3 squares, 20 things, and the number 6** is the **root of 11 plus 3**, and there will be two feigned ones: **3 minus** and the **root of 11 minus 3, minus**.

<margin>11</margin>
By the same reasoning, the chapters of the **cube and squares equal to things and number**, and the **cube and number equal to squares and things**, correspond to each other. Thus, where the chapter of the **cube and number equal to things and squares** does not have a true solution, it will have only one feigned one, equal to the true one of the other chapter. For example: **1 cube plus 72 is equal to 6 squares plus 3 things**. The feigned value of the thing is **minus 3**, and this is the true solution for **one cube and 6 squares equal to 3 things and 72**. And just as the chapter of **1 cube plus 72 equal to 6 squares plus 3 things** lacks a true solution, so the chapter of **1 cube plus 6 squares equal to 3 things and 72** lacks a feigned one. But where the chapter of the **cube and number equal to squares and things** has a true solution, it will have two, and one feigned one, corresponding to the two feigned and one true solution of the other chapter. For example: let a **cube plus 4 be equal to 3 squares plus 5 things**. Then the true values are **4**, or the **root of 1 1/4 minus 1/2**. <note>$\sqrt{1.25} - 0.5 \approx 0.618$</note> The feigned one is the **root of 1 1/4 plus 1/2, minus**, and this is the true value for the chapter of the cube...

B 2
<meta>catchword: bi (the start of the word "cubi" or "cube")</meta>

<summary>Cardano explains how different cubic equation forms are related, demonstrating that a "true" (positive) root in one algebraic configuration becomes a "feigned" (negative) root when terms are shifted to the opposite side of the equals sign.</summary>
<keywords>cubic equations, negative roots, algebraic transposition, Girolamo Cardano, Ars Magna, mathematical proofs</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of cubic and quadratic equations from page 5, where Cardano explains how "true" (positive) solutions in one form of an equation become "feigned" (negative) solutions in another form when terms are rearranged. Here, he begins to provide geometric proofs for these algebraic transformations.</meta>

# GIROLAMO CARDANO

...[the cube] and 3 squares equal to 5 things and the number 4, and the remaining two, namely 4 and the square root of 1 1/4 minus 1/2, <note>original: "℞ 1 1/4 m: 1/2" refers to the result of the quadratic formula applied to a specific case.</note> are minus in the same case and feigned. <note>Cardano uses "feigned" (ficta) to describe what we now call negative solutions or roots.</note>

<margin>12</margin>
It is also manifest that if biquadrates, <term>biquadrate<gloss>the fourth power of the unknown, or x⁴</gloss></term> things, and number are compared, the seventh rule will apply to them precisely as it does in the square, things, and number, by matching chapter to chapter; the same reasoning holds for the other derivative powers.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

<margin>13</margin>
It is now appropriate that we show these things by demonstration—as we shall do throughout this entire book—so that for such admirable matters, a rational basis of belief may be added beyond mere experience. 

For the sake of example, let **AB** be the cube, which with the number **BC** is equal to the squares **DE** with the things **EF**, and let **H** be the true estimate. <note>In this context, the "estimate" (æstimatio) refers to the value of the unknown, x.</note>

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;A &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; B &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; C
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; ———————
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; H
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; D &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; G &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; E &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; F
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; ———————

Because, therefore, by the supposition, **AC** is equal to **DF**, let **DG** be made equal to **AB**. Because **DE** exceeds **AB** by the segment **GE**, and **BC** is equal to **GF**, it follows by common sense <note>Common notions or axioms of geometry.</note> that **BC** will be greater than **FE** by the amount **GE**; and whatever the excess of **DE** over **AB**, such is the excess of **BC** over **EF**. 

Now, let **H** be assumed as a "minus" and feigned equation; therefore **AB** and **EF** will be minus, but **DE** and **BC** remain plus. Because the difference between **AB** and **DE** is **GE**, and the difference between **BC** and **EF** is also **GE**, and subtracting **AB** from **DE** and **EF** from **BC** is the same as adding them as "minus" terms, it follows that if the estimate of the position is set as minus **H**, then the cube (**AB**) with the squares (**DE**) is equal to the things (**EF**) and the number (**BC**). For each aggregate is the residue **GE**. Thus, the cube with the squares is equal to things and number in the same way, and the estimate of the thing is minus **H**, just as it was the same in the other equations.

It also follows that the sum of the parts in one is equal to the mutual difference in the other. For instance, if I say: a **cube and 10 are equal to 6 squares and 8 things**, and the estimate in this chapter is true, then in the chapter of the **cube and 6 squares equal to 8 things and the number 10**, the feigned estimate will be such that the sum of the cube and 6 squares is equal to the difference of the cube and 6 squares in the true estimate, or equal to the 10 and 8 things in that same true estimate; and such will be the sum of the 8 things and the number in the feigned equation.

## On the Number of All Chapters. Chapter II.

The chapters which it is generally fitting to know extend as far as the solid cube. The simple chapters, <note>Simple equations (like x³ = ax) where only two terms are compared.</note> because they are of one kind, we have contracted into one, although they extend to infinity. However, those that have the square and position along with a number are three; and although they may obtain two...

# ON ARITHMETIC, BOOK X.

...the solutions are considered as one type; however, because they are joined together, we will say there are only three chapters. But truly, there are three [chapters] of the cube, things, and number, yet since one of them has two equations, they result in four. Just as many come from the cube, squares, and number, so now there are twelve. Then there are seven [chapters] of the cube of squares, <note>original: "cubi quadratorum," likely referring to $x^6$.</note> positions, and number, but in four of them there are twin equations, wherefore all will become eleven chapters. Therefore, there are twenty-three primary and general chapters. Neglecting the first of these, any of them joins two derivatives to itself, one by reason of the square and the other by reason of the cube; there will therefore be forty-four general derivatives. After these, there are two others of an unknown quantity, one when it is multiplied, the other when it is taken by itself. There is, furthermore, one general [chapter] of means. <term>means<gloss>In this context, middle terms or proportional values in a sequence.</gloss></term> Thus, the number of all notable primary chapters is twenty-six, the derivatives are forty-four, and the collection of all is seventy. After these, we have added many other individual ones, but there is greater pleasure than necessity in them; therefore, we shall not number them among these.

<margin>a</margin>
The necessity of these is gathered as follows: when lines are known by surfaces, or surfaces by lines, the chapters of squares, positions, and number are appropriate. But if from a square <note>original: "Tetragonico," from the Greek for "four-angled" or square.</note> or solid side, <note>Cardano is referring to square roots and cube roots as "sides."</note> it is a simple chapter. However, when two unknowns of the three are assumed, and these pertain to surfaces and lines, the chapters of the unknown quantity and of the "thing" <note>original: "rei." In 16th-century algebra, the "thing" (res) is the unknown x.</note> must be explored. And these are simple if lines are compared to lines; but products are used when compared with surfaces. If bodies <term>bodies<gloss>three-dimensional volumes or solids.</gloss></term> are to be compared to lines, we use [chapters] of the cube of things and number; if to bodies, the surfaces of the cube of squares and number. But if the ratio of surface, bodies, and lines must be sought, the chapters of the cube of squares, positions, and number are more useful. Furthermore, in all of these, a comparison will always be made to a number. This method is the most important, although it is often necessary to use all of them in each case; nevertheless, it will be worthwhile to describe each of these individually and to join the derivatives to their primitives. They are as follows:

->Primitive chapters lacking derivatives.<-

<margin>1</margin>
Number equal to things, or number equal to squares, or number equal to cubes, or number equal to biquadrates, <note>original: "$qd^{ra}qd^{ra}$" or squared-squares, meaning $x^4$.</note> or number equal to the name or first relatum, <term>first relatum<gloss>The fifth power of the unknown, or $x^5$.</gloss></term> and so on by comparing the number to any denomination.

<margin>2</margin>
Number and squares equal to things, or number and cube equal to things, or number and cube equal to squares, or number and biquadrate equal to things, or number and the square of a biquadrate <note>original: "qdrati qdra," meaning $x^8$.</note> equal to the square, or number and biquadrate equal to cubes, or number and the first name <note>original: "nomen primum," referring again to the fifth power.</note> equal to things or squares or cubes, and so on without end.

### On the equations of simple chapters. Chapter III.

**T**he **evaluation** <term>evaluation: *estimatio*, referring to the numerical solution or value of the unknown</term> of the thing is the quantity in which we experience the truth of the matters proposed in the chapter and the question. An example is when someone says: I made two parts out of 10, and I multiplied each of them by itself, and the difference of the products was 60. Since we do not know what quantity might be larger or smaller, we will set the smaller to be the unknown thing, which we call the **position** <term>position: *positio*, the standard term for the unknown variable $x$</term>; therefore, the larger part will be the remainder of 10, namely **10 minus 1 position**. Then we follow what is proposed, and we multiply the parts by themselves: the square of the smaller becomes **1 square**,

| | |
|---|---|
| 1 position | 1 square. |
| 10 minus 1 position | 1 square plus 100 minus 20 positions. |
| --- | --- |
| 1 square plus 20 positions | 1 square plus 100. |
| --- | --- |
| **60 plus 20 positions are equal to 100.** | |
| --- | --- |
| **20 positions are equal to 40.** | |

and [the square] of the larger is **1 square plus 100 minus 20 positions**. Add that which is "minus" to the other part; it will become 1 square plus 100 on one side, and 1 square plus 20 positions [on the other]. The difference between these was 60 by hypothesis; we will therefore add 60 to the smaller part, and then they become equal: 1 square plus 100, and 1 square plus 20 positions plus 60. We cast out 1 square and 60 from each side; there will remain, therefore, **20 positions equal to 40**. For if equals are taken away from equals, those which remain are equal <note>This is a reference to Euclid's Common Notions.</note>. By dividing 40 by 20 (the number of positions), the result will be 2, the **evaluation** of the position. In this 2, therefore, we experience the truth of the proposed question: for if its square, which is 4, is cast out from 64 (the square of 8, the remainder of 10 minus 2), the proposed Number 60 is left. It is also true of 2 what is proposed in the chapter: namely, that its square, which is 4, along with 100, is equal to the square of the position (which is again 4) and 20 positions (which are 40) and 60 joined together.

Cardano provides a step-by-step example of solving a word problem by setting up an equation, simplifying it using Euclidean logic, and verifying the solution.</summary>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues Chapter III from the previous page, moving from the definition of the "evaluation" (finding the value of the unknown) into the geometric proof and general rules for solving simple algebraic equations where two different powers of the unknown are set equal to one another.</meta>

->GIROLAMO CARDANO<-
<meta>Catchword: I understand</meta>

for in both ways 104 is collected; we shall therefore say deservedly, on account of two things, that 2 is the evaluation <term>evaluation: *aestimatio*, the root or value that solves the equation</term> of the thing, and when you have operated correctly in the evaluation or equation, either experience succeeds.

### DEMONSTRATION.

<margin>2</margin>
In order that the truth of the thing <term>thing: *res*, the unknown variable, commonly 'x'</term> might be more clearly grasped, and with it the reason—for to know is to understand through demonstration, as they say—let there be, for example, three cubes equal to 24, and let **A C** be placed as the side of one cube, and **C D** of the second, and **D B** of the third. Therefore, because the cubes are equal to each other, the lines **A C**, **C D**, and **D B** will also be equal. Since, therefore, 24 is divided according to the number by which **A C** is in **A B** (which is 3), the quantity of the cubes will be made from the 19th [proposition] of the fifth [book] or the 17th of the seventh [book] of the *Elements* <note>original: "elementorum"; referring to Euclid's Elements, the foundation of geometry</note>, and the 31st [proposition] of the 11th [book] of the same: the cube of **A C** is equal to 8. Therefore, the side **A C** will be 2, the evaluation of the thing, from which a general rule is gathered.

### RULE.

<margin>3</margin>
Reduce the proposed two denominations <term>denominations: different powers of the unknown, such as $x^2$ or $x^3$</term> to a number, if a number is not present, by decreasing them equally. When one becomes a denomination and the other a number, divide the number by the number of the denomination; the result is the evaluation of that denomination. If that denomination is the "position" <term>position: *positio*, another term for the unknown 'x'</term>, you have the evaluation of the position. If it is another denomination, take the side or root of that number according to the quality of the denomination: if it is a square, take the square root; if a cube, the cube root; if a square-square <note>original: "q̃d' q̃dᵗⁱ", *quadrato-quadratum*, or $x^4$</note>, the root of the root; and so on. That side or root is the true evaluation of the position. 

**Example:** 20 cubes are equal to 180 first relates <term>first relates: *relata prima*, the fifth power of the unknown, $x^5$</term>. Because there is no simple number here, you shall set the lowest denomination—the cubes—as a simple number, namely 20. And the greater or higher denomination—the relates—you shall reduce by the cubes, and they become 180 squares. Therefore, divide the number 20 by the number of squares, 180; the result is 1/9, the evaluation of the square. But we seek the evaluation of the position, not the square; therefore, take the square root of 1/9, and it is 1/3, for the true evaluation. 

**Another Example:** 7 squares are equal to 21 cube-squares <note>original: "cub' q̃dᵗⁱ", *cubo-quadratum*, or $x^6$</note>. Reduce equally to a number, and they become 7 equal to 21 square-squares. Divide 7 by 21; the result is 1/3, and the square-square root <note>original: "℞' ℞' 1/3", the fourth root</note> of 1/3, which is the side of the square-square, is the evaluation of the thing. 

**Another:** 2 cubes are equal to 20 square-squares. Having reduced the cubes to a number, the square-square will arrive at the position; therefore, 20 positions are equal to 2. Divide 2 by 20; the result is 1/10. And because you divided with the number of positions, the evaluation of the position will be 1/10. 

**Another:** 20 are equal to 5 squares. Divide 20 by 5; the result is 4, the evaluation of the square; therefore, the evaluation of the thing is 2.

<margin>4</margin>
And so that I may satisfy all future chapters, you shall divide all the remaining terms and the number by the number of the greatest denomination; by the greatest

->I understand<-

<summary>Cardano provides a geometric proof for solving simple equations and establishes a general rule for reducing equations with two powers by dividing through to find the root.</summary>
<keywords>algebra, demonstration, rule of reduction, unknown, square root, cube root, fifth power, Girolamo Cardano, Euclid</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the reduction of algebraic equations. The text picks up mid-sentence from page 8, explaining how to simplify equations with higher powers by dividing through by the smallest common power of the unknown.</meta>

->GIROLAMO CARDANO<-

I understand [by this term] the higher power, and you shall lower [it] by a smaller denomination <term>denomination: the power or degree of the unknown, such as $x^2$ or $x^3$</term>, then you shall follow the rule of the chapter. For example, let there be 4 cubes equal to 12 squares and 8 positions <term>positions: *positionibus*, one of Cardano's terms for the unknown $x$</term>. The smaller denomination is the position; the number of the greater is 4. Therefore, you divide everything by 4, and you will have:

| | |
|----------|----------|
| 4 cubes | 12 squares plus 8 positions |
| 4 | |
| 1 square | 3 positions plus 2. |

1 square equal to 3 positions plus 2.

<margin>Corollary</margin>
From these things it is also clear that the simple position <term>position: the variable $x$</term> is far more widely applicable than "false positions" <note>The "Rule of False Position" was an ancient method of solving linear equations by guessing an answer and adjusting the result; Cardano is arguing that his algebraic method is more powerful because it handles higher powers like squares and cubes</note>. For it is extended to squares, to cubes, and to other denominations, and thus it has evaluations <term>evaluations: *aestimationes*, the roots or solutions to the equation</term> in roots, for which there is no use at all in false position. Indeed, what pertains to a number equal to positions is still more general than either [single or double] false position, as was clear in the first example; for by no false position is it possible to hunt down which parts vary ten squares whose difference might be 60, as was proposed there.

## On General and Particular Subject Equations. Chapter IV.

<margin>1</margin>
Those equations are called **particular** <term>particular: *singulares*, specific cases rather than universal rules</term> in which no chapter [of algebra] can be perfectly completed; and such are an integer or a fraction, and also every "side" <term>side: *latus*, a term for a root, as in the side of a square</term> of a number, whether it be square or cubic or of another kind, and, so to speak, every **simple quantity**. Likewise, all those consisting of two roots, one of which is a square [root] or a root of a root <note>original: "Rzⁱ Rzⁱ"; the fourth root, or $\sqrt{\sqrt{x}}$</note>, and generally an even root; hence those which consist of "two names" <term>two names: *binomium*, an expression like $a + \sqrt{b}$</term> and the **apotome** <term>apotome: a mathematical term for a binomial with a subtraction, like $a - \sqrt{b}$</term>, or what they call "recisa" of the third and sixth kind, are not suitable for a general equation.

<margin>2</margin>
Furthermore, every chapter that consists of a number, a square, a cube, and positions has those general equations which are derived from the chapter to which they are reduced, by adding or subtracting a third part of the number of squares, as will be shown in its proper place.

<margin>3</margin>
General evaluations, however, are: in the chapters of a square equal to "things" <term>things: *rebus*, another term for the unknown $x$</term> and a number, [the evaluation is] of the second kind consisting of two names, such as $\sqrt{19} + 3$; but in the chapter of a square and things equal to a number, a **second apotome**, such as $\sqrt{19} - 3$; but in the chapter of squares and a number equal to things, an apotome and a value consisting of two names of the first kind, such as $3 + \sqrt{2}$ and $3 - \sqrt{2}$. Where I say the "first kind," I also mean the fourth; and likewise where I say the "second," I also mean the fifth, both in the apotome and in the value consisting of two names.

<margin>4</margin>
But the universal equation of a single root belongs to derivative chap-

<meta>This page continues the discussion on algebraic "evaluations" (solutions or roots) from Chapter IV. The previous page ended while explaining how roots derived from different types of equations (like squares or cubes) correspond to specific algebraic rules.</meta>

->GIROLAMO CARDANO<-

...rules, whether cubic or square, and for those in which the square or cube of the root was the equation for the principals <term>principals: the primary terms of an equation, such as $x^2$ or $x^3$</term>, just as if this evaluation <term>evaluation: *aestimatio*, the value of the unknown $x$ that solves the equation</term> was suited to a square equal to things <term>things: *rebus*, Cardano's term for the unknown $x$</term> and a number, [such as] the root of 19 plus 3; in the chapter of the cube of the square <term>cube of the square: $x^6$</term> equal to cubes and a number existing under the same quantity, the equation will be the universal cubic root <note>original: "℞ v: cubica"</note> of the root of 19 plus 3.

<margin>5</margin> And just as a square root can be joined to nothing except a number to produce a general equation, so on the other hand, a cubic root joined to another cubic root can do so, but it cannot with a number. Therefore, when it is joined for a cube equal to things and a number <note>$x^3 = ax + b$</note>, it produces an equation, though not an entire one; but subtracted from each other, they produce the equation of the chapter for the cube and things equal to a number <note>$x^3 + ax = b$</note>. For example, the cubic root of 4 plus the cubic root of 2 is the equation for the chapter "cube equal to things and a number," and the cubic root of 4 minus the cubic root of 2 is the equation for the chapter "cube and things equal to a number."

<margin>6</margin> But the chapter of the cube equal to squares and a number <note>$x^3 = ax^2 + b$</note> has an equation consisting of three proportional quantities, of which the two extremes are cubic roots and the middle is a number, such as the cubic root of 16 plus 2 plus the cubic root of 4. But the chapter of the cube and squares equal to a number <note>$x^3 + ax^2 = b$</note> has an equation similar in all respects to the preceding, except that the number is minus <term>minus: *m:*, indicating a negative value</term>, such as the cubic root of 16 minus 2 plus the cubic root of 4.

<margin>7</margin> It must also be understood that simple roots are held to be for general equations, so that the chapters may also be simple; for instance, the cubic root serves the chapter of a number equal to a cube <note>$x^3 = n$</note>, the square [root for] a number equal to a square <note>$x^2 = n$</note>, and the *relatum* <term>relatum: the fifth power of the unknown, or $x^5$</term> for the chapter of a *relatum* equal to a number. And just as these simple roots cannot fit compound chapters, so no compound made from several incommensurable <term>incommensurable: roots that cannot be expressed as a simple ratio of one another</term> roots can fit a simple chapter.

### It shows the evaluation of the lesser compound chapters, 
### which are those of squares, the number, and things. Chapter V.

-> **D E M O N S T R A T I O N** <-

<margin>1</margin> **L**et the square FD and 6 things (for example) be equal to 91. Then I will make DB and DG, when they have been extended, to be 3—namely, half of 6, the number of things—and I will complete the square DGBC. From there, with CG and CB extended, [I will complete] the square AFEC, just as is done in the fourth [proposition] of the second [book] of the Elements <note>Cardano is referring to Euclid’s *Elements*, Book II, Prop. 4, which provides the geometric basis for squaring a binomial $(a+b)^2 = a^2 + 2ab + b^2$</note>. Therefore, because DB multiplied by AB, by the definition of the second book of the Elements, produces AD at the end, and from any number multiplied by the evaluation of the thing is produced the evaluation of those things—for instance, if the thing is 4, and there are five things, the five things will be 20, and just as much is produced...

<meta>This page continues the geometric demonstrations of algebraic solutions (evaluations) found in Cardano's Book 10, famously known as the *Ars Magna*. This page specifically deals with the geometric proof for equations of the type "things and a square equal to a number" (quadratic equations).</meta>

->ON ARITHMETIC BOOK X.<-

...is produced from the evaluation of the thing <term>thing: *res*, Cardano’s term for the unknown $x$</term> multiplied into the number of things, as we showed in chapter three. Therefore, since *BD* is 3 and *AB* is the evaluation of the thing, the surface *AD* will be equal to three things, or the evaluation of three things. Moreover, the surface *DE* is equal to *AD*, from the 43rd proposition of the first book of the Elements <note>original: "ex 43ª primi elementorum". This refers to Euclid’s *Elements*, which states that in any parallelogram, the complements of the parallelograms about the diameter are equal.</note>; therefore, it is also the evaluation of three other things. Thus, the two surfaces, *AD* and *DE*, are equal to 6 things, and consequently, they together with the square *FD* are 91. But the square *CD* is 9, because *BD* is 3; therefore the square *AC* is 100. For that reason, its side *AC* is 10. Since *BC* is 3, when *BC* is subtracted from *AC*, it leaves *AB* (the side *DF*) as 7.

## ANOTHER DEMONSTRATION.

<margin>2</margin>
Let *AB* now be the number of certain things, equal to a number $c$ and a square $D$. I shall make the square *BG* from half of *AB*, which shall be *GE*, from which I subtract the number $c$, so that the surface *EF* is equal to the number $c$. I set the side of the square of the surface *FB* to be *GH*. I say that the lines *BH* and *HA* are both sides of the square *D*; from which it follows that there will be two true evaluations <term>evaluations: *aestimationes*, the roots or solutions of the equation</term> for this chapter, the sum of which is equal to the number of things, namely *AB*. For it is established that the rectangle formed from *AH* into *HB*, together with the square *HG*, is equal to the square *BG*, from the 5th proposition of the 2nd book of the Elements <note>Euclid II.5: A fundamental geometric identity used to solve quadratic problems.</note>. However, the square *HG* was equal to the surface *FB*; therefore, the rectangle from *AH* into *HB* is equal to *EF*, and thus to the number $c$. Because that which is produced from *AB* into *HB*, from the 3rd proposition of the 2nd book of the Elements, is equal to the square *HB* and the rectangle *AH* into *HB*; therefore, that which is produced from the number of things *AB* into the evaluation of the thing which is *HB* is equal to the number $c$ and the square *HB*, which was to be proven. And similarly, by the same reasoning, the rectangle from *AB* into *AH* is equal to the square *AH* and the product of *AH* into *HB*. But from *AH* into *HB*, as was proven, the number $c$ is produced; therefore the rectangle from *AB* into *AH*—that is, from the number of things into the evaluation of the thing—is equal to the square of the thing and the proposed number.

From this it is clear that those who say (if *BH*, for the sake of example, is the evaluation of the thing and *GF* is 3) that the rectangle from *BH* into *GF* will be $3 GH$ or triple *GH* are mistaken. For this cannot be—namely, that a surface contains some line, neither in number nor by any other proportion—since infinite lines can exist in a surface. For a continuous quantity <term>continuous quantity: *quantitas continua*, a physical magnitude like a line or area that is infinitely divisible, unlike discrete numbers</term> receives no end to its division. But the truth is that if *GF* contains three units (for the sake of example), that is, three parts of the line *BH* divided...

<summary>Cardano provides two geometric proofs using Euclid's Elements to justify the solutions of quadratic equations, specifically demonstrating how a single equation can have two valid roots (evaluations). He also emphasizes the conceptual distinction between geometric areas and linear measurements.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the geometric demonstrations of algebraic solutions from the previous page. Cardano is concluding a discussion on how lines represent numbers and squares before moving to a new demonstration and general rules for solving quadratic equations.</meta>

->JEROME CARDANO<-

into as many parts as there are units in the number it is said to contain; for example, if **BH** is set as 12, **GF** will be 3, where **GF** is the fourth part of **BH**. It is then true that from **BH** into **GF** a surface is produced containing 36 square surfaces, of which the square side <term>square side: *tetragonicum latus*, the root or side of a square</term> of each is a unit—that is, one of those parts into which **BH** was divided into 12 and **GF** into 3. **Plato**, in the *Meno* <note>original: "Memnone". Cardano refers to the dialogue where Socrates teaches a slave boy how to find the side of a square with double the area of a given square.</note>, beautifully demonstrates this for both rational and irrational numbers.

Do not be surprised that this second demonstration is explained differently than by **Muhammad** <note>original: "Mahumete". This refers to Muhammad ibn Musa al-Khwarizmi, the 9th-century mathematician whose work founded algebra.</note>; for he, by changing the figure, showed it more practically, but nonetheless more obscurely, and only for one part—and that through many steps. Therefore, we, looking for ease and brevity, and so that we might satisfy both evaluations <term>evaluations: *aestimationes*, the roots or solutions of the equation</term> with a single demonstration, use this one.

# ANOTHER DEMONSTRATION.

<margin>3</margin> Now let the square **AC** in the third figure be equal to 6 things and the number 16 <note>In modern notation: $x^2 = 6x + 16$.</note>, and let **AD** be the number of things, namely 6. Therefore, the surface **AH** is 6 "positions" <term>positions: *positiones*, another term for the unknown $x$</term>, wherefore the remainder **DC** will be exactly 16. Let **AD** be divided into equal parts at **G**, and let squares **GB** and **GD** be made, which are **GK** and **GE**. Because **BC** is equal to **BA**, and **BK** is equal to **BG**, then **KC** will be equal to **GA**, and therefore also to **GD** and **FL**. And because **DE** and **DG** are equal, and likewise **DF** and **BG**, then **FE** will be equal to **DB**, and thus also equal to **FK**. Therefore, the two lines **FK** and **FH** are equal to **FL** and **FE**. Since the angles at **ADF** are right angles, the surface **FC** is equal to **LE**. But **FC** together with **FB** was 16; therefore **LE** together with **FB** was 16. Adding the square **GE**, which is 9 (for **GD** was 3), the square **GK** will be 25. Therefore, the side **GB** is 5. Adding **GA**, which is 3, the whole **AB** becomes 8, which is the evaluation of the thing <note>If $x = 8$, then $8^2 = 64$ and $6(8) + 16 = 48 + 16 = 64$. The geometry proves the solution $x=8$.</note>.

<margin>4</margin> Following these, we shall form three rules, for the memory of which we subjoin this **Verse**:
> *Querna, da bis. Nuquer, admi. Requan, Minue dami.*
<note>This is a mnemonic poem in Latin to help students remember how to solve three types of quadratic equations. The words are made of abbreviations for the terms involved.</note>

# RULE I.

It is common to each of these chapters that half the number of things be multiplied by itself. When, therefore, the square is equal to things and a number, which is signified by **Querna**—whether you understand only the first letter or include the following consonants after the first vowel—so that **Querna** signifies a **Square** <gloss>original: *quadratum*</gloss> equal to **things** <gloss>original: *rebus*</gloss> and a **number** <gloss>original: *numero*</gloss>; and **Nuquer** signifies a **Number** <gloss>original: *numerum*</gloss> equal to a square and things; and **Requan** signifies **things** <gloss>original: *res*</gloss> equal to a square and a number. In this **Querna** therefore, or the chapter of the square equal...

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of solving quadratic equations. Cardano is moving from geometric proofs to specific procedural rules and numerical examples for different cases of equations.</meta>

->## ON ARITHMETIC BOOK 10<-

...from these things and the number, you will add the number of the equation to the square of half the "things" <term>things: *res*, the unknown variable $x$</term>, and take the square root of the whole; to this, add half the number of things, and the sum is the evaluation <term>evaluation: *aestimatio*, the solution or root of the equation</term> of the thing. For example, let **1 square be equal to 10 things plus 144** <note>In modern notation: $x^2 = 10x + 144$</note>. Multiply 5 by itself, which makes 25, the square of half the things; add 144, which makes 169, the root <note>original: *Rz*, abbreviation for *radix*</note> of which is 13. To this, add 5, which is half the number of things; it makes 18, the evaluation of the thing. Again, let **1 square be equal to 2/3 of a thing plus 11**. Multiply 1/3 (half the number of things) by itself, which makes 1/9; add to it 11, which makes 11 and 1/9. Take the root, which is 3 and 1/3, to which you add 1/3 (half the number of things); it makes 3 and 2/3, the evaluation of the thing. Again, let **1 square be equal to 10 things plus 6**. Multiply 5 (half the number of things) by itself, which makes 25; add 6 to it, which makes 31. To the root of this, add 5 (half the number of things); the evaluation of the thing will be the **root of 31 plus 5**. Again, let **1 square be equal to the root of 12 things plus 22**. Multiply the root of 3 <note>Half of $\sqrt{12}$ is $\sqrt{3}$.</note> by itself, which makes 3, the square of half the number of things. Add 22 to it, making 25. The root of this is 5, to which you add the root of 3 (which is half the number of things). The evaluation of the thing will be **5 plus the root of 3**. And if in this case the number had been 20, the evaluation of the thing would be the root of 23 plus the root of 3. And if the number had been 9, the evaluation of the thing would be the root of 12 plus the root of 3—which is to say, the root of 27. And if **1 square was equal to the root of 12 things plus the cube root of 10** as the number: multiply as before the root of 3 (half the number of things) by itself, which makes 3. Add to it the cube root of 10, which makes 3 plus the cube root of 10. Take the root of this, which is the universal root <term>universal root: *radix universalis*, a root that applies to the entire following expression</term> of 3 plus the cube root of 10. To this, add half the number of things, and the evaluation of the thing will be the **root of 3 plus the universal root of 3 plus the cube root of 10**. We have used this variety of examples here so that you may understand that the same can be done in the rest, and also so you may experience the same in the two following rules, since we will be content with a double example. It is manifest, therefore, that here we add twice: namely, the number to the square of half the things, and half the things to the root of the sum; and this is what we said in the verse, "give, twice" <note>original: *da, bis*. A mnemonic device for the rule.</note>, as if to say, "join twice."

### RULE II.

<margin>5</margin> If, however, the number is equal to the square and the things <note>In modern notation: $x^2 + ax = n$</note>, you will add the number of the equation to the square of half the number of things, and take the root of the entire sum, from which you subtract half the number of things, and the remainder is the evaluation of the thing. For example: **144 is equal to 10 things and 1 square**. Multiply 5 (half of the 10 things) by itself, which makes 25. To this add 144, which makes 169. The root of this is 13, from which you cast away 5 (half the number of things); the evaluation of the thing 8 will be left. Again, let **6 be equal to 10 things plus 1 square**. 5 (half the things) multiplied by itself makes 25; add 6, making 31. From the root of this, cast away 5 (half the number of things); the equation is the **root of 31 minus 5**.

<margin>Corollary</margin> From this it is clear that this rule differs from the preceding one only in that it subtracts half the number of things from the root of the sum, whereas the other joined it. And this is what we said in the verse, "Add, diminish" <note>original: *Ad, mi*. Mnemonic for *Adde* (add) and *Minue* (subtract/diminish).</note>, as if to say, "add first, then..."

<sig>C 3</sig>

<summary>Cardano provides the operational rules and several numerical examples for solving two types of quadratic equations, using "things" to represent the unknown. He emphasizes the consistency of the method even when dealing with irrational numbers like square and cube roots.</summary>

<keywords>algebra, quadratic equations, square root, universal root, Jerome Cardano, mathematical rules, Ars Magna, irrational numbers</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Cardano's systematic explanation of solving quadratic equations, specifically addressing the case where the "things" (linear terms) are equal to the "squares" (quadratic terms) and a "number" (constant). This corresponds to the modern form $bx = x^2 + c$.</meta>

-># GEROLAMO CARDANO<-

...then subtract; that is, add the number to the square, and afterwards subtract half the number of things from the root of the sum. <note>This refers to the previous case, $x^2 + bx = c$, where the solution is $\sqrt{(b/2)^2 + c} - b/2$.</note>

<margin>Corollary</margin>
From this it is clear that the difference between the evaluation <term>evaluation: *aestimatio*, the value of the unknown root</term> of a square equal to things and a number <note>In modern terms: $x^2 = bx + c$</note>, and a number equal to things and a square <note>In modern terms: $c = bx + x^2$</note>, is exactly the number of things, provided they are established with the same number of things and the same constants. For example, the evaluation of **1 square equal to 10 things plus 144** is 18, and the evaluation of **144 equal to 1 square and 10 things** is 8, and the difference between 18 and 8 is 10.

### RULE III.

<margin>6</margin>
If, however, the things are equal to the squares and a number <note>The case $bx = x^2 + c$</note>, multiply, as before, half the number of things by itself, and having subtracted the number of the equation from it, subtract the root of the remainder from half the number of things, or add it; both the sum and the remainder are evaluations of the thing. Example: **1 square plus 16 is equal to 10 things**. 5 multiplied by itself makes 25, as before; then subtract 16 from 25, leaving 9, the root <note>original: *Rz*, for *radix*</note> of which is 3. This, being added to or subtracted from 5 (half the number of things), shows the evaluations of the thing: 8 when added, and 2 when subtracted. Thus, if 10 things are taken as 2, they will be 20, and the square of 2 with 16 will be just as much. Likewise, if 10 things are taken as 8, they will be 80, and the square of 8 plus 16 is just as much. Again, if I say **10 things are equal to 1 square plus 6**, 5 (half the number of things) multiplied by itself makes 25; having subtracted 6, 19 remains. Its root, added to or subtracted from 5, shows the evaluations of the thing: the greater being **5 plus the root of 19**, and the smaller being **5 minus the root of 19**.

<margin>Note</margin>
But if the subtraction of that number from the square of half the number of things cannot be done <note>i.e., if $(b/2)^2 - c$ results in a negative number</note>, the question itself is false, and what is proposed cannot be. For a universal rule in this entire treatise, it must always be observed that when the instructions cannot be carried out, then that which was proposed was not possible, nor could it be. Now, however, we will subjoin some problems, two from <term>Mahumete</term> <note>Cardano is referring to Al-Khwarizmi, the 9th-century Persian mathematician whose name was Latinized as Mahumete. He is considered the father of algebra.</note>, the rest our own—the most difficult of all those that use neither multiple substitutions nor specific rules.

### PROBLEM I.

<margin>First Problem</margin>
There is a number from whose square if you subtract 1/3 and 1/4 of that square, and in addition 4, and you then multiply the remainder by itself, the product will be equal to the square of that number plus 12. You should set the square of the unknown number you seek to be **1 thing** <term>thing: Cardano uses the variable "thing" (res) to represent the square of the final answer ($x^2 = y$) to simplify the calculation.</term>. Subtract 1/3 and 1/4 of it and also 4; it becomes **5/12 of a thing minus 4**. Multiply this by itself, and it makes **25/144 of a square plus 16 minus 3 and 1/3 things** <note>In our notation: $( \frac{5}{12}x - 4 )^2 = \frac{25}{144}x^2 - \frac{40}{12}x + 16$</note>. This is equal to one thing and 12. Cast away similar terms, and it becomes **1 thing equal to 25/144 of a square plus 4 minus 3 and 1/3 things**. Move the negative part to the other side, according to the universal rule; there will be **4 and 1/3 things equal to 25/144 of a square plus 4**. Therefore, by the fourth rule of the third chapter, divide the number of things and the 4 by 25/144 (the coefficient of the square), and it becomes **24 and 24/25 things equal to 23 and 1/25 plus 1 square**. Wherefore, by the third rule, you will multiply...
