# ON ARITHMETIC BOOK X.

...and 17 2/3 <term>minus</term> <note>original: "m:"</note>: <term>position</term> <note>original: "pos:", referring to the first unknown variable $x$</note>; having cast out 1/2 of the quantity from both sides, and restored the <term>minus</term> to the other side, it becomes 11 <term>quantities</term> <note>original: "quan:", referring to a second unknown variable $y$</note> <term>plus</term> <note>original: "p:"</note> 1/4 position equals 17 2/3. Therefore, 11 quantities plus 3 positions will equal 212, after multiplying all parts by the denominator 12. From there, you bring either of them to the equality of the other in the number of positions or quantities—for instance, by saying: 3 positions plus 11 quantities are equal to 212, but I now want there to be 7 positions. By the rule of four proportional quantities, 25 2/3 quantities will be equal to 494 2/3. You have therefore, as you see, 7 positions plus 3 quantities equal to 132, and 7 positions plus 25 2/3 quantities equal to 494 2/3. Therefore, since the 7 positions are the same, the difference of the quantities in both cases, namely 22 2/3, will be equal to the difference of the numbers, which is 362 2/3. Divide therefore, just as in a simple position, according to the <note>The "third chapter" refers to the rule for solving simple linear equations of the form $ax = b$.</note> third chapter, 362 2/3 by 22 2/3; the result is 16, the <term>valuation</term> <note>The "valuation" (aestimatio) is the solution or numerical value of the unknown.</note> of the quantity, and that is how much the second person had.

Again, let us set the first to be a <term>thing</term> <note>Cardano uses "thing" (res) and "position" (positio) interchangeably for the unknown.</note>, the second we already found to be 16, and let the third be the second quantity. Since the second person, with a third part of the first and third, has 28, and he himself has 16, then 1/3 of a position plus 1/3 of the quantity will be equal to 12 (the remainder of 16 and 28). Therefore, 1 position plus 1 quantity will equal 36. But truly, the first person, with half of the others, had 32; half of the others is 8 plus 1/2 a quantity. Therefore, 1 position plus 8 plus 1/2 a quantity equals 32. Thus, by subtracting 8, it becomes 1 position plus 1/2 a quantity equals 24. Because 1 position plus 1 quantity was equal to 36, the difference between 24 and 36, which is 12, is equal to half of the quantity. Therefore, by the method of the third chapter, dividing 12 by 1/2, the result is 24, the valuation of the quantity, or the number of <term>gold pieces</term> <note>original: "aureorum"</note> of the third person. It is now established that the second had 16 and the third had 24. Since the first, with half of the second and third, has 32, we subtract 20 (the half of the second and third) from 32, leaving 12 as the number for the first. Therefore, the first had 12 gold pieces, the second 16, and the third 24. The operation is lengthy, yet clear and easy; one <term>denomination</term> <note>The term for the variable being solved.</note> must always be reduced to the same number, and then the difference of the numbers will necessarily be equal to the difference of the other denomination, as you saw twice in this example.

# GIROLAMO CARDANO

Therefore, by giving $\frac{1}{3}$ and 3 <term>plus</term> <note>original: "p:", abbreviation for *plus*</note> of the second to the first, there is left to the second $\frac{2}{3}$ of the <term>quantity</term> <note>original: "quan:", referring to the second unknown variable, similar to our modern $y$</note> <term>minus</term> <note>original: "m:", abbreviation for *minus*</note> 3; and this is a third part of the first person's aggregate, which is 1 <term>position</term> <note>original: "pos:", referring to the first unknown variable, similar to our modern $x$</note> <term>plus</term> $\frac{1}{3}$ of the quantity <term>plus</term> 3. Therefore, by tripling the $\frac{2}{3}$ of the quantity <term>minus</term> 3, it becomes 2 quantities <term>minus</term> 9. This will be equal to 1 position <term>plus</term> $\frac{1}{3}$ of the quantity <term>plus</term> 3. Wherefore, by returning that which is <term>minus</term> to the other side <note>Cardano is describing the algebraic process of transposition—moving a negative term to the other side of the equation to make it positive.</note>, it will become 1 position <term>plus</term> 12, equal to 1 $\frac{2}{3}$ quantities. 

Again, because it was said that if the first gives half <term>plus</term> 2 to the second, the residue—namely $\frac{1}{2}$ position <term>minus</term> 2—will be a ninth part of the aggregate, which is 1 quantity <term>plus</term> $\frac{1}{2}$ position <term>plus</term> 2. Therefore, by multiplying such a residue by 9, it will become 4 $\frac{1}{2}$ positions <term>minus</term> 18, equal to 1 quantity <term>plus</term> $\frac{1}{2}$ position <term>plus</term> 2. By returning the <term>minus</term> to the other side and subtracting similar terms, we will have 4 positions equal to 1 quantity <term>plus</term> 20. 

You also had 1 position <term>plus</term> 12 equal to 1 $\frac{2}{3}$ quantities. Having reduced the parts to the equality of a single denomination <note>This means finding a common coefficient to eliminate one of the variables.</note>, and first by multiplying 1 position <term>plus</term> 12 equal to 1 $\frac{2}{3}$ quantity by 4, there will be 4 positions <term>plus</term> 48 equal to 6 $\frac{2}{3}$ quantities. And you will compare this, as you see in the figure, with 4 positions equal to 1 quantity <term>plus</term> 20. 

And similarly, by the same reasoning, reducing the number of quantities to equality, you will have 5 quantities equal to 36 <term>plus</term> 3 positions, and 5 quantities <term>plus</term> 100 equal to 20 positions. In both cases you will transfer them in turn, by the rule: *if you add equals to equals, the wholes also become equal* <note>Cardano is citing a fundamental axiom from Euclid's Elements to justify his algebraic manipulation.</note>. And you will have 4 positions <term>plus</term> 68 <term>plus</term> 1 quantity equal to 4 positions <term>plus</term> 6 $\frac{2}{3}$ quantities. Then, having cast out similar terms, there will be left 5 $\frac{2}{3}$ quantities equal to 68. Therefore, by dividing 68 by 5 $\frac{2}{3}$, the result is 12, the <term>valuation</term> <note>The solution or numerical value of the unknown variable.</note> of the quantity, and that which the second person had. 

By the same reasoning, you will transfer the dissimilar parts in the second equation, saying: if 1 quantity is equal to 36 <term>plus</term> 3 positions, and 5 quantities <term>plus</term> 100 are equal to 20 positions; therefore 5 quantities <term>plus</term> 20 positions are equal to 5 quantities <term>plus</term> 3 positions <term>plus</term> 136. Then, having cast out similar terms, there remain 17 positions equal to 136. Wherefore, by dividing 136 by 17, the result will be 8, the valuation of the position, or the number of the first. Thus the first had 8, the second 12. And although these things can also be solved otherwise, this way is nevertheless more appropriate and pure, so that the whole question may be resolved with one and the same impetus, even if the first example could be shown through the <term>thing</term> <note>original: "rem", likely referring to the *regula rei* or the standard rule of the unknown (the "thing") used in simpler algebra.</note> alone.

->ON ARITHMETIC BOOK 10.<-

# On the multiplication of a second unknown quantity. Chapter 10.

**W**hen indeed two unknown quantities are multiplied, or one is multiplied by itself, four modes are created, the majority of which consist of three members <note>Cardano refers to "members" as the individual terms in an algebraic equation, such as $x^2$, $x$, and $y$.</note>.

### DEMONSTRATION.

<margin>1</margin> The first [mode] is when the square of one and the quantities themselves are compared. Let there be first the square $AC$, whose side is $AB$, equal to double $AB$ and five times $E$, for the sake of example. Therefore, having set $BD$ equal to the number of <term>things</term> <note>original: "rerum". In this context, "things" refers to the coefficient of the first unknown variable, $x$.</note>, namely 2, $AD$ will be equal to double $AB$. Therefore $CF$ is equal to five times $E$. Wherefore from the 15th proposition of the sixth book of Euclid's *Elements*, $AB$ is to $E$ as 5 is to $CD$. Now $AB$ is the <term>position</term> <note>original: "positio", the standard term for the first unknown variable, $x$.</note>, and $CD$ is the position <term>minus</term> <note>original: "m:"</note> 2, and 5 is a known number, wherefore the rule is:

### RULE.

Having set the <term>thing</term> <note>the unknown $x$</note> as any amount you please, multiply it by itself, with the number of things subtracted, and divide what results by the number of the unknown <term>quantity</term> <note>original: "quantitatis", the second unknown variable, $y$.</note>; the value of the unknown quantity will result. 
**Example:** let the thing be 7. Let it be multiplied by itself <term>minus</term> 2—because it was set that [the square] was equal to two things and five quantities—it will become 35 <note>Calculation: $7 \times (7 - 2) = 35$. This represents $x^2 - 2x = 5y$.</note>. Divide 35 by 5, the number of quantities; the quantity also results as 7. 
And if the thing is set as 10, we will multiply it by 2 <term>minus</term> it [the thing], that is by 8, and it will become 80; whence having divided 80 by 5, the result is 16, the second quantity. 
But if the second quantity is set as known, we will multiply it by its number, and to the product we will add the square of half the number of things itself, and the <term>root</term> <note>original: "radix", the square root.</note> of the whole, with the half of the number of things added, is the value of the thing <note>This is a verbal description of the quadratic formula: $x = \sqrt{5y + (2/2)^2} + (2/2)$.</note>. 
**Example:** let the second quantity be 16. We will multiply it by 5, it becomes 80. Add 1, the square of half the number of things; it becomes 81. The square root of this is 9, to which, having added half the number of things [1], it becomes 10, the quantity of the thing itself.

### DEMONSTRATION.

<margin>2</margin> Again, let ten times $AB$ be equal to the square $AB$ and seven times $E$, for the sake of example. And let the square $AB$ be the surface $AC$, and let $BD$ be 10. Therefore, seven times $E$ is equal to the surface $FD$, and as in the preceding, $AB$ is to $E$ as 7 is to $CD$. Wherefore the rule is, when the things are equal to the square of the thing and the quantities.

### RULE.

Having set the thing as any amount you please, we will subtract it from the number of things, and we will multiply it [the thing] by the residue. We will divide the product by the number of quantities; what results is [the value] of the quanti<term>ty</term>

For example, let the <term>thing</term> <note>original: "res", the first unknown variable, $x$</note> in this case be 8; subtract it from 10 (the number of things), and 2 remains. Multiply this 2 by 8, which makes 16. Divide by 7 (the number of <term>quantities</term> <note>original: "quantitatum", the second unknown variable, $y$</note>), and the result is $2 \frac{2}{7}$, the valuation of the quantity. But if the second quantity is known, we multiply it by its coefficient, and the product is subtracted from the square of half the number of things; the root of the remainder, added to or subtracted from half the number of things, shows the valuation of the thing. For example, let the second quantity be $2 \frac{2}{7}$; multiply it by 7 (the number of quantities), which makes 16. Its root [3], added to or subtracted from 5 (half the number of things), shows 8 or 2 to be the valuations of that thing.

## DEMONSTRATION.

<margin>3</margin>
Let $E$ also be a number equal to the square of $AB$ (which is $AC$) and to the number of $AB$ (which is the surface $FD$). Therefore, having set $AB$ as the first [position], the number $E$ as the second, $E$ as the third, and $BD$ as the fourth, the proportion of $AB$ to $E$ will be as the number $E$ to $BD$. Wherefore, this will be the rule when quantities are equal to things and the square of things.

## RULE.

Having set the thing to whatever value you please, multiply it by the sum of itself and its coefficient, and divide the product by the number of quantities; what results is the valuation of the quantity. For example: 5 quantities are equal to 7 things and the square of a thing, and the thing is 3. We shall say, therefore: multiply 3 by 10 (the sum of the 3—the valuation of the thing—and 7—the number of things), which makes 30. Divide by 5 (the number of quantities), and the result is 6, the valuation of the quantity. But if the second quantity is known, multiply it by its coefficient, and to the product add the square of half the number of things. The root of the whole, after subtracting half the number of things, is the valuation of the thing. For example, let 6 be the valuation of the quantity, when 5 quantities are equal to 7 things and the square of a thing. Multiply 6 (the valuation of the quantities) by 5 (the number of quantities), making 30. To this, add the square of $3 \frac{1}{2}$ (half of 7, the number of things), which is $12 \frac{1}{4}$ <note>The Latin text says 42 1/4, which appears to be a typesetting error in the original; $3.5^2$ is $12.25$. However, the sum $30 + 12.25 = 42.25$. Cardano likely skipped a step or the typesetter conflated the sum with the square.</note>. From the root of this sum, which is $6 \frac{1}{2}$, if you take away $3 \frac{1}{2}$ (half the number of things), there remains 3, the valuation of the thing.

<margin>Note.</margin>
We are accustomed to use these <term>positions</term> <note>variable assignments</note> when no comparison is expressed between the two numbers that are set from the beginning—neither in their sum, nor difference, nor multiplication, nor division (or proportion), nor in their root. For numbers are compared in these five ways; wherefore, if one of these is established, there is no use for a second quantity, as the question is solved by a single position.

<meta>This page continues the demonstration of algebraic rules where the product of two unknown variables (the "thing" and the "quantity") is equal to a linear combination of those same variables. Cardano uses geometric proofs based on Euclid's Elements to justify his arithmetical shortcuts.</meta>

<page-num>24</page-num>

->ON ARITHMETIC BOOK X.<-

[the product] will be equal to the quantities and the things, or the things will be equal to the product and the quantities; let the <term>thing</term><note>original: "res", the variable x</note> be $AB$, the <term>quantity</term><note>original: "quantitas", the variable y</note> $AC$, the number of quantities $AD$, and the number of things $AE$. Therefore, by the given assumption, the two surfaces $DC$ and $BE$ will be equal to $AF$. However, $AF$ is equal to the four surfaces $GA$, $GB$, $GC$, and $GF$. Therefore, these four surfaces are equal to the surfaces $DC$ and $BE$. Having equally subtracted the three surfaces $GA$, $GB$, and $GC$, the remaining surface $GA$ will be equal to $GF$. For which reason, from the 15th proposition of the sixth book of the <term>Elements</term><note>Euclid’s Elements, a foundational Greek mathematical text</note>, as $AD$ is to $DB$, so is $CE$ to $EA$. Therefore, the proportion of the number of quantities to the remainder of the thing is as the remainder of the quantity (after subtracting the number of things) is to the number of things. According to this, the rule shall be:

### RULE.

If the thing is known, we will subtract from it the number of quantities, and with the remainder, we will divide the product of the number of things and the number of quantities. That which results must be added to the number of things, and the whole is the quantity. **Example:** let there be 10 things and 12 quantities, equal to the product of the thing and the quantity, and let the quantity be 18. Then, you subtract conversely the 10 (the number of things) from the 18 (the quantity), and 8 remains. Divide 120 (the product of 10, the number of things, by 12, the number of quantities) by this 8, and 15 results. Add this to 12 (the number of quantities), and it becomes 27, the valuation of the thing. Thus, 10 things are 270, and 12 quantities are 216, which joined together make 486—the product of 18 (the quantity) and 27 (the thing). We have posed the example of the rule in reverse so that you may understand the reasoning is one and the same. 

But if the product itself is known, divide that product by the number of quantities (if it is smaller than the number of things) or by the number of things (if that is smaller than the number of quantities). Take half of the resulting value and multiply it by itself <note>i.e., square it</note>. From this, subtract that which results when the product of the larger number and the product of the quantity and thing is divided by the smaller number (whether the number of things be larger or smaller). The root of the remainder, added to or subtracted from that which you had squared, shows the valuation of the quantity or the thing—specifically, the one described by the smaller number. Then,

<meta>This page continues Cardano’s exploration of equations involving two variables, which he calls the "thing" (x) and the "quantity" (y), and their product (xy). He is demonstrating how to solve for one variable when the other or their product is known, using geometric logic to support his algebraic rules.</meta>

# GIERONIMO CARDANO

from the product of 6 and 64, [divided] by 2, 64 is left, the root of which is 8; this, when added to or subtracted from the number 16 (which you multiplied by itself), reveals the <term>value of the thing</term><note>original: "æstimationem rei"; in modern algebra, this is the solution for x</note> as either 8 or 24. For this reason, if the thing is worth 8, the quantity will also be 8—for if 64 is divided by 8, the result is 8. And if the thing is worth 24, the quantity is $2 \frac{2}{3}$—for 64 divided by 24 [is $2 \frac{2}{3}$]. In both cases, 2 things and 6 quantities are equal to 64, the product of the thing and the quantity.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

<margin>5</margin> Because if one side is equal to the product of one by the other plus the remaining side: let that side be $AB$, and the remaining side be $AE$. Indeed, the number of the side $AB$ is the surface $AC$. Therefore, $EF$ is—by assumption—from $AE$ into its own number. The same is produced from $AB$ into $EC$. Therefore, the proportion of $AB$ to $AE$ is as the number of $AB$ is to $EC$. Furthermore, $EC$ is the remainder of the quantity $AB$, and $AC$ is the number of things. For which reason, the rule shall be:

->RULE<-

When the things are equal to the <term>product</term><note>original: "quantitati rei", literally "the quantity of the thing," Cardano's term for the product of two variables (xy)</note> and the quantities, and the quantity itself is known, we shall subtract it from the number of things. Then, we will multiply the quantity by its own number [the coefficient], and divide the product by that remainder; the result is the value of the thing. 

**Example:** 10 things are equal to the product and 4 quantities, and the quantity itself is 8. I subtract 8 from 10, leaving 2. I also multiply the quantity 8 by its number, 4, which makes 32. I divide this by the remainder, 2, and the result is 16, the value of the thing. Where the first subtraction cannot be performed, the case cannot exist in true numbers.

If, however, the thing itself is known rather than the quantity: because from $AB$ into $AC$ is produced as much as from $AE$ into the sum of $AB$ and the number $AE$. We shall divide the product of the number of things and the value of the thing by the sum of the thing and the number of quantities; the result is the value of the quantity.

**Example:** 10 things are equal to the product and 4 quantities, and the thing is 16. I multiply the thing, 16, by the number of things, 10, which makes 160. I divide by 20 (the sum of the number of quantities, 4, and the value of the thing, 16); the result is 8, the value of the quantity.

If, however, the product of the thing were known, you will multiply such a product by the number of quantities, and divide the product by the number of things. To the result, add the square of half of that which results from dividing the product by the number of things. The root of this sum, when the half you previously squared is added to it, is the value of the thing.

**Example:** Let 4 things be equal to 5 quantities and the product, which is 45. I will multiply 45 by 5 (the number of quantities), making 225. I divide by 4 (the number of things), which results in $56 \frac{1}{4}$. To this I add $31 \frac{41}{64}$, which is the square of $5 \frac{5}{8}$ (half of the result of 45 divided by 4). The total becomes $87 \frac{57}{64}$. If to its root, which is $9 \frac{3}{8}$, the half of the division's result ($5 \frac{5}{8}$) is added, it will make 15, the value of the thing.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

<margin>6</margin> When, however, the square of the thing, the product, and the thing are compared to one another...

<page-num>25</page-num>
<header>ON ARITHMETIC BOOK 10.</header>

...[are conside]red, three modes are formed. The first is when the **square of the thing** <note>original: "quadratum rei"; equivalent to $x^2$</note> is equal to the **quantities of things** <note>original: "quantitatibus rerum"; this refers to the product of two variables, $xy$, multiplied by a coefficient</note> and the **things** <note>original: "rebus"; the variable $x$</note>. Let $AB$ be the "thing," whose square is $AC$. Let $BF$ be the "quantity" <note>the second variable $y$</note>, and $AD$ be the number of "quantities of things" <note>the coefficient of the $xy$ term</note>. It will follow that however many times $BF$ is contained in $BD$, that will be the total number of the "quantity of the thing." Therefore, $DC$ will be the number of "things" <note>the coefficient of $x$</note>. Because $BC$ is equal to $AB$, and $CD$ is the number of things, it follows that by subtracting the number of things from the "thing" itself, $BD$ remains—which is the product of the number of the "quantity of the thing" multiplied by the "quantity." Hence the rule:

### R U L E.

When the square of the thing is equal to things and the quantities of things: if the "thing" <note>$x$</note> is known, we shall subtract the number of things <note>the coefficient of $x$</note> from it; we then divide the remainder by the number of the "quantity of the thing" <note>the coefficient of the $xy$ term</note>, and the result will be the **quantity** <note>$y$</note>. 

**Example:** 10 things with 4 quantities of things are equal to the square of the thing, and the "thing" is 30. I subtract 10 from 30, leaving 20, which I divide by 4 (the number of the quantity of the thing), and the result is 5, which is the <term>value of the quantity</term> <note>original: "æstimatio quantitatis"; the solution for $y$</note>. 

But if the quantity is known, we shall multiply it by the number of the quantity of the thing, and to the product we shall add the number of things; the result will be the value of the "thing." 

**Example:** 10 things and 4 quantities of the thing are equal to the square of the thing, and the quantity is 7. We multiply 7 by 4 (the number of the quantity), which makes 28; to this we add 10 (the number of things), and the value of the "thing" becomes 38. 

If, however, the **product of the thing and the quantity** <note>original: "productum ex re in quantitatem"; the value of $xy$ as a single known sum</note> is known, we shall multiply it by the number of the quantities of things, and to this we shall add the square of half the number of things; the square root of the whole, with half the number of things added to it, is the value of the "thing." 

**Example:** The square of the thing is equal to 10 things and four quantities of things, and the <term>product of the thing and quantity</term> is 50. We multiply 50 by 4 (its number, that is, the number of the quantities of things), which makes 200. To this we add 25 (the square of 5, which is half of the 10 things), making 225. To the root of this [15] I add 5 (half the number of things), and it makes 20, the value of the "thing." Then, by dividing 50 (the product of the thing and quantity) [by the thing, 20], the result is $2 \frac{1}{2}$, the value of the "quantity."

### D E M O N S T R A T I O N.

<margin>7</margin>
But if the quantity of the thing should be equal to the squares of the thing and the number of things: let the thing be $AB$, and the quantity be $BC$. The "quantity of the thing" <note>the product $xy$</note> is $AC$. For that reason, $DC$ will necessarily be the number of things, and $AD$ will be the sum of the squares. Therefore, having subtracted $DC$ from $BC$, $BD$ will remain; when this is divided by the number of squares, $BF$ will be produced, which is equal to $AB$. The rule, therefore, is:

### R U L E.

When the quantity of the thing is equal to the squares of the thing and the number...

[the nu]mber of things, and if the <term>thing</term> <note>original: "res"; the unknown variable $x$</note> is known, we shall multiply it by the number of <term>squares</term> <note>original: "quadratos"; terms involving $x^2$</note>, and to the product we will add the number of things, and the <term>quantity</term> <note>original: "quantitas"; here referring to the total value of the second variable $y$ or the product $xy$</note> will be formed. **Example:** the <term>quantity of the thing</term> is equal to 6 <term>squares of the thing</term> and 10 things, and the <term>thing</term> is 4; multiply 4 by 6 (the number of <term>squares</term>), which makes 24; add to it 10 (the number of things), which makes 34, the valuation of the <term>quantity</term>. But if the <term>quantity</term> is known, we shall subtract the number of things from it, and the remainder we shall divide by the number of <term>squares of things</term>; what results is the <term>valuation of the thing</term>. **Example:** the <term>quantity of the thing</term> is equal to 6 <term>squares of the thing</term> and 10 things, and the <term>quantity</term> itself is 34; I subtract 10 from 34, leaving 24, which I divide by 6 (the number of <term>squares</term>); the result is 4, the <term>valuation of the thing</term>. If indeed the <term>quantity of the thing</term> is known [and we seek the thing], we divide it by the number of <term>squares</term>, and to the result we shall add the <term>square</term> of half that which results from dividing the number of things by the number of <term>squares of things</term>; and the <term>root</term> of the whole, when that same half has been subtracted, will be the <term>valuation of the thing</term>. <note>Cardano is describing the quadratic formula here: $x = \sqrt{\frac{y}{a} + (\frac{b}{2a})^2} - \frac{b}{2a}$</note> **Example:** The <term>quantity of the thing</term> is equal to 6 <term>squares of the thing</term> and 60 things, and the <term>quantity of the thing</term> is 1200; divide 1200 by 6 (the number of <term>squares of the thing</term>), which results in 200; to this I add 25 (the <term>square</term> of 5, which is half the result of the 60 things divided by the 6 <term>squares</term>), making 225; from the <term>root</term> of this, which is 15, I subtract 5 (half of that same result), and 10 remains, the <term>valuation of the thing</term>; then by dividing 1200, which is the <term>quantity of the thing</term>, there comes 120, the <term>valuation of the quantity</term>.

### Demonstration.

<margin>8</margin>
But if the number of things is equal to the <term>square of the thing</term> and the <term>quantities</term> of things (for indeed, it is always permitted to reduce [the equation] to one <term>square</term> or to one <term>quantity of the thing</term> through <term>common division</term>, as has been said in all the chapters), we shall set $AB$ as the <term>thing</term>, its <term>square</term> as $AC$, and the number of things as $BD$; $ED$ will therefore be the number of the <term>quantity</term> of the <term>thing</term>, and $CD$ will be the number produced from the number of <term>quantities</term> into the <term>quantity</term>, which let be $CF$; because $CD$ is therefore the remainder of $AB$ and $BD$, this rule will be:

### Rule.

When the number of things is equal to the <term>quantities</term> of things and the <term>square of the thing</term>, and the <term>thing</term> is known, we shall subtract it from its number, and divide the remainder by the number of the quantity of the thing; what results is the <term>valuation of the quantity</term>. **Example:** 10 <term>things</term> are equal to the <term>square of the thing</term> and three <term>quantities of the thing</term>, and the <term>thing</term> is 4; we shall subtract 4 from 10, leaving 6; I divide by 3 (the number of <term>quantities of the thing</term>), and the result is 2, the <term>valuation of the quantity</term>. If, however, the <term>quantity</term> is known, we shall multiply it by the number of the <term>quantity of the thing</term>, and subtract the product from the number of things; the remainder is the <term>valuation of the thing</term>. **Example:** 10 <term>things</term> are equal to the <term>square of the thing</term> and the product of the <term>thing</term> by the <term>quantity</term> three times, and the <term>quantity</term> is 2,

# On Arithmetic, Book 10

Therefore, we will multiply 2 (the <term>valuation</term> <note>original: "æstimatio"; the numerical value or solution of a variable</note> of the <term>quantity</term> <note>original: "quantitas"; here representing a second unknown variable, like y</note>) by 3 (the number of the <term>quantity of the thing</term> <note>original: "quantitatis rei"; a term representing the product of the two unknowns, xy</note>), and 6 is produced, which I subtract from 10 (the number of <term>things</term>), leaving 4 as the <term>valuation</term> of the <term>thing</term> <note>original: "res"; the unknown variable x</note>. If, however, the product of the <term>thing</term> and the <term>quantity</term> is known, we will multiply it by its number, and we will subtract the product from the <term>square</term> of half the number of <term>things</term>; and the <term>root</term> of the remainder, added to or subtracted from that same half of the number of <term>things</term>, shows the <term>valuation</term> of the <term>thing</term>. **Example:** 10 <term>things</term> are equal to a <term>square of a thing</term> and 3 <term>quantities of things</term>, and the <term>quantity of the thing</term> is 8. I will multiply 8 by 3 (the number of the <term>quantity of the thing</term>), which makes 24; this we will subtract from 25 (the <term>square</term> of 5, which is half of 10); 1 remains, whose <term>root</term>—which is 1—added to or subtracted from 5, shows 6 or 4 as the <term>valuations</term> of the <term>thing</term>. From this, the <term>quantity of the thing</term>, 8, being divided by 6 or by 4, results in 1 1/3 or 2 as the <term>valuation</term> of the <term>quantity</term>.

## THE DEMONSTRATION.

<margin>9</margin>
But if the <term>square of the thing</term>, the <term>quantity of the thing</term>, and the <term>quantity</term> are compared to one another, three other modes arise. Let there be, first, the <term>square of the thing</term> equal to the <term>quantities of things</term> and the number of <term>quantities</term>. Let **AB** be the <term>thing</term> itself, whose <term>square</term> **AC** is equal to the <term>quantities of things</term> (which let be **AD**, so that **DE** is the <term>quantity</term>) and the number of <term>quantities</term> **DE**, which is **FH**. The surface **GF** will be, by supposition, equal to the surface **CK**. Therefore, from the 15th [proposition] of the 6th [book] of the <term>Elements</term> <note>Referring to Euclid’s Elements, a foundation of geometry</note>, **AB** is to **DE** as **HF** is to **DC**. Now, **AB** is the <term>thing</term>, **DE** is the <term>quantity</term>, **HF** is the number of <term>quantities</term>, and **CD** is the remainder of the <term>thing</term> and of the product of the number of the <term>quantity of the thing</term> and the <term>quantity</term> itself; therefore, the rule is as follows:

## THE RULE.

When the <term>square of the thing</term> is equal to the products of the <term>quantity</term> times the <term>thing</term> and the <term>quantity</term> times the number, and the <term>thing</term> itself is known, we will multiply the <term>thing</term> by the number of the <term>quantities of things</term>, and to the product we will add the number of <term>quantities</term>. By this sum we will divide the <term>square of the thing</term>; the result is the <term>valuation</term> of the <term>quantity</term>. **Example:** let the <term>square of the thing</term> be equal to six <term>quantities of things</term> and 20 <term>quantities</term>, and the <term>thing</term> itself is 12. I multiply 12 by 6 (the number of the <term>quantity of the thing</term>), which makes 72, to which I add 20 (the number of <term>quantities</term>), making 92. By this I divide 144 (the <term>square of the thing</term>), which results in 1 13/23 as the <term>quantity</term> itself. If, however, the <term>quantity</term> is known, we will multiply it by its number and save the product; then we will multiply the same [quantity] by the number of the <term>quantity of things</term>, and half of this product, multiplied by itself, we will add to the previous product. To the <term>root</term> of that sum, we will add the half that we had multiplied by itself,

# THE WRITINGS OF GIROLAMO CARDANO

...valuation, and the whole is the <term>valuation of the thing</term> <note>original: "æstimatio rei"; the "thing" (res) is the unknown variable, similar to our modern "x".</note>. **Example:** the <term>Square of the thing</term> <note>x²</note> is equal to 12 <term>quantities</term> <note>y</note> and 5 <term>quantities of the thing</term> <note>xy</note>, and the quantity itself is 2. I will multiply the quantity, 2, by its number, 12; it makes 24. Then I will multiply the same quantity, 2, by the number of the quantity of the thing, 5, and it makes 10. I take half of this, which is 5, and multiply it by itself, making 25. I add this to the 24 already saved, making 49. To the <term>root</term> of this, which is 7, I add that same half, which is 5; it makes 12, the valuation of the thing. 

Where, however, the quantity of the thing were known (and it is the surface EK in the figure), we shall multiply it by its own number, and we shall reduce a third part of the product to a <term>cube</term>. We shall also multiply the quantity of the thing by the number of quantities, and we shall multiply half of the product by itself; and from this we shall subtract the part which we had raised to a cube—that is, the cube itself of the third part of the first product which you saved. We shall then add and subtract the root of this remainder to and from half of the second product; and the <term>cube roots</term> of the sum and the remainder, joined together, are the valuation of the thing. 

**Example:** The Square of the thing is equal to 12 quantities and 2 quantities of the thing, and the quantity of the thing is 24. I will multiply 2 by 24, which makes 48. A third part of this, which is 16, I will raise to a cube, making 4096. I will also multiply 24 by 12, which makes 288, of which I multiply the half by itself—144 being the half, and its square 20736. From this I subtract 4096, leaving 16640. I add and subtract the root of this from 144, which results in 144 plus the root of 16640 and 144 minus the root of 16640. The cube roots of these added together are the valuation of the thing.

| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| | 12 | 2 |
| Quantity of the thing | 24 | |
| | 288 | 48 |

But if, by dividing the number into equal parts, the half that is taken does not produce a square equal to or greater than the cube of the third part of the first product, you will operate by the remainder of the rule in the chapter on the **"cube equal to things and a number"** <note>This refers to the solution for a cubic equation of the form x³ = ax + b.</note>. For when the multiplication is performed by the product (as in the example by 24, which number is the quantity of the thing), the cube will be equal to things and a number—the things being produced from the quantity of the thing multiplied by its number, and the number being produced from the quantity of the thing multiplied by the number of quantities, as was said in the example: that the square of the thing was equal to 2 quantities of the thing and 12 quantities, and that the quantity of the thing is 24. Therefore, we shall say the cube is equal to 48 things plus the number 288; and 48 is produced from 24 times 2, and 288 from 24 times 12. Therefore, let us suppose that the square of the thing is equal to 2 quantities of the thing and 3 quantities, and the quantity of the thing is 8. We will multiply 8 by 2 and 3, and 16 and 24 will be produced. Therefore, the cube will be equal to 16 things...

# ON ARITHMETIC, BOOK 10

...things plus 24, and the thing is worth the **Root of 13 plus 1**, according to its chapter <note>In Renaissance algebra, a "chapter" (capitulum) referred to a specific rule for solving a type of equation, such as $x^2 = ax + b$.</note>. Then, 8 (the quantity of the thing) having been divided by the **Root of 13 plus 1**, the result is the **Root of 5 7/9 minus 2/3**, which is the quantity itself. Moreover, the square of the **Root of 13 plus 1** is **14 plus the Root of 52** <note>original: "Rz 52"; 14 + √52 is the result of squaring (√13 + 1)</note>. And the quantity of the thing is **Root of 7 5/9 minus 2/3**, and it is 8, the double of which is 16. And the three quantities are **Root of 52 minus 2**, which, when joined with 16 (the double of the quantity of the thing), make **14 plus the Root of 52**, the square of the thing.

| Square of the thing | Quantity | Quantity of the thing |
|:---|:---:|:---|
| 3 | 8 | 2 |
| 24 | | 16 |

<margin>Note 1</margin>
Note that in this rule, the <term>thing</term> <note>original: "res"; the unknown variable, usually "x" in modern notation.</note> is always the <term>mean proportional</term> <note>In a sequence of three numbers, the middle one is the mean proportional if the first is to the second as the second is to the third (a/b = b/c).</note> between the quantity and the sum produced from the number of quantities and the product of the thing multiplied by the number of the quantity of the thing. For example, the **Root of 13 plus 1**, which is the thing, is the proportional between the **Root of 5 7/9 minus 2/3**, which is the quantity, and the **Root of 52 plus 5**, which consists of 3 (the number of quantities) and the product of the **Root of 13 plus 1** (the thing itself) multiplied by 2 (the number of the quantity of the thing).

<margin>Note</margin>
Note also that this rule depends on the chapter for a <term>cube</term> equal to things and a number <note>This refers to the solution for $x^3 = ax + b$.</note>, just as the following rule depends on the chapter for a cube and a number equal to things, and the last rule depends on the chapter for a cube and things equal to a number.

<margin>Note</margin>
Note also that the thing is the same as that which is sought in the chapter for a cube equal to things and a number, but the <term>quantity</term> is the number that results when any third number is divided by the thing itself. For to that same chapter (a cube equal to things and a number) there corresponds only one "thing" <note>one solution for x</note>, but infinite "quantities," just as it was said here that the thing is the **Root of 13 plus 1**. And we divided 8, the quantity of the thing; but if the cube were set equal to 16 things and 24 as the number, the thing will always be the **Root of 13 plus 1**, but with the quantity of the thing set at 4, the number of the quantity will be 6, and the quantity itself will be the **Root of 1 4/9 minus 2/3**.

### DEMONSTRATION

<margin>10</margin>
If the quantity of the thing were equal to the squares of the thing and the quantities, we shall set **AB** as the thing, and **BC** as the quantity, and the number of squares (according to which **BG**, equal to **AB**, is contained in **BD**). And the squares of **AD** joined together, and the remainder **BC**, will be equal to the number of quantities. Let **FC** be the number of quantities. Therefore, **FB** will be equal to **BC**. Wherefore, the ratio of the quantity **BC** to the thing **AB** is as the remainder of the thing **DC** (multiplied by the number of squares from the quantity to the number of quantities **CF**). From this, the remainder **EB** will also be equal to the remainder **AF**. Therefore, **AB** is the mean proportional between **AH** and **BC** (divided according to the number by which **BG** is contained in **BD**).

<margin>Note</margin>
Note, therefore, that in this whole rule, the thing is the mean proportional between the quantity divided by the number of squares and the remainder of the thing and the number of quantities.

<meta>This page continues Cardano's mathematical exploration of the relationship between the "thing" (the unknown variable) and the "quantity of the thing" (the total value of the expression). Having established the proportions on the previous page, he now codifies these into a formal Rule.</meta>

->GIROLAMO CARDANO<-

# GIROLAMO CARDANO
## THE RULE.

The rule, therefore, is: when the **quantity of the thing** <note>The "quantity of the thing" refers to the total value or the result of the algebraic expression, while the "thing" itself is the unknown variable, usually denoted as *x* today.</note> is equal to the **squares of the thing** and the **quantities**, and the **thing** is known, we multiply it by itself, then by the number of squares, and we divide the product by the remainder of the thing after the number of quantities is subtracted, and the result is the quantity. For example: the quantity of the thing equals three squares of the thing and 12 quantities, and let the thing be 20, for the sake of example. I multiply 20 by itself, making 400; I multiply 400 by 3 (the number of squares), making 1200; I divide 1200 by 8 (the difference between the thing and the number of quantities), and 150 results, which is the quantity itself. If, however, the quantity itself is known but not the thing, multiply it by the number of quantities, and divide the product by the number of squares; subtract the result from the square of half the result of the quantity divided by the number of squares, and the **root of the remainder** <note>original: "radix residui"</note>, added to or subtracted from half of that same result, shows the **valuation of the thing** <note>The numerical value of the unknown.</note>. For example: the quantity of the thing is equal to 4 squares of the thing and 3 quantities, and the quantity itself is 50. Multiply 50 by 3 (the number of quantities), making 150; divide 150 by 4 (the number of squares), which results in 37 $\frac{1}{2}$. Then divide 50 by 4, namely the quantity by the number of squares, which results in 12 $\frac{1}{2}$; multiply half of this, which is 6 $\frac{1}{4}$, by itself, making 39 $\frac{1}{16}$, from which subtract 37 $\frac{1}{2}$; 1 $\frac{9}{16}$ remains, the root of which is 1 $\frac{1}{4}$, which, added to or subtracted from 6 $\frac{1}{4}$, shows the valuations of the thing as 7 $\frac{1}{2}$ or 5. But if the product or quantity of the thing is known, we will multiply the quantity of the thing by the number of quantities, and we will divide the product by the number of squares; the result is the number which, along with the cube, is equal to as many things as the number which comes from dividing the quantity of the thing by the number of squares. For example: the quantity of the thing, which is 1500, is equal to 4 squares of the thing and 6 quantities. We will therefore multiply 6 by 1500, making 9000; divide by 4 (the number of squares), which results in 2250, a number

<insert>
| Quantity of the thing | Squares of the thing | Quantity |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 1500 | 4 | 6 |
| --- | --- | --- |
| | 375 | 1500 |
| 2250 | | 1000 |
</insert>

which, with the cube, is equal to 375 things. 375 is the number that comes from dividing the number 1500 (the quantity of the thing) by 4 (the number of squares). According to its own chapter <note>A "chapter" (capitulum) in Renaissance mathematics refers to a specific rule or algorithm for solving a specific type of equation.</note>, the thing is worth 10, or the <term>Root</term> <note>original: "Rz", standing for "Radix".</note> of 300 minus 5; and either of these numbers can be the valuation of the thing in this case, when the quantity of the thing, which is 1500, is equal to 4 squares of the thing and 6 quantities, and the valuation of the quantity is obtained by dividing 1500 (which is the valuation of the quantity of the thing) by the other valuation of the thing.

## DEMONSTRATION.

When indeed the quantities C D, in the number C F, are equal to the squares A B of the thing, and the quantity of the thing D E, reducing to a single quantity of the thing,

->ON ARITHMETIC, Book 10.<-

Once the common surface **D E** has been subtracted, the surface **G F** will be equal to **A C**. Therefore, the square **A B**—by the first proposition of the sixth book of the *Elements* <note>Referring to Euclid’s *Elements*, a foundational text for Renaissance mathematics.</note>—is equal to a surface formed by **B G** multiplied by a part of **E F** such that **A B** is the same part of **B C**. Thus, from the 16th proposition of the sixth book of the *Elements*, **A B** is the mean proportional between **D C** and that part of **E F**. From this, we derive the rule.

### THE RULE.

When there are quantities equal to the **quantity of the thing** <note>The "quantity of the thing" refers to the total value of the expression, while the "thing" (res) is the unknown variable.</note> and the **squares of the things**, and the **thing** is known, we shall multiply it by itself, then the product by the number of squares. We then divide this final product by the number of quantities after the **thing** has been subtracted, and the result will be the **quantity**.

**Example:** 12 quantities are equal to the quantity of the thing and three squares of the thing, and the **thing** is 4. I multiply 4 by itself, which makes 16. I multiply 16 by 3 (the number of squares of the thing), making 48. I divide 48 by 12 (the number of quantities) after subtracting 4 (the **thing**); this means dividing by 8. The result is 6, which is the **quantity** itself.

If, however, the **quantity** is known, multiply it by its own number, and divide the product by the number of squares of the thing. To this result, add the square of half the value obtained by dividing the **quantity** by the number of squares; the **root of the whole** <note>original: "radix totius"</note>, after subtracting that same half, is the **valuation of the thing** <note>The "valuation" (aestimatio) is the solution for the unknown variable, x.</note>.

**Example:** 12 quantities are equal to the quantity of the thing and 3 squares of the thing, and the **quantity** is 6. I multiply 12 by 6, which makes 72. I divide by 3 (the number of squares), which makes 24. Then I divide the 6 quantities by 3 (the number of squares), which results in 2, half of which is 1. I multiply 1 by itself, which is also 1. I add this to 24, making 25. The root of this is 5; after subtracting 1 (the half of 2), it leaves 4, which is the **valuation of the thing**.

If, however, the **quantity of the thing** is known, we shall multiply it by the number of quantities, and divide the product by the number of squares. What results is a number which is equal to a **cube** and **things** <note>This describes a cubic equation of the form x³ + ax = b.</note>, the number of which is the result of dividing the quantity of the thing by the number of squares. From this, the equation of the **thing** is the valuation sought; thus, once the quantity of the thing is divided by the valuation of the thing, the equation of the quantity will result.

| <term>Squares of the thing</term> | <term>Quantity of the thing</term> | <term>Quantity</term> |
| :---: | :---: | :---: |
| 3 | — 24 — | 12 |
| | 8 | 288 |
| | 96 | |

**Example:** 12 things are equal to the quantity of the thing and 3 squares of the thing, and the **quantity of the thing** is 24. I multiply 24 by 12, making 288. I divide by 3, which results in 96. Then I divide 24 by the same 3 (the number of squares), which results in 8. Therefore, the **cube** plus 8 **things** is equal to 96. Then, by its own chapter <note>referring to the specific section of the book that explains how to solve cubic equations</note>, the **thing** is worth 4. Therefore 4 is the **valuation of the thing**...

<margin>Note.</margin> Know that every chapter, or rule from the preceding ones, possesses all the properties contained in that same rule, in various ways, although sometimes we use one and sometimes another, according to whether that which is known makes something else known. For example, in the tenth rule there are five properties. **FIRST**, that the proportion of the quantity to the thing is [found] by multiplying the thing by the number of squares, and after the quantity has been subtracted, comparing it to the number of quantities. **SECOND**, that the thing is the proportional [mean] between the quantity divided by the number of squares and the difference of the thing from the number of quantities. **THIRD**, that when the thing is multiplied by itself, and then by the number of squares—the square having been so multiplied—it becomes as much as the result of the quantity multiplied into the remainder of the thing and the number of quantities. **FOURTH & FIFTH** are the remaining two ways of proceeding for that rule to find the thing; it was pleasing to add examples of these in the questions below.

## QUESTION I.

Find two numbers whose squares, when joined, are 100, and the product of one multiplied by the other is double their sum. We shall set the first as the <term>thing <note>original: "rem" (the unknown x)</note></term> and the second as the <term>quantity <note>original: "quantitatem" (here used as a second unknown, y)</note></term>. Therefore, the "quantity of the thing" <note>the product xy</note> is equal to 2 things and 2 quantities <note>xy = 2x + 2y</note>. Wherefore, from the 4th rule, the proportion of the remainder of the thing to 2 is as 2 is to the remainder of the quantity. Therefore, there will be three proportional quantities: the remainder of the thing, 2, and the remainder of the quantity. The "thing," however, consists of its remainder and 2, but the "quantity" consists of its remainder and 2. Therefore, the "thing" is the sum of the first and second of the three proportional quantities, and the "quantity" is the sum of the second and third.

Therefore, from what was said in the chapter on three proportional quantities <note>Referring to a geometric property where if a, b, c are in proportion, then (a+b)² + (b+c)² + b² = (a+b+c)²</note>, the square of the sum of the first and second, plus the square of the sum of the second and third, plus the square of the second, are equal to the square of the sum of those three quantities themselves. But in fact, the square of the sum of the first and second and the square of the sum of the second and third make 100 by the supposition <note>the initial problem statement that x² + y² = 100</note>, and the square of the second is 4, because the 2nd proportional quantity was 2. Therefore, the square of the sum of all three quantities is 104. 

Therefore, those three quantities joined together are the <term>root <note>original: "℞", shorthand for "Radix," meaning square root</note></term> of 104. And because the 2nd is 2, the remaining ones—namely the first and third—will be the root of 104 minus 2. Make, therefore, from the root of 104 minus 2, two parts whose product is 4 (the square of 2), and they will be:
The root of 26 minus 1, plus the <term>universal root <note>original: "℞ v:", a square root that applies to the entire following expression</note></term> of 23 minus the root of 104, 
and: 
The root of 26 minus 1, minus the universal root of 23 minus the root of 104. 

And because the "thing" consists of the first and second proportional, and the "quantity" from the 3rd and 2nd proportional, it follows that we add 2 to each part (namely, the second quantity). And the "thing" becomes the root of 26 plus 1, plus the universal root of 23 minus the root of 104, and the "quantity" becomes the root of 26 plus 1, minus the universal root of 23 minus the root of 104. The squares of these joined together are exactly 100, and the product of one by the other is the root of 416 <note>Which is exactly 2 times the root of 104, fulfilling the requirement that the product is double the sum.</note>.

### QUESTION II.

Find two numbers whose squares joined together are 100, and the square of the larger is equal to the product of the larger into the smaller four times, together with eight times the larger. We shall set the larger as the <term>thing <note>original: "rem"; the unknown variable, usually x.</note></term> and the smaller as the <term>quantity <note>original: "quantitatem"; a second unknown, usually y.</note></term>. The square of the thing will then be equal to 4 quantities of the thing and 8 things. Therefore, according to the 6th rule, we subtract 8 from the thing, and the remainder becomes the thing minus 8; when this is divided by 4, there results 1/4 of a thing minus 2, and this is the square of the quantity. <note>Cardano is simplifying the equation x² = 4xy + 8x by dividing by x to get x = 4y + 8, then solving for y.</note> Therefore, the [square of the] thing and [the square of] 1/4 of a thing minus 2 are equal to 100. Thus, $1 \frac{1}{16}$ of a square plus 4 minus 1 thing will equal 100, and the square will equal $\frac{16}{17}$ of a thing plus $90 \frac{6}{17}$. Therefore, the thing is the Root of $90 \frac{166}{289}$ plus $\frac{8}{17}$, and the quantity is 1/4 of this minus 2, namely the Root of $5 \frac{191}{289}$ minus $1 \frac{15}{17}$.

### QUESTION III.

Find two numbers whose squares joined together are 100, and the product of one into the other is equal to triple the square of the smaller and six times the same smaller. We shall set the thing as the smaller number and the quantity as the larger. Therefore, the quantity of the thing is equal to 3 squares of the thing and 6 things. Thus, by the 7th rule, the quantity is 3 things plus 6 <gloss>squares</gloss> <note>Cardano likely means the "quantity" itself is (3x + 6); the word "quadrata" here in the Latin text seems to be an error or refers to the square of the relation.</note>. Therefore, the [square of the] thing and the [square of] three things plus 6 joined together are 100. Thus, 10 squares plus 36 things plus 36 are equal to 100; and 1 square plus $3 \frac{3}{5}$ things is equal to $6 \frac{2}{5}$. The thing, therefore, is the Root of $9 \frac{16}{25}$ minus $1 \frac{4}{5}$, and the quantity is triple this plus 6—that is, the Root of $86 \frac{19}{25}$ plus $\frac{3}{5}$.

Having set the mean as the thing and the smaller as the quantity, the square of the thing will be equal to 2 quantities of the thing and 4 quantities. Wherefore, from the first note of the ninth rule, the mean thing is proportional between the quantity and the sum of the number of quantities (4) and the product of the thing into the number of the quantity of the thing (2). The third part, therefore, is 2 things plus 4. Because the 3rd quantity is 2 things plus 4, and the 2nd is the thing, and these [two] with the first constitute 10, the first will be 6 minus 3 things. Therefore, when the first is multiplied by the third, it becomes the square of the second:

### On the cube and things equal to a number. Chapter XI.

Scipione del Ferro of Bologna <note>A professor of mathematics at the University of Bologna who discovered the rule around 1515 but kept it secret.</note> found this chapter [the rule] almost thirty years ago. He passed it on to Antonio Maria Fior of Venice, who, when he once came into a contest with Niccolò Tartaglia of Brescia, gave the occasion for Niccolò to find it himself. When Niccolò gave it to us at our request, but suppressed the demonstration, we—relying on this help—sought the demonstration and, having reduced it into various modes (which was most difficult), we have set it forth as follows.

->DEMONSTRATION<-

Let there be, for the sake of example, the cube $GH$ plus six times the side $GH$ equal to 20. I will set up two cubes $AE$ and $CL$, the difference of which is 20, such that the product of the side $AC$ into the side $CK$ is 2—namely, the third part of the number of <term>things <note>The "things" (res) refers to the unknown variable, which we now call x.</note></term>. I cut off $CB$ equal to $CK$. I say that if this is so, the remaining line $AB$ is equal to $GH$, and therefore is the <term>estimation <note>The solution or value of the unknown.</note></term> of the thing. For it was already supposed that this was the case for $GH$. I shall complete, therefore, according to the manner of the first assumption of the 6th chapter of this book, the bodies $DA, DC, DE, DF$, so that by $DC$ we understand the cube of $BC$, by $DF$ the cube of $AB$, by $DA$ triple $CB$ into the square of $AB$, and by $DE$ triple $AB$ into the square of $BC$. Since, therefore, from $AC$ into $CK$ comes 2, from $AC$ into $CK$ thrice, there will be 6, the number of things; therefore, from $AB$ into triple $AC$ into $CK$ come 6 <term>things <note>original: "res"</note></term> of $AB$, or six times $AB$; wherefore the triple product of $AB, BC, AC$ is six times $AB$. But in truth, the difference of the cube $AC$ from the cube $CK$ (and that being equal to the cube $BC$ by the assumption) is 20. And from the first assumption of the 6th chapter, it is the sum of the bodies $DA, DE, DF$; these three bodies, therefore, are 20. Having set $BC$ as minus, the cube $AB$ is equal to the cube $AC$, plus triple $AC$ into the square of $CB$, and the cube $BC$ minus, and triple $BC$ into the square of $AC$ minus, through the things demonstrated there. Moreover, the difference between triple $BC$ into the square of $AC$ and triple $AC$ into the square of $BC$ is the product of $AB, BC, AC$. Wherefore, since this—as has been demonstrated—is equal to six times $AB$, therefore, adding six times $AB$ to that which comes from triple $AC$ into the square of $BC$, there will be triple $BC$ into the square of $AC$. Since $BC$ is minus, it has already been shown that the product of $CB$ <note>The text continues here into the next page.</note>

## On Arithmetic, Book 10.
<sig>H 2</sig>

Therefore, the difference between the cube $AC$ and the cube $BC$ is as great, according to common sense, as that which is produced from the cube $AC$, and triple $AC$ into the square of $CB$, and triple $CB$ into the square of $AC$ minus: and the cube $BC$ minus: and six times $AB$. This, therefore, is 20, because the difference of the cube $AC$ from the cube $CB$ was 20. Since, therefore, the cube $AB$ and six times $AB$ are equal to 20, and the cube $GH$ with six times $GH$ are equal to 20, it will be, from common sense and from what was said in the 35th proposition of the 1st book and the 31st of the 11th book of the *Elements* <note>Referring to Euclid’s Elements, the foundation of geometry.</note>, that $GH$ is equal to $AB$. Therefore $GH$ is the difference between $AC$ and $CB$; but $AC$ and $CB$, or $AC$ and $CK$, are the numbers or lines containing a surface equal to the third part of the number of things, the cubes of which differ by the number of the equation. Wherefore we shall have the rule.

-> R E G V L A. <-
-> [THE RULE] <-

Raise the third part of the number of <term>things <note>The unknown variable, x.</note></term> to the cube, to which you shall add the square of half the number of the <term>equation <note>The constant term in the equation.</note></term>, and take the root of the whole—namely, the square root—which you will duplicate; to one you will add the same half of the number that you had just multiplied by itself, and from the other you will subtract that same half. You will then have a <term>Binomial <note>An expression of the form a + √b.</note></term> with its <term>Apotome <note>An expression of the form a - √b.</note></term>. Then, having subtracted the cube root of the Apotome from the cube root of its Binomial, the residue which is left from this is the <term>estimation <note>The value of the unknown variable.</note></term> of the thing.

<insert>
| cube plus 6 things equal to 20 | |
| :--- | :--- |
| 2 | 20 |
| 8 | 10 |
| | 108 |
| | root of 108 plus 10 |
| | root of 108 minus 10 |
| universal cube root of the root of 108 plus 10 | |
| minus the universal cube root of the root of 108 minus 10 | |
</insert>

Example: A cube and 6 <term>positions <note>Another term for the unknown, x.</note></term> are equal to 20. Raise 2, the third part of 6, to the cube, which makes 8. Multiply 10, half the number [the constant], by itself, which makes 100. Join 100 and 8, making 108. Take the root, which is $\sqrt{108}$, and you will duplicate it. To one add 10, half the number, and from the other subtract just as much. You will have the Binomial $\sqrt{108} + 10$ and the Apotome $\sqrt{108} - 10$. Take the cube roots of these and subtract the one belonging to the Apotome from the one belonging to the Binomial. You will have the estimation of the thing: the <term>universal cube root <note>The root of the entire binomial or apotome.</note></term> of $\sqrt{108} + 10$ minus the universal cube root of $\sqrt{108} - 10$.

Another example: A cube plus 3 things is equal to 10. Raise 1, the third part of 3, to the cube, making 1. Multiply 5, half of 10, to its square, making 25. Join 25 and 1,

The formula involves creating a binomial and its conjugate (apotome) from the equation's constants and then subtracting their cube roots.</summary>

# THE WORKS OF HIERONYMUS CARDANUS
<note>Girolamo Cardano (1501–1576), a pioneering Italian mathematician</note>

You will have a <term>Binomial</term><note>A "Binomial" in this context refers to an irrational number formed by the sum of two parts, such as $\sqrt{26} + 5$</note> $\sqrt{26}$ plus 5, and an <term>Apotome</term><note>An "Apotome" is the counterpart to a binomial, formed by the difference of two parts, such as $\sqrt{26} - 5$</note> $\sqrt{26}$ minus 5. Therefore, the value of the <term>thing</term><note>Cardano uses "res" (thing) to refer to the unknown variable, which we call $x$</note> is the universal cube root of $\sqrt{26}$ plus 5 minus the universal cube root of $\sqrt{26}$ minus 5. > minus universal cube root of $\sqrt{26}$ minus 5. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; plus universal cube root of $\sqrt{26}$ plus 5

->The products of the parts into the tripled squares<-

> plus universal cube root of 49299354 plus 6885 minus $\sqrt{47385000}$ minus 7020
> minus universal cube root of 49299354 minus 6885 minus $\sqrt{47385000}$ plus 7020

Furthermore, these cube roots consisting of four terms can be reduced to two. For when you have subtracted 6885 from 7020, 135 remains; and after subtracting the root of 47385000 from the root of 49299354, $\sqrt{18954}$ remains. Therefore, such products will be the universal cube root of $\sqrt{18954}$ minus 135 minus the universal cube root of $\sqrt{18954}$ plus 135. Thus the entire cube, from what was demonstrated in the third book, is 10 plus the universal cube root of $\sqrt{18954}$ minus 135 minus the universal cube root of $\sqrt{18954}$ plus 135; but indeed, the three "things" <note>This refers to the term $3x$ in an equation like $x^3 + 3x = 10$</note> are the universal cube root of $\sqrt{18954}$ plus 135 minus the universal cube root of $\sqrt{18954}$ minus 135.

When all are joined together, since those universal cube roots mutually cancel each other out, the sum of the cube and the three things becomes 10, <term>to the nail</term><note>original: "ad unguem" — a Latin idiom meaning "perfectly" or "exactly," as a sculptor checks the smoothness of a joint with a fingernail</note>.

The third example: the cube and 6 things are equal to 2 <note>In modern notation: $x^3 + 6x = 2$</note>. Take 2, the third part of the number of "things," and cube it; it becomes 8. Take 1, half of the number 2, and square it; it becomes 1. Join 8 and 1, they become 9. The root of this is 3. Therefore, with the 3 doubled, to one part add 1 (half of the number), making 4; from the other subtract 1 (likewise the remaining half of the number), making 2. Subtract, therefore, the cube root of the smaller from the larger; you will have the value of the thing: the cube root of 4 minus the cube root of 2.

Remember, however, what we said in the chapter on extracting the cube root in the third book: sometimes those universal cube roots are equivalent to a whole number or a fraction, as we showed in the first example. For the universal cube root of $\sqrt{108}$ plus 10 minus the universal cube root of $\sqrt{108}$ minus 10 is 2, as is clear from the rule there, and as is also very well known by experiment.

<summary>Cardano provides numerical proofs for his cubic formula, demonstrating how complex radical expressions cancel out to yield exact integer solutions. He works through examples such as $x^3 + 6x = 20$ (yielding $x=2$) and $x^3 + 6x = 2$ (yielding $\sqrt[3]{4} - \sqrt[3]{2}$).</summary>

<header>On Arithmetic, Book 10.</header>

## Chapter XII: On the cube equal to things and a number.

### GEOMETRIC PROOF

Let there be a cube equal to things and a number <note>original: "cubus æqualis rebus & numero"</note>, and let there be two cubes, DC and DF, whose sides are AB and BC. Let the product of these sides be one-third of the number of things, and let the sum of these cubes be equal to the given number. I say that the total line AC is the valuation <note>The "valuation" (æstimatio) is the value of the unknown variable $x$.</note> of the thing sought. For since the product of AB and BC is one-third of the number of things, then three times the product of AB and BC will be the total number of things. And from the whole line AC multiplied by three times the product of AB and BC, the things themselves are produced, if AC is set as the "thing" <term>thing</term><note>The "thing" (res) is the standard Renaissance term for the unknown variable $x$.</note>. 

But from AC multiplied by three times the product of AB and BC, six bodies <note>In Cardano's geometry, "bodies" are three-dimensional rectangular prisms. This refers to the expansion of $(u+v)^3$, which contains two cubes ($u^3, v^3$) and six prisms ($3u^2v, 3uv^2$).</note> are created: three of which are from AB multiplied by the square of BC, and the other three from BC multiplied by the square of AB. These six bodies are therefore equal to the "things." These bodies, together with the cubes DC and DF, constitute the large cube AE, according to the first supposition of the sixth chapter. Since the cubes DC and DF are also equivalent to the proposed number, it follows that the cube AE is equal to the things and the number proposed—which was to be demonstrated.

It remains to show that three times AC multiplied by the product of AB and BC is equal to those six bodies. I will demonstrate this if I can prove that AB and BC multiplied by AC results in two bodies: AB multiplied by the square of BC, and BC multiplied by the square of AB. For that which is produced from AC multiplied by the product of AB and BC is equal to that which is produced from AB multiplied by the surface BE, as all sides are equal to all others. But this is equal to that which is produced from AB into the rectangles CD and DE. Furthermore, that which is produced from AB into DE is equal to that which is produced from CB into the square of AB, since all sides are equal. Therefore, the product of AC and the product of AB and BC is equal to those bodies formed by AB into the square of BC and BC into the square of AB, which is what was proposed.

### THE RULE

The rule, therefore, is this: when the cube of one-third the number of things is not greater than the square of half the constant number of the equation, subtract the former from the latter. Take the square root of the remainder, add it to half the constant number, and then subtract it from that same half. You will then have what are called a <term>Binomial</term> and an <term>Apotome</term> <note>Cardano uses "Binomial" for a sum like $a + \sqrt{b}$ and "Apotome" for a difference like $a - \sqrt{b}$.</note>, the sum of whose cube roots constitutes the value of the thing.

**Example:** A cube is equal to 6 things plus 40 <note>In modern notation: $x^3 = 6x + 40$.</note>. 
1. Take 2 (which is one-third of the number of things) and cube it; it makes 8. 
2. Subtract this from 400 (the square of 20, which is half the constant number); it makes 392. 
3. Add the square root of this to 20; it makes 20 plus $\sqrt{392}$. 
4. Also subtract it from the same; it makes 20 minus $\sqrt{392}$. 
5. The sum of the cube roots of these two values gives the valuation of the thing.

<sig>H 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: valuation,</meta>

<summary>This page presents the formal proof and step-by-step rule for solving cubic equations where the cube is equal to the "things" and a constant. It demonstrates Cardano's method of using geometric volumes to justify algebraic operations, specifically the formula for what is now known as Cardano's Method.</summary>

In modern notation, this is $\sqrt[3]{20 + \sqrt{392}} + \sqrt[3]{20 - \sqrt{392}}$. The "v" stands for *universalis*, meaning the root applies to the entire following expression.</note>. Another example: the cube is equal to 6 things plus 6 <note>x³ = 6x + 6</note>. Take the third part of the number of things, which is 2, and raise it to the cube; it makes 8. Subtract this from 9, which is the square of 3 (half of the number 6 in the equation). 1 remains, the root of which is 1. Add and subtract this from 3 (the half of the number); the resulting parts are 4 and 2. The joined cubic roots of these, which are the cubic root of 4 plus the cubic root of 2, make the valuation of the thing <note>The solution is x = $\sqrt[3]{4} + \sqrt[3]{2}$.</note>.

But where the cube of the third part of the number of things exceeds the square of half the number of the equation—which happens whenever the number of the equation is less than two-thirds of that cube <note>Cardano is describing the condition where the discriminant is negative, leading to the "irreducible case" where his formula requires taking the square root of a negative number.</note>, or where the product of two-thirds of the number of things and the root of one-third of the same number is greater than the number of the equation—then this is solved by the **Aliza** problem <term>Aliza</term><note>Referring to Cardano's book *De Regula Aliza*, where he attempted to solve the irreducible case of the cubic equation. The name "Aliza" may derive from an Arabic term or a Hebrew word for "difficult" or "exalted."</note>, of which it is spoken in the book on Geometric Problems. But if you merely wish to escape the difficulty, Chapter 25 of this book will generally satisfy you.

Let MK be half of HK, from which MN is cut off equal to CE. I say that both the cubes of HN and NK, together with the number F, are equal to the things AB, so that the number of things and the equation remain the same. And first let us show this regarding HN: for it is established that the cube of HN contains its side HN multiplied by the square of HN. However, the square AB (because GL is equal to AD, and GL is triple the square of HM) is equal to triple the square of HM and the square of MN. These, moreover, exceed the square of HN by double the product of HM and NK, according to the 4th proposition of the 2nd book of the *Elements* <note>Referring to Euclid's *Elements*, a foundational text of geometry.</note>. Therefore, they exceed it by the product of HK and NK, because HK is double HM. Therefore the cube of HN contains its side...

<term>N</term> in the surface <term>A B</term> is less than that which is produced from <term>H K</term> into <term>K N</term>. But truly, because the cube <term>G K</term> contained the "things" <note>the unknown x</note> or sides <term>H K</term> in the square <term>H K</term>, or in the square <term>A B</term>, with the number <term>F</term>, therefore by common consensus, the number <term>F</term> is equal to the product of <term>H K</term> into the difference of the squares <term>A B</term> and <term>G K</term>. Moreover, the difference between <term>G K</term> and <term>A B</term> is as great as the difference between <term>H L</term> and <term>C B</term>, because <term>A D</term> is equal to <term>G L</term>. However, the difference between <term>H L</term> and <term>C B</term> is as the squares <term>H M</term> and <term>M N</term>; therefore, from the difference of the square <term>H M</term> and <term>M N</term> into <term>H K</term>, the number <term>F</term> is produced. But truly, from the 4th [proposition] of the 2nd [book] of the Elements <note>Euclid's Elements II.4, which deals with the square of a sum</note>, the difference of the squares <term>H M</term> (or <term>M K</term>) and <term>M N</term> is double <term>M N</term> into <term>N K</term> with the square of <term>N K</term>, and likewise <term>M N</term> and <term>M K</term> into <term>N K</term>, and likewise <term>H N</term> into <term>N K</term>. Therefore, from <term>H K</term> into the product of <term>H N</term> into <term>N K</term>, the number <term>F</term> is produced. Let the number <term>F</term> therefore be added to the cube of <term>H N</term>, and on the other side, the product of <term>H K</term> into <term>K N</term> multiplied by <term>H N</term>, from the product of <term>H N</term> into the surface <term>A B</term> minus the product of <term>H K</term> into <term>K N</term>, the cube <term>H N</term> with the number will become equal to <term>H N</term> multiplied into <term>A B</term>, or the "things" from <term>A B</term>, which was to be proven. Similarly, because the difference of <term>G K</term> and <term>A B</term>, which is <term>H N</term> into <term>K N</term> multiplied by <term>K H</term>, produces <term>F</term>, the difference also between <term>A B</term> and the square <term>K N</term> (since <term>A B</term> is equal to the squares <term>H M</term>, <term>M K</term>, and <term>M N</term>, and to the product of <term>K M</term> into <term>M H</term>) is equal to the difference of double <term>K H</term> into <term>H N</term> from the square <term>N H</term>, with the rectangle <term>H N</term> into <term>N K</term> added to it. But that which is produced from <term>H N</term> into <term>N K</term> with the square <term>N H</term> is equal to the product of <term>K H</term> into <term>H N</term>, by the 3rd [proposition] of the 2nd [book] of the Elements <note>Euclid II.3: "If a straight line is cut at random, the rectangle contained by the whole and one of the segments is equal to the rectangle contained by the segments and the square on the first-mentioned segment."</note>. Therefore, the square <term>A B</term> exceeds the square <term>N K</term> by the product of <term>K H</term> into <term>H N</term> once. Since, therefore, the number <term>F</term> contains <term>N K</term> in the product of <term>K H</term> into <term>H N</term>, and the cube <term>K N</term> contains <term>K N</term> in the square <term>K N</term>, it will be that the cube <term>K N</term> with the number <term>F</term>—or with the product of <term>K N</term> into the rectangle <term>K H</term> into <term>H N</term>—is equal to the product of <term>A B</term> into <term>K N</term>. Therefore, the cube <term>K N</term> with the same number <term>F</term> is equal to the same number of things <term>A B</term>. From this demonstration, it is clear that the solution <note>original: "æquatio", here meaning the value of the unknown</note> of a cube equal to things and a number is equal to both solutions of a cube and the same number equal to as many things, when joined together. For example, if a cube is equal to 10 things and 12 <note>x³ = 10x + 12</note>, and the solution is the root of 7 plus 1 <note>√7 + 1</note>, the solutions of the cube plus 12 equal to 10 things <note>x³ + 12 = 10x</note>, which are 2 and the root of 7 minus 1 <note>√7 - 1</note>, when joined together, will make the root of 7 plus 1 <note>2 + (√7 - 1) = √7 + 1</note>.

## THE RULE.

The rule, therefore, is: when there is a cube and a number equal to things, find the valuation <note>solution</note> of the cube equal to as many things and the same number. Take half of this [valuation], multiply it by itself and triple it. Subtract this from the number of things, and the root <term>Rz</term> of the remainder, added to or subtracted from half the valuation of the cube equal to things and a number, shows the valuation of the cube and number equal to things. For example, the cube plus 3 is equal to 8 positions <note>x³ + 3 = 8x</note>; then I find the valuation of the cube equal to 8 things plus 3 from the preceding chapter, and it is 3. I multiply half of this [3] by itself; it makes 2 and 1/4. Triple it; it makes 6 and 3/4. Subtract this from 8
