number of things <note>the coefficient of the unknown x</note>, the remainder is 1 ¼, the <term>square root</term><note>original: "Rz", abbreviation for radix</note> of which, added to or subtracted from 1 ½ (which is half the <term>estimation</term><note>In this context, Cardano uses "estimation" to mean the value or root of the unknown x.</note> of the cube equal to things and a number), shows both of the sought-after estimations: one is 1 ½ plus the square root of 1 ¼, and the other is 1 ½ minus the square root of 1 ¼.

## DEMONSTRATION.

Now let us also show how, once one estimation is obtained, the other may be found without the help of the preceding chapter. Let it be such that from <term>AD</term> into the square of <term>AC</term> the number of the equation is produced, so that the squares of <term>AD</term> and <term>AC</term> joined together make the number of things. From the 8th chapter, <term>AD</term> will be the estimation of the thing. Let there be a line <term>FH</term>, to which if the square of half of <term>AD</term> were added, the whole would be equal to the square of <term>AC</term> and the square of half of <term>AD</term>. I say that <term>FH</term> is the remaining estimation, when the cube with the number (produced from <term>AD</term> into the square of <term>AC</term>) is equal to the things (the squares of <term>AC</term> and <term>AD</term> combined).

Let the square <term>EG</term> be formed, which together with the square <term>FH</term> is equal to the squares of <term>AC</term> and <term>AD</term> joined. Therefore, because the square of the line composed of <term>FH</term> and half of <term>AD</term> is equal to the squares of <term>CA</term> and half of <term>AD</term>, it follows from the 4th [proposition] of the 2nd [book] of the *Elements* <note>Euclid's Elements II.4, regarding the square of a sum of two segments</note> that, by removing the common square of half of <term>AD</term>, the square <term>AC</term> is equal to the square <term>FH</term> and double <term>FH</term> into half of <term>AD</term>. Thus, the square <term>AC</term> is equal to the rectangle of <term>FH</term> and <term>AD</term> once, plus the square of <term>FH</term>. Therefore, from the 16th [proposition] of the 6th [book] of the *Elements*, <term>AB</term> is the proportional between <term>FH</term> and the sum of <term>FH</term> and <term>AD</term>.

Because truly the square <term>EG</term>, added to the product of <term>FH</term> by itself and by <term>AD</term>, makes as much as if it were added to the square <term>AC</term>—since <term>EG</term> and the square <term>FH</term> are equal to the squares <term>AD</term> and <term>AC</term> by assumption—the square <term>AC</term> plus the square <term>AD</term> plus the product of <term>FH</term> and <term>AD</term> will be equal to the squares <term>AC</term> and <term>EG</term>. Then, by removing the common square <term>AC</term>, the square <term>EG</term> will be equal to that which is produced from <term>FH</term> into <term>AD</term> plus the square of <term>AD</term>. 

From the 16th [proposition] of the 6th [book], <term>EF</term> is therefore proportional between <term>AD</term> and the sum of <term>AD</term> and <term>HF</term>. Since similarly, as has been shown, <term>AB</term> is proportional between <term>FH</term> and the sum of <term>FH</term> and <term>AD</term>, it will follow from the 34th [proposition] of the 5th [book] of my work *On Euclid* that because <term>FH</term> and <term>AD</term> joined are the first quantity in each order, the proportion of <term>FH</term> to <term>AD</term> is as <term>AB</term> to <term>EF</term> squared. Therefore, from the 17th [proposition] of the 6th [book] of the *Elements*, <term>FH</term> is to <term>AD</term> as <term>AC</term> is to <term>EG</term>. Thus, from the 34th [proposition] of the 11th [book] of the *Elements*, the <term>solid</term> <note>original: "corpus", meaning a 3D volume or product of three dimensions</note> contained under <term>FH</term> and the square of <term>EG</term> is equal to the solid under <term>AD</term> and the square of <term>AC</term>, and therefore to the number of the equation. And since the squares <term>EG</term> and <term>HF</term> are equal to the number of things (because they equal the squares <term>AC</term> and <term>AD</term>), <term>HF</term> will also be an estimation of the thing according to the 8th chapter of this book. From which comes the rule.

## RULE.

Multiply half of the first estimation by itself, and triple it, and subtract this from the number of things; the square root of the remainder, with half of the prior estimation subtracted from it, is the equation <note>the value of the second root</note>.

## On the Cube Equal to Squares and a Number. Chapter 14.

But if the cube is equal to <term>squares</term><note>original: "quadratis", the second power of the unknown, $x^2$</note> and a number, the chapter is converted into a cube equal to things and a number by the first mode of conversion, which is "from the whole to the part." For the second mode is "from the part to the whole," the third "from the difference of parts," and the fourth "from proportion."

->DEMONSTRATION.<-

Let the cube AE, in the figure for chapter 12, be equal to 6 squares AC and 100. Since the square AC consists of the square AB and a <term>gnomon</term><note>original: "gnomone", a geometric shape that, when added to a square, forms a larger square; here used to visualize algebraic expansion</note> with its surrounding parts, the cube AC will be equal to 6 squares AB, plus 6 gnomons, and 100. The gnomon, however, consists of the square BC and twice the product of AB and BC. Therefore, the cube AC consists of 6 squares AB, 6 squares BC, 6 times twice the product of AB and BC, and 100. But twice AB times BC makes 4 things, because AB is the "thing" and BC is 2. And 6 squares of BC are triple the cube of BC, because BC is the third part of 6. Therefore, the cube AC is equal to 6 squares AB, 24 things, triple the cube of BC, and 100. Now it is established that the number 24 (the number of things) is formed from 6 (the number of squares) multiplied by 4 (which is double the third part of that same number). 

But from another side, it is also established that the cube AC [is composed of] the cubes AB and BC, plus triple AB into the square of BC, and triple BC into the square of AB; for this was shown in the first supposition of the 6th chapter. Therefore, the cube AC is equal to the cubes AB and BC, plus 6 squares and 12 things. Therefore, the cube AB, the cube BC, 6 squares, and 12 things are equal to 6 squares, 24 things, triple the cube of BC, and 100. 

It is certain, moreover, that the number of squares remains the same, because it is triple BC, and BC was the third part of the number of squares. And the number of things is [derived] from the number of squares multiplied by its own third part, for this is always equal to triple the square of the third part. Therefore, by subtracting from both sides one cube of BC, 6 squares, and 12 things (namely, as many things as are produced from the number of squares into its own third part), there remains the cube AB equal to 100, 12 things, and double the cube of BC. It is manifest, moreover, that the number 100 remains the same.

This transformation is achieved by substituting the variable with a value related to one-third of the square's coefficient.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the demonstration from Chapter 14 regarding equations of the form "cube equals squares and a number" ($x^3 = ax^2 + c$). It provides the general rule for calculation and then transitions to Chapter 15, which treats the case where the cube and squares together equal a number ($x^3 + ax^2 = c$).</meta>

->GIROLAMO CARDANO<-
<meta>catchword: and which</meta>

and that the number of <term>things</term><note>original: "rerum", the linear unknown $x$</note> is produced from the number of <term>squares</term><note>original: "quadratorum", the $x^2$ term</note> multiplied by its own third part, and that double the cube of $bc$ is 16, because $bc$ is 2; therefore, the cube $AB$ is equal to 12 things and 116 in number. Thus, according to the preceding chapter, once $AB$ is found, we shall add $bc$ (the third part of the number of squares) to it, and $AC$ will be composed. And because in seeking $AB$ we reduce the third part of the number of things to a cube, and this third part of the number of things is the square of the third part of the number of squares, this rule arises from the final contraction.

### RULE.

Add the cube of the third part of the number of squares to half the constant number of the equation, and multiply the whole resulting sum by itself. From this square, subtract the cube of the square of the third part of the number of squares. Take the square root of the remainder and both add it to and subtract it from half the sum you had previously squared. You will then have a **Binomial** and an **Apotome**<note>In Renaissance algebra, a Binomial is a sum of two terms (like $a + \sqrt{b}$) and an Apotome is their difference (like $a - \sqrt{b}$)</note>, the cubic <term>roots</term><note>original: "Rz", an abbreviation for "radix"</note> of which you must join together. To these, add the third part of the number of squares, and the resulting total is the <term>estimation</term><note>original: "æstimatio", the value of the unknown $x$</note> of the thing. For example, let the cube be equal to 6 squares plus 20 ($x^3 = 6x^2 + 20$). Add 8 (the cube of 2, which is one-third of 6) to 10 (half of 20), making 18. From the square of this, which is 324, subtract 64 (the cube of the square of 2); the remainder is 260. Add and subtract the root of this from 18, and you will have $18 + \sqrt{260}$ and $18 - \sqrt{260}$. The cubic roots of these, joined together and with the third part of the number of squares added, constitute the thing.

## On the Cube and Squares Equal to a Number. Chapter 15.

### DEMONSTRATION.

This chapter is converted in a second way. The difference, however, is that the first mode showed that the third part of the number of squares should be added, while the second shows it should be subtracted. Let there be then, in the figure of the 12th chapter, a cube $AB$ with 6 squares of $AB$ equal to 100. Let $BC$ be placed as the third part of the number of squares, and let the cube $AC$ be completed. Therefore, the cube $AC$ will be equal to the cube $AB$, plus 6 squares, plus 12 things, plus the cube of $BC$, according to the first supposition of the 6th chapter. In place of the cube $AB$ and 6 squares, let 100 be substituted, for those were equal to 100. Therefore, the cube $AC$ will be equal to 12 things, plus the cube $BC$, plus 100. But 12 things from $AB$ fall short of 12 things from $AC$ by 12 times $BC$. Moreover, that 12, as shown in the preceding, is produced from triple the square of $BC$. Therefore, $12 BC$ is triple the cube of $BC$. Thus, the cube $AC$ and triple the cube of $BC$ are equal to 12 things, the cube $BC$, and 100. Therefore, once the common cube $BC$ is subtracted once, the cube $AB$ with double the cube $BC$ will be equal to 12 things and 100. Double the cube of $BC$ is 16, and the number of things is triple the square of $BC$, the third part of the number of...

<meta>This page continues the demonstration from Chapter 15, which treats the equation "a cube and squares equal a number" ($x^3 + ax^2 = N$). The previous page established the geometric logic for substituting variables to simplify the equation; this page provides the generalized rule and worked examples.</meta>

<page-num>34</page-num>
<header>ON ARITHMETIC BOOK 10:</header>

of squares, and therefore once the <term>estimation</term><note>original: "æstimatione"; the value of the unknown variable</note> of AC is found, we will discard BC, the third part of the number of squares, and the known AB will be left; according to this, the rule will be as follows:

### RULE.

Raise the third part of the number of squares to the cube, and double that cube, and take the difference between it and the constant number of the equation. Then, triple the square of that same third part of the number of squares, and you will have the <term>things</term><note>original: "res"; the linear $x$ term</note>, which are equal to a cube and a number if the double of the cube was greater than the number of the equation; or the things with a number equal to a cube if the double of the cube was less than the number of the equation; or the things equal to the cube where there is no difference between the numbers. Then, once the equation is solved, subtract the third part of the number of squares from that solution, and the remainder is the estimation of the original thing. 

**Example.** A cube and 6 squares are equal to 100. Raise 2 <note>the third part of 6</note> to the cube, which makes 8; double it, making 16. Subtract this from 100 and you will have a cube equal to 84 plus 12 things (where 12 things is the triple of the square of 2, the third part of 6, the number of squares). Therefore, according to the 12th chapter, the thing is: the universal cubic root of 42 plus the square root of 1700, plus the universal cubic root of 42 minus the square root of 1700. From this, subtract 2 (the third part of 6) and it will be the sought estimation of the thing when a cube and 6 squares are equal to 100, namely: the universal cubic root of 42 + $\sqrt{1700}$ + the universal cubic root of 42 - $\sqrt{1700}$ - 2. 

Again, let a cube and 6 squares be equal to 25. I subtract 16 (the double of the cube of the third part of 6) from 25, which leaves 9; and there are 12 things, as before, equal to the cube. Therefore, the thing is worth $\sqrt{5 \frac{1}{4}} + 1 \frac{1}{2}$. Subtract 2, and the sought estimation remains: $\sqrt{5 \frac{1}{4}} - \frac{1}{2}$. 

Again, a cube and 6 squares are equal to 16. Subtract the double cube of 2 (which is 16) from the number 16, and nothing remains. Then take the triple of the square of that same third part of the number of squares, which is 12, the number of things equal to the cube. Wherefore the square is equal to 12, and thus the thing is $\sqrt{12}$. Subtract 2 (the third part of 6), and the estimation of the thing will be left as $\sqrt{12} - 2$. 

Again, let a cube and 6 squares be equal to 7. Take the difference between 7 and 16 (the double cube of 2), which is 9. Because the double of the cubes is greater than the number of the equation, and the number of things is 12 as before, we will have a cube plus 9 equal to 12 things. Therefore, the thing is worth 3, or $\sqrt{5 \frac{1}{4}} - 1 \frac{1}{2}$. Subtract 2, and the estimation of the cube and 6 squares will be 1, or $\sqrt{5 \frac{1}{4}} - 3 \frac{1}{2}$. This latter is a <term>negative solution</term><note>original: "in rè m:" (in re minus); Cardano recognizes negative roots here but calls them "fictitious" or "minus" compared to "true" positive roots</note>, because $3 \frac{1}{2}$ is greater than $\sqrt{5 \frac{1}{4}}$. The 6 squares are $105 - \sqrt{9261}$, and the cube is $\sqrt{9261} - 98$; if the cube and 6 squares are joined, they become precisely 7, as is evident.

<margin>Corollary.</margin> From this it is manifest why the chapter on "a cube and a number equal to squares" is not demonstrated from the chapter on "a cube and squares equal to a number." Just as the chapter on "a cube and a number equal to things" was demonstrated from the chapter on "a cube equal to things and a number." numbers of the things; it is better, therefore, to lead the chapter of the cube and the number equal to the squares immediately to the chapter of the cube and the number equal to the things, rather than to that same chapter by means of the chapter of the cube and the squares equal to a number, for the operation would be longer and the demonstration would turn out more confused.

# DEMONSTRATION.

Another demonstration by Ludovico <note>Ludovico Ferrari (1522–1565), Cardano's student and a brilliant mathematician who discovered the solution to the quartic equation.</note>, similar to our universal one in Chapter 7, and this was discovered by Ludovico Ferrari. Let there be a cube $AC$ and 6 squares, for the sake of example, $CD$ equal to 100; because $BD$ is the height of the 6 squares, $BD$ will be 6. Therefore, setting $AD$ as some square <note>In this substitution, Ferrari let the square of a new variable $y$ represent the segment $AD$.</note>, $AB$ will be the square minus 6. The surface $AC$, therefore, is 1 <term>squared-square</term><note>original: "qd'qd"; $x^4$</note> plus 36, minus 12 squares. This is the base of the body $AE$; wherefore the body $AE$ is 1 <term>cubed-square</term><note>original: "cu'qd"; $x^6$</note> plus 36 squares, minus 12 squared-squares. This is equal to 100. Therefore 10, the square root of 100, is equal to 1 cube [and] 6 things <note>original: "1 cub. 6 co: radici"; Cardano is performing a reduction where the square root of the expression $y^6 - 12y^4 + 36y^2$ is $y^3 - 6y$.</note> to the square root of 1 cubed-square plus 36 squares minus 12 squared-squares. Thus the estimation of the thing is known, which, when multiplied by itself—since $AD$ was set as 1 square—will give $AD$. From this, subtract $BD$, which was 6, and the remainder will be $AB$, the thing sought.

# RULE.

The rule, therefore, is: set the number of squares [as] the number of <term>things</term><note>original: "res"; the unknown $x$.</note>, which together with the square root [$\text{Rz}$] of the proposed number are equal to the cube. Multiply the found estimation by itself, and from the product cast away the number of squares or things; the remainder is the estimation of the thing. 

**Example.** Let a cube and 6 squares be equal to 40. You will say, therefore, a cube is equal to 6 things and the square root of 40. The estimation of the thing, according to its own chapter, is the universal cubic root of (the square root of 10 plus the square root of 2), plus the universal cubic root of (the square root of 10 minus the square root of 2). Multiply this by itself, and the product will be the universal cubic root of (12 plus the square root of 80), plus the universal cubic root of (12 minus the square root of 80), plus 4. Cast away 6, the number of things, and the sought estimation will remain: the universal cubic root of (12 plus the square root of 80), plus the universal cubic root of (12 minus the square root of 80), minus 2.

## On the Cube and Number Equal to Squares. Chapter XVI.

### RULE.

**T**his chapter is self-evident from the demonstration of the 7th chapter. The rule is: multiply the <term>cubic root</term><note>original: "℞ cubicam"</note> of the number by the number of squares; the result will be the number of <term>things</term><note>original: "rerum"; the unknown variable $x$.</note> equal to a cube and that same number. Then, once the <term>values</term><note>original: "æstimationibus"; the solutions or roots of the equation.</note> are found, multiply the cubic root of the number by itself, and divide the product by any found value; both sought values will result. 

*Example:* 1 cube + 64 is equal to 18 squares. Multiply 18 by 4 (the cubic root of 64), which makes 72. This is the number of things equal to the cube + 64 <note>i.e., $x^3 + 64 = 72x$</note>. The values for this equation, according to its own chapter, are 8 and the root of 24 minus 4. Divide 16 (the square of 4, which is the cubic root of 64) by these values. The results are 2 and the root of 96 plus 8; these are the values sought for the original equation.

### DEMONSTRATION.

<margin>A B</margin> And let one of the values obtained be $AB$. I wish to find the remaining one. I make the square of $AB$, which is $AC$, and I subtract $AB$ from the number of squares, and let $AD$ remain. Let $AD$ be multiplied by the sum of $AB$ and a fourth part of $AD$; and of the resulting surface, let the <term>side</term><note>original: "latus"; the square root.</note> that can be formed from it be taken, and to it let half of $AD$ be added, and let this be $EF$, which I say is the second value. 

Let the square $EF$ be made, and let $EG$ be taken, which, when joined with $EF$, is equal to the sum of $AB$ and $AD$. Therefore, because the square $EF$ is equal to the product of the side by itself, and half of $AD$ by itself, and the product of the side by $AD$ (from the 4th [proposition] of the 2nd [book] of the <term>Elements</term><note>Euclid's *Elements* of Geometry, the standard mathematical textbook of the Renaissance.</note>), the square $EF$ will be equal to the product of $AD$ and the sum of $AB$ and half of $AD$, and the side (from the 16th [proposition] of the 6th [book] of the *Elements*). Therefore $EF$ is a mean proportional between $AD$ and the sum of $AB$ and the side and half of $AD$. But half of $AD$ and the side constitute $EF$, by the assumption. Therefore $EF$ is proportional between $AD$ and the aggre...

the sum of <term>AB</term> and <term>EF</term>. Again, the product of (<term>AB</term> and <term>AD</term>) and (<term>AB</term> and <term>EF</term>) is equal to the product of (<term>EF</term> and <term>EG</term>) and the sum of <term>AB</term> and <term>EF</term>; this is because, by the assumption, <term>EF</term> and <term>EG</term> are equal to <term>AB</term> and <term>AD</term>, while <term>AB</term> and <term>EF</term> remain the same. Furthermore, from what has been proven, the product of <term>AD</term> and the sum of <term>AB</term> and <term>EF</term> is equal to the square of <term>EF</term>. Therefore, the product of <term>AB</term> and the sum of <term>AB</term> and <term>EF</term>, together with the square of <term>EF</term>, is equal to the product of (<term>EF</term> and <term>EG</term>) and the sum of <term>EF</term> and <term>AB</term>. Thus, by discarding the common square of <term>EF</term>, the product of <term>AB</term> and the sum of <term>AB</term> and <term>EF</term> will be equal to the product of (<term>AB</term> and <term>EF</term>) and <term>EG</term>, together with the product of <term>EF</term> and <term>AB</term>. Then, by subtracting the common product of <term>AB</term> and <term>EF</term> once more, the square of <term>AB</term> remains, which is equal to the product of (<term>AB</term> and <term>EF</term>) and <term>EG</term>. For this reason, <term>AB</term> is the <term>mean proportional</term><note>The geometric mean; in a series $a, b, c$, $b$ is the mean if $a:b = b:c$.</note> between <term>EG</term> and the sum of <term>AB</term> and <term>EF</term>. Indeed, it was already stated that <term>EF</term> is the mean between <term>AD</term> and the sum of <term>AB</term> and <term>EF</term>. There are, therefore, three proportional quantities in two series, of which the first term in each series is the same,

| AB | EF |
| EG | AD |

namely the sum of <term>AB</term> and <term>EF</term>. Therefore, from the 34th proposition of the 5th book of our <term>commentary on Euclid</term><note>Cardano is referencing his own published notes or lectures on Euclid's Elements.</note>, <term>EG</term> is to <term>AD</term> as the square of <term>AB</term> is to the square of <term>EF</term>. Thus, by the 17th proposition of the 6th book of the *Elements*, <term>EG</term> is to <term>AD</term> as <term>AC</term> <note>Presumably representing the square of AB in this context.</note> is to the square of <term>EF</term>. Therefore, from the 34th proposition of the 11th book of the *Elements*, the <term>solid</term><note>original: "corpus"; a three-dimensional product representing a cubic term.</note> formed by <term>AD</term> and <term>AC</term> is equal to the solid formed by <term>EG</term> and the square of <term>EF</term>. But <term>AB</term> was the <term>value of the thing</term><note>The solution or root of the equation, $x$.</note>; therefore, the solid formed by <term>AD</term> and <term>AC</term> is equal to the number of the equation, assuming the sum of <term>AD</term> and <term>AB</term> is the number of squares, according to the demonstration held in the 8th chapter. Consequently, the product of <term>EG</term> and the square of <term>EF</term> is equal to the number of the equation. Since <term>EF</term> and <term>EG</term> together are equal to the number of squares (because they equal the sum of <term>AB</term> and <term>AD</term>), and the product of <term>GE</term> and the square of <term>EF</term> produces the number of the equation, then by the 8th chapter, <term>E</term> will be the value of the thing, which was to be proven.

->**RULE.**<-

The rule, therefore, is: subtract the first solution from the number of squares, and multiply the remainder by the sum of the first solution and a fourth part of that same remainder. Take the root of this product, and to it add half of the same remainder; the sum is the sought value of the thing. 

*Example:* Let 1 cube plus 24 be equal to 8 squares, and the known solution be 2. I subtract 2 from 8 (the number of squares), leaving 6. I multiply this by $3 \frac{1}{2}$, which is composed of 2 (the first solution) and $1 \frac{1}{2}$ (a fourth part of the remainder 6); this makes 21. To the root of this, add half of the first solution, which is 1 <note>Note: Following the rule strictly, one would add half the remainder ($6 \div 2 = 3$), resulting in $\sqrt{21} + 3$. Cardano here writes "half the first solution," which is $2 \div 2 = 1$. The math for $x^3 - 8x^2 + 24 = 0$ actually yields roots $2, 3 + \sqrt{21},$ and $3 - \sqrt{21}$.</note>; the result is $\sqrt{21} + 1$, the sought value.

<meta>This page continues the mathematical proofs found in Cardano's Book 10 of his work on arithmetic, likely from his "Ars Magna" (The Great Art, 1545). This section focuses on the method for "depressing" cubic equations—that is, removing the square term ($x^2$) by substituting a new variable, which simplifies the equation into a solvable form.</meta>

<header>ON ARITHMETIC, BOOK 10.</header>

# ON ARITHMETIC, BOOK 10.

### PROOF.

<margin>Let</margin> For the sake of example, let there be the cube <term>AB</term>, plus 6 squares and 20 <term>positions</term><note>Cardano uses "positions" (positiones) and "things" (res) to refer to the unknown $x$ in an equation.</note> equal to 100. I shall add <term>BC</term> to <term>AB</term>, and let <term>BC</term> be 2, which is the third part of the number of squares <note>The coefficient of the square term is 6; a third of this is 2. This substitution $y = x + 2$ is the standard method for removing the $x^2$ term.</note>. A universal cube <term>AC</term> is described according to the way it is composed of its eight parts. Therefore, there will be the cube <term>AB</term>, the surface <term>FD</term> with its height, and the cube <term>BC</term> which is 8 (since <term>BC</term> is 2). The <term>bodies</term><note>Solid geometric figures representing terms in the algebraic expansion $(x+2)^3$.</note> of <term>AD</term> are equal to 6 squares of <term>AB</term>, and the bodies of 12 <term>AB</term>, or twelve times <term>AB</term>, are from the sixth chapter of this book. Since the cube <term>AB</term> and 6 squares and 20 positions are equal to 100, let 8 positions (which are the remainder to make up the 20 positions) be added to the cube <term>AC</term>. This cube <term>AC</term> was already equal to the cube <term>AB</term>, plus 6 squares, 12 positions, and the cube <term>BC</term>. Thus, the cube <term>AC</term> plus 8 positions will be equal to 108. For the cube <term>AC</term> exceeds the three bodies <term>DA</term>, <term>DE</term>, and <term>DF</term> by the cube <term>CD</term>, which is 8. But because 8 positions of <term>AB</term> fall short of 8 positions of the larger cube <term>AC</term> by 8 times <term>BC</term> (or eight times <term>BC</term>, which is 2), we will therefore add eight times <term>BC</term> to both sides. It becomes: the cube plus 8 things is equal to 124. Thus, <term>AC</term> is known from its own chapter <note>Refers to the solution for $x^3 + ax = N$ found earlier in the book.</note>; subtract <term>BC</term>, and <term>AB</term> is left. 

Again, let the cube <term>AB</term>, plus 6 squares and 12 things, be equal to 100. Therefore, by adding the common cube <term>BC</term>, the cube <term>AC</term> will be equal to 108. Then <term>AC</term> is the <term>cube root</term> of 108, and <term>AB</term> is 2 less than the known <term>AC</term>. 

Once more, let the cube and 6 squares of <term>AB</term> and 2 positions be equal to 100. Thus, by adding the 10 remaining positions to complete the bodies <term>DE</term>, and by adding the cube <term>BC</term>, the cube <term>AC</term> will be equal to the 10 extra positions and 108. But since 10 positions of <term>AB</term> fall short of 10 positions of <term>AC</term> by 10 times <term>BC</term>, we will add 10 times <term>BC</term> to both sides. The cube <term>AC</term> plus 20 will become equal to 10 positions plus 108. Subtract 20 from both sides, and there remains the cube <term>AC</term> equal to 10 positions plus 88. Once <term>AC</term> is found, subtract <term>BC</term>, and <term>AB</term> will necessarily be known.

### RULE.

The general rule, therefore, is: multiply the 1/3 part of the number of squares (which we demonstrate with this sign, <gloss>τρq̃d:</gloss><note>original: "τρq̃d:" - a shorthand for "triens quadratorum" or one-third of the squares.</note>) into a cube, and add it to the number. Then multiply the number of squares by its third part, and the difference between that product and the number of "things" <note>The linear $x$ term.</note> is the number of things to be added to the cube—where the product was less than the proposed number of things—or added to the number—where the product was greater than the proposed number of things. If, therefore, the difference between the product and the number of things is zero, it will be

<summary>This page demonstrates the geometric and algebraic method for "depressing" a cubic equation by removing the square term through substitution, specifically using the example of $x^3 + 6x^2 + 20x = 100$. Cardano provides three variations of the linear term to show how the final depressed equation ($y^3 + ay = N$ or $y^3 = ay + N$) is derived.</summary>
<keywords>cubic equations, Ars Magna, depressing the cubic, geometric algebra, square term, position, thing, Cardano, substitution</keywords>

--- Page 79 ---
<meta>This page continues Cardano’s explanation of how to solve cubic equations that include a square term ($x^2$). He is describing a method of variable substitution—shifting the "thing" (the unknown $x$) by a constant (one-third the coefficient of the $x^2$ term) to eliminate the square term and "depress" the equation into a simpler form that he has already taught how to solve.</meta>

->JEROME CARDANO<-

the cube will be equal to the number already accumulated; then, having taken the cubic root of that number, subtract from it the <term>third part of the squares</term><note>Latin: τp̃qd (tertia pars quadratorum). This is the value used to shift the variable, specifically $1/3$ of the coefficient of the $x^2$ term.</note>, and the remainder is the <term>valuation of the thing</term><note>The "valuation of the thing" (rei æstimatio) refers to the numerical value of the unknown $x$.</note>. But if the positions and the cube are equal to a number, you shall multiply the number of positions by the <term>third part of the squares</term>, and add the product to the number already aggregated, and you will have the cube and the things now found equal to the number already aggregated. Then, from that solution, subtract the <term>third part of the squares</term>, and the remainder is the valuation. But if the product is greater than the number of things, take the difference (which is the number of things) and multiply it by the <term>third part of the squares</term>, and subtract the product from the number you had aggregated. If nothing remains, you will have the cube equal to the proposed things only; therefore, by reducing it to a lower denomination, you will have the square equal to a number, and the thing will be the square root of the number of things, from which you subtract the <term>third part of the squares</term>, and the remainder will be the valuation of the thing. But if, in the subtraction of the product (of the number of things and the <term>third part of the squares</term>) from the aggregated number, something remains, that number together with the things already proposed is equal to the cube; then, from that solution, subtract the <term>third part of the squares</term>, and the remainder is the sought-after valuation. But if the product of the number of things and the <term>third part of the squares</term> were greater than the number already aggregated, the difference is the number which, with the cube, is equal to the things already proposed; then, having the solution, subtract the <term>third part of the squares</term>, and the remainder is the valuation of the thing.

<margin>Corollary</margin>
From this it is clear that such a case is resolved into five cases, which are those placed here in the margin; and they cannot be resolved into more. In some of the following, however, the resolution is made into the last three only. In all cases of four denominations, it is common that when 

<insert>
| cube and things equal to a number.
| cube equal to a number.
| cube equal to things.
| cube equal to things and a number.
| cube and a number equal to things.
</insert>

they have been resolved into a case of three or two denominations, the <term>third part of the squares</term> is added to or subtracted from the found valuation. In this chapter, it is always subtracted. It is also common to every case that the number of things and the number itself are constituted in the same way; just as here, the number of things is the difference between the number of things assumed in the case of four denominations and the product of the number of squares multiplied by its own third part. And the number of the case into which it is resolved is the difference between the product of the number of things now found multiplied by the <term>third part of the squares</term>, and the sum of the cube of the <term>third part of the squares</term> and the first number of the equation.

You will say, therefore: a cube and 6 squares and 12 things are equal to 22. Therefore, cube 2 (the third part of the number of squares); it becomes 8. Add this to 22; it becomes 30. Then multiply 6 (the number of squares) by 2 (its third part)...

### QUESTION II.

Example of the second mode. Find four numbers in <term>continuous proportion</term><note>A geometric progression, where $a:b = b:c = c:d$.</note>, of which the first is 3, and the [sum of the] remaining three is 19. Let the second be 1 thing, then the third will be $1/3$ of a square, and the fourth will be $1/9$ of a cube. Therefore, 1 <term>position</term><note>Latin: positio, another term for the unknown $x$.</note>, $1/3$ of a square, and $1/9$ of a cube are equal to 19. Multiply to obtain whole numbers and you will have a cube plus 3 squares plus 9 things equal to 171, for all are multiplied by 9. Then add the cube of the third part of the number of squares to 171—this is 1—making 172. Next, multiply 3 (the number of squares) by its third part, which makes 3; the difference between this product and 9 (the number of things) is 6, which is the number of things that, together with the cube, are equal to the number (because the product was less than the things). Therefore, multiply 6 (the number of things) by 1 (the third part of the squares), which makes 6; add this to 172, making 178. Thus, the cube and 6 things are equal to 178. The valuation of the thing is the <term>universal cube root</term><note>Latin: Rz v: cubica (radix universalis), referring to the cube root of the entire binomial expression that follows.</note> of the square root of 7,929 plus 89, plus the universal cube root of the square root of 7,929 minus 89. From this, subtract 1 (the third part of the squares); you will have the second quantity [the thing]: the universal cube root of the square root of 7,929 plus 89, minus the universal cube root of the square root of 7,929 minus 89, minus 1. From this, you will have the others.

Example of the third mode. A cube and 6 squares and 1 position are equal to 14. Add the cube of 2 (the third part of the squares), which is 8, to 14, making 22. Then multiply 6 (the number of squares) by 2 (the third part), which makes 12. The difference between this and the number of things is 11, the number of things equal to the cube with the number (because the produced number 12 was greater than the number of things). Therefore, multiply 11 by 2 (the third part of the squares), which makes 22. The difference between this and the previous aggregate number is zero. Therefore, we will have the cube equal to 11 things; thus, the square is equal to 11. The thing, therefore, is the square root of 11, from which subtract 2 (the third part of the squares). The valuation of the thing becomes the square root of 11 minus 2. Note that you took the difference in the number and did not add it because the things were equal to the cube, and the cube with things was not equal to the number as in the preceding example.

### QUESTION III.

Example of the fourth mode. A prince commands a sacred temple to be built according to an oracle, the space of which is 400 cubits; its length is to be 6 cubits greater than the width, and the width 3 cubits greater than the height. The quantities are sought. Let the height be the thing; the width will be 3 plus [the thing], and the length will be 9 plus [the thing]. Multiply...

From this, subtract 4 (the <term>third part of the squares</term>), and you will have the height. Once this is obtained, by adding 3 and 9, you will have the width and the length, as you see:

Length: Universal cubic root of 190 plus the square root of 35,757, plus the universal cubic root of 190 minus the square root of 35,757, plus 5.

Example of the fifth mode. A cube and 6 squares and 2 <term>things</term> are equal to 3. Add 8 (the cube of the <term>third part of the squares</term>) to 3, which makes 11. Then multiply 6 by its third part, which makes 12; the difference from 2 (the number of things) is 10. Multiply this number of things by 2 (the <term>third part of the squares</term>), which makes 20; the difference between this and 11 is 9, the number which, with the cube, is equal to 10 things (because the second product is greater than the aggregate number). I call the "second product" that which is made from the number of things just found multiplied by the <term>third part of the squares</term>. Therefore, the <term>valuation</term><note>The numerical value or root of the equation.</note> of the thing when the cube and 9 are equal to 10 things is 1, or the square root of 9 1/4 minus 1/2. Subtract 2 (the <term>third part of the squares</term>) and these will be the two sought valuations: the one is the square root of 9 1/4 minus 2 1/2, the other is minus 1.

### On the cube and things equal to squares and a number. Chapter XVIII.

### DEMONSTRATION.

In the same figure, let there be a cube AC with 33 AC, equal to 6 squares of AC plus 100 (for the sake of example). Let the cube AC be divided—with BC being the <term>third part of the squares</term>, namely 2—into its parts. The cube of AC will be equal to the cube of AB, the cube of BC, six squares of AB, and 12 positions of AB. Furthermore, 33 AC are 33 AB and 33 BC, which makes 66 (since BC is 2). Therefore, the cube of AC and 33 AC are equal to the cube of AB, the cube of BC, six squares of AB, 45 positions of AB, and 66. These same things, therefore, are equal to 6 squares of AC and 100. But the 6 squares of AC, with AC divided at point B (by the 4th [proposition] of the 2nd [book] of the <term>Elements</term><note>A reference to Euclid's Elements, which Cardano uses to provide geometric proofs for algebraic identities.</note>), are equal to 6 squares of AB, 6 squares of BC, and 12 surfa<margin>ces</margin><meta>The catchword 'ciebus' is the end of 'superficiebus', meaning 'surfaces' or 'areas'.</meta>

It is therefore clear that once AB is found from its own chapter<note>Cardano refers to the previous chapters in the *Ars Magna* where he provides the specific formulas for cubics of the form $x^3 + px = q$.</note>, and BC (which is 2) is added to it, AC is produced. 

It is also manifest that where the positions which were with the cube were equal to the products, we would have a cube equal to a number only; and where the positions with the cube were fewer, we would have the "things" on one side and the cube on the other; and then if the number which is with the cube were equal to the other, the positions would be equal to the cube; and if it were less, we would have the "things" and the number equal to the cube; and if greater, we would have the "things" equal to the cube and the number, by the same demonstration as in the preceding chapter.

### THE RULE.

The rule therefore is, that first you should always establish the number of "things" as in the preceding chapter, which is done by multiplying the number of squares by its <term>third part</term><note>Latin: τρ q̃d (tertia pars quadratorum). This is the coefficient of the $x^2$ term divided by 3, used to shift the variable and eliminate the square.</note>. The difference between this product and the [original] number of things is the [new] number of things. If this difference is nothing, we will have a cube equal to a number. If, however, the product is less than the number of things, the difference will be the number of things which, together with the cube, are equated to a number. If the product is greater, we will have things equal to the cube; and then if the numbers are equal, the cube will be equal to the things. 

And if that which is produced from the number of things multiplied by the <term>third part of the squares</term> is less than the number of the equation with the addition, the cube will be equal to the things and the number. But if the number produced from the number of things by the <term>third part of the squares</term> is greater than the number of the equation with its addition, we will have things equal to the cube and the number. 

The number of the equation is obtained as follows: multiply the first number of things by the third part of the number of squares, and take the difference of the product with the sum of the number of the equation and twice the cube of the <term>third part of the squares</term>. This difference will be the number to be added to the cube or the things, as the case requires, or the number equal to the cube where there are no things. Then, having obtained the value (the <term>estimate</term><note>Latin: æstimationem. The "estimate" refers to the solution or root of the equation.</note>), add or subtract the <term>third part of the squares</term> from it, as you will be taught in the examples, and you will have the sought-after value.

**First Example.** A cube and 12 things are equal to 6 squares and

**Second Example.** A fleeing merchant agrees to return 3/4 of a debt proportionally over three years, in such a way that if he had agreed to return 19/27, in the first year he would have returned 9/27, in the second 6/27, and in the third 4/27, so that the remainders are in the same proportion with the remaining capital. The portion for each year is sought, returning only 3/4. Let us set the capital at 4 to avoid fractions; he therefore wishes to return 3. Let it be assumed then that he restores in the first year a **thing** ($x$); therefore in the second year he will restore the **thing** minus 1/4 of a **square** ($x - \frac{1}{4}x^2$); and in the third year, the **thing** minus 1/2 of a **square** plus 1/16 of a **cube** ($x - \frac{1}{2}x^2 + \frac{1}{16}x^3$). Therefore, in three years he will restore 3 **things** plus 1/16 of a **cube** minus 3/4 of a **square**, and this is already supposed to be 3. Wherefore, reduced to integers by multiplying the cube by 16, you will have 1 **cube** plus 48 **things**, equal to 12 **squares** plus 48. Multiply 12 by 4, its third part, which makes 48; therefore the difference of the **things** is zero, and the cube will be equal to a number. Therefore, multiply 48, the number of **things**, by 4, the <term>root of the square part</term>; it makes 192. From this, subtract 176 (the sum of twice the cube of 4 and 48, the number of the equation); 16 remains, and this is equal to the cube. Therefore, the <term>estimate</term> of the **thing** is the cube root of 16. Subtract this from 4, the <term>root of the square part</term>; the sought <term>estimate</term> will be 4 minus the cube root of 16. He will therefore return in the first year 4 minus the cube root of 16; in the second, the cube root of 16 minus the cube root of 4; and in the third, the cube root of 4 minus 1. And the remainders of these are proportional with 4, and joined together they make 3. This is the converse of the first example, and the remainders themselves are the cube root of 16, the cube root of 4, and 1.

**Third Example.** A **cube** and 15 **things** are equal to 6 **squares** and 24. Multiply 6 by its third part, it makes 12, the difference of which from 15 (the number of **things**) is 3. And because the product was less, the **cube** and 3 **things** will be equal to a number. Therefore, multiply 15, the number of **things**, by 2 (the <term>root of the square part</term>); it makes 30. Subtract this from 40 (the sum of 24 and twice the cube of the <term>root of the square part</term>); 10 remains. Therefore, 10 is equal to the **cube** plus 3 **things**. The <term>estimate</term> of the **thing** is the <term>universal cube root</term><note>Latin: ℞ v: cubica (radix universalis). This indicates the root applies to the entire following expression.</note> of the square root of 26 plus 5, minus the universal cube root of the square root of 26 minus 5. To this, add 2 (the <term>root of the square part</term>), and you will have the sought <term>estimate</term>.

**Fourth Example.** A **cube** and 15 **things** are equal to 6 **squares** plus 10. Again, I will have a **cube** and 3 **things** equal to a number, and the number produced will be 30, as before. But the sum of twice the cube of 2 (the <term>root of the square part</term>) and 10 (the number of the equation) is 26. The difference, therefore, is 4. Since the **cube** and 3 **things** are equal to 4, the <term>estimate</term> of the **thing** is 1. And because the produced number [30] is greater than the sum—that is, 30 is greater than 26—we will subtract the <term>estimate</term> of 1...

### On Arithmetic, Book 10.

...of the equation found from 2, the <term>third part of the squares</term><note>Latin: tp̄qd, an abbreviation for tertia pars quadratorum. This is the value used for substitution—one third of the coefficient of the $x^2$ term.</note> and there will remain 1, the sought <term>estimate</term><note>Latin: æstimatio, meaning the root or solution for the variable $x$.</note> of the cube and 15 <term>things</term><note>Latin: rerum. In this era, the unknown variable $x$ was called the "thing."</note>, equal to 6 squares and 10.

Therefore it is clear that in this case, where the cube and the things are equal to a number, if there were no difference between the numbers—for instance, if we had put 14 instead of 10—the estimate of the thing would be the <term>third part of the squares</term>, namely 2, because in the resulting equation, we would have had nothing to add or subtract, since the cube and 3 things would have equaled nothing.

**Fifth Example.** The cube and 10 things equal 6 squares plus 4. Multiply the number of squares [6] by its third part [2], as before; it makes 12. The difference between this and the number of things [10] is 2; and because the product [12] is greater than the number of things, therefore 2 things will be equal to the cube. For the number, therefore, multiply 10 (the first number of things) by 2 (the <term>third part of the squares</term>); it makes 20. The difference between this and 20 (the sum of twice the cube of the <term>third part of the squares</term><note>Twice the cube of 2 is $2 \times 8 = 16$. Adding the constant 4 gives 20.</note> and 4) is nothing. Therefore we will have no number, but the cube will be equal, as was said, to 2 things. Therefore, by depressing the degree, the square will be equal to 2; thus the estimate of the thing is the square root of 2. Add or subtract this from the <term>third part of the squares</term> [2]; you will have the true sought estimates: 2 plus the square root of 2, or 2 minus the square root of 2. And it can also be 2, and so this case has three estimates.

**Sixth Example.** Let the cube and 21 things be equal to 9 squares plus 5. Then, as before, I multiply 9 by 3 (its third part); it makes 27. The difference between this and 21 is 6, which is the number of things to be equated to the cube. Because the product 27 is greater than the number of things 21, I therefore add 54 (twice the cube of the <term>third part of the squares</term><note>$2 \times 3^3 = 54$.</note>) to 5 (the number of the equation); it makes 59. The difference between this and 63 (the product of the prior number of things [21] by the <term>third part of the squares</term> [3]) is 4. Therefore, because the product [63] is greater than the sum [59], we will add the number to the cube, and it becomes: 1 cube plus 4 is equal to the 6 things already found. Now, there are three estimates for this: the first is 2, the second is the square root of 3 minus 1, and the third is a <term>false</term><note>Latin: ficta. Cardano used this term for negative roots, which were considered "fictitious" or "false" in 16th-century mathematics.</note> one, minus the square root of 3 minus 1. Add these to 3 (the <term>third part of the squares</term>); you will have those true estimates that you see on the side.

**Seventh Example.** The cube and 26 things are equal to 12 squares plus 12. Multiply 12 (the number of squares) by its third part, which is 4; it makes 48. The difference between this and 26 (the number of things) is 22; and because the product is greater than the number of things, things will equal the cube. Then multiply 26 (the number of things) by 4 (the third part of the number of squares); it makes 104. Subtract this from 140 (the sum of 12 and 128, which is twice the cube of the <term>third part of the squares</term>); the result is 36, which is the number to be added to the things, because the sum [140] is greater than the product [104]—the opposite of the preceding example. Therefore the cube will be equal to 22 things plus 36. Wherefore there will be three estimates for it: the first is the square root of 19 plus 1, and it is true; the second is a <term>false</term> one, minus the square root of 19 plus 1; the third is also <term>false</term>, which is minus 2. # ON ARITHMETIC, BOOK 10.

<margin>b</margin> $bc$, which is 8, is 2 <term>minus</term><note>Latin: m:, short for "minus".</note> than the cube of $ab$ with 100 things; I say 2 <term>minus</term> because the cube of $bc$, which is joined to 6 squares, ought to be 10, but is only 8. Yet the cube of $ab$ with 100 things exceeds the cube of $ab$ and **triple** $ab$ into the square of $bc$ (which is 12 things) by 88 things. Therefore, the difference of the cube of $bc$ and triple $bc$ into the square of $ab$, from the cube of $ab$ with triple $ab$ into the square of $bc$, is 88 $ab$ <term>minus</term> 2. Let $bg$ be set as the thing; then $gc$ (or $ab$) will be 2 <term>minus</term> the thing. The quantity of this, taken 88 times as was said, is equal to the cube of $bg$ <term>plus</term><note>Latin: p:, short for "plus".</note> 2. ## ANOTHER DEMONSTRATION.

Suppose again, a cube with 5 things is equal to 6 squares and 10. Let $ef$ be the thing, $de$ the <term>third part of the squares</term>, and $eh$ the difference of $de$ and $ef$. It will follow from a similar demonstration to the previous one that the cube of $eh$ is equal to 7 <term>things</term><note>Latin: reb⁹, an abbreviation for rebus.</note>

-> <gloss>thing</gloss> <-
-> D — E — H — F <-

<term>plus</term> 16. Once the estimate is found there, if the <term>third part of the squares</term> (which is 2) is added to $hf$, the sought thing $ef$ will be obtained. I will not add more words to this, because the demonstration is similar to the previous one, and its operation in this part is clearer in our demonstration.

## THE RULE.

The rule, therefore, taken from this demonstration, is: if the number of things is equal to the product of the number of squares and its third part, calculate the cube of the <term>third part of the squares</term>. Find the difference between this cube and the constant number of the equation. Take the <term>cube root</term><note>Latin: ℞ cubica.</note> of this difference. Add this to the <term>third part of the squares</term> when the cube is less than the constant number, or subtract it when it is greater; the result is the <term>estimate</term> of the thing. It is manifest, however, that where the cube of the <term>third part of the squares</term> and the constant number are equal, we shall neither add nor subtract, but the <term>third part of the squares</term> itself will be the estimate of the thing.

**Example.** A cube and 12 things are equal to 6 squares <term>plus</term> 8. Then, because 6 (the number of squares) multiplied by 2 (its third part) makes 12 (the number of things) exactly, you therefore calculate the cube of 2 (the <term>third part of the squares</term>), which is 8. Its difference from the constant number of the equation is zero; therefore the estimate of the thing is 2 (the <term>third part of the squares</term>).

And if the cube and 12 things are equal to 6 squares <term>plus</term> 9, then because the cube of the third part is less than the number, we take 8 (the cube of the <term>third part of the squares</term>) from 9, leaving 1. Its <term>cube root</term> is 1. I add this to the <term>third part of the squares</term> because the cube of the <term>third part of the squares</term> is less than the constant of the equation; the estimate of the thing becomes 3. 

By the same reasoning, if the cube <term>plus</term> 12 things were equal to 6 squares <term>plus</term> 7, having subtracted 7 from 8 (the cube of the <term>third part of the squares</term>)...

<summary>Cardano demonstrates a geometric proof for solving cubic equations and provides a specific rule for cases where the linear coefficient equals the product of the quadratic coefficient and its third part. He illustrates this with numerical examples, showing how to adjust the solution based on the difference between the constant term and the cube of one-third the quadratic coefficient.</summary>

# GIROLAMO CARDANO
<meta>Catchword: grega-</meta>

[There] remains 1, whose <term>cube root</term><note>original: "Rz cubicam"</note> is 1; subtract this from 2, the <term>third part of the squares</term><note>Latin: tpqd, abbreviation for "tertia pars quadratorum." This represents 1/3 of the coefficient of the $x^2$ term, used here to shift the variable and simplify the equation.</note>. There remains 1, which is the <term>estimate of the thing</term><note>The value of the unknown variable, or the "solution."</note>.

But if the number of positions <note>the coefficient of the linear term $x$</note> is greater than the product of the number of squares <note>the coefficient of $x^2$</note> and its third part, the difference will be the number of things <note>the coefficient of the new linear term</note>, as in the first demonstration and its rules. Multiply this by the third part of the squares [tpqd] and add to it the cube of the third part of the squares. The difference between this sum and the number of the equation <note>the constant term</note> is the number of the equation for the cube. If there is no difference between them, the estimate of the thing is the third part of the squares [tpqd]. And if the number of the equation is less than the sum, subtract the found estimate [from the tpqd]; if it is greater, add the third part of the squares [tpqd], and that which results will be the estimate of the thing. 

**Example:** A cube and 20 things are equal to 6 squares and 24 <note>In modern notation: $x^3 + 20x = 6x^2 + 24$</note>. Multiply 6 by 2 (its third part), which makes 12. The difference between 12 and 20 (the number of things) is 8; this is the number of things which, with the cube, are equal to a number. Now multiply 8 (the number of things) by 2 (the third part of the squares), which makes 16; add to this 8 (the cube of the third part of the squares), which makes 24. There is no difference between this and 24 (the number of the equation); therefore, the estimate of the thing is the third part of the squares [tpqd], namely 2. 

Suppose again a cube with 20 things is equal to 6 squares and 15 <note>$x^3 + 20x = 6x^2 + 15$</note>. We will have, as before, a cube and 8 things for the number. Multiply as before 8 (the later number of things) by 2 (the third part of the squares), making 16; add the cube of the third part of the squares [8], making 24. Subtract 15; there remains 9. Therefore, the cube and 8 things are equal to 9 <note>$y^3 + 8y = 9$</note>, and the estimate of the thing is 1. Subtract this from 2 (the third part of the squares); there remains 1, the true estimate of the thing. You subtracted because 15 (the number of the equation) is less than the sum of the cube and the product, which is 24. If you notice well, it is done the same way in the first part of the rule, when the number of things is equal to the product of the number of squares and its third part. 

Again, let a cube with 20 things be equal to 6 squares <term>plus</term><note>original: "p:", short for "plus".</note> 33. You will have, as before, a cube and 8 things equal to the difference of the sum 24 and the number of the equation 33. Therefore, the cube and 8 things will equal 9, and the estimate of the thing will be 1. This must be added to the third part of the squares [tpqd] because the number of the equation (33) is greater than the sum (24). Thus, the estimate of the thing will be 3.

But if the number of positions is less than the product of the number of squares and its third part, the difference will nonetheless be the number of things as before, but these will not be joined to the cube; rather, they will be equal to it. Then multiply this later number of things by the third part of the squares [tpqd], and join the product to the number of the equation. The difference between this sum and the cube of the third part of the squares is the number for the second equation. If there is no difference, the cube will be equal to the things, and the square root of the number of things added to the third part of the squares [tpqd] is the estimate of the thing. But if the sum is greater than the cube, the difference will be the number which, together with the things, is equal to the cube. Having found the estimate from that, add it to the third part of the squares [tpqd], and that will be the true estimate. But if the cube was greater than the sum—

<summary>Cardano provides algorithmic rules for solving cubic equations by transforming them into "depressed cubics" using a variable shift based on the "third part of the squares" (tpqd). He illustrates these rules with three numerical examples where the linear term coefficient is greater than the product of the square coefficient and its third part.</summary>

# On Arithmetic, Book X.
L
<margin>Regula</margin>

...added together, the difference will be the number which, when the <term>cube</term><note>original: "cubo"</note> is added to it, is equal to the <term>things</term><note>original: "rebus"; the linear unknown, or $x$.</note>. Once the <term>solution</term><note>original: "æstimatione"; literally the "estimate" or value of the unknown.</note> is obtained from this, add the <term>third part of the squares</term><note>Latin: tpqd, abbreviation for "tertia pars quadratorum." This is 1/3 of the coefficient of the $x^2$ term.</note> to it. The resulting sum is the true solution of the thing, and it must be taken as many times as we taught in our rule—though as far as the rule is concerned, this is also our own. 

**Example:** Let the cube and 9 things be equal to 6 squares plus 2 <note>In modern notation: $x^3 + 9x = 6x^2 + 2$</note>. Then the second number of things will be: multiply 3 <note>the number of squares</note> by 2 <note>the third part of the squares</note>, which makes 6. Add this to 2 (the number of the equation), which makes 8. Now, the cube of the third part of the squares [2] is 8. There is no difference [between 8 and 8]; therefore, the cube is equal to 3 things <note>The new equation is $y^3 = 3y$</note>. Thus, the solution is the <term>root</term><note>original: "Rz", abbreviation for "radix."</note> of 3, and the true solution of the thing is 2 plus the root of 3 <note>$x = 2 + \sqrt{3}$</note>. 

**Again:** Let the cube plus 9 things be equal to 6 squares plus 4. We will have, as before, the cube equal to 3 things. For the number [constant]: multiply 3 (the second number of things) by 2 (the third part of the squares), which makes 6. Add 4 (the number of the equation), which makes 10. Subtract 8 (the cube of the third part of the squares), which leaves 2 to be added to the things, because the sum was greater than the cube of the third part of the squares. Therefore, the cube is equal to 3 things plus 2 <note>$y^3 = 3y + 2$</note>. The solution will be 2; adding the 2 (the third part of the squares) makes 4, the true solution. 

**Again:** Let the cube plus 21 things be equal to 9 squares plus 5. There will be 6 things in the second equation because 9 (the number of squares) multiplied by 3 (its third part) produces 27. <margin>21 is 6 less than 27</margin> Multiply 6 (the second number of things) by 3 (the third part of the squares), which makes 18. Add 5 to it, making 23. The difference between this and the number produced from the cube of the third part of the squares [27] is 4. And because the sum [23] is less than the cube [27], the cube and 4 will be equal to 6 things <note>$y^3 + 4 = 6y$</note>. The solution is therefore 2, or the root of 3 minus 1, or the "fictitious" solution <note>A negative solution or a complex root treated as a mathematical placeholder.</note> which is the root of 3 plus 1. If you add the 3 (the third part of the squares) to these, you will have the sought-after solutions: 5, and 4 plus the root of 3, and 2 plus the root of 3. In any of these, it is true that the cube and 21 things are equal to 9 squares and the number 5.

### On the cube and squares equal to things and a number.
## Chapter XIX.
### DEMONSTRATIO.

**L**et there be a cube AB and 6 squares, equal to 20 things plus 200, for the sake of example <note>$x^3 + 6x^2 = 20x + 200$</note>. We shall set BC as 2, the third part of the squares. Therefore, AC will be the thing plus 2 <note>$y = x + 2$</note>, and its cube will be the cube and 6 squares and 12 things and 8. Now, it was already supposed that the cube AB and 6 squares are equal to 20 things plus 200. Therefore, let 20 things and 200 be placed in the position of the cube and 6 squares. The cube AC will then become equal to 32 things plus 208. But because 32 things of AB are less than 32 things of AC by 32 of BC, let 32 of BC be added to both sides. Therefore, 32 things plus 208 will be equal to the cube plus 64 (for 32 of BC [where BC is 2] is 64). Subtract 64 from both sides; the cube will be equal to 32 things plus 144. Once the solution is found from this, subtract BC (the third part of the squares), and AB will remain.

### RULE.

<summary>This page concludes Cardano's examples for solving cubic equations by variable substitution (shifting the root by the "third part of the squares") and begins Chapter 19, demonstrating how to solve equations of the form x³ + ax² = bx + c.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the specific mathematical rules for transforming cubic equations involving squares, things, and a constant. # GIROLAMO CARDANO

The rule therefore is: multiply the number of <term>squares</term><note>original: "quadratorum"; the $x^2$ term.</note> by its third part; add the product to the number of <term>things</term><note>original: "rerum"; the linear unknown or $x$.</note>, and the sum will be the [new] number of things. Then multiply this number by the <term>root of the part of the squares</term><note>Latin: "rpq̄d" (radix partis quadratorum). In this context, it refers to the third part of the coefficient of the squares, which Cardano uses to transform the equation.</note> and take the difference of the product from the sum of the equation's number and the <term>cube</term><note>original: "cubo"; the $x^3$ term.</note> of the root of the part of the squares. If this difference is zero, you will have a cube equal to things. But if the product is less than the sum, the difference is the number which, together with the things, is equal to the cube. If the product is greater than the sum, the difference is the number which, together with the cube, is equal to the things. Then, having obtained the <term>solution</term><note>original: "æstimatione"; the value of the unknown in the transformed equation.</note>, subtract the root of the part of the squares; the remainder is the true, sought-after solution.

**Example:** Let the cube and 6 squares be equal to 20 things and 56 <note>In modern notation: $x^3 + 6x^2 = 20x + 56$</note>. Multiply 6 by 2 (its third part), which makes 12. Add this to 20, which makes 32. Multiply 32 by 2 (the root of the part of the squares), which makes 64. Add 56 (the number of the equation) to 8 (the cube of the root of the part of the squares), which makes 64. The difference between the product and the sum is zero; therefore, the things will be equal to the cube <note>$y^3 = 32y$</note>. Thus, by reducing the power, the square is equal to 32, and the thing is the <term>root</term><note>original: "Rz", abbreviation for "radix".</note> of 32. The true solution is the root of 32 minus 2 <note>$x = \sqrt{32} - 2$</note>. 

**Again:** Let the cube and 6 squares be equal to 20 things plus 112. Multiply 6 by 2, as before, making 12. Add to 20, making 32, the [new] number of things. Multiply by 2 (the root of the part of the squares), making 64. Subtract this from 120 (the sum of the cube of the root of the part of the squares and the number of the equation); the remainder is 56, the number which, with 32 things, is equal to the cube <note>$y^3 = 32y + 56$</note>. The thing [y] is therefore the root of 29 plus 1. Subtract the root of the part of the squares [2]; there remains the solution of the thing: the root of 29 minus 1 <note>$x = \sqrt{29} - 1$</note>. 

**Again:** Let the cube and 6 squares be equal to 20 things plus 41. You will have, as before in the second equation, 32 things and the number 15; for by subtracting 49 (the sum of the equation's number and 8, the cube of the root of the part of the squares) from 64 (the product of 32 and the root of the part of the squares), 15 remains. Because the product was greater than the sum, 15 plus the cube will be equal to 32 things <note>$y^3 + 15 = 32y$</note>. The thing [y] will be 5, or the root of 13 1/4 minus 2 1/2, or the <term>fictitious</term><note>original: "ficta"; Cardano uses this term for negative roots.</note> root of 13 1/4 plus 2 1/2. Subtract 2 (the root of the part of the squares), and you will have the true solution 3, and two fictitious solutions by subtraction: namely, 4 1/2 plus the root of 13 1/4, and 4 1/2 minus the root of 13 1/4, just as we said in the first chapter.

## On the cube equal to squares, things, and a number. Chapter XX.

### D E M O N S T R A T I O N.

Let again the cube AC be equal to 6 squares, 5 things, and 88 (for the sake of example). Let BC be the root of the part of the squares, namely 2. It is therefore manifest that the cube AC is equal to 6 squares AB, 12 AB, and the cubes AB and BC. These same things, then, are equal to 6 squares AC, 5 things AC, and 88. Let the common cube BC (namely 8) now be cast out; there will remain the cube AB, 6 squares AB, and 12 AB, equal to 6 squares AC, 5 things AC, plus 80. But 6 squares AC exceed 6 squares AB by 6 <term>gnomons</term><note>A geometric figure remaining after a smaller similar figure is removed from a larger one; here used to describe the difference between two squares.</note> of the square AB, and there will be 24 things from AC...

minus 6 squares of <term>B C</term>, which are 24; therefore 6 squares of <term>A B</term> and 29 <term>things</term><note>original: "res"; the unknown variable $x$.</note> of <term>A C</term>, and 56, are equal to the cube of <term>B</term>, and 6 squares of <term>A B</term>, and 12 things of <term>A B</term>. Let the 6 common <term>squares</term><note>original: "q̃drata"; the $x^2$ term.</note> of <term>A B</term> be removed, and there will remain 29 things of <term>A C</term> plus 56, equal to the cube of <term>A B</term> and 12 things of <term>A B</term>. And 29 things of <term>A C</term> exceed 29 things of <term>A B</term> by 29 <term>B C</term>, which is 58, because <term>B C</term> is 2. Therefore, let the number be added to the number; there will be 29 <term>A B</term> and 114, equal to the cube of <term>A B</term> and 12 things of <term>A B</term>. Again, remove the 12 common things; there will be 17 things plus 114, equal to the cube. From there, having obtained the <term>solution</term><note>original: "æstimatione"; the value of the unknown.</note>, add <term>B C</term> to it.

->RULE.<-

The rule therefore is: Multiply the number of squares by its third part, and add the product to the number of things; the sum will be the [new] number of things equal to the cube. For the constant number, however, multiply the second number of things by the <term>root of the part of the squares</term><note>original: "τρq̃d.", shorthand for "radix partis quadratorum." This refers to $1/3$ of the coefficient of the $x^2$ term.</note> and add the product to the number of the equation; from this, subtract the cube of the <term>root of the part of the squares</term>. The remainder is the number which, together with the things, is equal to the cube. Then, having found the solution, add the <term>root of the part of the squares</term> to it, and you will have the true solution.

->QUESTION.<-

Example: in this question, a certain person gave 1,728 gold pieces <note>original: "aureos"</note> at the start of the year (under "interest upon interest" <note>original: "usura rediviva"; compound interest</note> as they say), on the condition that he would receive in the third year, from capital and interest, an amount equal to half the capital, and half of what would have been owed at the end of the first year, and half of what would have been owed at the end of the second year, had the money been retained and he wished to pay under the same interest. Suppose, therefore, that at the end of the first year he had 144 things; at the end of the second year he will have 12 squares; at the end of the third year he will have a cube. And this will be equal to the halves of the other years added together. Therefore, the cube will be equal to 6 squares, 72 things, and 729. Multiply 6 (the number of squares) by 2 (its third part), which makes 12. Add this to 72, which makes 84, the number of things. Multiply 84 by 2 (the <term>root of the part of the squares</term>), making 168. Add this to 729, making 897. Subtract 8 (the cube of the <term>root of the part of the squares</term>), making 889. Therefore, the cube is equal to 84 things plus 889. The solution of this will be the **universal cubic root of 444 1/2 plus the root of 175,628 1/4, plus the universal cubic root of 444 1/2 minus the root of 175,628 1/4**. To this, add 2 (the <term>root of the part of the squares</term>), and you have the sought-after solution: **the universal cubic root of 444 1/2 plus the root of 175,628 1/4, plus the universal cubic root of 444 1/2 minus the root of 175,628 1/4, plus 2**, the cube of which is the quantity of money owed to him in the third year. Then, by subtracting 1,728, you will have the principal, through proportional terms.

# GIROLAMO CARDANO'S
## DEMONSTRATION.

Let the cube and 100 be equal to 6 squares and 24 things, and let that cube be $AC$, and let $BC$ be the <term>tpqd</term><note>An abbreviation for "triplum partis quadrati" or "tertia pars quadrati," referring to the root of one-third of the coefficient of the squares. Here, since the squares are 6, the third part is 2.</note>. Since the cube $AC$ is equal to the cube $AB$ and 6 squares of $AB$, and 12 things of $AB$, and the cube of $BC$ (which is 8), then the cube $AB$, plus 6 squares of $AB$, plus 12 things of $AB$, and 108 <note>This 108 comes from the original constant 100 plus the cube of 2, which is 8.</note> will be equal to 6 squares of $AC$ and 24 things of $AC$. However, 6 squares of $AB$ are smaller than 6 squares of $AC$ by 6 <term>gnomons</term><note>A gnomon is the L-shaped figure remaining when a smaller square is removed from a larger one; Cardano uses this to represent the difference between $(y+2)^2$ and $y^2$.</note> $ADE$, and 24 things of $AB$ are smaller than 24 things of $AC$ by 24 $BC$. Therefore, the cube $AB$, and 6 squares of $AB$, and 12 things of $AB$, and 108, are equal to 6 squares of $AB$, and 6 gnomons $ADE$, and 24 things of $AB$, and 48 (for 24 times $BC$ is 48). Therefore, by casting out from both sides 6 squares of $AB$, and 12 things of $AB$, and 48, there will remain the cube $AB$ plus 60, equal to 6 gnomons $ADE$ and 12 things of $AB$. Furthermore, the 6 gnomons $ADE$ are 24 things of $AB$ <term>plus</term><note>original: "p:"; shorthand for "plus".</note> 24, because each of the surfaces $AD$ and $DE$ is 2 things (since $BD$ is 2), and the square $BC$ is 4. Therefore, 36 things of $AB$ plus 24 are equal to the cube $AB$ <term>plus</term><note>original: "p:"; shorthand for "plus".</note> 60. Subtract 24 from both sides, and the cube $AB$ plus 36 will be equal to 36 things of $AB$. Then, once $AB$ is known, we shall add it to $BC$ (which is the <term>tpqd</term>), and the <term>solution</term><note>original: "æstimatio"; the numerical value of the unknown variable.</note> will be produced.

### RULE.

The rule, therefore, is this: Multiply the number of squares by its third part, add the product to the number of things, and the sum will be the [new] number of things. Multiply this by the <term>tpqd</term>, and take the difference between this product and the sum of the number of the equation and the cube of the <term>tpqd</term>. If this difference is zero, the things will be equal to the cube. If, however, the product is greater than the sum, the difference is the number which, with the things, is equal to the cube. And if the sum is greater than the product, the difference is the number which, with the cube, is equal to things. Then, having obtained the <term>solution</term><note>original: "æstimatiōe".</note>, you add it to the <term>tpqd</term>, and the true solution will be produced. Remember, however, that when this section results in a case of the cube equal to things and a number, its true solution must be added, and the smaller of the <term>fictitious</term><note>original: "fictæ"; Cardano's term for negative roots.</note> solutions must be subtracted by <term>minus</term><note>original: "m:"; shorthand for "minus".</note> the <term>tpqd</term>, so that you have both solutions for the case of the cube and a number equal to things and squares, since the case of the cube equal to things and a number has only one true solution.

Example: Let the cube and 64 be equal to 6 squares and 24 things. Multiply 6, the number of <insert>squares</insert><note>The original text says "rerum" (things), but as the OCR note suggests, the mathematical context requires "quadratorum" (squares).</note>, by 2 (its third part); it makes 12. Add this to 24; it makes 36, the [new] number of things. Multiply this by 2 (the <term>tpqd</term>); it makes 72. Then cube 2; it makes 8. Add this to 64 (the number of the equation); it also makes 72. Therefore, because the difference of these numbers is zero, we will have the cube equal to 36 things. From which, the square will be equal to 36, and therefore the thing is 6, according to the simple rule. Add this to 2 (the <term>tpqd</term>); it makes 8, the solution for the thing. Again, let the cube and 128 be equal to 6...

He provides a specific rule for the substitution $x = y + (a/3)$ and illustrates it with a worked example where the constants cancel out.</summary>

with squares and 24 things: multiply 6 by 2, as before, which makes 12; add this to 24, making 36, which is the number of things. Multiply 36 by the <term>tpqd</term><note>Abbreviation for "triplum partis quadrati" or "tertia pars quadrati," here referring to the value 2, which is one-third of the coefficient of the squares (6).</note>, which makes 72. The difference between this and 136 (the sum of 128, the number from the equation, and 8, the cube of the <term>tpqd</term>) is 64. This is the number to be added to the cube because the sum 136 is greater than the product 72. Therefore, the cube and 64 will be equal to 36 things. The solutions, moreover, are 2 and the square root of 33 minus 1. Add these to the <term>tpqd</term> (which is 2), and the true solutions become 4 or the square root of 33 plus 1. 

Again, let the cube and 9 be equal to 6 squares and 24 things. Multiply, as before, 6 by 2 (its third part), making 12; add this to 24 (the number of things), making 36, which is the new number of things as before. Then multiply 36 by 2 (the <term>tpqd</term>), making 72. The difference between this and 17 (the sum of 8, the cube of the <term>tpqd</term>, and 9, the number from the equation) is 55. Because the product [72] is greater than the sum [17], we add 55 to the things. We will then have a cube equal to 36 things plus 55. The true solution for this is the square root of $17 \frac{1}{4}$ plus $2 \frac{1}{2}$. The larger false <note>Cardano uses "false" to refer to negative roots, which were not yet fully accepted as standard numbers in the 16th century.</note> solution is minus 5, and the smaller false solution is the negative universal square root of $27 \frac{1}{4}$ minus $2 \frac{1}{2}$—or to understand more clearly: $2 \frac{1}{2}$ minus the square root of $17 \frac{1}{4}$. Therefore, add this solution, and similarly the true one, to the <term>tpqd</term> (which is 2), and you will have the sought-after solutions: one being $4 \frac{1}{2}$ plus the square root of $17 \frac{1}{4}$, and the other $4 \frac{1}{2}$ minus the square root of $17 \frac{1}{4}$.
