## On the cube, things, and number, equal to the squares.
## Chapter XXII.

### DEMONSTRATION.

<margin>S</margin> Let there be again a cube $AC$, with 4 things and the number 16, equal to 6 squares. Let $BC$ be the <term>tpqd</term> [2] as before. We will therefore resolve the cube $AC$, which is equal to the cube $AB$, 6 squares of $AB$, 12 things of $AB$, and the cube of $BC$ (which is 8). This whole sum, together with 4 things of $AC$ and 16, will be equal to 6 squares of $AC$. Since 4 things of $AC$ are 4 things of $AB$ <term>plus</term><note>original: "p:"; shorthand for plus.</note> 4 of $BC$ (and thus plus 8), the total will be the cube $AB$ plus 6 squares of $AB$ plus 16 things of $AB$ plus 32, equal to 6 squares of $AC$. 

Now, 6 squares of $AC$ are equal to—as has been demonstrated—6 squares of $AB$ plus 24 things of $AB$ plus 24. Therefore, the cube $AB$, 6 squares of $AB$, 16 things of $AB$, and 32 are equal to 6 squares of $AB$ plus 24 things of $AB$ plus 24. Cast out from both sides the 6 squares of $AB$, 16 things, and 24; there will remain the cube $AB$ plus 8, equal to 8 things. From this, $AB$ becomes known; add $BC$ (the <term>tpqd</term>) to it, and $AC$ becomes known, which is the solution for the "thing" <note>The value of $x$.</note>. 

Again, let the cube and 4 things and 1 equal 6 squares. Therefore, the 6 squares of $AC$ will be, as before, 6 squares of $AB$, 24 things of $AB$, and 24. But the cube $AC$ with 4 things $AC$ plus 1 is equal to the cube $AB$ and 6 squares of $AB$ and 16 things and 17. Therefore, by casting out the common terms—6 squares of $AB$, 16 things of $AB$, and 17—the remainder will be equal to the remainder: namely, the cube equal to 8 things plus 7. From this, $AB$ being known, you have $AC$ as before by adding $BC$ (the <term>tpqd</term>).

It transitions into the formal statement of the "Rule" for equations of the form "cube, things, and number equal to squares."</meta>

# GIROLAMO CARDANO
<meta>catchword: Concerning</meta>

## RULE.

The rule, therefore, is this: Multiply the number of squares by its third part, and from the product subtract the number of things. If this cannot be done, the case is impossible in terms of a true solution. The remainder will then be the new number of things. Next, multiply the first <note>The original coefficient of the "things" (x) before transformation.</note> number of things by the <term>tpqd</term><note>Latin: "triplum partis quadrati" (the triple of the part of the square), which Cardano uses to mean the value of one-third the coefficient of the square term. If the equation has $6x^2$, the tpqd is 2.</note> and add the product to the constant number of the equation. Take the difference between this sum and the double of the cube of the <term>tpqd</term>. If the difference is zero, you have a cube equal to things alone. If, however, the double of the cube of the <term>tpqd</term> is greater, the difference is the number to be added to the things. If the double of the cube is less than the sum, the difference is the number to be added to the cube. Then, to the solution you find, add the <term>tpqd</term> so that you may have the true solution.

Example: A cube and 4 things and 8 are equal to 6 squares <note>In modern notation: $x^3 + 4x + 8 = 6x^2$</note>. Multiply 6 by 2 (its third part); it makes 12. Subtract 4; the [new] number of things becomes 8. Also multiply 4, the previous number of things, by 2 (the <term>tpqd</term>); it makes 8. Add this to 8, the constant of the equation; it makes 16. Between this and the double of the cube of the <term>tpqd</term> (which is also 16) there is no difference. Therefore, the cube is equal to 8 things, and the solution for the thing is the square root of 8. To this, add 2 (the <term>tpqd</term>), and the true solution of the thing will be the square root of 8 plus 2.

Again: A cube plus 4 things plus 16 is equal to 6 squares. I multiply 6 by 2 (the <term>tpqd</term>) as before; it makes 12. Subtract 4 (the number of things); it makes 8, the [new] number of things. I multiply 4 (the previous number of things) by 2 (the <term>tpqd</term>); it makes 8. Add this to 16, the constant of the equation; it makes 24. Subtract 16, the double of the cube of the <term>tpqd</term>; 8 remains. Therefore, we shall add 8 to the cube (because the sum was greater than the double of the cube of the <term>tpqd</term>), and it will become a cube plus 8 equal to 8 things. The solution for the thing is therefore 2, or the square root of 5 minus 1. Thus, by adding 2 (the <term>tpqd</term>), the true solution will be 4, or the square root of 5 plus 1.

Again: A cube and 4 things and 1 are equal to 6 squares. There will be, as before, 8 things. Having multiplied the previous number of things (which is 4) by 2 (the <term>tpqd</term>), it makes 8. Adding 1 (the constant of the equation), it makes 9. The double of the cube of the <term>tpqd</term> is 16. The difference is 7. And because the double of the cube is greater than the sum, there will be 8 things and 7 equal to the cube. For this, the solution is the square root of $7 \frac{1}{4}$ plus $\frac{1}{2}$. Or, in the false solution <note>Cardano refers to negative roots as "falsas æstimationes" or false solutions.</note>, the smaller solution will be 1 minus <note>Original: "1 m:". Cardano's shorthand for $x = -1$.</note>. Add 2 (the <term>tpqd</term>) to either, and you will have two true solutions: namely 1, and the square root of $7 \frac{1}{4}$ plus $\frac{1}{2}$.

You should remember here also what we said in the preceding chapter: when the equation results in a cube equal to things only, because the false solution does not differ in number from the true one, therefore for the second solution—since nothing is added, neither plus nor minus the <term>tpqd</term>—the <term>tpqd</term> itself will be the true solution in both cases. For example, here the solution of a cube and 4 things and 8 equal to 6 squares will be the square root of 8 plus 2, or [simply] 2. And in the preceding chapter, the solution of a cube and 64 equal to 6 squares and 24 things will be 8, as was said, and also 2 (namely, the <term>tpqd</term>). This is because all additions and subtractions must be made using the third part of the number of squares.

<meta>This page continues the technical exposition of Cardano's "Ars Magna," moving from the theoretical setup of cubic equations to a specific demonstration and rule for the case where a cube, squares, and a constant are equal to "things" (the linear unknown). It follows the previous page's discussion on transforming equations by substituting variables to eliminate the quadratic term.</meta>

<page-num>44</page-num>
->ON ARITHMETIC BOOK 10:<-

# ON ARITHMETIC BOOK 10:
## On the cube, squares, and number, equal to things:
### Chapter XXIII.

### DEMONSTRATION.

Suppose there is a cube, 6 squares, and 4, equal to 41 things <note>In modern notation: $x^3 + 6x^2 + 4 = 41x$.</note>, and let the cube be AB, to which I add BC, the <term>tpqd</term> <note>Abbreviation for "triplum partis quadrati" (triple the part of the square). It represents one-third of the coefficient of the square term. Here, $6 / 3 = 2$.</note>. Thus AC will be a cube, equal to cube AB, 6 squares, 12 things, and 8 <note>This represents the expansion of $(x + 2)^3$.</note>. In place of cube AB, 6 squares, and 4, let 41 things be substituted as equal to them; then cube AC will be equal to 53 things AB, and 4, which is the difference between the cube of BC <note>The cube of 2 is 8.</note> and the number 4 from the first equation. Therefore, to complete the 53 things of AC, let 53 [times] BC be added <note>Since $x = AC - 2$, then $53x = 53(AC) - 106$.</note>, and there will be cube AC plus 106, equal to 53 things of AC, plus 4. Subtract 4 from each side, and the cube plus 102 will be equal to 53 of its things <note>$y^3 + 102 = 53y$.</note>. Once the solution <note>Original: *aestimatio*, meaning the root or value of the unknown.</note> for AC is found there, subtract BC, the <term>tpqd</term>; AB will remain known, and it is the "thing" <note>The root $x$ of the original equation.</note> itself.

### RULE.

The rule, therefore, is this: Multiply the number of squares by its third part, add the product to the number of things, and this becomes the second number of things. From this, subtract the square of the <term>tpqd</term>, and multiply the remainder by the <term>tpqd</term>. Add this entire product to the constant number of the equation, and a number will be formed which, together with the cube, will equal the things already assigned. Then, from its solutions, subtract the <term>tpqd</term>; the remainders are the sought-after solutions. One example will therefore suffice.

A cube and 6 squares and 12 are equal to 31 things <note>$x^3 + 6x^2 + 12 = 31x$.</note>. Multiply 6, the number of squares, by 2 (its third part); it makes 12. Add this to 31; it makes 43, the [new] number of things. From this, subtract 4 (the square of the <term>tpqd</term>); 39 remains. Multiply this by 2 (the <term>tpqd</term>); it makes 78. Add this to 12, the constant of the equation; it makes 90. Therefore, the cube plus 90 equals 43 things <note>$y^3 + 90 = 43y$.</note>. The thing [y] is therefore 5, or the square root of 24 1/4 minus 2 1/2. Subtract 2, the <term>tpqd</term>; you will have the true solutions: 3, or the square root of 24 1/4 minus 4 1/2. And in both of these, it is true that the cube and 6 squares and 12 are equal to 31 things. You should remember, therefore, that all solutions for these chapters are obtained by always adding the true and fictitious solutions <note>Original: *fictas aestimationes*. Cardano refers to negative roots as "fictitious" or "false" numbers.</note> of the chapters into which they are resolved to the <term>tpqd</term>; and as long as a number remains—even if that which is added is a pure minus <note>Original: *m: purum*, meaning a negative value.</note>—that remainder is the true solution of the thing. They can also be resolved into other chapters of four denominations <note>Equations involving four terms, such as those including $x^3, x^2, x$, and a constant.</note>, as is clear.

### On the 44 derivative chapters. Chapter XXIV.

### DEMONSTRATION.

# Gerolamo Cardano

<margin>

    C--------D
    |        |
    |        |
    A--------B
    |        | G
    |        |
    E--------F
</insert>
</margin>

**L**et there be then (for the sake of example) a cube of a square <note>original: *cubus quadrati*; this refers to the sixth power, $x^6$</note>, with 6 square-squares <note>original: *qd'qdratis*; the fourth power, $x^4$</note>, equal to 100. And let the cube of the square be the body $ABCD$, having altitude $AB$. It will therefore be a square, because the side of the cube of the body $ABCD$, which is assumed to be the cube of a square, is clearly the surface $ABCD$. This is a square-square <note>$x^4$</note>, because $AB$ is already assumed to be a square. Therefore, six times the surface $ABCD$, together with the body $ABCD$, is equal to 100 by the assumption. Now, let $AB$ be the "thing" <note>The "thing" (*res*) is Cardano’s term for the unknown variable, usually $x$.</note>; then the body $ABCD$ will be a cube, and the surface $ABCD$ will be a square. It was assumed, however, that the body $ABCD$ with six times the surface $ABCD$ was equal to 100; therefore, the cube of $AB$ and 6 squares of $AB$ are equal to 100. Consequently, by its own chapter <note>Referring to the chapter providing the rule for solving equations of the form $x^3 + ax^2 = N$.</note>, $AB$ is known. But in the first inquiry, $AB$ was a square; therefore the solution <note>original: *aestimatio*; the numerical value of the root</note> of the square in the first inquiry—where the cube of a square and 6 square-squares are equal to 100—will be known, since it is the same as the solution for the "thing" in the second question. But in the first question, we want the solution for the "thing" [itself]; the "thing," however, is always the root <note>original: $\text{Rz}$; *Radix*</note> of the square. Therefore, the root of the solution $AB$ found through the second question is the solution for the "thing" in the first question, as was proposed.

By the same reasoning, if we set the cube of a square and 6 cubes equal to 100 <note>$x^6 + 6x^3 = 100$</note>, the body $ABCD$ will be the cube of a square and $AB$ will be the square. If some square surface is set equal to it, say $EFG$, then six times the body formed from $EF$ and $EFG$, together with the body $ABCD$, will be equal to 100. Now let the body $EFG$ be the "thing." Since $EF$ is the root of $AB$, then by assumption the cube of $EF$ will be the root of the cube of $AB$. Therefore, the body $ABCD$ is the square of the body formed from $EF$ and $EFG$. Setting the body $ABCD$ as a square, the cube of $EF$ will be the "thing," and six times it will be "six things." And since six times the cube of $EF$ plus the body $ABCD$ was equal to 100—and the bodies do not change but remain the same—and six times the cube of $EF$ is 6 things, and the body $ABCD$ is a square, therefore the square and 6 things are equal to 100 <note>$y^2 + 6y = 100$, where $y = x^3$</note>. Thus the "thing" is known, namely the cube of $EF$. But since $EF$ is the side of the cube <note>the cube root</note>, $EF$ will be known, which is the cube root of the solution found. And since $EF$ is the "thing" in the first question (because it is the square root of $AB$, and $AB$ is assumed to be a square when the body $ABCD$ is the cube of a square), then by setting the body $ABCD$ as the cube of a square, the "thing" will be $EF$. This is known as the cube root of the solution found through the second question, which is what we wanted.

->From this, the rules for all derivative chapters are made manifest.<-

<meta>This page continues Cardano’s systematic classification of algebraic equations. Having established the logic of "derivatives" (higher-order equations that follow the rules of simpler ones), he now provides a comprehensive list of the 16 primary compound types and calculates the total number of general rules.</meta>

<page-num>45</page-num>
# ON ARITHMETIC, BOOK 10

...of all. For we have shown in general that there are 16 primitive compound chapters <note>Cardano uses "chapters" (*capitula*) to mean specific types or cases of algebraic equations.</note>, and they are these:

1st, a square equal to things and a number. <note>$x^2 = ax + n$</note>
2nd, things equal to a square and a number. <note>$ax = x^2 + n$</note>
3rd, a number equal to a square and things. <note>$n = x^2 + ax$</note>
4th, a cube equal to things and a number. <note>$x^3 = ax + n$</note>
5th, things equal to cubes and a number. <note>$ax = x^3 + n$</note>
6th, a number equal to a cube and things. <note>$n = x^3 + ax$</note>
7th, a cube equal to a square and a number. <note>$x^3 = ax^2 + n$</note>
8th, squares equal to a cube and a number. <note>$ax^2 = x^3 + n$</note>
9th, a number equal to a cube and a square. <note>$n = x^3 + ax^2$</note>
10th, a cube equal to a square, things, and a number. <note>$x^3 = ax^2 + bx + n$</note>
11th, squares equal to a cube, things, and a number. <note>$ax^2 = x^3 + bx + n$</note>
12th, a number equal to a cube, a square, and things. <note>$n = x^3 + ax^2 + bx$</note>
13th, things equal to a cube, a square, and a number. <note>$ax = x^3 + bx^2 + n$</note>
14th, a cube and a number equal to a square and things. <note>$x^3 + n = ax^2 + bx$</note>
15th, a cube and things equal to a square and a number. <note>$x^3 + ax = bx^2 + n$</note>
16th, a cube and a square equal to things and a number. <note>$x^3 + ax^2 = bx + n$</note>

It is evident, however, that among these, the 2nd, 5th, 8th, 11th, 13th, and 14th naturally have two <term>solutions</term> <note>original: *aestimationes*; the numerical values for the unknown variable</note> that are entirely different and depend on different rules. Hence, by doubling these headings, there will be 22 compound primitive chapters. To each of these, moreover, two derivative chapters are owed: one from the nature of the square, the other from the nature of the cube. For although the derivatives are infinite within any given chapter, they are all nevertheless reduced to one of these two modes, speaking of those for which a general rule can be had.

Therefore, it is manifest that they are exactly 44; for although the chapter of things equal to squares and a number has two different solutions, it should not for that reason be called "doubled," because those solutions are obtained together by a single rule. Similarly, although the chapter of the cube and things equal to the square and a number has three true solutions, this is not unique to it, and it matters little to me to speak of the number, provided you know that all primitives have two derivatives of a different kind, and that the primitive compound chapters cannot be reduced to fewer than 18. Therefore, the number being contracted, there will be at least 36 derivatives. For the chapters of things equal to a number and a cube, and of squares equal to a cube and a number, are necessarily doubled; for it is clear how much one solution differs from the other.

When a chapter is presented consisting of three or four <term>denominations</term> <note>Cardano refers to the powers of the unknown (e.g., $x, x^2, x^3$) as denominations.</note>, if a constant number is not present, first reduce all the denominations by the smallest one, so that the smallest becomes a constant number. Then take the lower denomination and see (if the chapter consists of three denominations) whether the smaller is the square or cubic root of the larger, or whether the square root of the smaller is the cubic root of the larger. Then you shall seek the solution in the corresponding chapter among the 16; then, for that solution, take a root of the same kind as the smaller denomination compared to the least—one order to the next—for the sake of ease. I have arranged all the derivatives directly across from their primitives in the second chapter, even those consisting of four denominations, in which, if you pay close attention, the smaller denomination—that is, the lower—

<summary>Cardano systematically lists 16 basic types of algebraic equations involving squares and cubes, then calculates how these expand into 44 distinct rules by accounting for multiple roots and higher-order derivative forms.</summary>

--- Page 96 ---
<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion where Cardano listed the 16 primary types of compound equations. He is now demonstrating how "derivative" equations (those with higher powers that mimic the structure of simpler ones) can be solved using the same rules as their "primitive" counterparts.</meta>

-># OF GIROLAMO CARDANO<-

...the latter after the number, is the square root of one, and the cube root of the other, of the <term>denomination</term> <note>original: *denominationis*; in Renaissance algebra, this refers to the power or degree of the unknown, such as $x^2$ or $x^3$.</note> of that same chapter. For example:

<margin>1st</margin> If someone should say, a **squared-square plus 2 squares are equal to 10** <note>$x^4 + 2x^2 = 10$</note>, you see that its <term>primitive</term> <note>The simpler base equation upon which this higher-order version is modeled.</note> is a square and things equal to a number <note>$x^2 + 2x = 10$</note>. Therefore, seek the <term>estimate</term> <note>original: *aestimationem*; the value of the solution or root.</note> for a square plus 2 things equal to 10, which is the root of 11 minus 1 <note>$\sqrt{11} - 1$</note>. And because the "thing" in the derivative is the square root of the squared-square, say that the estimate is the **universal root of the root of 11 minus 1** <note>$\sqrt{\sqrt{11} - 1}$</note>.

<margin>2nd</margin> **A cubed-square plus 2 cubes are equal to 10** <note>$x^6 + 2x^3 = 10$</note>. Its primitive is also a square plus things equal to a number. Since a square and 2 things are equal to 10, the estimate of the thing is the root of 11 minus 1. Since the thing here is the cube root of the cube (the smaller denomination of the two), the requested estimate will be the **universal cube root of the root of 11 minus 1** <note>$\sqrt[3]{\sqrt{11} - 1}$</note>.

<margin>3rd</margin> **A square of the first relationship and 2 first relationships are equal to 10** <note>$x^{10} + 2x^5 = 10$. Cardano uses "first relationship" (*relatum primum*) for the 5th power.</note>. You see that the relationship is the square root of the squared first relationship. Therefore, say this is a derivative of the square type. If a square and 2 things equal 10, the estimate is the root of 11 minus 1. Therefore, since the thing is the <term>related root</term> <note>A 5th root.</note> of the relationship, you will say the requested estimate is the **universal related root of the root of 11 minus 1** <note>$\sqrt[5]{\sqrt{11} - 1}$</note>.

<margin>4th</margin> **A cube of a square plus 3 squared-squares are equal to 20** <note>$x^6 + 3x^4 = 20$</note>. Then you see its primitive is a cube and squares equal to a number <note>$x^3 + 3x^2 = 20$</note>. Since a cube and 3 squares equal 20, the estimate of the thing is 2. And because the square is the square root of the squared-square, the estimate of the thing will be the **root of 2** <note>$\sqrt{2}$</note>.

<margin>5th</margin> **A cube of a square plus 3 squared-squares plus 10 are equal to 15 squares** <note>$x^6 + 3x^4 + 10 = 15x^2$</note>. You see that its primitive in the table (or from the stated rule) is a cube, squares, and a number equal to things <note>$x^3 + 3x^2 + 10 = 15x$</note>. Therefore, seek the estimate for a cube, 3 squares, and 10 equal to 15 things, which is 2. And because the thing is the square root of the square, you will say the estimate is the **root of 2** <note>$\sqrt{2}$</note>.

<margin>6th</margin> **A cube of a cube and 3 cubed-squares and 10 are equal to 15 cubes** <note>$x^9 + 3x^6 + 10 = 15x^3$</note>. You say as before: the primitive is a cube, squares, and a number equal to things. Therefore, if a cube, 3 squares, and 10 are equal to 15 things, the thing is 2. And because the thing is the cube root of the cube, we say the estimate will be the **cube root of 2**. And because the primitive has two estimates <note>solutions</note>, as is well known, the derivative will have just as many. The cube root of each in this example, and the square root in the preceding one, will satisfy the equation. This is a general rule for all derivatives: they have as many estimates as their primitives.

<margin>7th</margin> Let there also be a **cube of a cube equal to 3 cubed-squares and 16** <note>$x^9 = 3x^6 + 16$</note>. Then, because the cube root of the cube of the square is the cube, and the cube multiplied by the cubed-square results in the cube of the cube, the thing will be in the general derivative chapter. Its primitive will be a cube equal to squares and a number <note>$x^3 = 3x^2 + 16$</note>. If, therefore, a cube is equal to 3 squares plus 16, the estimate of the thing will be 4. Since the square (the smaller denomination in the second equation) is the cube root of the cubed-square, I say that the **cube root of 4** must be taken for the estimate. And so it is for others.

<summary>Cardano demonstrates how to solve higher-order "derivative" equations by reducing them to "primitive" forms (like the quadratic or cubic) and then extracting the appropriate root (square, cube, or fifth) of the result.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of derivative equations, specifically how higher-order powers like the "cube of the cube" (x⁹) or "cube of the square" (x⁶) can be solved by reducing them to the rules for simple cubes and squares. It then transitions into Chapter 25, which focuses on specific "imperfect" rules for solving cubic equations when certain conditions are met between the coefficients.</meta>

On Arithmetic, Book 10. 46

And you will say similarly of the cube-cube <note>original: *cubo cubi*; x⁹</note> and the cube <note>x³</note>, for it can be referred back to the 8th rule of the root and the cube. For just as the "thing" <note>x</note> is the cube root of the cube, so the cube is the cube root of the cube-cube. It can also be referred back to the square <note>x²</note> and the cube of the square <note>x⁶</note>, for from each multiplied by its own root, a comparable <term>denomination</term> <note>A term for the power or degree of the unknown.</note> is produced. For from a square multiplied by the "thing," a cube is made; and from a cube of the square multiplied by the cube, a cube-cube is made; but the first method is easier.

## On imperfect and particular chapters. Chapter XXV.

<margin>R</margin> These rules are called general, and this for two reasons. First, because the method itself is general, even if it is contrary to the nature of an <term>estimate</term> <note>original: *aestimationis*; the value or root of the equation.</note> that it should be universal. For instance, if someone says, "every number produced from a number multiplied by itself is a square," the rule is general; yet it does not follow that through this rule I can know every square number, because it is not possible to know every number produced from another multiplied by itself. A rule is also called general because it exhausts an entire class of estimates, even if the estimate does not exhaust the rule. Nevertheless, these rules are "particular" because we cannot solve every proposed question through them <note>Cardano is distinguishing between a rule that is mathematically "universal" in theory but only applicable in practice when specific numerical relationships exist between the numbers.</note>.

<margin>1st.</margin> When, therefore, a **cube is equal to things and a number**, and you have made two parts from the number of things, from one of which multiplied by the root of the other the number of the equation is produced: then add a fourth part of that part whose root was to be taken to the other part, and the root of this sum, with the addition of half the root of the part whose root you took, is the estimate of the thing.

<insert>
1 cube equals 20 things plus 32
16 — 4
Root of 17 plus 1
</insert>
Example: Let a **cube be equal to 20 things and 32**. Then from 16 multiplied by the root of 4 (which is 2), 32 is produced. Therefore, I add 1 (a fourth part of 4) to 16, which makes 17. The root of this plus 1 (half the root of 4) is the estimate of the thing; wherefore the thing is the **root of 17 plus 1**. <note>In modern terms: if x³ = px + q, and we can split p into r + s such that r√s = q, then x = √(r + s/4) + √s/2. Here, 16√4 = 32, so x = √(16+1) + 1.</note>

<margin>2nd.</margin> When a **cube is equal to things and a number**, and you have found two numbers which produce the number of the equation, one of which is the root of the sum of the other and the number of things, that one which is the root is the estimate of the thing.

<insert>
cube equals 24 plus 32 things
6 — 4 — 32
36
6
</insert>
Example: A **cube is equal to 24 plus 32 things**. There are two numbers producing 24, which are 6 and 4, of which 6 is the root of the sum of 32 (the number of things) and 4 (the other producer); for 6 is the root of 36. Therefore, 6 is the estimate of the thing. <note>If x³ = px + q, and q = ab, where a = √(b + p), then x = a. In this example, 24 = 6 × 4, and 6 = √(4 + 32), so x = 6.</note>

<margin>3rd.</margin> When a **cube is equal to things and a number**, and you have made two parts from the number of things, such that from each multiplied by the root of the other interchangeably the result is half the number of the equation: the roots of those parts, joined together, constitute the estimate of the thing.

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from general rules for solving cubic equations to specific "shortcut" rules or "imperfect chapters." These rules apply to cases where the coefficients of the equation—the "things" (x) and the "number" (constant)—stand in a particular mathematical relationship to one another.</meta>

<page-num>27</page-num>
<header>OF GIROLAMO CARDANO</header>

## GIROLAMO CARDANO

Example: A cube is equal to 10 things plus 24 <note>In modern notation: x³ = 10x + 24</note>. From 10, two parts are made, 9 and 1. From the mutual multiplication of one of these by the <term>root</term> <note>original: *℞* or *radix*; the square root unless specified as cubic.</note> of the other, 9 and 3 are produced. These, when joined together, make 12, which is half of 24. Therefore, the roots of 9 and 1, which are 3 and 1, when joined, constitute 4, which is the <term>estimate</term> <note>original: *aestimatio*; the value of the unknown x.</note> of the thing.

> cube equals 10 things plus 24
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;12
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;9 — 1
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;3 ✕ 1
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;12

<margin>4th rule.</margin> When a cube is equal to "things" and a "number," and from the number of things you have made three <term>proportional parts</term> <note>A geometric progression, such as 4, 6, 9 where 4/6 = 6/9.</note>, and from the multiplication of the middle part by the sum of the roots of the first and third parts, the number of the equation is produced—or from the third multiplied by the root of the first and the first by the root of the third, which is the same thing—then the sum of said roots is the estimate of the thing.

Example: A cube is equal to 19 things plus 30 <note>x³ = 19x + 30</note>. From 19, three proportional parts are made: 9, 6, and 4. From the second part, which is 6, multiplied by 5 (the sum of the roots of the first and third), 30 is produced. Therefore 5, the sum of the roots, is the estimate of the thing.

> cube equals 19 things plus 30
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;4 — 6 — 9
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;2 \ / 3
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;12 —— —— 18 — 30

<margin>5th rule.</margin> When a cube is equal to things and a number, and you have found two numbers whose sum, multiplied by the product of one by the other, produces a third part of the number of the equation, and their squares are equal to the sum of the number of things and the product of one by the other, then the sum of those numbers is the estimate of the thing.

Example: Let a cube be equal to 7 things plus 90 <note>x³ = 7x + 90</note>. 3 and 2 multiplied by each other produce 6; this multiplied by 5 (their sum) produces 30, which is a third part of 90. Furthermore, the difference of 13 (the sum of their squares) from 6 (the product of one by the other) is 7, the number of things. Therefore 5, the sum of those numbers, is the estimate of the thing.

> cube equals 7 things plus 90
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;9  3
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;4  2  6 — 7 — 13
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;13  5 / 30

<margin>6th rule.</margin> When a cube is equal to things and a number, and a cubic number is found whose cube root, multiplied by the number of things, produces the sum of the found cubic number and the number of the equation (or their difference), then the "thing" plus that same cube root will be a common divisor of the cube plus that same cubic number, and of the number of things with the sum of the number of the equation and the cube number. Or, the "thing" minus the same cube root will be a common divisor of the cube minus the found cube number, and of the number of things minus the difference of the number of the equation and the found cube number. From this, you will arrive at the estimate of the thing.

Example: A cube is equal to 16 things plus 21. Then, because by adding...

<margin>7th Rule.</margin>
> When a cube is equal to things and a number, and from the number of things you subtract 3/4 of the square of the thing, and the <term>root</term> of the remainder is added to or subtracted from half of the thing: the sum multiplied by the square of the remainder, and the remainder multiplied by the square of the sum, produce the number of the equation. <note>In modern terms, if x is the root, this rule describes the relationship between the coefficients and the parts of the root.</note>

Example: A cube is equal to 14 things plus 8, and the estimate of the thing is 4. Its <term>square</term> is 16; 3/4 of this is 12. Subtract this from 14 (the number of things) and the remainder is 2. Add and subtract the root of this remainder from 2 (half of the estimate 4), and the results are 2 plus <term>root</term> of 2, and 2 minus <term>root</term> of 2. I say, therefore, that from the mutual multiplication of one by the square of the other, 8 is produced, which is the number of the equation.

->2 minus root of 2 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;/ \ &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 6 minus root of 32<-
---
->8<-
</insert>

<margin>8th Rule.</margin>
> When a cube is equal to things and a number, and you divide half the number of the equation by the estimate of the thing, and you add the result to the number of things, and from that sum you subtract 3/4 of the square of that same thing: the <term>root</term> of the remainder, added to and subtracted from half the estimate, shows the parts from whose mutual multiplication (one by the square of the other) half the number of the estimate is produced.

Example: A cube is equal to 14 things plus 8, and the estimate is 4. Divide 4 (half of 8) by 4 (the estimate), which gives 1. Add this to 14 to make 15. Subtract 12 (which is 3/4 of the square of the estimate), and 3 remains. Add and subtract the root of this from 2 (half the estimate), and you will have 2 plus <term>root</term> of 3, and 2 minus

<margin>9th [Rule]</margin>
When <term>things</term> <note>the unknown variable, $x$</note> are equal to a <term>cube</term> <note>$x^3$</note> and a number, and you have found a number which, when multiplied by the root of the sum of itself and the number of things, produces the number of the equation, then half of that root, added to or subtracted from the root of the difference between the number of the equation and 3/4 of that same sum, constitutes the <term>estimate</term> <note>the value or solution for $x$</note> of the thing.

<margin>Step calculation for example below</margin>
| cube & 12 equal 34 things |
| :--- |
| 2 |
| ——————— |
| 36 |
| 12 —— 2 —— 6 |
| 34 |
| 27 |
| ——————— |
| 7 |

Example: A cube plus 12 is equal to 34 things ($x^3 + 12 = 34x$). Then, because by adding 2 to 34, the product of that 2 and the root of the sum 36 (which is 6) is 12, the number of the equation; therefore I say that if to 3 (half the root of 36) there is added or subtracted the root of 7 (the difference between 34, the number of things, and 27, which is 3/4 of the square of 6, or of such a sum), there arises the estimate of the thing: 3 plus $\sqrt{7}$, or 3 minus $\sqrt{7}$.

<margin>10th [Rule]</margin>
When things are equal to a cube and a number, and you subtract such a number from the number of the equation so that the <term>cube root</term> <note>original: Rz cuba</note> of the difference, multiplied by the number of things, produces the number subtracted, then the thing minus the cube root of the difference will be a common divisor once the subtraction is made; and this rule is similar to the sixth, just as the preceding was to the second.

<margin>Step calculation for example below</margin>
| cube & 21 equal 16 things |
| :--- |
| 48 |
| —————— |
| 27 ————— 3 |
| 48 |
| —————— |
| thing minus 3 |
| cube minus 27 \| 16 things minus 48 |

Example: 16 things are equal to a cube and 21. By subtracting 48, there remains 27, whose cube root is 3; this, multiplied by 16 (the number of things), produces 48. Therefore, by subtracting 48 from both sides, there will be a cube minus 27 and 16 things minus 48. From there, the common divisor will be "thing minus 3" ($x - 3$), and there will result a square and 3 things and 9, equal to 16. Wherefore the square and 3 things will be equal to 7, and the estimate of the thing will be the root of 9 1/4 minus 1 1/2. <note>Solving $x^2 + 3x = 7$ yields $x = \sqrt{7 + (3/2)^2} - 3/2 = \sqrt{9.25} - 1.5$.</note>

<margin>11th [Rule]</margin>
When things are equal to a cube and a number, and from the number of things you have made three <term>proportional parts</term> <note>three numbers $a, b, c$ such that $a+b+c = \text{total}$ and $a/b = b/c$</note>, from whose second [part] multiplied by the difference of the roots of the first and third—or from the multiplication of the first by the root of the third and the third by the root of the first—the difference is equal to the third part of the number of the equation, then the difference of those roots will be the estimate of the thing; and it is similar to the 4th [rule].

Example: 19 things are equal to a cube and 18 ($x^3 + 18 = 19x$). When from 19 there have been made three proportional parts 4, 6, 9, from whose middle part 6 multiplied by...

<margin>12th [Rule]</margin>
When <term>things</term> <note>the variable $x$</note> are equal to a <term>cube</term> <note>$x^3$</note> and a number, and you have divided the number of things by the cube root of the number of the equation, and from what results you have made two parts—from the multiplication of one of which into the square of the other the number of the equation is produced—then the proportional quantity between the cube root of the equation’s number and the part which you multiplied by the square of the other to produce the equation’s number is the estimate of the thing.

Example: 18 things are equal to a cube plus 8 ($18x = x^3 + 8$). Dividing 18 by 2 (the cube root of 8), 9 results, from which two parts 8 & 1 are made. From one of these, which is 8, multiplied by the square of the other, which is 1, there is produced 8 (the number of the equation). Therefore 4, the proportional number between 8 (the part of 9 which you multiplied by the square of 1, the other part) and 2 (the cube root of 8, the number of the equation), is the estimate of the thing. <note>In modern terms: 2, $x$, and 8 are in geometric progression ($2:x = x:8$), so $x^2 = 16$, thus $x = 4$.</note>

<insert>
| 18 things equal a cube plus 8 |
| :--- |
| 2 ———————————————— 2 |
| 9 ——— 1 —————————— 8 |
| 1 / ———————— 4 |
| 8 |
</insert>

<margin>13th [Rule]</margin>
When a cube and a number are equal to things, and from the third part of the number of things you have made two parts which, when multiplied by their own roots, produce two numbers that, when joined, are equal to half the number of the equation, then the sum of those roots is the estimate of the thing; and this is similar to the third rule.

Example: 15 things are equal to a cube and 18 ($x^3 + 18 = 15x$). I take 5, the third part of 15, from which I make two parts, 4 & 1. These, multiplied by their roots 2 & 1, produce 8 & 1, the sum of which, 9, is half of 18 (the number of the equation). Therefore I say that 3, the sum of such roots, is the estimate of the thing. And you already know, also from the general rule, that whenever two parts can be made from the number of things, one of which multiplied by the root of the other produces the number of the equation, such a root is the estimate of the thing; and that this can be done in two ways, and how it may fall into a <term>Binomial</term> <note>an expression like $a + \sqrt{b}$</note> or a <term>reciso</term> <note>an expression like $a - \sqrt{b}$</note> and whole numbers; therefore, although these were similar to the first rule, because we are drawn into it almost by force from the general chapter, it will have been enough to have advised of it here.

| :--- |
| 5 |
| 1 ————— 4 |
| 1 ————— 2 ————— thing 3 |
| 1 ————— 8 ————— 9 ————— 9 |
</insert>

# GIROLAMO CARDANO

[...the square] of the other, makes the number of the <term>equation</term> <note>the constant term in the equation</note>, then you multiply the part that is not squared by the sum of the part that is squared and a fourth part of the part not squared; the <term>root</term> <note>square root, symbolized by ℞</note> of the product, with half the part not squared subtracted, is the <term>estimate</term> <note>the value of the unknown $x$</note> of the thing.

<margin>15th [Rule]</margin>
Example: A cube and 20 squares are equal to 72 ($x^3 + 20x^2 = 72$). From 20, two parts are made, 18 and 2, and from one [multiplied] into the square of the other, 72 is produced; for from 18 times 4 <note>the square of 2</note> comes 72. I say that if 18 is multiplied by 6 ½ (the sum of 2, the remaining part, and 4 ½, which is a fourth part of 18), it will make 117. The root of this, with 9 (half of 18) subtracted, shows the estimate of the thing: $\sqrt{117} - 9$.

When squares are equal to a cube and a number, and you have found a number not less than a fourth part of the number of squares, nor greater than a third part, by which—when the number of the equation is divided—there results a square number, the half of whose root added to the number of squares makes four times the divisor itself; then the estimate of the thing is twice the number of the divisor, plus or minus the root of the product of four times the divisor and the difference between the number of things <note>original: "numeri rerum"; here referring to the coefficient of the squares</note> and three times that divisor.

Example: A cube plus 48 is equal to 10 squares ($x^3 + 48 = 10x^2$). Then, because [we take] 3—which is not less than a fourth part of 10 (the number of squares) nor greater than its third part—you divide 48, which produces 16. Half of its root, which is 2, added to 10 (the number of squares), constitutes 12, which is four times the divisor 3. Therefore I say that if to twice the divisor, which is 6, there is added or subtracted the root of the product of 12 (four times the divisor) and 1 (the difference between 10, the number of things, and 9, three times the divisor), and such a product is also 12, it establishes both estimates: $6 + \sqrt{12}$ or $6 - \sqrt{12}$.

<margin>Note.</margin>
And know that through these chapters, the rules and the questions formed upon them are understood with ease, which otherwise would scarcely be solved. Indeed, these rules are taken from the demonstrations in the sixth chapter, and I have not included them here because, for one who understands our books on Euclid, they are manifest by themselves; and one who does not understand will not care for them, nor seek them, since they are not necessary for him.

<margin>16th [Rule]</margin>
It would be worthwhile now to show that these rules cannot be general with respect to the estimate, and the method in one will suffice for [the others...]

->ON ARITHMETIC BOOK 10<-

to show in the remaining chapters. Let us take, therefore, the nearest chapter, and the one for which this might most likely be believed because of the multiple <term>estimates</term> <note>original: "æstimationem"; refers to the possible values or roots of the unknown $x$</note>, and let there be a <term>cube</term> <note>$x^3$</note> plus a number equal to 7 <term>squares</term> <note>$x^3 + n = 7x^2$</note>. Let $2 \frac{2}{3}$ be the number positioned—that is, the number first known in the sixth chapter, the second rule. Therefore, from that rule, the estimate of the <term>thing</term> <note>original: "res"; the unknown $x$</note> will be the <term>root</term> <note>original: "℞" or "radix"; here meaning square root</note> of 16 plus $2 \frac{2}{3}$, which is $6 \frac{2}{3}$. Thus, the remainder relative to the number of squares is $1/3$ <note>since $7 - 6 \frac{2}{3} = 1/3$</note>. Because of the demonstration set forth at the beginning of the third book, the product of $6 \frac{2}{3}$ multiplied by the square of $1/3$ is a fractional number, namely $20/27$. Conversely, when $1/3$ is multiplied by the square of $6 \frac{2}{3}$, the result is also a fractional number, specifically $14 \frac{22}{27}$. Therefore, if the number of squares is set as an integer and the estimate is composed of fractional numbers, the number of the <term>equation</term> <note>the constant term $n$</note>—which is the excess of the rational parts of the squares over the cube—can never be an integer. Rather, the number of such an equation is produced from one part of the number of [squares] multiplied by the square of the other.

Having shown this, I take a cube and a number equal to 7 squares. It is evident from the demonstrations on the seventh [book] of Euclid, and from the rules of the sixth book, by reducing the number to a square and a cube, that the maximum product of the parts of 7 multiplied by the square of the other is $50 \frac{22}{27}$. Therefore, 7 can be divided so that it produces integers through the multiplication of one part by the square of the other, from 1 up to 50, but not into fractions. According to what has been demonstrated for integers, however, only a triple division can be made in whole numbers, as shown in the table:

| | 7 | | |
|:---:|:---:|:---:|:---:|
| 1 | 6, | 36, | 6. |
| 2 | 5, | 50, | 20. |
| 3 | 4, | 48, | 36. |

Since no more can be produced than 6, 20, 36, 48, and 50, it follows that the remaining 45 numbers <note>the other integers between 1 and 50</note> cannot be exhausted by this type of estimate in any way. Therefore, this rule is particular—and indeed very particular—yet do not believe that in other chapters the number for one part of a <term>Binomial</term> or <term>Recisum</term> <note>technical terms for sums or differences involving irrational roots, e.g., $a + \sqrt{b}$</note> cannot serve, as we have frequently shown in examples.

<margin>17th [Rule]</margin>
When there is a cube and a number equal to things <note>$x^3 + n = ax$</note>, and from the root of the number of things you have made two parts, from the multiplication of the first by double the square of the second, and the second by the square of the first, the number of the equation is produced; then the second part will be the estimate of the thing.

<insert>
cube & 48 equal to 25 things
   2   \ /   3 —— 5 <note>the root of 25</note>
        X
   4   / \   18 <note>double the square of 3</note>
  12 ——————— 36
       48
</insert>

Example: A cube and 48 are equal to 25 things ($x^3 + 48 = 25x$). Then, because from 5 (the root of 25) the parts 3 and 2 are made, and from the multiplication of 2 by 18 (double the square of 3) and from 3 by 4 (the square of 2) 48 is produced, I say therefore that the part 3, whose square is doubled, is the estimate of the thing.

<margin>18th [Rule]</margin>
When there are a cube and squares equal to a number, and two numbers differing by the number of the equation, multiplied by each other, have produced so much...

# He shows the greater rules, which are entirely unique.
## Chapter XXVI.

<margin>1st Rule</margin>
**W**HEN a <term>square-square</term> <note>original: "quadratum quadrati"; $x^4$</note> and the <term>things</term> <note>original: "res"; linear terms, e.g., $10x$</note> are equal to <term>squares</term> <note>original: "quadratis"; $x^2$ terms</note> and a number, and after dividing the coefficient of the things and the constant of the equation by the number of squares, if half of the result from the things is the square root of the result from the constant, then take the square root of the first constant of the equation, add to it a fourth part of the number of squares, and take the <term>universal root</term> <note>original: "radicem universalem"; a root that applies to the entire following expression, essentially a nested radical: $\sqrt{...}$</note> of the whole, from which subtract the square root of that same fourth part of the number of squares; the remainder is the estimate of the thing.

<margin>Question</margin>
Example. Four men entered into a partnership. The first put in a certain quantity. The second put in the square-square of a tenth part of the first man's share. The third put in five times the square of a tenth part of the first man's share. The fourth put in 5, and the first and second together put in as much as the third and fourth together. It is asked how much each put in? Suppose the first put in 10 things <note>If the first is $10x$, then a tenth of his share is $x$</note>; therefore the second put in a square-square <note>$x^4$</note>, the third 5 squares <note>$5x^2$</note>, and the fourth, as was said, put in 5. Therefore, the square-square and 10 things are equal to 5 squares and 5 <note>$x^4 + 10x = 5x^2 + 5$</note>. Thus, by dividing the number of things by the number of squares, 2 results, half of which is the square root of 1, which is what results when the number of the equation, 5, is divided by the number of squares, 5. Therefore, take the square root of 5 (the constant of the equation), to which add a fourth part of the number of squares [5], and it becomes $\sqrt{5} + 1 \frac{1}{4}$. Take the universal root of this, which is $\sqrt{\sqrt{5} + 1 \frac{1}{4}}$, and from it subtract [the root of] a fourth part of the number of squares; you will have the estimate of the thing: $\sqrt{\sqrt{5} + 1 \frac{1}{4}} - \sqrt{1 \frac{1}{4}}$, and they will have shares as you see.

<insert>
| Person | Share Calculation |
| :--- | :--- |
| 1st | $\sqrt{\text{universal}: \sqrt{50000} + 125} - \sqrt{125}$ |
| 2nd | $17 \frac{1}{2} + \sqrt{500} - \sqrt{\text{universal}: \sqrt{612500} + 781 \frac{1}{4}}$ |
| 3rd | $12 \frac{1}{2} + \sqrt{125} - \sqrt{\text{universal}: 78125 + 156 \frac{1}{4}}$ |
| 4th | 5 |
</insert>

<margin>2nd Rule</margin>
In the same way, where a square-square is equal to squares, things, and a number under the same conditions, a similar rule will hold, and the method for the estimate will be the same, except that at the end we shall add the root of the fourth part of the number of qua-

<meta>catchword: qua (abbreviation for "quadratorum" or squares)</meta>

<summary>Cardano introduces Chapter 26, presenting specialized rules for solving certain fourth-degree equations (quartics) where the coefficients meet specific proportional criteria. He illustrates this with a "partnership" word problem involving four investors, leading to a complex solution using nested radicals.</summary>

## BOOK 10 ON ARITHMETICA

...of the squares, to the <term>universal root</term><note>original: "radici vniuersali"; a nested radical covering the following terms</note>, which we were subtracting in the previous rule, as in the example: if a <term>square-square</term><note>original: "qd' qd^m" or "quadratum quadrati"; $x^4$</note> were equal to 5 <term>squares</term><note>$x^2$</note>, 10 <term>things</term><note>original: "rebus"; the unknown $x$</note> and the number 5, the <term>estimate</term><note>original: "æstimatio"; the value of $x$</note> of the thing would be the universal root of: the root of 5 plus 1 1/2, plus the root of 1 1/4.

And the reason in these rules is, that the root of a square-square is a square, and the root of 5 squares minus 10 things plus 5, is the root of 5 minus the root of 5 [times] squares, or [minus] things [times] the root of 5; therefore the square and the thing [multiplied by] the root of 5 are equal to the root of 5, and the estimate is known, which is the same as that [from the equation] where the square-square plus 10 things is equal to 5 squares and 5. And by the same reasoning, if the square-square is equal to 5 squares, 10 things and 5, the square will be equal to the things [multiplied by] the root of 5 plus the root of 5, wherefore the thing is known.

<margin>3rd Rule</margin>
When a square-square and squares and the thing shall be equal to <term>cubes</term><note>$x^3$</note> and a number—where the number is 2 more than the number of squares, and the number of things and cubes are the same, and half the number of things is the square root of the [constant] number—then multiply into itself a fourth part of the number of things, and to the product add 1; from this subtract the square root of the sum of the square of half the number of things and one. The square root of the remainder, added to or subtracted from a fourth part of the number of things, will be the estimate of the thing.

Example. **A square-square** and 34 squares and 12 things are equal to 12 cubes and 36. Here you see that the cubes are equal to the things in number (12), and half the number of things (6) is the root of the number 36, and the number itself (36) is 2 more than the number of squares (34). Therefore, multiply 3 (the fourth part of the 12 things) by itself, making 9; add 1 by the rule, making 10. Subtract the root of 37 (the sum of the square of half the things and one); this makes 10 minus the root of 37. Take the universal root of this and subtract it from or add it to 3 (the fourth part of the number of things). You will have the estimate of the thing: 3 plus the universal root of 10 minus the root of 37, or 3 minus the universal root of 10 minus the root of 37.

<margin>4th Rule</margin>
The method for finding such rules is obtained from the "Greater Rule"—from which we have given this chapter its name—and it is this: that you solve some question simply, then by the Greater Rule, or even another; then you will observe the necessary conditions in the transition from one to the other. Afterward, observe how you arrived at the estimate of the thing, and you will make a new rule in this manner for an unknown type of equation.

Example. Divide 6 into two parts, so that the cube of the smaller, the square of the larger, and the product of that same larger [part] and 8, these three products, are proportional. I say you will arrive by the Greater Rule at this: that the proportion of such parts will be the cube root of 8, which is 2. Wherefore we will divide 6 by the cube root of 8 plus 1, and the estimate of the thing will result. Yet by following the <term>position</term><note>original: "positionem"; the algebraic setup of the problem</note>, we will have 1 square-square plus 24 squares plus 144, equal to 8 cubes plus 96 positions. We shall say, therefore, when a square-square and squares and a number are equal to cubes and things, and we can find some number which, multiplied by the number of the equation, produces a number whose root...

Then, if you take that first number already mentioned, which you multiplied into the number of the equation, and add 3 according to the rule,

And when multiplied into the <term>root</term><note>original: "Rz" or "radix"</note> of the root of the number which you produced from the beginning, there should result a number which, when divided by the first number found, produces the number of cubes. And if the number of <term>things</term><note>original: "rerum"; plural of "res" ($x$)</note> led through the first number is four times the cube of its <term>fourth root</term><note>original: "Rz'Rz"; the root of the root</note>, then I say that by subtracting 1, according to the rule, from the first number you multiplied, and taking the <term>cube root</term><note>original: "Rz cubica"</note> of the remainder, and adding 1 to it also according to the rule, and dividing the resulting fourth root by this total, the result is the <term>estimate</term><note>original: "æstimatio"; the value of $x$</note> of the thing. 

The reason for this is that in such a problem, the number of the square-square arises from multiplying, with one added; the number of cubes from dividing into the multiplicand plus 4; the number of squares from six times the square of the dividend; the number of things from four times the cube of the dividend; and the number of the equation is the square-square of the dividend. In this problem, I call the dividend 6 and the multiplicand 8. 

For example, a square-square plus 6 squares plus 4 is equal to $3 \frac{1}{2}$ cubes plus 8 things. Let the first number be a square, multiply it by 4, it becomes 4 squares; the root of this is 2 things; multiply by 6 according to the rule, it becomes 12 things; divide these by the squares, and the result is equal to 6. Therefore, 6 squares are equal to 12 things; thus the thing is 2. However, in our <term>assumption</term><note>original: "positione"</note> we set it as a square, therefore the first number or multiplicand will be 4. Since the other stated conditions match, 2 will be the number to be divided; having divided this by the cube root of 3 plus 1, the result will be the estimate of the thing, and we spoke of this in the sixth chapter.

## On the transition from a particular chapter to a particular chapter.
### Chapter XXVII.

<margin>1.</margin>
**T**here is also a transition from a singular <term>chapter</term><note>original: "capituli"; Cardano uses "chapter" to mean a specific type or category of algebraic equation</note> to another singular one, in this manner: a cube and 2 squares and 56 are equal to 41 things, and one estimate of the thing is 3 plus the root of 2. I ask, in that same estimate, a cube with 7 squares will be equal to how many things? And with what number?

number: multiply the difference of the number of <term>squares</term><note>original: "quadratorum"; $x^2$</note>, which is 5, by double the part which is the number in the <term>estimation</term><note>original: "æstimatione"; the value of the unknown $x$</note>, namely by 6, which makes 30; to this add 41, the number of <term>things</term><note>original: "rerum"; $x$</note>, making 71, the number of things. Then multiply the parts of the estimation by themselves, which makes 2 and 9; the difference of these products, which is 7, multiply by 5 (the difference of the number of squares), which makes 35. This you add to 56—because 3 is greater than the <term>root</term><note>original: "Rz"; radix</note> of 2—and it makes the number of the equation 91. Therefore, the <term>cube</term><note>original: "cubus"; $x^3$</note> and 7 squares and 91 are equal to 71 things, with the estimation being 3 plus the root of 2. And where the root had been greater than the number, you would have subtracted 35 from 56, and the number 21 would have remained.

<margin>2.</margin>
I also say, that it is not permitted to pass from chapter to chapter <note>In this context, "chapters" refer to the specific types or forms of algebraic equations, such as "cubes equal to things and a number."</note> while the same genus of denominations remains, such that the estimation of the thing is the same and not rational—that is, not a whole number or a fraction. For example, let the cube plus 3 things be equal to 10; the estimation of the thing is the <term>universal cubic root</term><note>original: "Rz v: cubica"; a cube root of a binomial</note> of the root of 26 plus 5, minus the universal cubic root of the root of 26 minus 5. I say that under this estimation, a cube with some other number of things cannot be equal to any other number, all the way to infinity. For let us suppose (for the sake of example) that the cube plus 9 things is equal to 18; since the thing is the same—namely the said cubic root—the cube will be the same in both cases interchangeably. Therefore, from the third book, the cube plus 9 things plus 10 is equal to the cube plus 3 things plus 18. I cast out the common cube, and there will be 9 things plus 10 equal to 3 things plus 18; therefore 6 things are equal to 8, and the estimation of the thing is $1 \frac{1}{3}$, a rational number, and not the said cubic root, which is contrary to what was supposed.

| |
| :--- |
| **cube plus 3 things equal 10** |
| **cube plus 9 things equal 18** |

<margin>3.</margin>
Similarly, neither will many cubes with many things be equal to any number while the same estimation remains, as is clear from the preceding. For once all parts are divided by the number of cubes, we will have, as before, a cube and things equal to a number, which I have already shown to be impossible. The same reasoning therefore holds in all cases; for if I say the cube equals 6 things plus 2, or the <term>square-square</term><note>original: "q̃d'q̃dratum"; $x^4$</note> equals 6 things plus 2, I will then say that in the same estimation, the cube or the square-square can be equal to no other things and rational number. I say "rational," because it does not forbid that an equation might follow if irrational things or numbers are assumed.

<margin>4.</margin>
And from this it also follows that the rule holds for other denominations where the estimation of the thing is neither a rational number nor a simple root of the middle denomination's genus. For example, 2 cubes and 10,

...are equal: to the **biquadrate**<note>original: "qd'qdratum"; the fourth power, $x^4$</note> and the **thing**<note>original: "rei"; the unknown variable $x$</note>, the **estimation**<note>original: "æstimatio"; the value or solution for $x$</note> is neither a number, nor a simple cubic **root**<note>original: "Rz"; radix</note> of any rational number. I say that the biquadrate, under the same estimation, can be equal to no cubes and number; it is clear, because having made the transformation, and having discarded the biquadrate, there will remain cubes equal to a number; therefore the estimation of the thing will necessarily be the cubic root of a number, or a number, which is against the assumption.

### On the operations of Pronic or mixed and Allelar roots. Chapter XXVIII.

<margin>1</margin>
**We** have already shown in the previous sections that there are three species of **Pronic roots**<note>A "Pronic" number traditionally refers to the product of two consecutive integers ($n(n+1)$). Cardano expands this to higher powers here: $x^2+x$, $x^3+x$, and $x^4+x$.</note>. The **Minor**, when the square root is compared to the sum of its square and itself; this sum is called the minor pronic. The **Middle**, when the cubic root is compared to the sum of itself and its cube; this sum is called the middle pronic. But the **Major** pronic root is when the **root of the root**<note>the fourth root, $\sqrt[4]{x}$</note> of some number is compared to the sum of itself and that number of which it is the root of the root; this sum is called the major pronic, as in the example. The major pronic of 3 is 84 <note>If $x=3$, the major pronic is $3^4 + 3 = 81 + 3 = 84$.</note>, and 3 is the major pronic root of 84. However, common operations—multiplication, division, addition, or subtraction—do not apply to these, as they are like **anomalous verbs** in Grammar; instead, they have a certain specific operation called **transition**<note>original: "transitus"; a method of converting between different types of pronic values.</note>.

<margin>2</margin>
When, therefore, you multiply a minor pronic by its pronic root, and to the product you add that same pronic, the square root of the sum will be the middle pronic of the square root of the minor pronic root. For example: I multiply 3 (the minor pronic root of 12) by 12; it makes 36. I add 12 (the minor pronic) to it, making 48. The root of this (which is the root of 48) is the middle pronic of the root of 3 (which was the minor pronic root of 12). For when the root of 3 is raised to the cube, it becomes the root of 27; adding the root of 3 to it produces the root of 48. Therefore, the root of 3 is the middle pronic root of the root of 48, as was proposed.

<margin>3</margin>
When you multiply a middle pronic by its pronic root, the result is the minor pronic of the square of the middle pronic root. For example: I multiply 3 (the middle pronic root of 30) by 30; it makes 90. This is the minor pronic of 9 (the square of 3), which was the middle pronic root of 30. <note>Checking the math: $3^3 + 3 = 30$. Then $30 \times 3 = 90$. The minor pronic of $3^2$ ($9$) is $9^2 + 9 = 81 + 9 = 90$.</note>

<margin>4</margin>
When a major pronic is multiplied by itself, and the product is divided by the square of its major pronic root, the result, relative to the cube of the same root, is like **1 square plus 2 positions plus 1**<note>In modern notation, $(x^4+x)^2 / x^2 = (x^3+1)^2 = x^6 + 2x^3 + 1$. If we treat $x^3$ as the "position," this is indeed a quadratic expansion: $y^2 + 2y + 1$.</note>. For example: I take 18 as the major pronic <note>Where the root is 2, since $2^4 + 2 = 18$</note>, I multiply it by itself, making 324; I divide this by 4, the square...

<summary>Cardano defines and explores "Pronic" roots—mathematical relationships involving powers of $x$ plus $x$ (specifically $x^2+x$, $x^3+x$, and $x^4+x$). He describes these as "anomalous" and provides rules for "transitioning" between these different types of algebraic expressions.</summary>
<keywords>Pronic roots, biquadrate, middle pronic, major pronic, minor pronic, transition, algebraic operations, Jerome Cardan</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from page 51, concluding the discussion on major pronic roots before introducing "allelar roots" and the "Rule of Mode," a method for creating general algebraic formulas from specific problems.</meta>

-># ON ARITHMETIC, BOOK 10<-

...square of 2, the root of the major pronic 18, the result is 81, which is 1 **biquadrate**<note>original: "qdratum"; the fourth power, $x^4$</note> plus 2 **positions**<note>original: "positionibus"; the unknown variable $x$</note> plus 1, with respect to 8, the cube of 2, of the same pronic root.

<margin>5.</margin>
Roots are called **allelar**<note>original: "allelæ", from the Greek 'allelon' meaning 'reciprocal' or 'of each other'. These are roots that have a mutual relationship when squared and multiplied.</note> when, from the mutual multiplication of two numbers by the square of the other, two numbers arise. For example, I take 2 and 3; they are called the allelar roots of 12 and 18, for from 2 times 9 [the square of 3] comes 18, and from 3 times 4 [the square of 2] comes 12. However, these roots are found in this way: multiply each of them by itself, and divide the product by the remaining number; the **cubic roots**<note>original: "Rz cubicæ"</note> of the results are the allelar roots. For example, I want the allelar roots of 4 and 8: multiply 8 by itself, it makes 64; divide by 4, the result is 16. Also multiply 4 by itself, it makes 16; divide by 8, the result is 2. Therefore, the cubic root of 16 and the cubic root of 2 are the allelar roots of 4 and 8. Likewise, the allelar roots of 6 and 18 are the cubic root of 54 and the cubic root of 2.

| 4 | 8 |
|---|---|
| 16 | 64 |
| 2 | 16 |

<margin>Corollary</margin>
From this it is clear that all allelar roots are cubic roots of numbers maintained in a **triplicate proportion**<note>A ratio of the cubes of the terms.</note>, in which their proposed prior solids were held; and these are the mean proportionals.

<margin>6.</margin>
The operations for these are therefore manifest from this; for when they have been found, they are reduced to cubic roots, with which you will operate again, and once the operation is completed, you reduce them back to allelar roots.

---

### On the Rule of Mode. Chapter XXIX.

Therefore this rule (because it shows the mode of constructing any number of rules for trade) is the Rule of **Mode**<note>original: "Modi"; referring to a method or manner of calculation</note>, most useful to masters of Arithmetic so that, having found certain easier things, they might teach the art; with its help, we also completed the greatest part of the sixth book. This rule is as follows: solve any proposed question by whatever means you can, whether by **position**<note>algebraic substitution using a variable</note> or by the help of the sixth book; then you shall take away the "position" and the other rules, and keep the operations, which you should reduce to brevity as much as possible, and you will have a rule of mode for every similar question.

Example: 7 **paces**<note>original: "passus"; a unit of measurement</note> of green silk and 3 paces of black are sold for 72 **denarii**<note>original: "denariis"; a standard silver coin/unit of currency</note>, and for the same price 2 paces of green silk and 4 of black are sold for 52 denarii. The price is sought. You set the "position" to be the value of one pace of green silk; therefore 7 paces of green sell for 7 positions. Wherefore 3 paces of black sell for 72 denarii minus 7 positions, and one pace will be worth $\frac{1}{3}$ of these, specifically 24 denarii minus $2 \frac{1}{3}$ positions. Thus 4 paces of black will be worth 96 denarii minus $9 \frac{1}{3}$ positions. But two paces of green are worth 2 positions by the assumption; therefore 2 paces of green silk and 4 of black are worth 96 denarii minus $7 \frac{1}{3}$ positions, and these same were valued at 52 denarii; therefore...

<meta>The catchword at the bottom is "tur", indicating the word "igitur" (therefore) continues on the next page.</meta>

<summary>This page defines 'allelar roots' (reciprocal relationships between cubic roots and integers) and introduces the 'Rule of Mode,' a pedagogical method for converting specific algebraic solutions into general arithmetic rules.</summary>
<keywords>allelar roots, Rule of Mode, cubic roots, proportion, silk trade problem, position method, Cardano, algebraic rules</keywords>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstration from page 52. Having previously discussed "allelar roots," Cardano here concludes a practical algebraic word problem involving the price of silk before introducing a general method he calls the "Rule of Mode" and transitioning to Chapter 30 on the "Golden Rule."</meta>

-># THE WRITINGS OF GIROLAMO CARDANO<-

...is derived from: 96 minus 7 1/3 **positions**<note>original: "positionibus"; the unknown variable, similar to our modern 'x'.</note>, are equated to 52; wherefore 44, which is the difference between 96 and 52, will be equal to 7 1/3 positions. Therefore, one position is worth 6 **pence**<note>original: "denarios"; a common unit of currency.</note>, and it is appropriate that such is the valuation for a **pace**<note>original: "passus"; a unit of length for measuring fabric.</note> of green silk. Wherefore, 7 paces of green silk are sold for 42 pence, and 3 paces of black silk for the remaining pence out of 72, which is to say 30 pence; therefore, one pace costs 10 pence. Thus, you have the price for each type of silk. Up to this point, you have worked by the method of position; now I come to the **rule**<note>Cardano is moving from solving a specific problem to providing a general formulaic shortcut.</note>. I say that in such cases, divide the more numerous paces, namely 7, and the number of pence, namely 72, by the fewer paces, namely 3; and let that which results be multiplied by the paces set in the second position corresponding to the fewer [paces]. From the product of the number of paces, subtract the remaining paces of the second position, and with the remainder, divide the difference between the second price and the product; the result will be the value of the more numerous pace in the first position.

**Example:** Divide 7 and 72 by 3; the result is 2 1/3 and 24. Multiply by 4, making 9 1/3 and 96. From 9 1/3, take away 2; from 96, take away 52. There remain 7 1/3 and 44. Divide 44 by 7 1/3; the result is 6, the price of one pace of green silk.

Hence, from this emerges a shorter rule, as in the third figure: divide 4 by 3, namely the number of paces of the same kind of silk in the two requests; the result is 1 1/3. Multiply this by 7 and 72, which makes 9 1/3 and 96. From these, take away the numbers placed above in the second position, which are 2 and 52, aligning like with like. There remain 7 1/3 and 44. Divide the number of pence, 44, by 7 1/3, the number of paces; the result is 6, the price of the green silk. In this way, you establish a very brief rule from such a long operation of position, whence this **Rule of Mode**<note>original: "modi regula"; a method for finding a general procedure.</note> may rightly be called the **mother of rules**.

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page transitions from specific practical problems (like the silk problem on the previous page) to a general mathematical method for finding the roots of complex equations. Cardano is describing a method of numerical approximation similar to the "Rule of False Position" or linear interpolation.</meta>

<page-num>53</page-num>
->### BOOK 10 ON ARITHMETIC###<-

**H**is rule covers the majority of matters that come into practical use; for once a question has been reduced to a **position**<note>original: "positionem"; in this context, it refers to setting up an algebraic equation where the "thing" or "position" is the unknown variable 'x'.</note> and the operation is completed, it seeks the closest estimate, which is obtained as follows. First, hunt for the nearest whole numbers, one greater and one smaller, which satisfy the equation; these will not be difficult to find. We shall call the smaller of these the **first discovery** and the larger the **second discovery**. The difference between their products we call the **greater difference**; the difference between the first product and the target number of the equation we call the **first difference**; and the difference between the second product and the target number of the equation we call the **second difference**. Therefore, divide the first difference by the greater difference; add the result to the first discovery, and we will complete an **imperfect estimate**. We then reduce this estimate to the equation—namely, through the denominations of the equation, just as with the first and second discoveries—and subtract the result from the second product. Next, subtract the imperfect estimate from the second discovery; multiply the remainder by the recorded second difference, and divide such a product by the difference between the product of the imperfect estimate and the second product. Subtract the result from the second discovery; the remainder is a very close estimate of the "thing," which one may always approach more closely through repeated operations. The same will occur where the equation is of some denomination equal to a number and other denominations, as will be clear in the examples.

Let there be, for example, 1 <term>quadratum-quadratum</term><note>original: "q̃d'q̃dratum"; the fourth power of the unknown (x⁴).</note> and 3 <term>cubes</term><note>original: "cubi"; the third power of the unknown (x³).</note> equal to 100. You see that if the "thing" is 2, the <term>quadratum-quadratum</term> and 3 <term>cubes</term> are 40<note>2⁴ + 3(2³) = 16 + 24 = 40.</note>; and if the "thing" is 3, the <term>quadratum-quadratum</term> and 3 <term>cubes</term> will be 162<note>3⁴ + 3(3³) = 81 + 81 = 162.</note>. Therefore, the first discovery is 2 and the first product is 40; the second discovery is 3 and the second product is 162. The greater difference is 122, the first difference is 60, and the second difference is 62. Note that the first discovery always differs by one unit from the second discovery; otherwise, you have not operated correctly. Once these are known, divide 60 by 122, which results in 30/61; add this to 2 (the first discovery), making the imperfect estimate 2 30/61. Bring this to the <term>quadratum-quadratum</term> and three <term>cubes</term>, which makes nearly 85. Subtract this product of 85 (from the imperfect estimate) from 162 (the second product), and you will have 77. Also subtract 2 30/61 from 3 (the second discovery), leaving 31/61; multiply this by 62 (the second difference), which makes 1922/61. Divide this by 77, which results in 1922/4697. Subtract this from 3 (the second discovery), and the estimate will be sufficiently [close]...

<meta>catchword: "pros" (likely referring to "proxima" or "proxime", meaning "very close")</meta>

<summary>Cardano explains a general method for approximating the roots of higher-order equations (like x⁴ + 3x³ = 100) using a technique of interpolation between two whole-number guesses. He provides a step-by-step arithmetic procedure to refine these guesses into a highly accurate "estimate" of the unknown variable.</summary>
<keywords>root approximation, linear interpolation, Rule of False Position, quadratum-quadratum, cubes, Girolamo Cardano, algebra, 16th-century mathematics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Cardano’s explanation of numerical approximation for higher-order equations. Here, he provides worked examples for a quartic equation, a quadratic equation, and a cubic equation using this method of interpolation.</meta>

-># HIERONYMUS CARDANO #<-

the nearest <term>squared-square</term><note>original: "qđ' qdrati" (quadratum quadrati); the fourth power of the unknown, or $x^4$.</note> plus 3 <term>cubes</term><note>original: "cubis"; $x^3$.</note> equal to 100 is this: $2 \frac{2775}{4697}$. And if you wished, you could approach as much closer as you like by alternating the operations.

But if a **square** and 20 were equal to 10 **things**<note>The equation $x^2 + 20 = 10x$.</note>, then if the **thing** were 7, we would have the square plus 20 equal to $9 \frac{6}{7}$ things. And if the **thing** were 8, we would have the square plus 20 equal to $10 \frac{1}{2}$ things. Therefore, as before, the **first discovery** is 7, the **first product** is $9 \frac{6}{7}$, the **second discovery** is 8, and the **second product** is $10 \frac{1}{2}$. The **greater difference** is $\frac{9}{14}$, the **first difference** is $\frac{1}{7}$, and the **second difference** is $\frac{1}{2}$. We shall therefore divide the **first difference** by the **greater difference**, which results in $\frac{2}{9}$. We add this to 7 (the **first discovery**), and it becomes the **imperfect estimate** $7 \frac{2}{9}$. Because the square of this plus 20 is equal to 9 things and $\frac{116}{117}$, which differs almost imperceptibly from 10 (the number of **things** in the equation), we do not use another operation, but shall say the close estimate is $7 \frac{2}{9}$.
