Suppose also a **cube** is equal to 6 **things** plus 20.<note>The equation $x^3 = 6x + 20$.</note> We say: if 3 were the **thing**, then 6 **things** plus 20 would be equal to $1 \frac{11}{27}$ of the **cube**. And if the **thing** were 4, 6 **things** plus 20 would be equal to $\frac{11}{16}$ of the **cube**. Therefore, the **first discovery** is 3 and the **first product** is $1 \frac{11}{27}$; the **second discovery** will be 4 and the **second product** $\frac{11}{16}$. The **first difference** is $\frac{11}{27}$, the **second difference** $\frac{5}{16}$, and the **greater difference** $\frac{311}{432}$. Divide the **smaller difference** by this, which gives $\frac{176}{311}$; add this to 3, and it becomes the **imperfect estimate** $3 \frac{176}{311}$. Follow the equation—namely, by taking 6 **things** plus 20—and they will be $\frac{1245186154}{1363938029}$ of its **cube**. This, however, is very close to $3 \frac{1}{4}$. From this we shall subtract the **second product**, and there remains $\frac{61}{271}$ and $\frac{5}{16}$. Similarly, I subtract $3 \frac{176}{311}$ (the **imperfect estimate**) from 4 (the **second discovery**), and $1 \frac{35}{311}$ remains. I multiply this by $\frac{5}{16}$ (the **second difference**), just as in the first example; it becomes $\frac{675}{4976}$. Divide this by the difference between the **second product** and the **product of the estimate**, which is $\frac{61}{271}$; it results in $\frac{182925}{303536}$. Subtract this from the **second discovery**, as before, and the remainder is the estimate of the **thing**: $3 \frac{120611}{303536}$. This is very close to $3 \frac{201}{506}$, and therefore to $3 \frac{2}{5}$. Now, 6 **things** plus 20 are $40 \frac{2}{5}$, and the **cube** of $3 \frac{2}{5}$ is $39 \frac{38}{625}$. If you wished to work more closely, you could perform a third operation just as you did the first, and without doubt you would arrive at an imperceptible difference. This method is universal and needs no other rule.

...with two others, or three, but with double or triple entry, you can also reduce everything to numbers, as in the first example; and the operations in that case are far easier. For instance, if I say a <term>squared-square</term><note>original: "qd'qdratum"; the fourth power of the unknown ($x^4$).</note> and 6 <term>squares</term><note>original: "qdrata"; $x^2$.</note> and 200 are equal to 10 <term>cubes</term><note>original: "cubis"; $x^3$.</note> and 12 <term>things</term><note>original: "rebus"; the unknown ($x$). The equation is $x^4 + 6x^2 + 200 = 10x^3 + 12x$.</note>, the **first discovery**<note>A first guess for the value of $x$.</note> will be 9, and the **product**<note>The result of the equation when $x=9$ is substituted.</note> will be minus 152, because the 10 cubes and 12 things exceed the squared-square, 6 squares, and 200. The **second discovery** will be 10, and the **second product** will be 680 plus, by which the squared-square, 6 squares, and 200 exceed the 10 cubes and 12 things. Then the **first difference** is equal to the first product, and the **second difference** to the second product, and the **greater difference** is the sum of both <note>$152 + 680 = 832$. This represents the total "swing" between the two guesses.</note>. Then it will suffice for the first operation to divide, as before, the first difference by the greater difference; that which comes out is $\frac{152}{832}$. We shall add this to the first discovery, and the **imperfect estimate** becomes $9 \frac{152}{832}$. Then, if you wish to approach more closely, reduce this estimate to its denominations on both sides, and collect the difference which shall be called A. Multiply this by the difference between the imperfect estimate and the second discovery, and divide the product again by the greater difference; add or subtract that which comes out, as is appropriate, and you will have what you intend. In this way, one could also operate in the second and third examples, but we wished to declare both methods for greater ease in various circumstances. Say the same for extracting roots.

## On the Great Rule. Chapter XXXI.

**T**his rule is for solving great questions, and from it were found the rules for alloying gold and silver. It sharpens the wit, and is performed through demonstrations; it requires an experienced man and is taught through questions, since it is manifold. And the foundation of the rule is **commutation**<note>original: "commutatio"; the process of substitution or exchanging one variable/term for another to simplify an equation.</note>.

->QUESTION I.<-

Divide 8 into two parts, from whose **cubes** multiplied by each other, 16 is produced. You will say, therefore, from one [part] into the other will be produced the <term>cube root of 16</term>. Divide 8 into two parts, from whose mutual multiplication the <term>cube root of 16</term> is produced, and they will be $4 + \sqrt{V: 16 - \sqrt[3]{16}}$ and $4 - \sqrt{V: 16 - \sqrt[3]{16}}$<note>original: "Rz v: 16 m: Rz cubica 16"; "Rz v:" refers to a "universal root" or a root of the entire subsequent expression. Cardano is solving $x(8-x) = \sqrt[3]{16}$, which leads to this quadratic solution.</note>.

->QUESTION II.<-

Divide 8 into three proportional parts, of which the square of the first is equal to the remaining [two]. Therefore, there will be two first parts, of which the square of one is equal to the other <note>If the parts are $a, b, c$ in proportion ($a/b = b/c$), then $b+c = a^2$. Since $a+b+c=8$, then $a+a^2=8$.</note>. Next we shall divide the greater into two parts...

### PROBLEM III

<margin>
| | |
|---|---|
| | 1st: Root of 8 1/4 minus 1/2 |
| 2nd | Universal Root: Root of 63 1/4 minus 10 3/8 plus 1/4 minus Root of 2 1/16 |

Divide 8 into three proportional parts, such that the square of the larger part is the proportional mean between the cubes of each of the other parts. You will say, therefore, that the cube of the smaller is the <term>cube root</term><note>original: "℞ cubica"</note> of the cube of the larger; and this is because the proportion of the cube of the larger to its own square is the larger part itself, and this same proportion exists between the square of the larger and the cube of the smaller. Therefore, the cube of the smaller is the root of the square of the larger, and equal to the larger part itself. Wherefore, 8 consists of the smaller part and its own cube. Thus, 1 cube plus 1 <term>thing</term><note>original: "re"; the unknown value, usually denoted as x.</note> is equal to 8, and the value of the thing is the smaller part.

### PROBLEM IV

<margin>
| | 8 | |
|---|---|---|
| A | 7 | B |
| C | E | D |
</margin>

Divide 8 into two parts, so that seven times the larger is the proportional mean between the square of the larger and the cube of the smaller. Let A be the larger, and C its square; let B be the smaller, and D its cube. Let E also be seven times A. Since, therefore, from A times A comes C, and from A times 7 comes E, the ratio of A to 7 will be as C to E. Wherefore, from the 11th [proposition] of the 5th [book of Euclid], as B is to D, so A is to E. Therefore, from A times D comes seven times E. But E is seven times A; therefore, from A times D comes 49 A. Thus D is 49, the square of 7. Wherefore the cube of the smaller B is 49, and B is the cube root of 49, and A is the remainder.

---

### PROBLEM V

<margin>
| | 8 | |
|---|---|---|
| A | 7 | B |
| C | E | D |
</margin>

Divide 8 into two parts, so that seven times the larger is the proportional mean between the cube of the larger and the square of the smaller. Let A be the larger, and C the cube of A; let B be the smaller, and D the square of B; and let E be the product of 7 and A. Because, therefore, from A times the square of A comes C, and from A times 7 comes E, the ratio of the square of A to 7 will be as C to E, and therefore as E to D. However, the proportion of the square of A to the square of B is composed of the proportion of the square of A to 7, and 7 to the square of B. This is the same as the proportion of E to D and 7 to the square of B. But D is the square of B; therefore the proportion of the square of A to the square of B is composed of the proportion of seven times A (which is E) to D, and 7 to that same D. The proportion of the square of A to D is therefore composed of the proportion of E to D and 7 to D. Therefore, by the <term>rule of six quantities</term><note>A method in proportion theory, often used in spherical trigonometry or complex ratios, here applied to algebraic composition.</note>, or from the composition of proportions, from 7 times E comes the square of A times D. But E is seven times A; therefore from 49 times A comes the square of A times D. Therefore from A times D (which is the square of B) comes 49. Wherefore, from the chapter on the <term>cube and number equal to squares</term><note>Referring to the specific algebraic solution for equations of the form $x^3 + c = ax^2$.</note>, B is the root of 7 1/4 plus 1/2, and A is 7 1/2 minus the root of 7 1/4.

### PROBLEM VII

Divide 8 into two parts, such that the product of the larger and the smaller is the <term>proportional mean</term><note>A middle value in a geometric progression (a:b = b:c), where b is the mean.</note> between the square of the smaller and ten times that same smaller part. You will say, therefore, because the smaller is that which is multiplied by itself, by the larger, and by 10, that the larger is proportional between the smaller and 10. Therefore, the square of the larger is equal to ten times the smaller, and the solution is known, for the larger will be the root of 105 minus 5, and the smaller will be 13 minus the root of 105.

### PROBLEM VIII

Divide 8 into two parts, of which the square of the larger is the proportional mean between the square of the smaller and the product of the whole [8] by the larger. Set the larger as A, and the smaller as B. Because, therefore, that which is formed from <insert>|    8    |<br>|-------|<br>| C  A  B |</insert> 8 times A is proportional between 64 and the square of A—as demonstrated in the second [book] of the commentary on Euclid—64 will be the fourth proportional quantity with those three products. Wherefore 64 is to the square of A as the square of A is to the square of B squared <note>Literally "duplicata," meaning the ratio is squared.</note>. Therefore, 8 is to A as A is to B squared, from the 17th [proposition] of the 6th [book] of the Elements; for each is the mean of the proportions of their own squares. Wherefore the <term>cube</term><note>original: "cubus"; the third power.</note> of A is equal to the product of 8 times the square of B; for this was demonstrated in the seventh book. Wherefore we will set A as a square, its cube will be the cube of the square of the root of A, which we call *c*. Therefore, the square of B is equal to 1/8 of the square of the cube of *c*. Therefore B is the root of 1/8 of the square of the cube of *c*. Since the root of the cube of the square is a cube, B will be equal to the cube of *c* times the root of 1/8. And since A is the square of *c*, then 1 square plus the cube times the root of 1/8 will be equal to 8. And so, by multiplying everything by the root of 8, the cube plus the square times the root of 8 will be equal to the root of 512. Solve this, therefore, by the 15th chapter, as in the case of <term>rational numbers</term><note>Numbers that can be expressed as a fraction or whole number.</note>, by working through the rules of the third book.

# GEROLAMO CARDANO

<margin>
| A B C D |
-----------
</margin>
BC to AB, from the supposition of the question, and BC to AB as C is to B, from the 12th [proposition] of the fifth [book] of the <term>Elements</term><note>Euclid's *Elements*, the standard geometry textbook of the time.</note>, it will be that ABC to BC is as B to C from the 11th [proposition] of the same book. But since from the proportion in B comes C, therefore from the proportion in BC comes ABC. Let it be, therefore, that from the proportion in C comes D. Since, therefore, from the proportion in B comes C, and from the same [proportion] in C comes D, therefore from the proportion in BC comes CD; and from the same [proportion] in BC there also came ABC. Therefore, ABC is equal to CD. By taking away C, there remains AB equal to D. But D is the fourth <term>proportional quantity</term><note>The fourth term in a geometric progression: $a, ar, ar^2, ar^3$.</note>. Therefore, it will be necessary to find four quantities in <term>continuous proportion</term><note>A sequence where the ratio between successive terms is constant ($a, ar, ar^2, ar^3$).</note>, of which the fourth is equal to the first two [combined]. Therefore, by setting the 1st as 1, the 2nd as 1 <term>thing</term><note>original: "re"; the unknown variable $x$.</note>, the 3rd as 1 square, and the 4th as 1 cube, the cube will be equal to 1 thing plus 1 ($x^3 = x + 1$). The value of the "thing," which is the ratio, is known from the [relevant] chapter. You will then divide 8 into four quantities continued under that ratio, as is taught in the sixth book; we also solved this question otherwise in the fourth book.

### PROBLEM X

<margin>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;8
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;-------
A &nbsp;&nbsp;|&nbsp;&nbsp; B &nbsp;&nbsp;|&nbsp;&nbsp; 7
---|-----|---
C &nbsp;&nbsp;|&nbsp;&nbsp; D &nbsp;&nbsp;|&nbsp;&nbsp; E
</margin>
Make two parts from 8, such that seven times the larger is the <term>proportional mean</term><note>The middle term $b$ in the proportion $a:b = b:c$.</note> between the cube of the smaller and the product of the larger by the smaller. Let A be the smaller (its cube being C) and B be the larger; let the product of B times A be E, and seven times B be D. Because, therefore, from B times A comes E, and from B times 7 comes D, A will be to 7 as E is to D. Therefore, A is to 7 as D is to C. It follows that A times C makes seven times D. But D is seven times B, therefore 49B is equal to the <term>square-square</term><note>original: "quadrato quadrati"; the fourth power $x^4$.</note> of A. Therefore, B is equal to 1/49 of the square-square of A. Since A with B is 8, and B is 1/49 of the square-square of A, then A plus 1/49 of its square-square is equal to 8. Wherefore, the "thing" plus 1/49 of the square-square equals 8 ($x + \frac{1}{49}x^4 = 8$). Therefore, the square-square plus 49 things equals 392 ($x^4 + 49x = 392$). And although there is no general chapter <note>Cardano refers to a general formula or "rule" for solving this specific type of quartic equation.</note> for this, it is nevertheless beautiful to have brought the question this far.

Sometimes the impossibility of proposed questions is also discovered in the same way, as is easy to see.

### On the Rule of Equal Position. Chapter XXXII.

This rule is more useful than <term>simple position</term><note>Setting the unknown as $x$. "Equal position" involves setting the unknowns symmetrically, such as $y+x$ and $y-x$.</note> in all questions where the parts are multiplied equally; it is otherwise where they are multiplied unequally. For in those cases, simple position is easier. For example, if I say: divide 8 into two parts, one of which multiplied into the square of the other, or into the cube, makes 20. By simple position, you will arrive at 8 squares minus 1 cube equals 20 ($8x^2 - x^3 = 20$), or at 8 cubes minus 1 square-square equals 20 ($8x^3 - x^4 = 20$) in the second question. But by putting <insert>the parts as</insert> 4 plus 1 position and 4 minus 1 positio- <note>The text cuts off as he begins to demonstrate the substitution $4+x$ and $4-x$.</note>

# ON ARITHMETIC, BOOK X.

By position, you will arrive at **16 things plus 44, equal to 1 cube plus 4 squares**, and in the second question, at **128 things plus 236, equal to 1 squared-square plus 8 cubes**; it is obvious, therefore, how much more difficult these are than the previous ones. Even in a simple position, we find by the first operation the estimation of the thing in an equal difference, which, when added to and subtracted from half of the number to be divided, shows the sought-after numbers, which are truly the estimation of the thing—even though we set the "thing" as the difference. I call it "simple position" when I say, "divide 10 into two parts producing 20," and then we set one part as the thing <note>original: "rem"; the unknown variable $x$.</note> and the other as 10 minus the thing ($10 - x$). But it is an "equal position" when I set one part as 5 plus the thing ($5 + x$) and the other as 5 minus the thing ($5 - x$). Therefore, since the simple method is already known through itself, the method of equal position must be discussed through questions and examples, as its use is certainly very frequent and useful.

### QUESTION I.

<margin>
<note>Diagram of a triangle with area 3 and side labels. These labels represent the lengths of the sides of a triangle where the middle side is the unknown.</note>
**Left side:** Root of the Root of 52 plus 2, minus 1 <note>Calculated as $\sqrt{\sqrt{52}+2}-1$</note>
**Top side:** Root of the Root of 52 plus 2 <note>This is the middle side $x$</note>
**Right side:** Root of the Root of 52 plus 2, plus 1 <note>Calculated as $\sqrt{\sqrt{52}+2}+1$</note>
**Inside (Area):** 3
</margin>

There is a triangle whose difference of the first side to the second is 1, and again of the second to the third is also 1, and the area is 3. Therefore, you set the second side as the position <note>The variable $x$</note>, and the first will be the position minus 1 ($x - 1$), and the third the position plus 1 ($x + 1$). Follow the rule for triangles given in the following book <note>This likely refers to a formula for area based on sides, similar to Heron's formula.</note>, and it will become the universal Root of $\frac{3}{16}$ of a squared-square <note>A term for $x^4$.</note> minus $\frac{3}{4}$ of a square, equal to 3. Therefore, $\frac{3}{16}$ of a squared-square will be equal to $\frac{3}{4}$ of a square plus 9. Thus, 1 squared-square is equal to 4 squares plus 48. By the chapter on derivative cases, the thing <note>The value of $x$.</note> will be the universal Root of the Root of 52 plus 2; and this is the second side. Therefore, add and subtract 1, and you have the remaining sides, as you see in the figure.

### QUESTION II.

Divide 10 into two parts, whose cubes joined with their squares make 400. You will set the first

| | | |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| part as 5 plus 1 thing, | 5 + 1 thing | 25 + 1 square + 10 things |
| and the second | | 125 + 15 squares + 75 things + 1 cube |
| part as 5 minus 1 thing; | 5 - 1 thing | 25 + 1 square - 10 things |
| follow the problem | | 125 + 15 squares - 75 things - 1 cube |
| by reducing the | | --- |
| parts to cubes | | 300 + 32 squares equal to 400 |
| and to squares. | | |

You will finally collect—with the [opposing] parts falling away in turn <note>The $x^3$ and $x$ terms cancel out because of the symmetry of $(5+x)$ and $(5-x)$.</note>—32 squares plus 300 equal to 400. Therefore, a square will be equal to $3 \frac{1}{8}$, and the thing, which is the difference, will be the Root of $3 \frac{1}{8}$. Thus the parts are 5 plus the Root of $3 \frac{1}{8}$ and 5 minus the Root of $3 \frac{1}{8}$.

# THE WORKS OF GIROLAMO CARDANO

## QUESTION III.

Divide 6 into two parts, the sum of whose squares is equal to the difference of their cubes. Set the larger part as 3 plus 1 position <note>original: "1 positione"; the unknown variable $x$.</note>, and the smaller as 3 minus 1 position. Follow the logic of the question: you will have the sum of the squares as **2 squares plus 18**, and the difference of the cubes as **2 cubes plus 54 positions**. These are equal to each other; therefore, 1 cube and 27 positions are equal to 1 square and 9. Follow the appropriate chapter <note>Cardano refers to his earlier chapters in *Ars Magna* that provide formulas for specific types of cubic equations.</note>, and the value of the thing <note>The "thing" (res) is the value of $x$.</note>—that is, the difference—will be: the universal cube root of the square root of 702 1/3 plus 1/27, minus the universal cube root of the square root of 702 1/3 minus 1/27, plus 1/3. <note>In modern notation: $x = \sqrt[3]{\sqrt{702 \frac{1}{3}} + \frac{1}{27}} - \sqrt[3]{\sqrt{702 \frac{1}{3}} - \frac{1}{27}} + \frac{1}{3}$.</note> Therefore, the parts will be: for the larger, 3 1/3 plus [the result of the cubic roots], and for the smaller, 2 2/3 plus [the adjusted cubic roots].

| | |
|:---|:---|
| 3 plus 1 position | 9 plus 1 square plus 6 things |
| 3 minus 1 position | 9 plus 1 square minus 6 things |
| | **18 plus 2 squares (the sum)** |
| | |
| 3 plus 1 position | 27 plus 9 squares plus 27 positions plus 1 cube |
| 3 minus 1 position | 27 plus 9 squares minus 27 positions minus 1 cube |
| | **54 positions plus 2 cubes (the difference)** |

## QUESTION IV.

Divide 8 into two parts, such that the product of the larger and the smaller is a mean proportional between nine times the larger and the smaller itself. <note>This means $9 \times \text{larger} : \text{product} = \text{product} : \text{smaller}$.</note> Set the first part as 4 plus 1 position, and the smaller as 4 minus 1 position. Follow the proposal: you will find the product of the larger and 9 to be 36 plus 9 positions; the product of the larger and smaller to be 16 minus 1 square; and the smaller to be 4 minus 1 position. Since these are proportional, the product of (36 plus 9 positions) and (4 minus 1 position) is equal to the square of (16 minus 1 square). Multiply (36 plus 9 positions) by (4 minus 1 position); the "positions" cancel out due to the mutual proportion, leaving a product of 144 minus 9 squares. This is equal to the square of 16 minus 1 square, which is 256 plus 1 squared-square <note>term: <term>squared-square</term> (quadratum quadratum): the fourth power of the unknown, or $x^4$.</term> minus 32 squares. By moving the minus terms to the opposite side, 112 plus 1 squared-square will equal 23 squares. You will find the value of the thing to be the universal root of 11 1/2 minus the square root of 20 1/4, which is the square root of 7. Add and subtract this from 4, and the sought-after parts will be **4 plus the square root of 7** and **4 minus the square root of 7**. Although you could have solved this by simple position <note>Setting the parts as $x$ and $8 - x$.</note>, you would have arrived at a chapter involving cubes and things equal to squares and numbers; the labor would have been incomparably more difficult—indeed, you would need more than ten additional operations before reaching the true value, which is always in the nature of a true Binomial or a true Recisum <note>term: <term>Recisum</term>: A mathematical term used by Cardano to describe a "cut-off" or conjugate surd, such as $a - \sqrt{b}$.</term>, not an improper one.

| | | |
|:---|:---|:---|
| 9 | | 4 minus 1 position |
| 4 plus 1 position | | |
| **36 plus 9 positions** | **16 minus 1 square** | **4 minus 1 position** |
| | 256 plus 1 squared-square minus 32 squares | |
| | 144 minus 9 squares | |
| | **112 plus 1 squared-square equals 23 squares** | |

<meta>This page continues the application of the "Rule of Equal Position," a technique where Cardano simplifies complex algebraic problems by defining two unknown parts as being equally distant from a central value (for example, if the total is 10, the parts are $5 + x$ and $5 - x$). This often allows terms to cancel out during calculation.</meta>

On Arithmetic, Book 10.

### ->Question V.<-

Divide 10 into two parts, such that when the square of the first is subtracted from 100, and the square of the second is subtracted from 97, the <term>universal roots</term> <note>original: "R v." or "radix universalis"; this refers to the square root of an entire resulting expression.</note> of the remainders, when added together, equal 17. If you wish to avoid labor, you will first see by the <term>method of trial</term> <note>original: "via tentatiua"; a preliminary check using simple numbers to see if the problem is solvable.</note> whether the case is possible. Having recognized this, set the first part as 5 plus 1 <term>position</term> <note>original: "positione"; the unknown variable $x$.</note>, and the remaining part as 5 minus 1 position. Multiply the parts by themselves <note>i.e., square them.</note>, and subtract the larger square from 100 and the smaller from 97. You will have the remainders as shown in the figure, the roots of which, when joined, ought to equal 17. Therefore, 17 minus one of those roots equals the other. Because of this, we will multiply 17 minus the universal root of 75 minus 1 square minus 10 positions by itself, and we will have 364 minus 1 square minus 10 positions minus the universal root of 86700 minus 1156 squares minus 11560 positions, equal to the square of the other root, namely 72 minus 1 square plus 10 positions. Cast out similar terms from each side, and place the universal root alone on the opposite side of everything else, as we taught in the fourth book; and as in the fifth, you will have 292 minus 20 <term>things</term> <note>original: "rebus"; another term for the unknown $x$.</note> equal to the universal root of 86700 minus 1156 squares minus 11560 positions. Wherefore, by squaring the parts again, you will have 86700 minus 1156 squares minus 11560 positions equal to 85264 plus 400 squares minus 11680 positions. Lead this to an equation by reducing it to 1 square; you will find the <term>value of the thing</term> <note>original: "rei æstimationē"; the solution for $x$.</note> to be the root of 139876/151321 plus 15/389. But the root of 139876/151321 is 374/389; therefore, when 15/389 is added, it becomes 389/389. Thus, the thing is 1, and the parts are 4 and 6.

### ->Question VI.<-

There is also a case where the equal position does not entirely solve the question, and a simple position solves it. For example: from 8 make two parts, the square of the larger of which is a <term>mean proportional</term> <note>In a sequence $a, b, c$, $b$ is the mean proportional if $a/b = b/c$. Here, the sequence is (product of parts), (square of larger part), (60).</note> between the product of the larger and the smaller, and ten times the whole—namely 60 <note>Cardano states "ten times the whole, namely 60," though ten times 8 would be 80. He likely adjusted the constant to 60 for the sake of this specific demonstration.</note>. Therefore, having set the larger as 1 position, you will have 60, and 1 square, and 8 positions minus 1 square as the proportionals. Therefore, the middle term being multiplied by itself:

| |
|:---|
| 60 \| 1 square \| 8 positions minus 1 square |
| 1 squared-square <note>original: "1 q̃d. q̃."; $x^4$.</note> equals 480 positions minus |
| 60 squares |

->On the rule of unequal positioning, or proportion.<-
->Chapter XXXIII.<-

**T**his rule teaches us that, having set unequal numbers, we should attach equal positions in such a way that, in the multiplication, the like parts cancel each other out in turn. I will teach this through examples, although the questions solved by this could also be resolved by the rule of working backward from the position, which was discussed in Chapter Five.

->QUESTION I.<-

Example. There are two numbers whose difference is 4, and the square of the smaller with the square of half the larger, plus the square root <note>original: "Rz"; abbreviation for *radix*.</note> of the sum of those same squares, makes 110. You could say by working backward: therefore 110 is composed of the sum of the squares and the root of that sum. Thus, if the sum is set as a square, 110 will be equal to the square plus one thing. Therefore the thing is 10, and the sum is 100. Then you make two parts out of 100 such that twice the root of one exceeds the other root by 4, and the solution is clear. But in this method, we will set it thus: let the first, smaller number be 2 positions; because the part <note>referring to the half mentioned in the problem</note> is 1/2, the larger will be 2 positions plus 4. Then take the part

<margin>
| | |
|---|---|
| 2 pos. | 2 pos. plus 4 |
| 2 pos. | 1 pos. plus 2 |
| | 1/2 --- 1/4 ) 5 |
| | 1/2 = 4/4 + 1/4 = 5/4 |
| 1/2 | |
| 1/2 $\times$ 4/5 = 8/10 | |
| 2 pos. minus 4/5 | |
| 1 pos. plus 8/5 | |
| --- | --- |
| 4 sq. plus 16/25 minus 16/5 pos. | |
| 1 sq. plus 2 14/25 plus 16/5 pos. | |
| 5 sq. plus 3 1/5 | |
</margin>

of the second, which is to be multiplied by itself, and it is 1/2. Therefore the part to be multiplied will be 1 position plus 2, and the first number, as was said, is 2 positions. This being held, the goal is—without changing the nature of the question—to fit the parts of the number with the things in such a way that the things cancel out entirely in the squares. You will do it like this: consider the second number to be multiplied by itself, and what part it is of the first; as in the example, 1 position plus 2, which part is it of 2 positions? You will find that it is 1/2 to 2 <note>Cardano is comparing the coefficients of the "positions" (1 and 2).</note>. Therefore, multiply the denominator and the numerator of the fraction by themselves and join the products, and you will have 5 for a divisor. Then multiply the numerator by itself, and the product by the number of the difference, which is 4; that also makes 4, for the dividend. Divide therefore—

# ON ARITHMETIC BOOK X.

...multiplied 4 by 5, the result is $\frac{4}{5}$. You will subtract this from 2 <term>positions</term> <note>original: "positionibus"; the algebraic unknown, similar to our $x$.</note>, namely the larger part, and you will have 2 positions minus $\frac{4}{5}$. Then divide $\frac{4}{5}$ by the $\frac{1}{2}$ part; the result is $\frac{8}{5}$. Add this to 1 position, and you will have 1 position plus $\frac{8}{5}$. Now you see, since you have 2 positions minus $\frac{4}{5}$ and 1 position plus $\frac{8}{5}$, the proportion of $\frac{4}{5}$ to $\frac{8}{5}$ is as 2 positions to 1 position. And if you take double the larger—namely 2 positions plus $3 \frac{1}{5}$—it will exceed the smaller—namely 2 positions minus $\frac{4}{5}$—by exactly 4. This being done, according to the universal rule, multiply the parts by themselves. You will have 4 <term>squares</term> <note>original: "quadrata"; $x^2$.</note> plus $\frac{16}{25}$ minus $\frac{16}{5}$ positions, and 1 square plus $2 \frac{14}{25}$ plus $\frac{16}{5}$ positions. Join them, and you will have 5 squares plus $3 \frac{1}{5}$. These, with their root, are equal to 110. Therefore, the <term>root</term> <note>original: "Rz"; Radix.</note> is equal to 110 minus this aggregate. Thus, $106 \frac{4}{5}$ minus 5 squares is equal to the <term>universal root</term> <note>original: "Rz v:"; Radix Universalis, meaning the square root of the entire following expression.</note> of 5 squares plus $3 \frac{1}{5}$. Multiply the parts by themselves; you will have 5 squares plus $3 \frac{1}{5}$ equal to $11406 \frac{11}{25}$ plus 25 <term>squared-squares</term> <note>original: "qd'qdratis"; the fourth power of the unknown, $x^4$.</note> minus 1068 squares. Give what must be given to the other side <note>i.e., rearrange the equation to isolate the terms.</note> and divide by the number of squared-squares, which is 25. You will have 1 squared-square plus $456 \frac{76}{625}$ equal to $42 \frac{23}{25}$ squares. Therefore, the <term>thing</term> <note>original: "res"; the value of $x$.</note> is worth the universal root of $21 \frac{23}{50}$ minus the root of $4 \frac{1025}{2500}$. But the root of $4 \frac{1025}{2500}$ is $2 \frac{1}{10}$. Therefore, the thing is the root of $19 \frac{36}{100}$. But this is $4 \frac{2}{5}$. Therefore, the thing was $4 \frac{2}{5}$. But the first or larger part was 2 positions minus $\frac{4}{5}$; therefore it was 8. And the smaller was 1 position plus $\frac{8}{5}$; therefore it was 6. And double the latter was 12, which exceeds 8 by 4, and this is what we wanted.

### QUESTION II.

There is a <term>triangle</term> <note>original: "trigonus".</note> $ABC$, whose base $AB$ is 8 plus the <term>altitude</term> <note>original: "catheto"; the perpendicular height.</note> $CD$, and $AD$ is triple $DB$. The square of $BC$ plus the side $CB$ is 182. Therefore, setting $CD$ as the thing <note>original: "re"; the unknown.</note>, and $AB$ as the thing plus 8; or rather, setting $CD$ as 4 positions and $AB$ as 4 positions plus 8, then $BD$ will be 1 position plus 2, and the proportion is $\frac{1}{4}$. Therefore, as before, multiply 4 by itself, which makes 16; multiply 1 by itself, which makes 1. Join them; it makes 17, which is the divisor. Then multiply 1 (the numerator of $\frac{1}{4}$) by itself, which makes 1. Multiply by the difference, 8, which makes 8. Divide by 17; the result is $\frac{8}{17}$, the part to be subtracted from 4 positions. Then divide $\frac{8}{17}$ by the proportion, which is $\frac{1}{4}$; the result is $\frac{32}{17}$, the part to be added to one thing. Thus, $BD$ will be 1 position plus $\frac{32}{17}$, and $CD$ will be 4 positions minus $\frac{8}{17}$. Multiply the parts by themselves, and you will have the squares of $CD$ and $BD$ taken together, and consequently, the square of $BC$ is 17 squares plus $3 \frac{221}{289}$. Proceed as in the previous case: by adding to it the side $BC$, the universal root of 17 squares plus $3 \frac{221}{289}$ plus 17 squares plus $3 \frac{221}{289}$ will be equal to 182. Wherefore, $178 \frac{68}{289}$ minus 17 squares is equal to the universal root of 17 squares plus $3 \frac{221}{289}$. Follow the operation, therefore, as before; you will find the value of the thing to be $3 \frac{1}{17}$. Since $BD$ is 1 position plus $\frac{32}{17}$, $BD$ will be 5 and...

|---|---|
| 4 positions | 1 position plus 2 |
| | $\frac{1}{4}$ --- 16 ) 17 |
| | $\frac{1}{4}$ --- 1 --- 8 --- $\frac{8}{17}$ |
| | $\frac{8}{17} \times \frac{4}{1} \rightarrow \frac{32}{17}$ |
| 4 positions minus $\frac{8}{17}$ | 1 position plus $\frac{32}{17}$ |
| 16 squares plus $\frac{64}{289}$ minus $\frac{64}{17}$ positions | 1 square plus $\frac{1024}{289}$ plus $\frac{64}{17}$ positions |
| **17 squares plus $3 \frac{221}{289}$** | |

# OF GIROLAMO CARDANO

### ->QUESTION III.<-

<margin>
10 pos. --- 3/10 \ 9/100 ) 109
3 pos. --- 3/10 — 9 — 12 — 108
/ 109
$\frac{108}{109}$ X $\frac{10}{3}$ $\frac{360}{109}$
3 pos. <term>plus</term>: $\frac{360}{109}$
10 pos. <term>minus</term>: $\frac{108}{109}$

And similarly, if one were to say: there are two numbers whose difference is 12, and the <term>square</term> <note>original: "quadratum"</note> of the smaller with the <term>square</term> of 3/10 of the larger, and the <term>square</term> of the sum, is equal to 1000; then you will operate as before. By multiplying the numerator and denominator by themselves <note>i.e., squaring them</note> and joining them, the divisor becomes 109 <note>The sum of $10^2$ and $3^2$</note>. Next, I multiply the numerator 3 by itself, and the product by 12, which makes 108. I divide this by 109, and I have the part to be subtracted from 10 <term>positions</term> <note>original: "positionibus"; the unknown variables, or $x$.</note>. Then I divide $\frac{108}{109}$ by $\frac{3}{10}$, which results in $\frac{360}{109}$, the part to be added to 3 <term>positions</term>. If, therefore, 3 <term>positions</term> <term>plus</term> <note>original: "p:"; abbreviation for plus.</note> $\frac{360}{109}$ are multiplied by $\frac{3}{10}$, the number produced will be 12 <term>plus</term> than 10 <term>things</term> <note>original: "res"; another term for the algebraic unknown.</note> <term>minus</term> $\frac{108}{109}$. And such is the proportion of $\frac{360}{109}$ to $\frac{108}{109}$ as 10 is to 3. And thus, because these rules have infinite modes—as if we were to say: 1/2 of the first and 1/3 of the second number, differing by 12, multiplied by themselves with the <term>root</term> <note>original: "radice"; square root.</note> added, make 100—then you would seek its rule in the same way, through the <term>rule of manner</term> <note>original: "regulam de modo"</note>. For this rule is a branch of that one, by seeking numbers differing first by 12, whose 1/2 of one is so divided into 1/2 of a "thing" and a number, and the remainder into 1/3 of a "thing" and a number, so that the products of the "things" are equal. By setting one number as <term>plus</term> and the other as <term>minus</term>, it is found through Chapter 9, involving a <term>surd quantity</term> <note>An irrational number, like a square root that cannot be simplified to a whole number.</note>, as in such cases. I will set down the rule joined to an example; I say that if someone says:

Find two numbers whose difference is 14, and 1/3 of one multiplied by itself <note>squared</note>, with 1/4 of the other multiplied by itself, and with the <term>root</term> <note>original: "Rz"; Radix.</note> of the sum of such products, becomes 110. I say first: multiply the denominators by the numerators alternately, namely 4 by 1 and 3 by 1; and of the products, which are also 4 and 3, join the <term>squares</term>, and you will have 25 for the divisor. Then multiply the denominators by each other, 3 by 4 makes 12; and what results [multiply] by the difference which was 14, which makes 168. This you shall multiply by the product of the numerators, which was 1, so it is also 168 for the dividend. Therefore, divide 168 by 25, which results in $\frac{168}{25}$. Multiply this by the parts themselves, namely 1/3 and 1/4; you will have $2 \frac{6}{25}$ to be added, and $1 \frac{17}{25}$ to be subtracted, because always, as has been said, the smaller part of the number is subtracted from the larger, and the larger is added

## On the rule of the middle. Chapter XXXIIII.

<margin>1</margin>
**T**his is so called by me because the middle is sought, namely the proportion; and because to avoid the confusion of unity, we set one part as half of a unit, and its use is only for seeking quantities which are multiplied equally and preserve a proportion; when however they do not preserve it, the use of the rule is not helpful; truly it is explained in two quantities only, but we shall speak of more in chapter 39. It is clear, however, that if someone were to say: find two numbers whose squares joined are 10, and whose cubes joined are 30, that this rule will not serve, because the proportion of 30 to 10, which is triple, is not preserved between the cubes and the squares.

<meta>catchword: various</meta>

Therefore the quantities are in the proportion of 1 <term>plus</term> the square root of $\frac{3}{4}$, and $\frac{1}{2}$; which is to say in the proportion of 2 <term>plus</term> the square root of 3, and 1. Again, therefore, let us seek two quantities in this proportion, whose sum multiplied by the sum of the squares makes 20 (for such will necessarily also possess the other condition). <note>The term "qd-qd" or "quadrato-quadratum" refers to the fourth power ($x^4$). Cardano is setting up a ratio between the product of the sums and the product of the differences (30:10, or 3:1).</note>

You proceed as in the preceding [question], but you will set the parts as 1 <term>position</term> and 1 for greater ease, and you will follow as in the preceding until you come to 1 <term>quadrato-quadratum</term> <term>plus</term> 1 equal to 2 <term>cubes</term> <term>plus</term> 2 <term>positions</term>. Therefore, I have five quantities in continuous proportion, of which the sum of the first and fifth is double the sum of the second and fourth. Therefore, by the chapter on five quantities in continuous proportion, I seek the proportion by assuming, for example, 2 and 4 (of which 4 is double the other), and making 4 the first and fifth, and 2 the second and fourth. Therefore, such a proportion will be as $1/2$ <term>plus</term> root of $3/4$ <term>plus</term> universal root of (root of $6\frac{3}{4}$ <term>minus</term> $2\frac{1}{4}$) <term>plus</term> universal root of ($3/4$ <term>minus</term> $3/4$) <note>The root of zero here suggests a simplification or a specific case in Cardano's proportional rules.</note> to one. 

You will then again place the "things" under these numbers, namely 1 <term>thing</term>, and the "things" $1/2$ <term>plus</term> root of $3/4$ <term>plus</term> universal root of (root of $6\frac{3}{4}$ <term>minus</term> $2\frac{1}{4}$) <term>plus</term> universal root of ($3/4$ <term>minus</term> $3/4$). Then multiply to the cube by the rules of the third book (which will not be difficult), then multiply the <term>thing</term> $1/2$ <term>plus</term> root of $3/4$ [etc.], namely the difference of the numbers, into the difference of the cubes, which is obtained by subtracting 1 cube from the cube of the said [expression] composed of four names <note>A "name" refers to a term in a polynomial; thus, an expression with four terms.</note>, and the product will equal 10. Divide 10 by such a product, and the <term>root of the root</term> <note>original: "℞ ℞"; the fourth root.</note> of the result will be the value of the first quantity. When this is multiplied by $1/2$ <term>plus</term> root of $3/4$ <term>plus</term> universal root of (root of $6\frac{3}{4}$ <term>minus</term> $2\frac{1}{4}$) <term>plus</term> universal root of ($3/4$ <term>minus</term> $3/4$), the second quantity, or second number, will arise.

...the sum of the cubes multiplied by the sum of the squares makes 25, you set out the parts as in the preceding [question], 1 <term>position</term> <note>original: "positionem"; the unknown $x$.</note> and 1; and their first <term>relata</term> <note>original: "relati primi"; the fifth powers, $x^5$ and $1^5$.</note> are 1 <term>1st relatum</term> and 1, and the product of the sum of the squares times the sum of the cubes is: 1 <term>1st relatum</term> <term>plus</term> 1 <term>cube</term> <term>plus</term> 1 <term>square</term> <term>plus</term> 1. And this relates to 1 <term>1st relatum</term> <term>plus</term> 1 as 25 relates to 20, which is as 5 is to 4. Therefore, by the rule of proportional quantities, by multiplying 1 <term>1st relatum</term> <term>plus</term> 1 <term>cube</term> <term>plus</term> 1 <term>square</term> <term>plus</term> 1 by 4, we make as much as by multiplying 1 <term>1st relatum</term> <term>plus</term> 1 by 5. Thus, 4 <term>1st relata</term> <term>plus</term> 4 <term>cubes</term> <term>plus</term> 4 <term>squares</term> <term>plus</term> 4 are equal to 5 <term>1st relata</term> <term>plus</term> 5. Wherefore, finally, we will have 1 <term>1st relatum</term> <term>plus</term> 1, after the subtraction is made, equal to 4 <term>cubes</term> <term>plus</term> 4 <term>squares</term>. 

Divide the parts by <term>position</term> <term>plus</term> 1: [yielding] 1 <term>squared-square</term> <note>original: "qd'qdratum"; the fourth power, $x^4$.</note> <term>minus</term> 1 <term>cube</term> <term>plus</term> 1 <term>square</term> <term>minus</term> 1 <term>position</term> <term>plus</term> 1, equal to 4 <term>squares</term>. Therefore, 1 <term>squared-square</term> <term>plus</term> 1 is equal to 1 <term>cube</term> <term>plus</term> 3 <term>squares</term> <term>plus</term> 1 <term>position</term>. There are, therefore, five quantities in continuous proportion, the sum of the first and fifth of which is, for the sake of example, 10, and the sum of the second and fourth with triple the third is also 10. Therefore the proportion will be known through the chapter on five quantities in continuous proportion, and it will be: the root of $3 \frac{2}{7}$ <term>minus</term> 1 <term>plus</term> the universal root of the root of $1 \frac{2}{7}$ <term>minus</term> $\frac{6}{7}$. 

And this is the proportion of those quantities. In the second <term>position</term>, therefore, you will place 1 <term>thing</term>, and the <term>thing</term> in the above-mentioned number or proportion, or the reduced proportion as in the preceding question. Once the division is made by the numerator, lead it to the <term>relatum</term> by its own rule, to which add 1 (the first <term>relatum</term> of 1), and with that sum divide 20. The [first] <term>relata</term> root of the result is the smaller number; then multiply it by the proportion, and the larger will come forth. To complete such an operation is a matter almost beyond human labor, and if there were not the rules of the third book, it would be scarcely possible at all.

## On the rule of the sum. Chapter XXXV.

### RULE I.

Just as from the preceding [chapter] and the iterated rule the proportion itself is sought, so through this [rule] we have the sum. It is, moreover, very useful where no proportion is assumed between the parts. For the medi...

# ON ARITHMETIC BOOK X.

The means for seeking several numbers simultaneously is either proportion, the sum, or the difference. Therefore, since from the preceding [chapter] and the repeated rule proportion is obtained, and with this [rule] the sum and the difference, it is clear enough by how great an interval this rule precedes those others. We have also called this the "Rule of Two" <note>original: "regulam dupli"</note>, because it contains two parts, or two sought numbers; its reasoning, as with the rest, is clear through examples.

### QUESTION I.

Find two numbers whose squares joined together are 20, and the product of one by the other, together with the numbers themselves, is 10. I say that although it can be solved from the sixth book, we shall do it thus by the rule. Set the sum to be 1 <term>position</term> <note>original: "positionem"; the unknown $x$. Cardano uses this to represent the sum of the two numbers.</note>, or "thing." And because the product of one by the other [plus the sum] makes 10, the product itself must be 10 minus the sum; therefore, the product of one by the other will be 10 minus 1 <term>position</term>. Now divide the <term>position</term> <note>the sum</note> into two parts that produce 10 minus 1 <term>position</term> [when multiplied]. According to the rule in the chapter on operations in the sixth book, the parts will be:

| $\frac{1}{2}$ <term>pos.</term> <term>plus</term> <term>univ. root</term>: $\frac{1}{4}$ <term>sq.</term> <term>plus</term> 1 <term>pos.</term> <term>minus</term> 10 | $\frac{1}{4}$ <term>sq.</term> <term>plus</term> $\frac{1}{4}$ <term>sq.</term> <term>plus</term> 1 <term>pos.</term> <term>minus</term> 10 |
| $\frac{1}{4}$ <term>sq.</term> <term>plus</term> $\frac{1}{4}$ <term>sq.</term> <term>plus</term> 1 <term>pos.</term> <term>minus</term> 10 | $\frac{1}{4}$ <term>sq.</term> <term>plus</term> $\frac{1}{4}$ <term>sq.</term> <term>plus</term> 1 <term>pos.</term> <term>minus</term> 10 |
| $\frac{1}{2}$ <term>pos.</term> <term>minus</term> <term>univ. root</term>: $\frac{1}{4}$ <term>sq.</term> <term>plus</term> 1 <term>pos.</term> <term>minus</term> 10 | $\frac{1}{4}$ <term>sq.</term> <term>plus</term> $\frac{1}{4}$ <term>sq.</term> <term>plus</term> 1 <term>pos.</term> <term>minus</term> 10 |

| $\frac{1}{4}$ <term>sq.</term> <term>plus</term> $\frac{1}{4}$ <term>sq.</term> <term>plus</term> 1 <term>pos.</term> <term>minus</term> 10 | $\frac{1}{4}$ <term>sq.</term> <term>plus</term> $\frac{1}{4}$ <term>sq.</term> <term>plus</term> 1 <term>pos.</term> <term>minus</term> 10 |

| | 1 <term>sq.</term> <term>plus</term> 2 <term>pos.</term> <term>minus</term> 20 |
</insert>

the parts are $1/2$ <term>position</term> <term>plus</term> the <term>universal root</term> <note>original: "Rv" or "Radix Universalis"; this refers to taking the square root of the entire subsequent expression.</note> of $1/4$ <term>square</term> <term>plus</term> 1 <term>position</term> <term>minus</term> 10, and $1/2$ <term>position</term> <term>minus</term> the <term>universal root</term> of $1/4$ <term>square</term> <term>plus</term> 1 <term>position</term> <term>minus</term> 10. The squares of these joined together must be 20. And because one part is a <term>binomial</term> and the other a <term>recisum</term> <note>A "recisum" (literally "cut off") is the conjugate of a binomial, e.g., $a - \sqrt{b}$ vs $a + \sqrt{b}$.</note> with respect to $1/2$ <term>position</term>, it will suffice to multiply the parts by themselves, not one by the other, as we taught in the 3rd, 4th, and 5th books. Therefore $1/2$ <term>position</term> multiplied by itself becomes $1/4$ <term>square</term>, and the <term>universal root</term> of $1/4$ <term>square</term> plus 1 <term>position</term> minus 10 multiplied by itself becomes $1/4$ <term>square</term> plus 1 <term>position</term> minus 10; and just as much from the other part, as in the figure. Wherefore the squares of the <term>binomial</term> and the <term>recisum</term> joined together are 1 <term>square</term> <term>plus</term> 2 <term>positions</term> <term>minus</term> 20. And this is equal to 20, as was said; therefore 1 <term>square</term> <term>plus</term> 2 <term>positions</term> is equal to 40. The estimate of the thing <note>The value of $x$, representing the sum of the two numbers.</note> will be the <term>root</term> of 41 <term>minus</term> 1. Divide the <term>root</term> of 41 <term>minus</term> 1 into two parts whose squares joined together are 20, and they will be, through a new position or through the rules of the chapter on operations in the sixth book, the parts which you see to the side.

### QUESTION II.

Find two numbers which, when joined together, make as much as they do when multiplied together, and their squares joined together are 20. If, therefore, the sum is 1 <term>position</term>, the product of one by the other is also 1 <term>position</term>. Divide 1 <term>position</term> into two parts producing 1 <term>position</term> [when multiplied], through the rules of the chapter on operations placed in the sixth book, or through the fifth [proposition] of the second book of the *Elements* <note>Referring to Euclid's Elements, Book II, Prop. 5, which Cardano frequently uses as a geometric basis for solving quadratic-style divisions of a line.</note>. And the parts will be those I have placed to the side, of which the squares joined together...

## QUESTION III.

Find two numbers whose multiplication produces their sum, and the squares of these numbers together with the numbers themselves make 20. Proceed as in the preceding [question], and you will have the sum [as] <term>Root</term> of $20 \frac{1}{4}$ <term>plus</term> $\frac{1}{2}$, which is 5. And because the squares of the parts together with the numbers themselves must equal 20, therefore the squares alone without the numbers will be 15. Make, therefore, two parts from 5 whose squares joined together are 15, and you will have the numbers which you see.

<margin>
$2 \frac{1}{2}$ <term>plus</term> <term>Root</term> $1 \frac{1}{4}$
$2 \frac{1}{2}$ <term>minus</term> <term>Root</term> $1 \frac{1}{4}$
</margin>

You should remember, however, that in the first operation, when you come to 1 <term>square</term> minus 2 <term>positions</term> for the sum of the squares, you should add 1 <term>position</term> (which is the sum of the numbers), and you will arrive at 1 <term>square</term> minus 1 <term>position</term> equal to 20.

## QUESTION IIII.

Find two numbers which, when multiplied by each other, produce their sum; and if 12 is divided by each of them, the squares of the resulting values joined with the sum of the dividers make 80. This [problem], along with the preceding ones, belongs to <term>Brother Luca</term> <note>Luca Pacioli (c. 1447–1517), a seminal figure in mathematics and a Franciscan friar</note>, found in a certain writing that had been lost. Set the sum to be one <term>thing</term>; divide it into parts that produce one <term>thing</term> [when multiplied], and you will have the parts, as you see, with which you divide 12, as in the figure.

### QUESTION V.

Find two numbers whose squares joined together are 20, and the product of one multiplied by the other is equal to the square of their difference. This is quite clear, since it is necessary for those numbers to be in the proportion which is said to have a <term>mean and two extremes</term> <note>This refers to the "Golden Ratio," where the ratio of the whole to the larger part is the same as the larger part to the smaller.</note>. It can also be solved by the <term>rule of equal position</term> <note>A method where the two parts are represented as $x + y$ and $x - y$ to simplify the algebra.</note>, for many questions can be solved by many and diverse rules. We shall do it from this rule thus: having set the sum as the <term>thing</term>, we will divide it into parts whose:

| | |
|:---|:---|
| squares joined are 20, and they will be, as you see: | $\frac{1}{2}$ <term>pos.</term> <term>plus</term> <term>univ. root</term> of 10 <term>minus</term> $\frac{1}{4}$ <term>sq.</term> |
| therefore the square of the difference is 40, | $\frac{1}{2}$ <term>pos.</term> <term>minus</term> <term>univ. root</term> of 10 <term>minus</term> $\frac{1}{4}$ <term>sq.</term> |
| <term>minus</term> 1 square, and this is equal to the product | Difference: <term>univ. root</term> of 40 <term>minus</term> 1 <term>sq.</term> |
| of the parts in turn, which is $\frac{1}{2}$ square | Square of the difference: 40 <term>minus</term> 1 <term>sq.</term> |
| <term>minus</term> 10, whereby $1 \frac{1}{2}$ squares, | Product: $\frac{1}{2}$ <term>sq.</term> <term>minus</term> 10 |

are equal to 50. Therefore the <term>thing</term> is the <term>Root</term> of $33 \frac{1}{3}$. From this, make two parts whose squares joined together are 20, and they will be the <term>Root</term> of $8 \frac{1}{3}$ <term>plus</term> the <term>Root</term> of $1 \frac{2}{3}$, and the <term>Root</term> of $8 \frac{1}{3}$ <term>minus</term> the <term>Root</term> of $1 \frac{2}{3}$. And from this rule, eight questions are derived, which I have called the **Sisters** because of their strong similarity, [fitting] the chapters better than others. There follow eight questions, which are called the **Sisters**, of which the last one alone is declared as an example for the others.

### QUESTION VI.

Find two numbers whose squares joined together are 10, and whose cubes are 30. You will set the sum of the numbers as a <term>position</term>, and make parts from it whose squares joined together are 10; then join the cubes of those parts, and you will have the <term>cube</term> <term>plus</term> 60, equal to 30 <term>things</term>.

### QUESTION VII.

Find two numbers whose squares joined together are 10, and the difference of their cubes is 15. Set their sum as before, as a <term>thing</term>, and you will have 1 <term>cube-square</term> <note>original: "cub'q̃dratum"; likely referring to a sixth-power equation $x^6$ resulting from the algebraic expansion</note>, equal to 300 <term>squares</term> <term>plus</term> 1100.

### QUESTION VIII.

Find two numbers whose squares joined together are 10, and from the multiplication of either of them into the square of the other, the products joined together make 20. In the same way, set the sum of the numbers as a <term>thing</term>, and you will have 1 <term>cube-square</term> <term>plus</term> 300 <term>squares</term> <term>plus</term> 800 <term>positions</term>, equal to 40 <term>square-squares</term> <note>original: "q̃d'q̃dratis"; $x^4$</note> <term>plus</term> 1600.

## QUESTION IX.
Find two numbers whose squares joined together are 10, and the products of one by the square of the other reciprocally <note>This means the product of the first number by the square of the second, and the second number by the square of the first.</note>, differ by 4. You will set, as before, the sum as the <term>thing</term> <note>original: "rem"; the unknown $x$</note>, and you will have 1 <term>cube-square</term> <note>original: "cub'qdratum"; $x^6$</note> plus 500 <term>squares</term> <note>original: "quadratis"; $x^2$</note> equal to 40 <term>square-squares</term> <note>original: "qd'qdratis"; $x^4$</note> plus 1936.

## QUESTION X.
Find two numbers whose difference of squares is 10, and the sum of their cubes is 100. You will set the sum of the numbers as the <term>thing</term>, and from it you will make parts whose squares differ by 10; you will then lead these to the cube, and you will have 1 <term>square-square</term> plus 300, equal to 400 <term>positions</term> <note>original: "positionibus"; another term for the unknown $x$.</note>.

## QUESTION XI.
Find two numbers whose difference of squares is 10, and the difference of their cubes is 100. Set the sum of the numbers as the <term>thing</term>, as before, and you will have 1 <term>square-square</term> plus 33 $\frac{1}{3}$, equal to 13 $\frac{1}{3}$ <term>cubes</term> <note>$x^3$</note>.

## QUESTION XII.
Find two numbers whose difference of squares is 10, and the sum of the products of one by the square of the other reciprocally, is 100. Set their sum as the <term>thing</term>, as before, and you will have 1 <term>square-square</term> equal to 400 <term>things</term> plus 100.

## QUESTION XIII.
Find two numbers whose difference of squares is 10, and the difference of the products of one by the squares of the other, is 100. I will explain this one carefully, so that it may be a method of operating and an exemplar for the rest—not only for the seven preceding ones, but for many others that can be formed in this class. I will therefore set their sum as the <term>thing</term>, and by the rule of method (or from the chapter on operations in the sixth book), I will make from it two parts whose difference of squares is 10. This is done by dividing that difference, namely 10, by double the dividend <note>Cardano is referring to the sum $x$ being divided into two parts.</note>, which is 2 <term>positions</term>; the result is $\frac{5}{1 \text{ pos.}}$. You will add and subtract this from half of the dividend, which is $\frac{1}{2}$ <term>position</term>. You will then have the parts and their squares, which you should place with their roots in swapped order, as shown in the figure. Then multiply the lower terms by their upper terms; in these cases where we wish to multiply the difference, it is sufficient to multiply the "dissimilar" parts—that is, those which produce a plus in one and a minus in the other—just as in additions it suffices to multiply the "similar" parts, for the remainder fall away by themselves. Therefore, multiply:

| | |
| :--- | :--- |
| $\frac{1}{2}$ <term>pos.</term> plus $\frac{5}{1 \text{ pos.}}$ | $\frac{1}{2}$ <term>pos.</term> minus $\frac{5}{1 \text{ pos.}}$ |
| $\frac{1}{4}$ <term>sq.</term> plus $\frac{25}{1 \text{ sq.}}$ minus 5 | $\frac{1}{4}$ <term>sq.</term> plus $\frac{25}{1 \text{ sq.}}$ plus 5 |
| --- | --- |
| $2 \frac{1}{2}$ <term>pos.</term> minus $1 \frac{1}{4}$ | $1 \frac{1}{4}$ <term>pos.</term> minus $2 \frac{1}{2}$ |
| <term>pos.</term> minus $\frac{125}{1 \text{ cu.}}$ | <term>pos.</term> plus $\frac{125}{1 \text{ cu.}}$ |
| --- | --- |
| The Difference: $2 \frac{1}{2}$ <term>pos.</term> minus $\frac{250}{1 \text{ cu.}}$ equals 100 | |
| 1 <term>sq-sq.</term> equals 40 <term>cubes</term> plus 100 | |

therefore $1/2$ <term>position</term> <note>original: "positione"; the unknown $x$</note> times minus 5, and $\frac{5}{\text{1 Position}}$ times $1/4$ of a <term>square</term> plus $\frac{25}{\text{1 square}}$, because where one produces minus, the other produces plus. And subtract the minus from the plus, and this is nothing other than doubling one of those products; you will have the difference of one product from the other, $2 \ 1/2$ positions minus $\frac{250}{\text{1 cube}}$. Therefore this is equal to 100. Divide everything by $2 \ 1/2$, and multiply by 1 <term>cube</term>, and you will have 1 <term>square-square</term> <note>original: "qd'qdratũ"; $x^4$</note> equal to 40 cubes plus 100, and so in other cases. You could establish a rule regarding this method, by saying: when two numbers, the difference of whose squares is formed from reciprocal multiplication into the squares, must produce some difference between those products, then the square-square will be equal to the square of the difference of the squares, and as many cubes as the number which results from dividing the number of the difference of the products by the fourth part of the difference of the squares. For example, if I say: find two numbers the difference of whose squares is 6, and the difference of the products of one by the square of the other is 60, we will say then that 1 square-square will equal 40 cubes plus 36, and so for the others.

## RULE II.

<margin>2</margin>
There is also another method for the rule of the sum, far more subtle than the preceding one, and it performs two <term>positions</term> and two conversions simultaneously. Nothing is more subtle than these in the rules. I found it in a certain fragment by Brother Luca <note>original: "fratris Luçę"; referring to Luca Pacioli (c. 1447–1517), the "Father of Accounting" and a major influence on Cardano</note>, and I finally restored it after much labor, because it could scarcely be read in that part, or the form of this rule perceived. I will explain it easily. If it were not for the fact that this method is not very general in terms of showing the <term>estimate</term> <note>original: "æstimationẽ"; the numerical solution or root</note> of the thing (though regarding the setting of the position it is most ample), nothing else more excellent could be devised. The example and the rule will be found in the questions.

## QUESTION XIV.

Find two numbers, such that from the multiplication of one by the other 8 is produced, and the squares joined with the numbers themselves make 40. You will set the sum of those numbers as $1/2$ <term>quantity</term> <note>original: "quantitatem"; Cardano uses "quantity" (quantitas) as a second type of unknown to distinguish it from "position" (positio)</note>, and one of them as 1 position. The remainder, therefore, is $1/2$ quantity minus 1 position. Multiply them together; they become $1/2$ quantity-position minus 1 square, and this is equal to 8. Therefore you have 1 square plus 8 equal to a certain quantity of <term>things</term> <note>original: "rerum"; the unknown $x$</note>. Follow the chapter, therefore: take half the number of things, that is $1/4$ of the quantity, as you are taught in the fifth chapter; when the square and the number are equal to the things, then multiply $1/4$ of the quantity by itself, which makes $1/16$ of a <term>square-quantity</term> <note>original: "qd'quan"; the square of the "quantity" variable ($y^2$)</note>. Subtract 8, the number of the equation; it becomes $1/16$ square-quantity minus 8. Take the <term>universal root</term> <note>original: "Rz v:"; Radix Universalis, meaning the square root of the entire following expression</note>, which you add to and subtract from $1/4$ of the quantity (the half of the number of things), and the estimate of the thing—or the sought-after numbers—will be produced. One of these is $1/4$ of the quantity plus the universal root of $1/16$ square-quantity minus 8, and the other is $1/4$ of the quantity minus the universal root of $1/16$ square-quantity minus 8. Therefore, the squares of these, with the sum of the numbers added—

# JEROME CARDANO

...of the numbers, that is $1/2$ of the quantity, are equal to 40; therefore, the squares of the parts—with the reciprocal multiplications of $1/4$ of the quantity by the **Universal Root of $1/16$ the square of the quantity minus 8** <note>original: "Rz v: 1/16 qd. quan: m: 8"; Cardano uses "Universal Root" (Radix Universalis) to indicate the square root applies to the entire following expression</note> canceling out, because they are equal, one minus and one plus—will be $1/8$ of the square of the quantity minus 8, and $1/8$ of the square of the quantity minus 8. Thus, joined together, they make $1/4$ of the square of the quantity minus 16. This, being equal to $1/2$ of the quantity added to the sum of the numbers reaching 40; therefore, put a <term>position</term> <note>original: "rem" or "positione"; the unknown variable, commonly referred to as 'x' today</note> for the quantity. It will be $1/4$ of a square plus $1/2$ a position, equal to 56. Therefore, 1 square plus 2 positions is equal to 224, for which the <term>value</term> <note>original: "res ualet"; the solution for x</note> is the root of 225 minus 1, which is 14. And the quantity is worth just as much. But we set the sum as half of the quantity; therefore the sum of the numbers is 7. Divide 7 into two parts which, when multiplied together, produce 8: they will be $3 \frac{1}{2}$ plus the root of $4 \frac{1}{4}$, and $3 \frac{1}{2}$ minus the root of $4 \frac{1}{4}$. These are the sought numbers, whose squares added to the numbers themselves are 40.

<margin>Note.</margin>
And if anyone should ask what benefit this rule brings, and whom it might help besides the first rule: I respond that the first rule requires a particular rule from the sixth book to operate, whereas this one acts freely all the way to the end, leading to a conclusion that is as beautiful as it is useful, requiring no outside assistance. There is also another example.

## QUESTION XV.

Find two numbers from whose multiplication 6 is produced, and whose cubes joined together make 100. We will set $1/2$ the quantity for the sum, and let one part be the "thing" <note>the unknown $x$</note>; the other will be $1/2$ the quantity minus the thing. Multiply the parts by each other, and you will have $1/2$ quantity of the position minus 1 square, equal to 6. Follow the equation as if $1/2$ the quantity were some number, and you will have the <term>valuation</term> <note>the solution</note>—specifically two valuations for the position: $1/4$ of the quantity plus the **Universal Root of $1/16$ the square of the quantity minus 6**, and $1/4$ of the quantity minus the **Universal Root of $1/16$ the square of the quantity minus 6**. The cubes of these must equal 100. Raise them to the cube, setting aside the parts that are plus in one and minus in the other; you will have $1/16$ of the cube of the quantity minus $4 \frac{1}{2}$ quantities for... <note>The calculation continues into the table below.</note>

| | $1/2$ quantity |
|---|---|
| 1 position | $1/2$ quantity minus 1 position |
| $1/2$ quantity of the position minus 1 square | |
| equals 6 | |
| $1/4$ quantity plus Universal Root of $1/16$ square of the quantity minus 6 | position |
| $1/4$ quantity minus Universal Root of $1/16$ square of the quantity minus 6 | position |
| $1/16$ cube of the quantity minus $4 \frac{1}{2}$ quantity | cube |
| $1/16$ cube of the quantity minus $4 \frac{1}{2}$ quantity | cube |
| $1/8$ cube of the quantity minus 9 quantities equals 100 | |
| 1 cube equals 72 things plus 800 | |

### QUESTION XVI.

Find two numbers whose difference of squares is 10, and from the larger of them joined with its own squares, the result is 40. Set the sum of the numbers as the <term>position</term> <note>original: "positioni"; the unknown variable</note>, and one part as $1/2$ the quantity; the remainder will be the unknown minus $1/2$ of the quantity. Multiply the parts by themselves: you will have $1/4$ the square of the quantity, and 1 square plus $1/4$ the square of the quantity minus 1 unknown quantity. Take the difference, which will be 1 unknown quantity minus 1 square, and this is equal to 10. Therefore, the value of the unknown is $1/2$ the quantity plus the **Universal Root of $1/4$ the square of the quantity minus 10**, and the quantity minus the **Universal Root of $1/4$ the square of the quantity minus 10**. Either of these is equal to 1 position. And since the position was already divided into $1/2$ the quantity and the position minus $1/2$ the quantity, therefore since $1/2$ the quantity is common to both sides, the **Universal Root of $1/4$ the square of the quantity minus 10** will be equal to 1 position minus $1/2$ the quantity. Therefore, the squares of the parts—which are $1/4$ the square of the quantity and $1/4$ the square of the quantity minus 10—joined with one of the parts, namely $1/2$ the quantity, are equal to 40. Wherefore 1 square of the quantity plus 1 quantity is equal to 100. Thus the unknown, which is the quantity, is the **Root of $100 \frac{1}{4}$ minus $1/2$**. And because we set $1/2$ the quantity [as a part], one part will be the **Root of $25 \frac{1}{16}$ minus $1/4$** (which is half of the Root of $100 \frac{1}{4}$ minus $1/2$), and the smaller part will be the **Universal Root of $15 \frac{1}{8}$ minus the Root of $6 \frac{17}{64}$**. Generally in this rule, he who is more gifted in intellect is more successful in the operation, for the methods are numerous, and to speak of them all would be tedious; let these, then, suffice, and let us pass again to the examples of the first rule, seeking in this manner:

## On the rule of free position. Chapter XXXVI.

**T**his is a rule for questions that follow the universal properties of numbers which a man might not know; searching for them through other rules, he labors in vain. For they do not require a specific proportion, nor yet can they be found in all quantities; such cases are as follows.

I will therefore place them in whatever proportion I wish, beginning from one <term>position</term>. ### Question II.

Find two numbers in a double proportion <note>a 1:2 ratio</note> whose squares, or cubes, or <term>first relatives</term> <note>original: "relati"; the fifth power or $x^5$</note> are equal to the numbers themselves. We will therefore set the numbers in a double proportion as 1 <term>position</term> <note>original: "positionem"; the unknown $x$</note> and 2 positions. Their first relatives will be 32 first relatives <note>$32x^5$</note> and 1 first relative <note>$x^5$</note>. Therefore, by the simple chapter <note>referring to a standard algebraic rule</note>, the thing will be the <term>Fourth Root</term> <note>original: "R'R'"; literally "root of roots" or $\sqrt[4]{x}$</note> of 1/11, after dividing 3 by 33. The other quantity, therefore, will be the Fourth Root of 1 5/11, which is to say, double the Fourth Root of 1/11.

### Question III.

This is 1/7 of the 3 positions. Therefore, 73 15/16 squares are equal to 3 positions, whereby the position is 48/1183, and the second quantity will be 144/1183, and the third will be 432/1183.

### Question IIII.

Find three proportional numbers whose second is 10, and 1/20 of the sum of all, multiplied by itself, produces seven times the second. We will set the first as a thing; therefore the third will be 100 divided by 1 thing. Since 1/20 of the sum multiplied by itself produces seven times the second, it therefore produces 70. And the Root of 70 is 1/20 of the sum; therefore the sum is the Root of 28,000. Because of this, the first and third together will be the Root of 28,000 minus 10, and this is equal to 1 position plus 100 divided by 1 position. Therefore, 1 square plus 100 is equal to the Root of 28,000 minus 10, <insert>multiplied by</insert> the positions. Thus the first quantity was the Root of 7,000 minus 5 minus the <term>Universal Root</term> <note>original: "R v:"; a square root that applies to the entire following expression</note> of (6925 minus the Root of 700,000), and the third quantity will be the Root of 7,000 minus 5 plus the Universal Root of (6925 minus the Root of 700,000). This could also be done more briefly, but without using a position.

## On the rule of positing the false. Chapter XXXVII.

### Rule I.

**T**his rule is threefold: it either posits a <term>minus</term> <note>original: "m:"; a negative number</note>, or it seeks the Root of a minus, or it seeks that which does not exist. First, therefore, we seek solutions to questions which can by no means be verified through a <term>plus</term> <note>original: "p:"; a positive number</note>. For instance, if someone says: a square is equal to 4 things plus 32, and in the same estimation, a square is equal to 1 thing plus 20. Then if you wished to follow the true estimation <note>the positive solution</note>, in the first case the thing would be 8, but in the second question the thing would be 5. But if you say, by converting them, that the square plus 4 things is equal to 32, the thing will be 4, and in this...

### QUESTION I.

The dowry of Francisco's wife is 100 gold coins more than Francisco's <term>personal assets</term> <note>original: "peculium"; private savings or capital</note>, and the dowry of his wife multiplied by itself <note>squared</note> is 400 gold coins more than Francisco's assets multiplied by itself. —is 400 gold coins. Therefore, 1 square plus 400 plus 200 positions is equal to 10,000 plus 1 square. Cast out the common terms <note>subtract $x^2$ from both sides</note>, and you will have 9,600 equal to 200 positions. Therefore, the thing is 48. And he had that much as "minus"—that is, as debt—and the dowry will be the remainder to 100, namely 52. Therefore, Francisco had 48 gold coins of debt, without any capital or assets, and his wife's dowry was 52 gold coins. By working otherwise, you would arrive at most difficult and inextricable questions. This one is also of such a kind:

### QUESTION II.

I have 12 gold coins more than Francisco, and the cube of mine is 1,161 gold coins more than the cube of Francisco's. Let 1 thing minus be set for Francisco; I have 12 gold coins minus 1 position. Cube the parts: they become 1 cube minus, and 1,728 plus 36 squares minus 432 things minus 1 cube. And the difference between these is 1,161. Therefore, 1 cube minus plus 432 things plus 1,161 will be equal to 1,728 plus 36 squares minus 1 cube. Cast out 1 cube minus and 1,161 from both sides: there remain 432 things equal to 36 squares plus 567. Wherefore 1 square plus 15 3/4 is equal to 12 things. Therefore the thing is 1 1/2. And Francisco had this as "minus," and I had 10 1/2 plus; and these are the amounts of gold coins sought.

### QUESTION III.

And in the same way, if I also say this: my gold coins are 12 more than those of Francisco. And the square of mine is 128 more than the cube of Francisco's gold coins. We will give Francisco one thing minus; I truly have 12 gold coins minus 1 thing. And the square of mine will be 144 plus 1 square minus 24 things, and this is equal to 1 cube minus plus 128. Therefore, 16 plus 1 square plus 1 cube is equal to 24 things. And the thing will be 4 minus; and Francisco has that much in debt, while I have 8 gold coins in assets.

### RULE II.

The second kind of false position is through the <term>square root of a negative</term> <note>original: "radicem m:"; literally the "root of minus." This is a foundational moment in the history of mathematics where Cardano begins to explore what we now call imaginary numbers.</note>. And I will give an example.

<meta>This page continues the transition from purely negative solutions to "sophistic" or imaginary solutions. Having discussed how negative numbers can solve certain problems, Cardano now introduces the case where the mathematical rule requires taking the square root of a negative number to solve a division of a line.</meta>

## ON ARITHMETIC BOOK X.

...example: if someone should say, divide 10 into two parts, from the multiplication of one of which into the other, 30 or 40 is produced. It is manifest that the case or question is impossible. Nevertheless, we will work it thus: we divide 10 into equal parts, and its half becomes 5. Multiply this by itself and it becomes 25. Subtract from 25 the very thing to be produced—namely 40—as I taught you in the chapter on operations in the sixth book. The remainder will be minus 15 <note>The result of $25 - 40$</note>, the square root <note>original: "Rz"; radix</note> of which, added to and subtracted from 5, shows the parts which, when multiplied together, produce 40. These will therefore be 5 plus the square root of minus 15, and 5 minus the square root of minus 15.

### DEMONSTRATIO

<margin>2</margin>
So that the true understanding of the rule may lie open, let AB be a line, which is called 10, to be divided into two parts, the rectangle <note>The area formed by the two parts</note> of which ought to be 40. However, 40 is four times 10; therefore we want four times the whole of AB. Therefore, let there be AD, the square of AC (the half of AB). From AD let the quadruple of AB be taken away, without a number <note>meaning as a geometric magnitude</note>. Therefore the root of the remainder, if anything remained, added to and subtracted from AC, would show the parts. But because such a remainder is less <note>less than zero</note>, you will therefore imagine the square root of minus 15—that is, the difference between AD and the quadruple of AB. Add and subtract this from AC, and you will have what was sought: namely, 5 plus the root of the whole of 25 minus 40, and 5 minus the root of the whole of 25 minus 40; or 5 plus the root of minus 15, and 5 minus the root of minus 15. 

<insert>
      A      c      B
      |______|______|
      |      |
      |  25  |
      |______|D
</insert>

Multiply 5 plus the root of minus 15 by 5 minus the root of minus 15. Setting aside the cross-multiplications <note>The middle terms of the FOIL method, which cancel out</note>, the result is 25 minus minus 15, which is plus 15. Therefore, this product is 40. Yet the nature of AD is not the same as the nature of 40, nor of AB, because a surface is remote from the nature of a number and a line. 

<insert>
5 plus root of minus 15
5 minus root of minus 15
______________________
25 minus minus 15 which is 40
</insert>

Nevertheless, this quantity is closer to this—which is truly "sophistic" <note>Cardano uses "sophistic" to mean abstract, imaginary, or intellectually subtle but not physically real</note>—because through it, unlike in pure "minus" or other cases, one may not perform ordinary operations; nor is it possible to hunt for what it is. It is as if you add the square of the half of the number to the number to be produced, and from the square root of the sum, you subtract and add half of the number to be divided. 

For example, in this case: divide 10 into two parts producing 40. Add 25 (the square of half of 10) to 40; it becomes 65. From the root of this, subtract 5, and also add 5. You will have the parts according to the similarity: the root of 65 plus 5 and the root of 65 minus 5. But these numbers differ by 10; joined together they do not make 10, but the root of 260. And thus far does arithmetic subtlety progress, the extreme of which, as I have said, is so subtle as to be useless.

### QUESTION IIII.

Make two parts out of 6, the squares of which joined together are 50. This is solved through the first rule, not the second. For it concerns pure "minus." Therefore, multiply 3 (the half of 6) by itself; it becomes 9. Subtract from half of 50, which is 25; the remainder is...

<summary>In this landmark passage, Cardano introduces "sophistic" numbers (what we now call imaginary numbers) to solve a quadratic equation where the discriminant is negative. He demonstrates that while these quantities are intellectually "subtle" and seem physically impossible, the mathematical rules for multiplying them still produce a correct result of 40.</summary>
