...remainder 16, the <term>square root<note>original: "℞"; radix</note></term> of which is 4; add and subtract [this] from 3, which is half of 6, and the parts become 7 and minus 1. The squares of these joined together are 50, and their sum is 6.

### QUESTION V.

By the same [method], this question is solved: make two parts out of 6, one of which multiplied by the other produces minus 40. Multiply 3, the half of 6, by itself, which makes 9; add this to 40, which makes 49. The square root of this is 7; add this to 3 (the half of 6) and subtract it, and you will have 10 <term>plus<note>original: "p:"; plus</note></term> and 4 <term>minus<note>original: "m:"; minus</note></term>. These, multiplied together, produce minus 40, and joined together, they make 6. Likewise, 10 minus and 4 plus produce minus 40, and joined together, they make minus 6. Therefore, this question also concerns the pure minus and pertains to the first rule.

<margin>Corollary</margin>
From this it is clear: if someone says, "make two parts of 6, from the multiplication of which 40 is produced," the question concerns the <term>sophistic minus<note>What we now call imaginary numbers, specifically roots of negative numbers.</note></term> and pertains to the second rule. But if he says, "make two parts of 6, from the multiplication of which minus 40 is produced," or "from minus 6 let two parts be made producing minus 40," in both ways the question will be about the <term>pure minus<note>Standard negative numbers.</note></term> and will pertain to the first rule; the parts will be those which were stated. And if he says that from minus 6 two parts are to be made whose product is 40 plus, the question will be about the sophistic minus and will pertain to the second rule; the parts will be minus 3 plus the square root of minus 15, and minus 3 minus the square root of minus 15.

### RULE III.

We are indeed able to hunt for another genus of minus, which is neither a pure minus nor the square root of a minus, but a thing altogether false. This rule is composed, as it were, of both, and I will give one example of it, which is this.

### QUESTION VI.

Find three <term>proportional numbers<note>A geometric progression where the first is to the second as the second is to the third.</note></term>, the square root of the first of which, subtracted from the first, makes the second; and the square root of the second, subtracted from the second, makes the third. We will therefore set the first as 1 <term>square<note>original: "quadratū"; $x^2$</note></term>, and the second will be 1 square minus 1 <term>position<note>original: "positione"; the unknown variable $x$.</note></term>, and the third will be 1 square minus 1 position minus the <term>universal root<note>original: "℞ v:"; a square root applied to the entire following expression.</note></term> of 1 square minus 1 position. Multiply the first by the third and the second by itself; you will have the quantities themselves, by operating as you see, and the product of the first by the third is minus 1/16 plus <insert>| 1/4 minus | 1/4 | minus | the square root of minus 1/4 |</insert> the square root of 1/64, which is minus 1/8; and the second number multiplied by itself becomes just as much.

# How parts and denominations drop out in multiplication. Chapter XXXVIII.

### RULE I.

--- Page 139 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstration from the previous discussion of "pure minus" (negative numbers) and "sophistic" numbers. Here, Cardano introduces a specific "particular rule" for solving equations involving a combination of a cube and a square, which he had previously explored through geometric proofs but now simplifies using algebra.</meta>

# ON ARITHMETIC, BOOK 10

Likewise, we require rules, the first of which is specific, discovered for the sake of those chapters which were demonstrated by us above through <term>geometric reasoning<note>Mathematical proofs based on the visual properties of shapes and volumes, common before symbolic algebra was fully developed.</note></term> in four <term>denominations<note>The powers of the unknown variable, such as the thing (x), square (x²), and cube (x³).</note></term>. Now that these have been found, the utility of this rule is largely obsolete; nevertheless, we shall teach it to enrich the art and out of admiration for its ingenuity, as it is also useful for other things to which it can be conveniently applied, even though it cannot be adapted to any general use. Therefore, the rule is this: If you want different numbers whose square of one joined with the cube of the other makes a given number, you then divide that difference into two parts, such that three times the square of one is equal to twice the other, through <term>position<note>The method of "position" refers to setting up an algebraic equation by letting a 'thing' (the unknown) stand in for a value.</note></term>. Then, once the parts are found, you set the <term>thing<note>original: "rem"; the unknown value, our modern "x".</note></term> <term>plus<note>original: "p:"; plus</note></term> the part whose triple square was taken as the part to be cubed, and for the part to be squared, the thing <term>minus<note>original: "m:"; minus</note></term> the part whose double was taken. Then, once the operation is completed, you will arrive at a cube and squares equal to a number, with the things <note>the linear terms (x)</note> cancelling each other out.

### QUESTION I.

Example: Find two numbers whose difference is 8, and the cube of one joined with the square of the other makes 100. First, through position, make two parts [of 8] such that three times the square of one is equal to twice the other. You will find these to be 2 and 6, for the triple of 4 (the square of 2) is 12, which is the double of 6, the remainder. Therefore, set the part to be cubed as the thing plus 2, and the part to be squared as the thing minus 6. Join the cube of 1 thing plus 2 with the square of 1 thing minus 6; you have 1 cube plus 7 squares plus 44, equal to 100. Therefore, 1 cube plus 7 squares is equal to 56, and the value of the <term>thing<note>The solution for x in the equation x³ + 7x² = 56.</note></term> will be:

<term>universal cubic root<note>original: "℞ v: cubica"; a root that applies to the entire following expression.</note></term> of 15 8/27 <term>plus<note>original: "p:"</note></term> the root of 72 16/27 <term>plus<note>original: "p:"</note></term> the universal cubic root of 15 8/27 <term>minus<note>original: "m:"</note></term> the root of 72 16/27 minus 2 1/3. And because the parts were the thing plus 2 and the thing minus 6, therefore add 2 to this [result] and subtract 6, and you will have the parts, as you see to the side. It is moreover manifest that one of these is a <term>pure minus<note>original: "m: purum"; a negative number.</note></term>; and if you wished for both to be <term>plus<note>original: "p:"; positive</note></term>, it would have been necessary to set the cube and square of such numbers to be equal to a larger number, such as 1000 instead of 100.

And you shall do the same if you wish that for numbers differing by some amount, the cube and the square differ by an assigned number. By the same rule, you will find the parts of the difference; once found, you set the opposite: namely, the thing minus the number whose triple square is taken, and the thing plus the number whose double is taken. Then follow the operation as in the example.

OF GIROLAMO CARDANO

# GIROLAMO CARDANO
## QUESTION II.

<margin>
| |
|:---|
| thing minus 2 |
| thing plus 6 |
| cube plus 12 things minus 6 squares minus 8 <note>Expansion of (x - 2)³</note> |
| plus 12 things plus 1 square plus 36 <note>Expansion of (x + 6)²</note> |
| cube minus 7 squares minus 44 <note>The result of subtracting the square from the cube</note> |
| equals 100 |
</margin>

Find two numbers whose difference is 8, and the difference of the cube of one from the square of the other is 100. You shall make two parts from 8, as was said, and they will be 2 and 6. <note>Cardano often splits the difference to center the variable, a technique similar to modern substitution.</note> Therefore, set the <term>thing<note>original: "rem"; the unknown variable x.</note></term> minus 2, and the thing plus 6. Cube the thing minus 2, and square the thing plus 6, and take the difference; you will have the cube minus 7 squares minus 44, equal to 100. Wherefore the cube will be equal to 7 squares plus 144. And the <term>value of the thing<note>original: "rei æstimatio"; the solution for x.</note></term> will be the <term>universal cubic root<note>original: "Rz v: cubica"; a cube root that applies to the entire following binomial expression.</note></term> of 84 and 19/27 plus the square root of 7013 and 1/3, plus the universal cubic root of 84 and 19/27 minus the square root of 7013 and 1/3, plus 2 and 1/3. And because we set the parts as the thing minus 2 and the thing plus 6, the sought numbers will be as you see.

And similarly, if he says: make two parts from some number, the square of one of which joined with the cube of the other makes some number. For you shall make two parts from the number to be divided, as above: to one (namely, the one of which the triple of the square is taken), you shall add the thing; from the other (of which the double of itself is taken), you shall subtract the thing. Then you shall complete the operation, as in the example.

Make two parts from 8, whose cube of one with the square of the other makes 400. Wherefore the value of the thing is the universal cubic root of 165 and 8/27 plus the square root of 27161 and 13/27, plus the universal cubic root of 165 and 8/27 minus the square root of 27161 and 13/27, minus 2 and 1/3. # ON ARITHMETIC, BOOK X.

by multiplying it by itself, then by making a subtraction, you will have a cube and <term>things<note>original: "res"; the unknown variable $x$.</note></term> equal to a number, wherefore both parts will be known immediately.

### QUESTION IIII.

Example: Divide 8 into two parts, the cube of one of which exceeds the square of the other by 10. Therefore, we will set the first part as $\frac{1}{3}$ and the second as $7\frac{2}{3}$, and we will add a <term>thing<note>original: "rem"; the variable $x$.</note></term> to $\frac{1}{3}$, and it becomes $\frac{1}{3}$ plus 1 <term>position<note>original: "positione"; another term for the variable $x$.</note></term>, and we will subtract the thing from $7\frac{2}{3}$, and it becomes $7\frac{2}{3}$ minus the thing; then we will follow the operation, and we will have for the cube of $\frac{1}{3}$ plus 1 position, this:

| | | |
|:---|:---:|:---|
| $\frac{1}{3}$ | plus | 1 pos. |
| $7\frac{2}{3}$ | minus | 1 pos. |
| $\frac{1}{27}$ plus $\frac{1}{3}$ pos. plus 1 sq. plus 1 cu. | | |
| $58\frac{20}{27}$ minus $15\frac{1}{3}$ pos. plus 1 sq. | | |
| $58\frac{20}{27}$ | \| | $15\frac{1}{3}$ pos. plus 1 cub. |

and for the square, 1 square minus $15\frac{1}{3}$ positions plus $58\frac{20}{27}$. The difference between these will be 1 cube plus $15\frac{2}{3}$ positions minus $58\frac{20}{27}$, and this is equal to 10; therefore, the cube and $15\frac{2}{3}$ positions are equal to $68\frac{20}{27}$, and the <term>value of the thing<note>original: "rei æstimatio"; the solution for $x$.</note></term> is known, to which we will add $\frac{1}{3}$ for the first part, and subtract it from $7\frac{2}{3}$ for the second part. And if we had wanted the square to exceed the cube, we would have subtracted the number of the equation, 10, from $58\frac{20}{27}$, and we would have 1 cube plus $15\frac{2}{3}$ positions equal to $48\frac{20}{27}$. The ways of this first rule are innumerable, and they are like a part of the rule of method.

### RULE II.

But another rule, which is much in use among us and is easier, is such, and will be explained more easily by examples like the rest.

### QUESTION V.

Therefore, make two parts from 8, which, when their squares are taken together and likewise their cubes together, and one sum is multiplied by the other, a <term>perfect number<note>A perfect number is a positive integer that is equal to the sum of its positive divisors, excluding the number itself. Examples include 6, 28, 496, and 8128.</note></term> is produced. I could say that it might even produce a fixed number, like 10,000 or another; the maximum which it can produce is also given, and it is 32,768 <note>This occurs if one part is 8 and the other is 0: $(8^2 + 0^2) \times (8^3 + 0^3) = 64 \times 512 = 32,768$.</note>, and it is produced from the cube of the whole multiplied by the square of the whole. The minimum is also given, than which a smaller cannot be produced, and it is 4,096 <note>This occurs if both parts are 4: $(4^2 + 4^2) \times (4^3 + 4^3) = 32 \times 128 = 4,096$.</note>. First, it must be seen whether a perfect number falls between these two numbers, and it is 8,128; if it did not fall there, the question would be impossible. Therefore, set one part as 4 minus 1 position, and the other as 4 plus 1 position. The squares will become 16 plus 8 positions plus 1 square, and 16 minus 8 positions plus 1 square, which joined together will be 32 plus 2 squares, with the "things" falling out. The cubes also will be 64 plus 12 squares plus 48 positions plus 1 cube, and 64 plus 12 squares minus 48 positions minus 1 cube, which joined together are 128 plus 24 squares. Wherefore we will multiply 32 plus 2 squares by 128 plus 24 squares, and it will become 4,096 plus 1,024 squares plus 48 <term>square-squares<note>original: "q̃d’q̃dratis" or "quadrato-quadratum"; the fourth power $x^4$.</note></term>, and these are equal to 8,128. Therefore, after making the subtraction and division, we will have:

## QUESTION VI.

Divide 10 into two parts whose square roots, when cubed, make 26. Set such roots to be 1 <term>position<note>original: "positio"; the unknown variable $x$.</note></term>. Make from 1 position two parts whose squares joined together are 10, because the roots of such parts must aggregate to 1 position. Therefore, by the rules of the sixth book, or from Euclid, you will have the parts, as you see: that is, $\frac{1}{2}$ position plus the Universal Root of 5 minus $\frac{1}{4}$ of a square, and $\frac{1}{2}$ position minus the Universal Root of 5 minus $\frac{1}{4}$ of a square.

<insert>$\frac{1}{2}$ pos. plus $\sqrt{V}: 5$ minus $\frac{1}{4}$ sq. | $\frac{1}{2}$ pos. minus $\sqrt{V}: 5$ minus $\frac{1}{4}$ sq.</insert>

These must be reduced to a cube; and because in cubing a <term>Binomial<note>A mathematical expression consisting of two terms.</note></term>, one must multiply each of the parts by itself, and triple it, and add it to the square of the other part, and multiply the product by that other part. Therefore, since such products are matched and equal, and one is plus and the other minus when we multiply the triple square of the first part with the square of the second by the second, it will suffice to multiply the triple square of the second part (which is 15 minus $\frac{3}{4}$ of a square) with the square of the first part (which is $\frac{1}{4}$ of a square). The whole becomes 15 minus $\frac{1}{2}$ of a square, multiplied by the first part which is $\frac{1}{2}$ position. But because this operation must be doubled on account of the two parts, we will have the multiplication of 15 minus $\frac{1}{2}$ of a square by 1 position (which is the double of the $\frac{1}{2}$ position of the first part). Therefore, finally, there will be produced 15 positions minus $\frac{1}{2}$ of a cube, equal to 26. Wherefore 1 cube plus 52 will equal 30 positions, and the <term>value of the thing<note>original: "rei aestimatio"; the numerical solution for $x$.</note></term> will be from its own chapter: the Root of 27 minus 1. From this you will have the parts, as you see, and in the verification of the operation, you need this rule much more for ease; but we have spoken of this in the third book in its proper place.

<insert>$\sqrt{6\frac{3}{4}}$ minus $\frac{1}{2}$ plus $\sqrt{V}: \sqrt{6\frac{3}{4}}$ minus 2 | $\sqrt{6\frac{3}{4}}$ minus $\frac{1}{2}$ minus $\sqrt{V}: \sqrt{6\frac{3}{4}}$ minus 2 </insert>

## QUESTION VII.

And to this is reduced that question: A certain man buys 1 lb. of Saffron, 2 lbs. of Cinnamon, 5 lbs. of Pepper, at proportional prices, such that as the price of all the pepper was to the price of the cinnamon, so was the price of the cinnamon to the price of the saffron, so that the price of the saffron was the least, and the pepper the greatest, and the cinnamon the medium; and these three prices, joined together, were 6 <term>gold coins<note>original: "aurei"; gold currency.</note></term>. Again, under the same prices, he buys 30 lbs. of saffron, 50 lbs. of cinnamon, 40 lbs. of pepper, for 100 gold coins. The price of each is sought. This question was posed by <term>Brother Luca<note>Luca Pacioli (c. 1447–1517), a Franciscan friar and seminal mathematician who published 'Summa de arithmetica'.</note></term>, but in proportional numbers, for he considered it to be quite difficult; but it is not, for since these prices (of 5 pounds of pepper, 2 of cinnamon, and 1 of saffron) are proportional, they will also remain proportional in their aggregates. Divide...

# ON ARITHMETIC, BOOK X.

[We divide]<note>The text begins with the suffix "-mus," likely completing the word "dividimus" (we divide) from the previous page.</note> therefore 30 lbs. of saffron by 1, and this is the second quantity by the first; and likewise 50 of cinnamon by 2, and 40 of pepper by 5, and the numbers will emerge in the margin—that is, 30 for saffron, 25 for cinnamon, and 8 for pepper. It is manifest, therefore, that these are the numbers of three proportional quantities, which are the prices of 1 lb. of saffron, 2 of cinnamon, and 5 of pepper; and that the first quantity or price, taken 30 times, the second 25 times, and the third 8 times, makes 100 gold coins. But indeed these quantities, as was said, are 6 gold coins when simply added together. Therefore, make from 6 three proportional quantities, of which the first multiplied by 30, the second by 25, and the third by 8, makes 100. We will set, therefore, the middle [value] as 2 <term>positions<note>original: "positiones"; the unknown variable $x$. Cardano often uses "position" to represent the unknown in an equation.</note></term>. There will remain for the others: 3 minus 1 position plus the <term>Universal Root<note>original: "Rz v:"; the square root of the entire following expression.</note></term> of (9 minus 3 squares minus 6 positions), and 3 minus 1 position minus the Universal Root of (9 minus 3 squares minus 6 positions).

| 1 | 2 | 5 | 6 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 30 | 25 | 8 | 100 |

Each of these must then be multiplied by their respective numbers. Because the first parts of the <term>Binomials<note>A mathematical expression consisting of two terms, such as $a + b$.</note></term> are equal, and both are plus, multiplying them by 30 and by 8 is the same as multiplying them by 38. Similarly, because one of the Universal Roots is minus (to be multiplied by 30) and the other is plus (to be multiplied by 8), since they are equal, it will be the same as if they were multiplied by 22 (the difference between 30 and 8). Thus the parts will be produced:

# GIROLAMO CARDANO

If, therefore, you divide these proportional prices by the number of their pounds, referring each to each—the first by 1, the second by 2, and the third by 5—you will have the prices of the individual pounds of each kind. And if you multiply those by the two numbers in the second purchase—the price of saffron by 30, cinnamon by 50, and pepper by 40—you will have how much money was spent on each.

## QUESTION VIII.

In the same way, this question is solved: divide 14 into three proportional parts, of which the largest multiplied by 2, the middle by 3, and the smallest by 4, and these products, when added together, make 36. You will arrive, by the method used above, at 1 <term>square<note>original: "qdratum" or "quadratum"; represents the $x^2$ term in the equation.</note></term> plus $9 \frac{1}{3}$ <term>positions<note>original: "positionibus"; represents the unknown variable $x$.</note></term>, equal to $53 \frac{1}{3}$. Therefore, the <term>thing<note>original: "res"; the value of the unknown $x$.</note></term> is the $\sqrt{75 \frac{1}{9}} - 4 \frac{2}{3}$, and the middle quantity is 4, where the middle quantity was set as 1 position, rather than as 2 positions as in the previous case.

## QUESTION IX.

Divide 14 into three proportional parts, such that the first multiplied by 2 and the second by 3, the resulting products are equal to the third multiplied by 7. You will set the second [part] to be 2 positions; the remaining parts will be as you see:

| | |
|:---|:---|
| 2nd | 2 positions |
| 1st | $7 - 1 \text{ position} + \sqrt{\text{V: } 49 - 14 \text{ positions} - 3 \text{ squares}}$ <note>Cardano uses "Rz V:" (Radix Universalis) to indicate the square root of the entire following expression.</note> |
| 3rd | $7 - 1 \text{ position} - \sqrt{\text{V: } 49 - 14 \text{ positions} - 3 \text{ squares}}$ |

The second multiplied by 3 makes 6 positions. Now, the first must be multiplied by 2, and the third by 7, and they must be subtracted [from one another]. Therefore, since both parts are similar, and the first part of both is plus, while the second part is plus in the first and minus in the third: it is sufficient to multiply the first part by the difference between 7 and 2, which is 5. This will produce, for the third part, $35 - 5 \text{ positions}$. Subtracting 6 positions (the product of the second part) from this, we will have $35 - 11 \text{ positions}$ for the difference between the third and second products. However, the first [term of the equation] will be produced by multiplying 9 (the sum of the multipliers 7 and 2) by the universal root, which becomes $\sqrt{\text{V: } 3969 - 1134 \text{ positions} - 243 \text{ squares}}$. This, therefore, is equal to $35 - 11 \text{ positions}$. Squaring both sides, you get $1225 - 770 \text{ positions} + 121 \text{ squares}$, which is equal to $3969 - 1134 \text{ positions} - 243 \text{ squares}$. Balancing the parts, you will have 2744 equal to 364 positions plus 364 squares. Wherefore, 1 square plus one position is equal to $7 \frac{7}{13}$. Thus the value of the "thing" is known, and its double is the second part, namely $\sqrt{31 \frac{2}{3}} - 1$.

Divide 8 into three proportional parts, such that the sum of the squares of the first and second is triple the square of the second <note>The Latin text here says "first and second," but the subsequent calculation and logical context of these problems suggest Cardano likely meant the "first and third," as he clarifies in the very next line.</note>. You will set the middle quantity as 2 positions; its square is 4 squares, triple of which is the sum of the squares of the first and third. But the first is 4 minus...

# ON ARITHMETIC, BOOK 10

...by the position plus the <term>universal root<note>original: "Rz v" or "Radix universalis"; this refers to a square root that applies to an entire multi-term expression, equivalent to the modern $\sqrt{a+b+c}$.</term> of 16 minus 8 positions minus 3 <term>squares<note>original: "quadratis"; the $x^2$ term.</note></term>, and the third is 4 minus 1 position minus the universal root of 16 minus 8 positions minus 3 squares. Reducing these therefore to squares, you see that it is necessary to multiply the universal root by itself once, and the first part by itself once, and all are plus; wherefore it will suffice to double such products. Then it would be necessary to multiply the universal root by the first part twice; however, since in one case it results in plus and in the other minus (the parts being assumed equal), it will produce nothing. Therefore, we will have the sum of the squares [as] 64 minus 32 positions minus 4 squares, and this is equal to 12 squares, the triple of the square of the second. Therefore, 1 square plus 2 positions equals 4, and the <term>thing<note>original: "res"; the value of the unknown variable $x$.</note></term> is the square root of 5 minus 1. Double this is the middle quantity, namely the square root of 20 minus 2. The remaining parts, as you see: the square of the second is 24 minus the square root of 320; the squares of the first and third together are 72 minus the square root of 2880. This has been proven.

But if it were said that the squares of the first and third were triple to the squares of the second and third, then it is solved with difficulty by this rule; yet it is far easier by the first rule of the 39th chapter, by setting the quantities as 1, 1 position, and 1 square; you will have 1 squared-square plus the triple of 1 square plus 1, wherefore the thing is known.

## QUESTION XI.
If you say: make two parts from 8, which when divided in turn by the square of the other, the sum of the results is 10. You will set the parts as 4 plus 1 position and 4 minus 1 position, and by this rule, you will arrive at a derivative chapter of the <term>squared-square<note>original: "qd' qdrati"; the $x^4$ term.</note></term>, the square, and the number, and it is easy.

## QUESTION XII.
Find four numbers in <term>continued proportion<note>original: "continue proportionales"; a geometric progression where the ratio between successive terms is constant (a, ar, ar², ar³).</note></term>, the sum of the first, second, and fourth of which is 15, and the sum of the first, third, and fourth is 17. Then you say: therefore, since these sums differ by the difference between the second and the third, the third is 2 more than the second. I will therefore set the second as 1 position minus 1, and the third as 1 position plus 1, for thus the difference between them will be 2. There remains, therefore, the sum of the first and fourth as 16 minus 1 position. Multiply the second by the third, which is 1 square minus 1; make two parts from 16 minus 1 position, which when multiplied by each other produce 1 square minus 1, and the parts will be as you see, because

<sig>S 2</sig>

-># GIROLAMO CARDANO<-

...Therefore, the <term>proportion<note>original: "pportio"; the ratio between numbers.</note></term> of the fourth to the third is as the second to the first, as I have established; because the <term>product<note>The result of multiplication.</note></term> of the second and the third is equal to the product of the first and the fourth. It will suffice to demonstrate that they are in continuous proportion, since the <term>cubes<note>original: "cubi"; a number raised to the third power ($x^3$).</note></term> of the second and third joined together are equal to the products of the quantities of the fourth and first into their mutual <term>squares<note>original: "quadrata"; a number raised to the second power ($x^2$).</note></term>. But such cubes are produced only from the multiplication of triple the square of the second part, with the square of the first, into that same first [part]; for this reason, the remaining multiplication of triple the square of the first part, with the square of the second into that same second, cancels out, because in one it is <term>plus<note>original: "p:"; plus.</note></term> and in the other <term>minus<note>original: "m:"; minus.</note></term>. Therefore, we have the cubes joined as 2 cubes plus 6 <term>positions<note>original: "positionibus"; the unknown variable $x$.</note></term>, and just as much must be produced from the multiplication of the squares of the first and fourth quantity into those same quantities alternately. This, however, as has been demonstrated, is equal to the drawing of one quantity into the other, multiplied by the sum of those same quantities, according to what was said in the sixth book.

Multiply, therefore, the quantities by each other; and because the <term>universal roots<note>original: "Rz v:"; a square root covering a whole expression.</note></term> are similar, the cross-multiplication will be null. Wherefore it will suffice to square each part and subtract one from the other, because minus times plus makes minus. There will be produced, therefore, from the similar parts, 1 square minus 1; the sum of the roots is also 16 minus 1 position, because the universal roots cancel out. Therefore, the product will be 16 squares minus 1 cube plus 1 position minus 16. And this is equated to 2 cubes plus 6 positions. Therefore, 3 cubes plus 5 positions plus 16 are equal to 16 squares. Wherefore the <term>matter is in the chapter<note>original: "res est in capitulo"; a common phrase in Renaissance algebra meaning the equation is now in a standard form that can be solved by a known rule or "chapter" in a textbook.</note></term>. You see, however, how an inexplicable question is reduced to great ease, and it could be reduced to a general rule; for where the difference is 2, always 3 cubes plus 5 positions plus a number halfway between two sums by equal distance are equal to as many squares as that number is.

### QUESTION XIV.

There is a <term>right-angled triangle<note>original: "trigonus orthogonius"; a triangle with one 90-degree angle.</note></term> ABC, and its perpendicular to the base is AD. Side AB with [segment] BD is 36, and AC with CD is 24. The area is sought. Set BC as 1 position; therefore the square of BC will be 1 square. And so, since AB and BD are 36, and again AC and CD are 24, all the sides of the triangle together will be 60. <margin>Therefore AB and AC will be 60 minus 1 position.</margin> It is therefore necessary to make two parts out of AB and AC, the joined squares of which are equal to the square of BC, according to the teaching of the 47th [proposition] of the first book of <term>Euclid's Elements<note>The Pythagorean theorem.</note></term>. Wherefore, according to the rules of our sixth book, divide 60...

# On Arithmetic, Book 10.

From 60 minus 1 <term>position<note>original: "positione"; the unknown variable, represented today as $x$.</note></term> divided into equal parts, comes 30 minus 1/2 positions; multiply this by itself, and it becomes 900 minus 30 positions plus 1/4 of a <term>square<note>original: "quadrati"; the square of the unknown, or $x^2$.</note></term>. Subtract this from half of the square of BC; there remains 1/4 of a square plus 30 positions minus 900. The <term>universal root<note>original: "Rv" (Radix universalis); the square root of the entire following expression.</note></term> of this, added to and subtracted from half the sum of AB and AC, shows the parts. Therefore:

AB is 30 minus 1/2 position plus the universal root of: 1/4 square plus 30 positions minus 900.
And AC is 30 minus 1/2 position minus the universal root of: 1/4 square plus 30 positions minus 900.

Wherefore, if AB is subtracted from the sum of AB and BD, there remains BD: 6 plus 1/2 position minus the universal root of: 1/4 square plus 30 positions minus 900. And similarly, having subtracted AC from the sum of AC and CD, there remains CD: 1/2 position minus 6 plus the universal root of: 1/4 square plus 30 positions minus 900. 

It is evident from the demonstration of the <note>This refers to the Pythagorean theorem, which states that in a right-angled triangle, the square of the hypotenuse is equal to the sum of the squares of the other two sides.</note> 47th [proposition] of the first [book] of Euclid’s *Elements* that the difference between the square of AB and the square of AC is equal to the difference between the square of BD and the square of CD. Now, the difference between two quantities is always found in the <term>dissimilar parts<note>Cardano is explaining that when you square these complex terms and subtract them, the identical parts cancel out, leaving only the parts that were different.</note></term>; for those parts which are similar produce no difference. Therefore, since the squares of these parts consist of nine multiplications (of which three are squares of the parts), those three will be entirely similar when comparing AB to AC and BD to CD. Similarly, the two multiplications of 30 by 1/2 position are common to both AB and AC, since both produce a "minus" result; and so in BD and CD, the multiplications of 6 by the universal root are common, for the same "minus" result arises on both sides. Therefore, the difference of AB and AC, on the side of AB, is the multiplication of 30 by the universal root; and on the side of AC, the multiplication of 1/2 position by the universal root. 

Wherefore, the difference of the squares of AB and AC is that of which the universal root of 225 squares plus 27,000 positions minus 810,000 exceeds the universal root of 1/16 <term>square-square<note>original: "qd'qd" (quadrato-quadrati); the unknown raised to the fourth power, $x^4$.</note></term> plus 7 1/2 <term>cubes<note>original: "cubis"; $x^3$.</note></term> minus 225 squares. By the same reasoning, the difference of the squares of BD and CD is that by which 3 positions exceed the universal root of 1/16 square-square plus 7 1/2 cubes minus 225 squares. It would have been necessary, in completing the operation, to quadruple everything, but we have avoided this, because if the quadruple is equal to the quadruple, then the simple is equal to the simple. These differences are therefore assumed to be equal, and the universal roots are also the same; therefore—

| AB | 30 minus 1/2 position plus universal root of: 1/4 square plus 30 positions minus 900 |
| AC | 30 minus 1/2 position minus universal root of: 1/4 square plus 30 positions minus 900 |
| BD | 1/2 position plus 6 minus universal root of: 1/4 square plus 30 positions minus 900 |
| CD | 1/2 position minus 6 plus universal root of: 1/4 square plus 30 positions minus 900 |
| --- | --- |
| Dissimilar part of square of AB | Universal root of: 225 squares plus 27,000 positions minus 810,000 |
| Dissimilar part of square of AC | Universal root of: 1/16 square-square plus 7 1/2 cubes minus 225 squares |
| Part of square of BD | 3 positions |
| Part of square of CD | Universal root of: 1/16 square-square plus 7 1/2 cubes minus 225 squares |

### QUESTION XIV.

Again, let a right-angled <term>triangle<note>original: "trigonus"; a three-sided figure.</note></term> ABC be arranged with a perpendicular line AD. Let AB with CD be 29, and AC with BD be 31. The area is sought. We will set BC as the position, and AB and AC will again be the same as in the previous question. But take care not to place the larger side with the larger number, as was done before. Therefore, subtract AB from 29 and AC from 31, and you will have the quantities as you see here. Therefore, the difference between the squares of AB and AC is equal to the difference between the squares of BD and CD. Moreover, the difference between the squares of AB and AC is as before, but the difference of the squares of BD and CD is as you see, taken in the same manner as in the previous question, though it is an absolute excess, not mutual as in the previous case. Because the square of AB exceeds the square of AC by the difference of the square of BD to the square of CD, the difference of the squares of BD and CD added to the square of AC will constitute the square of AB. Wherefore, the universal root of 225 squares plus 27,000 positions minus 810,000 will equal 1/2 of a position plus the universal root of 1/4 of a <term>squared-square<note>original: "qd’ qdrati" (quadrato-quadratum); the unknown raised to the fourth power, $x^4$.</note></term> plus 30 <term>cubes<note>original: "cubis"; $x^3$.</note></term> minus 900 squares. ## Concerning the rule by which we find an unknown quantity by means of several positions. Chapter XXXIX.

### RULE I.

This rule is similar to the "rule of the middle"; it is as follows: Set up as many quantities in free denominations as there are numbers to be sought; from this you will find the <term>proportion<note>original: "proportionem"; in this context, the common ratio of a geometric progression.</note></term>. Once the proportion is found, you will then place the <term>things<note>original: "res"; the unknown variable $x$.</note></term> under the number of quantities found, and as proposed, complete the operation, and you will have the <term>equation<note>original: "æquationem"</note></term>; having this, you will have the <term>value<note>original: "æstimatio"</note></term> of the thing.

### QUESTION I.

Example: Find three numbers <term>in proportion<note>original: "proportionales"; numbers in a geometric progression, like $a, ar, ar^2$.</note></term>, of which the square of the first is equal to the second and third, and the square of the third is equal to the squares of the first and second. Since the square of the third is equal to the squares of the second and first, let it be 1 <term>square-square<note>original: "q̃d' q̃d." (quadratum-quadratum); the fourth power, $x^4$.</note></term>, equal to 1 square plus 1; wherefore the thing, or the proportion, is the <term>universal root<note>original: "Rz V" (Radix universalis); a square root that applies to everything that follows.</note></term> of the root of $1\frac{1}{4}$ plus $\frac{1}{2}$. Therefore we will set the things as 1, and the universal root of the root of $1\frac{1}{4}$ plus $\frac{1}{2}$, and the root of $1\frac{1}{4}$ plus $\frac{1}{2}$. Thus the square of the first quantity, which is 1 square, is equal to

<insert>
| | | |
|---|---|---|
| I | 1 pos. | 1 q̃d. |
| I | 1 q̃d. | 1 q̃d' q̃d. |
</insert>

the second and third—namely, to that many <term>positions<note>original: "rebus"; Cardano uses "things" and "positions" interchangeably for the unknown.</note></term>. Therefore, the value of the thing is the sum of the second and third, because to divide something by unity (which is the number of squares) is to not divide at all. Therefore the value of the thing is the root of $1\frac{1}{4}$ plus $\frac{1}{2}$ plus the universal root of the root of $1\frac{1}{4}$ plus $\frac{1}{2}$. The second quantity is what is produced from this by multiplying by the universal root of the root of $1\frac{1}{4}$ plus $\frac{1}{2}$; and the third will be had by leading the thing you have into the root of $1\frac{1}{4}$ plus $\frac{1}{2}$.

### QUESTION II.

Find three numbers in proportion, of which the third is equal to the second and first, and the square of the first is equal to the sum of the second and third. You will set the first as a square, the second as a thing, and the third as a unit. And because the third is equal to the second and first, therefore 1 square is equal to 1 thing plus 1, and the proportion will be the root of $1\frac{1}{4}$ plus $\frac{1}{2}$. The parts therefore will be: 1 position, and positions of the root of $1\frac{1}{4}$ plus $\frac{1}{2}$, and positions of $1\frac{1}{2}$ plus the root of $1\frac{1}{4}$. And because the square of the first is equal to the sum of the second and third, therefore 1 square is equal to the positions of the root of

Here, he applies this to higher-order powers and introduces the groundbreaking work of his student, Lodovico Ferrari.</meta>

# GIROLAMO CARDANO
## QUESTION III.

Find four <term>proportional quantities<note>A geometric progression, where each term is multiplied by a constant ratio to find the next, e.g., $1, r, r^2, r^3$.</note></term>, of which the square of the fourth is equal to the squares of the first and second, and the quantities joined together make 10. I will take 1, the <term>thing<note>original: "rem"; the unknown $x$.</note></term>, the square, and the cube; therefore, the <term>square-cube<note>original: "q̃d'cubus"; the sixth power, $x^6$.</note></term> is equal to 1 square plus 1. Wherefore, the thing is worth, according to the chapter on <term>derivatives<note>Equations that can be reduced to a simpler form, such as treating $x^6 = x^2 + 1$ as a cubic equation $y^3 = y + 1$ by substituting $y = x^2$.</note></term>, the <term>universal root<note>original: "Rz vᵐᵃ"; Cardano's notation for the root of an entire expression.</note></term> of: the universal cube root of $1/2$ plus the root of $23/108$, plus the universal cube root of $1/2$ minus the root of $23/108$. Therefore, having set the first quantity as <insert>1 | 1 position 1 square 1 cube <br> 1 | 1 square --- 1 square-cube</insert> unity, this is the second quantity, and the third will be the square of this (namely, the universal cube root of $1/2$ plus the root of $23/108$, plus the universal cube root of $1/2$ minus the root of $23/108$), and the fourth will be the cube of the second (or of the proportion). Then, having joined the four quantities—namely the unity, the thing, the square, and the cube—and having divided 10 by their sum, the first quantity will result. When this is multiplied by the thing, we will have the second; then, multiplied again by the thing, we will have the third; and multiplied once more by the thing, we will have the fourth.

## QUESTION IV.

Find four <term>continuously proportional<note>A sequence where the ratio between consecutive terms is constant.</note></term> quantities, of which the square of the fourth is equal to the squares of the first and third, and their sum is 10. I will take, as in the preceding case, 1, the thing, the square, and the cube. Therefore, the square-cube will be equal to the <term>square-square<note>original: "q̃d'q̃drato"; the fourth power, $x^4$.</note></term> plus 1. Wherefore, from the chapter on derivatives, the value of the thing is the universal root of: the universal cube root of $29/54$ plus the root of $31/108$, plus $1/3$, plus the universal cube root of $29/54$ minus the root of $31/108$. And its square (which is the same, once the universal root is removed) is the third quantity. Then, by multiplying the second and third together, or the second by its own cube, or the third by the square, and adding unity, the fourth arises. Having joined these four quantities, if you divide 10 by them, you will have the first of those sought; which, when multiplied by the second, third, and fourth of the preceding values, you will have the second, third, and fourth quantities which you were seeking.

## RULE II.

<margin>2</margin>
There is another rule, more noble than the preceding one, and it is by **Lodovico Ferrari**, who found it at my request. Through it, we have almost all the <term>values<note>original: "æstimationes"; the solutions or roots of the equations.</note></term> for the chapters of the square-square, the square of things, and the number; or the square-square of the cube, squares, and the number. I will set them down in order, in this manner as you see.

# ON ARITHMETIC Book 10.

7 **Square-square** with cubes equal to a number <note>$x^4 + ax^3 = n$</note>
8 **Square-square** with things equal to a number <note>$x^4 + ax = n$</note>
9 **Square-square** with **squares** equal to cubes and a number
10 **Square-square** with **squares** equal to things and a number
11 **Square-square** with **squares** and things equal to a number
12 **Square-square** with **squares** and cubes equal to a number
13 **Square-square** with **squares** and a number equal to cubes
14 **Square-square** with **squares** and a number equal to things

17 **Square-square** with a number equal to things and **squares**

19 **Square-square** with cubes and a number equal to **squares**
20 **Square-square** with things and a number equal to **squares**

In all these <term>chapters<note>original: "capitulis"; refers to the specific types or cases of algebraic equations.</note></term>, which are indeed the most general, like all the sixty-seven previous ones, it is necessary to reduce the chapters in which the cube enters to the chapters in which the <term>thing<note>original: "res"; the unknown $x$.</note></term> enters—such as the seventh to the fourth, and the second to the first—then we shall seek the demonstration in this manner.

### DEMONSTRATION.

<margin>3</margin>
Let the square **A F** be divided into two squares **A D** and **D F**, and two <term>supplements<note>original: "supplementa"; the rectangles that complete a square when smaller squares are drawn inside it.</note></term> **D C** and **D E**, and I wish to add the <term>gnomon<note>An L-shaped figure that, when added to a square, forms a larger square.</note></term> **K F G** all around, so that the whole square **A H** remains. I say that such a gnomon will consist of twice the added line **G C** multiplied by **C A**, together with the square of **G C**. For **F G** consists of **G C** times **C F**, from the definition given at the beginning of the <term>second [book] of the Elements<note>Refers to Euclid's *Elements*, Book II, which deals with geometric algebra.</note></term>, and **C F** is equal to **C A** from the definition of a square; and by the 44th [proposition] of the <term>first [book] of the Elements<note>Euclid's *Elements*, Book I, Prop. 44.</note></term>, **K F** is equal to **F G**. Therefore, the two surfaces **G F** and **F K** consist of **G C** times twice **C A**. And the square of **G C** is **F H**, from the corollary of the fourth [proposition] of the <term>second [book] of the Elements<note>Euclid II.4, which states that $(a+b)^2 = a^2 + b^2 + 2ab$.</note></term>. Thus the proposition is clear. If, therefore, **A D** becomes 1 <term>square-square<note>original: "q̄d'q̄d^m"; $x^4$.</note></term>, and **C D** as well as **D E** become 3 squares, and **D F** is 9, then **B A** will necessarily be 1 square and **B C** will be 3. Since we then wish to add some **squares** to **D C** and **D E**, and they were **C L** and **K M**, the surface **L N M** will be necessary to complete the whole square; this, as has been demonstrated, consists of the square of **G C**, the number of halved squares.

<summary>Cardano lists various forms of quartic equations and begins a geometric demonstration for "completing the square" (or "completing the square-square") using Euclidean geometry. This method provides the logical foundation for solving higher-order equations by visualizing them as areas and volumes.</summary>
<keywords>quartic equations, square-square, Euclid's Elements, geometric algebra, gnomon, Girolamo Cardano, Ars Magna, mathematical proof</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical demonstration of solving fourth-degree equations (biquadratic equations), following the geometric proofs of how to manipulate "square-squares" (x⁴) and "squares" (x²) by adding terms to both sides to complete a perfect square.</meta>

# GIROLAMO CARDANO
<meta>catchword: -torm (of the [squares])</meta>

For **c l** is the surface from **g c** into **a b**, as has been shown, and **a b** is 1 **square-square** <note>original: "qd'qdratum"; the fourth power of the unknown, $x^4$.</note>, because we posit **a b** [as] 1 **square-square**; moreover, **f l** and **m n** are formed from **g c** into **c b**, from the 42nd [proposition] of the first [book] of the *Elements* <note>Referring to Euclid's Elements, which Cardano uses to justify his geometric movements of algebraic areas.</note>, wherefore the surface **l n m**, which is the number to be added, is formed from **g c** into double **c b**—that is, into the number of **squares** <note>original: "quadratorum"; $x^2$.</note>, which was 6—and **g c** into itself—that is, by the number of **squares** added; and this is our demonstration.

<margin>4</margin>
Having completed this, always reduce the **square-square** part to a **root** <note>original: "℞" or "radix".</note>, that is, by adding so much to both sides so that 1 **square-square** with the **square** and the number has a [square] root; this is easy when you have placed half the number of **squares** [as] the root of the number. Likewise, ensure that the outer denominations <note>The leading and trailing terms of the equation.</note> are "plus" in both equations; for otherwise, a <term>trinomial<note>An algebraic expression with three terms.</note></term>, or a binomial reduced to a trinomial, would necessarily lack a root.

<margin>5</margin>
Once these things are finished, you add so much of the **squares** and the number to one side, through the third rule, so that the same [amount] added to the other side—in which there will be **things** <note>original: "res"; the linear unknown $x$.</note>—forms a trinomial having a square **root** through the <term>position<note>original: "positionem"; the algebraic method of setting up an unknown.</note></term>. You will then have the number of **squares** and the number to be added to each side. Once this is obtained, you extract the square **root** from both; which will be, in one [side], 1 **square** plus a number, or minus a number; from the other, 1 **position** or more, plus a number, or minus a number, or a number minus **positions**. Wherefore, through the fifth chapter of this [book], you have the solution.

### QUESTION V.

**Example.** Divide 10 into three proportional parts, from the multiplication of the first into the second of which, 6 is produced. This [problem] was proposed by **Johannes Colla**, and he said it could not be solved; I, however, said it could be solved, though I was ignorant of the method until **Ferrari** <note>Lodovico Ferrari, Cardano's student, who discovered the general solution for the quartic equation.</note> found it. You place, therefore, the middle [part] as 1 **position**; the first will be $\frac{6}{1\ pos.}$, and the third will be $\frac{1}{6}$ of a **cube** <note>original: "cubi"; $x^3$.</note>. Wherefore these are equated to 10. By multiplying everything by 6 **positions**, we will have 60 **positions** equal to 1 **square-square** plus 6 **squares** plus 36. Add, according to the fifth rule, 6 **squares** to each side; you will have 1 **square-square** plus 12 **squares** plus 36, equal to 6 **squares** plus 60 **positions**. For if equals are added to equals, the wholes will be equal. Now, 1 **square-square** plus 12 **squares** plus 36 has a root,

<margin>
and it is 1 **square** plus 6; if 6 **squares** plus 60 **positions** also had [a root], we would already
</margin>

| | |
| :--- | :--- |
| 1 sq.-sq. plus 6 sq. plus 36 | equal to 60 pos. |
| ->6 sq.<- | ->6 sq.<- |
| 1 sq.-sq. plus 12 sq. plus 36 | equal to 6 sq. plus 60 pos. |
| ->2 pos.<- | ->1 sq. plus 12 pos.<- |

have the business [settled]; but they do not have [a root]. Therefore, a certain number of **squares** and a number are to be added to each side, so that in the former a trinomial having a root is left, and in the other it is created. Let therefore the number of **squares**...

<summary>Cardano explains how to complete the square for fourth-degree equations and presents a problem once thought impossible by Johannes Colla, solved by his student Lodovico Ferrari. The method involves adding terms to both sides of an equation to create perfect square trinomials.</summary>

-># ON ARITHMETIC, BOOK 10<-

<margin>T 2</margin>
<meta>catchword: [of the] squares,</meta>

...of the [squares] 1 **position** <note>original: "positio"; Cardano uses "position" or "thing" to represent the unknown variable, our modern $x$.</note>, and because, as you see in the figure of the third rule, **cl** and **mk** are formed from double **gc** into **ab**, and **gc** is 1 **position**, I will always set the number of **squares** <note>original: "quadratorum"; $x^2$.</note> to be added as 2 **positions**, that is, double **gc**. And because the number to be added to 36 is **lnm**, and therefore the **square** of **gc** with that which is produced from **gc** doubled into **cb**, or from **gc** into double **cb**—and this is 12, the number of the former **squares**—I will therefore multiply 1 **position** (the half of the number of **squares** added) always into the number of the former **squares** and into itself. There will then be 1 **square** plus 12 **positions** to be added on the other side, and also 2 **positions** for the number of **squares**. We have therefore, according to common notions <note>original: "ex communi animi sententia"; a reference to the "Common Notions" or axioms in Euclid's Elements.</note>, the quantities written below, which are equal to each other, and both have a root: the first from the third rule, but the second...

<insert>
<margin>
...quantity from the supposition; therefore, the first part of the **trinomial** <note>An expression with three terms, e.g., $ax^2 + bx + c$.</note> multiplied by the third becomes the **square** of half the second part.
</margin>
1 **square-square** plus 2 **positions** plus 12 **squares** plus 1 **square** plus 12 **positions** of the added number plus 36 [are] equal.
2 **positions** [of] 6 **squares**, plus 60 **positions**, plus 1 **square**, plus 12 **positions** of the added number.
</insert>

...of the **trinomial**. Because, therefore, from half of the second part [multiplied] into itself, 900 **squares** are produced, and from the first into the third, there are 2 **cubes** plus 30 **squares** plus 72 **positions** of the **squares**, it will be similar by reducing through the **squares** <note>original: "deprimendo per quadrata"; dividing the terms of the proportion by $x^2$ to lower the degree.</note>. Since equals divided by equals produce equals, 2 **cubes** plus 30 **squares** plus 72 **positions** are equal to 900; wherefore 1 **cube** plus 15 **squares** plus 36 **positions** are equal to 450.

It suffices, therefore, in leading it to the rule, to always have 1 **cube** plus the number of former **squares** with its fourth part added to it, plus a number of **positions** such as the first number of the equation. For example, if we had 1 **square-square** plus 12 **squares** plus 36 equal to 6 **squares** plus 60 **positions**, we will have 1 **cube** plus 15 **squares** plus 36 **positions** equal to 450 (which is half of the **square** of half the number of **positions**). And if we had 1 **square-square** plus 16 **squares** plus 64 equal to 80 **positions**, we would have 1 **cube** plus 20 **squares** plus 64 **positions** equal to 800. And if we had 1 **square-square** plus 20 **squares** plus 100 equal to 80 **positions**, we would have 1 **cube** plus 25 **squares** plus 100 **positions** equal to 800. 

Therefore, having this, in the former example we had 1 **cube** plus 15 **squares** plus 36 **positions** equal to 450. Therefore the solution for the thing <note>original: "rei aestimatio"; the numerical value of the unknown $x$.</note>, through the 17th chapter, is the universal cubic root <note>original: "Rz v: cubica"; $Rz$ stands for Radix (Root), $v$ for Universalis. This refers to the cubic formula Cardano published earlier in the book.</note> of $287 \frac{1}{2} + \sqrt{80449 \frac{1}{4}}$, plus the universal cubic root of $287 \frac{1}{2} - \sqrt{80449 \frac{1}{4}}$, minus 5. This, therefore, is the number of **squares** which, doubled, is to be added to each side, because 2 things <note>The auxiliary variable used to complete the square.</note> are supposed to be added; and the number to be added on each side, from the demonstration, is the **square** of this, together with that which is produced from this into 12, the number of the **squares**...

...[of the squares], it is evident, however, that the **square root** <term>radix; the side of a square or the solution to an equation</term> of the first aggregate is always 1 **square** <term>quadratum; $x^2$</term> plus half the number of **squares**, without anything else—that is, plus the **universal cube root** <note>original: "℞ v: cubica"; a root applied to the entire following expression</note> of 287 1/2 plus the **root** of 80449 1/4, plus the **universal cube root** of 287 1/2 minus the **root** of 80449 1/4, plus 1. And this is because half of the previous number of **squares** was 6, and in the added **trinomial** it was minus 5; therefore the total was, as I said. 

But the remaining part was 6 **squares** plus double this number; therefore the number of **squares** was the **universal cube root** of 2300 plus the **root** of 5148752, plus the **universal cube root** of 2300 minus the **root** of 5148752, minus 4. And the number of **things** <term>res; the unknown variable $x$</term> by supposition was 60, and the **number** <term>numerus; the constant term in an equation</term> is (as has been shown) the **square** of the said quantity, plus twelve times that same quantity. 

However, because by supposition, from the number of **squares** multiplied by the **number** of the equation, the **square** of half the number of **things** is produced, therefore by dividing 900 (the **square** of half the number of **things**) by the number of **squares**, the **number** will result. # ON ARITHMETIC BOOK XI.

...of the things, and you will have the thing which was the mean of the proportional quantities sought.

### QUESTION VI.

Find a number which is equal to its **square root** <term>radix quadrata; the number multiplied by itself to produce the original</term> and to two **cube roots** <term>radix cubica; the number multiplied by itself three times to produce the original</term> added together. You will say, therefore, if such a number were a **square-cube** <term>cu’quadratum; the sixth power ($x^6$)</term>, its **square root** is necessarily 1 **cube** <term>cubus; $x^3$</term>, and the two **cube roots** are 2 **squares** <term>quadrata; $x^2$</term>. Therefore, 1 **square-cube** will be equal to 1 **cube** plus 2 **squares**. Reducing this to lower denominations by dividing by the **square**, it becomes 1 **square-square** <term>quadratum-quadratum; the fourth power ($x^4$)</term> equal to 1 **position** <term>positio; the unknown variable $x$</term> plus 2.

I have set it as 2 **cube roots**, for although the rule is general, this can nevertheless be solved in two ways, as will be shown. For if 1 **square-square** equals 1 **position** plus 2, then 1 **square-square** minus 1 will equal 1 **position** plus 1 (since if equals are taken from equals, the remainders are equal). Divide both of these by 1 **position** plus 1, which is a common divisor; you will have 1 **cube** minus 1 **square** plus 1 **position** minus 1, equal to 1. Therefore, 1 **cube** plus 1 **position** equals 1 **square** plus 2. Thus, from the 18th chapter, the **value of the thing** <term>rei æstimatio; the solution for $x$</term> is the **universal cube root** <note>original: "Rz v: cubica"; a root applied to the whole following expression</note> of the **root** of 2241/2916 plus 47/54, minus the **universal cube root** of the **root** of 2241/2916 minus 47/54, plus 1/3. And the **square-cube** of this is the number sought, whose **square root** and 2 **cube roots** are equal to it; and such roots are double the **square** of this quantity together with its **cube**.

| 1 **square-square** minus 1 |
| :--- |
| 1 **position** plus 1 |
| 1 **position** plus 1 |
| 1 **cube** minus 1 **square** plus 1 **position** minus 1 |
| 1 |

But by the general rule, we will proceed as follows: because 1 **square-square** equals 1 **position** plus 2, we will add to each side 2 "positions of squares" <note>This refers to an auxiliary variable used to complete the square in Ferrari's method</note>, which we have labeled as **sq.** so you may understand they are not of the same kind as the previous denominations, but are **positions of squares**. Therefore, the number to be added is 1 **square** of the number of **squares**, and this is (as in the third rule of this chapter) the **square** DF; for here the addition of supplements is like DC, AC, DE, to the simple **square** AD. Therefore, it is sufficient to add the **square** DF without the addition of the surfaces FL and MN, which were necessary in the example of the fifth question.

Therefore, once 2 **positions** plus 1 **square** of the number of **squares** are added to 1 **position** plus 2, the total becomes 2 **positions** of the number of **squares** plus 1 **position** plus 2, plus 1 **square** of the number of **squares**. And for this to have a **root**, it is necessary that the **square** of half the middle quantity (which is 1 **position**) be equal to:

-># THE WORKS OF GIROLAMO CARDANO<-
->### [BOOK XI]<-

<margin>Signature: Q v</margin>
...to the product of the extremes, therefore 1/4 of a **square** <term>quadratum; $x^2$</term>, will be equal to the square of 2 **cubes** <term>cubus; $x^3$</term> plus 4 **positions** <term>positio; the unknown variable $x$</term> of the former number. Wherefore, by removing the squares from both sides, it will be 1/4 equal to 2 **cubes** plus 4 **positions**, and 1/8 equal to 1 **cube** plus 2 **positions**. Wherefore the **value of the thing** <term>rei æstimatio; the solution for the unknown</term> is the **universal cube root** <note>original: "R v: cubica"; a root applied to the entire following expression</note> of the square root of 2075/6912 plus 1/16, minus the **universal cube root** of the square root of 2075/6912 minus 1/16. This, therefore, is the number of **squares** to be added to each side, and it is doubled, and the square of this will be the number to be added to each side; and for the sake of clearer understanding, I have placed it here:

First: 1 **square-square** <term>quadratum-quadratum; the fourth power $x^4$</term> plus **square** times the universal cube root of the square root of 19 and 23/2075 plus 1/2, minus the universal cube root of the square root of 19 and 23/2075 minus 1/2, plus the number which is the universal cube root of 1051/3456 plus the square root of 2075/442368, plus the universal cube root of 1051/3456 minus the square root of 2075/442368, minus 1 and 1/2.

Second: **square** times the universal cube root of the square root of 19 and 23/2075 plus 1/2, minus the universal cube root of the square root of 19 and 23/2075 minus 1/2, plus 1 **position**, plus the number which is the universal cube root of 1051/3456 plus the square root of 2075/442368, plus the universal cube root of 1051/3456 minus the square root of 2075/442368, plus 2/3.

It is manifest, therefore, that the root of the first is 1 **square** plus the universal cube root of the square root of 2075/6912 plus 1/16, minus the universal cube root of the square root of 2075/6912 minus 1/16; and the root of the second is the **unknown** [res] times the **universal root** <note>original: "R v^ma"; the root of the following multi-part expression</note> of the universal cube root of the square root of 19 and 23/2075 plus 1/2, minus the universal cube root of the square root of 19 and 23/2075 minus 1/2, plus the number which is the universal root of the universal cube root of 1051/3456 plus the square root of 2075/442368, plus the universal cube root of 1051/3456 minus the square root of 2075/442368, plus 2/3. And this is because, as I said, the side of the square is composed of its extreme sides, without further mention of the supplements. It is also manifest that the number which is with the 1 **square** is less than the number which is with the **positions**; therefore we will have 1 **square** equal to the **positions** times the universal root of the universal cube root of the square root of 19 and 23/2075 plus 1/2, minus the universal cube root of the square root of 19 and 23/2075 minus 1/2, plus the number, which is this universal root of the universal cube root of 1051/3456 plus the square root of 2075/442368, plus the universal cube root of 1051/3456 minus the square root of 2075/442368, plus 2/3, minus the universal cube root of the square root of 2075/6912 plus 1/16, minus the universal cube root of the square root of 2075/6912 minus 1/16. Wherefore we shall multiply half of the number of **positions** by itself—which is the same as multiplying the whole by itself and then taking a fourth part of the product—and it is half of the last universal root which was subtracted above; therefore, when added, there remains the whole composite number: the universal root of the universal cube root of 1051/3456 plus the square root of 2075/442368, plus the universal cube root of 1051/3456 minus the square root of 2075/442368, plus 2/3, minus the universal cube root of the square root of 2075/442368 plus 1/128, minus the universal cube root of the square root of 2075/442368 minus 1/128; and the universal root of this whole, added to half the number of **positions**—that is, to this number, the universal root of the universal cube root of the square root of 2075/442368 plus 1/128, minus the universal cube root of the square root of 2075/442368 minus 1/128—constitutes the **thing** <note>the final solution for the variable $x$</note>.

And if it had been said that the proposed number were equal to the square root and the cube root taken together, it could not have been solved except by this second method, through the general rule. However, how to reduce equal values to the same form, as with the first value to the second, I have already taught you in the book on **irrational quantities** <term>quantitates irrationales; numbers like roots that cannot be expressed as simple fractions</term>, although the operation is most difficult. Therefore, the completion of these operations is like the extreme limit to which the perfection of the human intellect—or rather of the imagination—reaches; for in this you will recognize the difference between them.

<summary>Cardano concludes a highly complex algebraic solution for a quartic equation, providing the final value of the unknown through nested universal roots. He remarks that such operations represent the very limit of human intellectual and imaginative capability.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of algebraic resolutions from the previous page, moving from the specific solution of a quartic equation to new problems (Questions VII and VIII) that demonstrate the transformation and reduction of complex equations involving roots and proportions.</meta>

<page-num>76</page-num>
-># ON ARITHMETIC, BOOK 10:<-

### QUESTION VII.

If someone were to say, find a number which, when multiplied by its **cube root** <term>radix cubica; $\sqrt[3]{x}$</term> plus 6, makes 64, you would say then, by setting that number as 1 **cube** <term>cubus; $x^3$</term>, we will have 1 **square-square** <term>quadratum quadratum; $x^4$</term> plus 6 **cubes** equal to 64. Wherefore, by the seventh rule of transformation in the seventh chapter of this book, we will have 1 **square-square** equal to 6 **things** <term>res; the unknown $x$</term> plus 4. Then, having obtained the **value** <term>aestimatio; the solution</term> from this chapter by the ninth rule of the same chapter, we will have what was intended. And to some, these operations will seem so difficult that they will scarcely believe them to be true; however, we have shown the method by which these **irrational quantities** <note>Numbers that cannot be expressed as simple fractions, often involving roots.</note> equivalent to numbers may be reduced to numbers, and we have given both demonstrations: the **Geometric** from the cause, and the **Arithmetic** from the effect.

### QUESTION VIII.

Divide 6 into three **proportional parts** <note>A geometric progression where $a/b = b/c$, or $ac = b^2$.</note>, of which the squares of the first and second joined together make 4. We will set the first as 1 **position** <term>positio; the unknown $x$</term>; its square is 1 **square**. Therefore, the remainder to 4 is the square of the second quantity, which is 4 minus 1 **square**; take the root of this, and subtract 1 **position** from 6, and you will have the third quantity, as you see. Wherefore, having multiplied the first by the third...

<margin>
...the first into the third, you will have 6 **positions** minus 1 **square** minus the **universal root** <term>radix universalis; $\sqrt{...}$</term> of 4 **squares** minus 1 **square-square** equal to 4 minus 1 **square-square** of the second; discard 1 **square** minus from [the parts]...
</margin>

---
->1 **pos.** | **univ. root:** 4 **m:** 1 **sq.** | 6 **m:** 1 **pos. m: univ. root:** 4 **m:** 1 **sq.**<-
---
->6 **pos. m:** 1 **sq. m: univ. root:** 4 **sq. m:** 1 **sq.-sq.**<-
---
->4 | 6 **pos. m: univ. root:** 4 **sq. m:** 1 **sq.-sq.**<-
---
->6 **pos. m:** 4 **equal to univ. root:** 4 **sq. m:** 1 **sq.-sq.**<-
---
->36 **sq. p:** 16 **m:** 48 **pos. are equal to**<-
->4 **sq. m:** 1 **sq.-sq.**<-
---
->32 **sq. p:** 16 **p:** 1 **sq.-sq. equal to** 48 **pos.**<-
---
->1 **sq.-sq. p:** 32 **sq. p:** 256 **equal to** 48<-
->**pos. p:** 240<-
---

...you will have from the parts, you will have 4 equal to 6 **positions** minus the **universal root** of 4 **squares** minus 1 **square-square**. Wherefore 6 **positions** minus 4 are equal to the **universal root** of 4 **squares** minus 1 **square-square**. Therefore, the squares of these are also equal; from which, discard 4 common **squares** from each side, and you will finally have 32 **squares** plus 16 plus 1 **square-square**, equal to 48 **positions**. Wherefore, by adding 240 to each side—which is the remainder of the square of half the number of **squares** <note>Cardano is completing the square. Half of 32 is 16; $16^2 = 256$. Since he already has 16, he adds 240 to reach 256.</note>—you will have 1 **square-square** plus 32 **squares** plus 256, equal to 48 **positions** plus 240. You will then add 2 **positions of squares** <note>This refers to the term $2yx^2$ used in Ferrari’s method for solving quartics.</note> plus 1 **square** plus 32 **positions** of the number of **squares** to each side; the first part will therefore necessarily have a root, and because we want the second also to have one, which is 2 **positions of squares** plus 48 **positions**, from the previous...

### QUESTION IX.

Find a number, whose **square-square** <term>quadratum quadratum; $x^4$</term>, with four times itself, and 8 is equal to ten times its **square**, we will say therefore 1 **square-square** plus 4 **positions** plus 8, are equal to 10 **squares**. Wherefore we will always give the **positions** to the **squares**, and we will take them away from the **square-square**, and we will have 1 **square-square** plus 8, equal to 10 **squares** minus 4 **positions**, and because we see the number of **squares** to be large, and the **things** <gloss>the linear terms</gloss> small, we will therefore strive to decrease the number of **squares** rather than increase it, and we will make it so the reduction is 2 **squares** from both sides; for one must almost always begin from a smaller amount, namely from 2 **squares**, because it is not proper that you should come to a negative number <note>original: "m:" for minus</note> of **squares** on the side of the **things**, because then they would not have a **root**; having subtracted 2 **squares** from each side, therefore, you will have:

...you will have 1 **square-square** <term>quadrato-quadratum; a term for $x^4$</term> minus 2 **squares** <term>quadratum; $x^2$</term> plus 8, equal to 8 **squares** minus 4 **positions** <term>positio; the unknown $x$, also called the "thing"</term>. It is clear, however, that if 1 **square-square** minus 2 **squares** is to have a **root** <term>radix; here meaning to form a perfect square trinomial</term>, it is necessary that the number be plus 1; but it was plus 8, therefore it will be necessary to take away 7 from both sides. We will have, therefore, 1 **square-square** minus 2 **squares** plus 1, equal to 8 **squares** minus 4 **positions** minus 7. 

We will then add by "minus," as was said, 2 **positions of squares** <note>Cardano is introducing a new variable, often represented as $y$ in modern notation, to both sides to facilitate solving the fourth-degree equation.</note> to the remaining 2 **minus squares** according to the rule; and we will add by "plus," as in the same rule, 1 **square** plus 2 **positions** to the number 1 on both sides. Wherefore we will have equal parts, for those things which are added and subtracted are equal. Therefore, 8 minus 2 **positions of squares** minus 4 **positions**, plus 1 **square** plus 2 **positions** minus 7 of the number, have a root. By multiplying, therefore, the first part, which is 8 minus 2 **positions of squares**, by the third, which is 1 **square** plus 2 **positions** minus 7, that results which you see to the side for the number of **squares**; and this must be equal to 4 **squares**, which is the number produced from [multiplying] half of the middle part by itself. Wherefore, by casting out the **squares** on both sides, that multinomial will be made equal to 4. Finally, with the parts reduced to their like terms, there will be 2 **cubes** <term>cubus; $x^3$</term> plus 60, equal to 4 **squares** plus 30 **positions**; and 1 **cube** plus 30, equal to 2 **squares** plus 15 **positions**. Wherefore the "thing" <note>The value of the newly introduced variable $y$.</note> is worth 2, either by the [rule in the] chapter, or even by testing through common sense alone.

|:---|:---|:---|
| 8 minus 2 **pos. of sq.** | 4 **pos.** | 1 **sq.** plus 2 **pos.** minus 7 |
| 4 **squares** | | 8 minus 2 **pos.** |

| plus 14 **pos. of sq.** | | |
| --- | --- | --- |

| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 **cube** plus 30 equals 2 **sq.** plus 15 **pos.** | | |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **pos.** 2 | | |

<margin>Note.</margin>
Regarding this, three things must be noted. First, that I reduced the matter to a numerical experiment so that you might see the truth of the matter more easily, for it is foolish always to add difficulty to difficulty. Second, that 1 **cube** plus 30 equal to 2 **squares** plus 15 **things** <note>Cardano uses "positions" and "things" interchangeably for the unknown.</note> has another **value** <term>aestimatio; a numerical solution</term> for the thing besides 2, which is known from its own chapter; but for now, lest the operation becomes too long, we leave it aside. The third thing to be noted is that you see the demonstration holds just as well in "minus" <note>original: "m:"</note> as in "plus" <note>original: "p:"</note>; and that the number is always necessarily to be added, because it arises from the square of the number of squares with the number of the prior squares—whether the squares are to be added or subtracted—multiplied by half the number of the squares to be subtracted. This being established, we said that the value of the thing is 2, and 2 **things** are to be added by "minus" of the **squares**; therefore we will diminish [by] 4 **squares** from both sides. We will have, therefore, 1 **square-square** minus 6 **squares** plus 1, equal to 4 **squares**—

...[from the previous page: 8 squares] minus 4 **positions** minus 7. Similarly, the product of the number of the prior **squares** by the **estimation of the thing** <note>The "estimation of the thing" refers to the value of the unknown variable $x$.</note>, which product is 4, must be added. It is clear, however, that both of these have double **roots** <note>In this context, "roots" refers to the square roots of the expressions on each side of the equation, which are perfect square trinomials.</note>, as you see. ->QUESTION X.<-

Find three proportional numbers <note>A series of three numbers in geometric progression, such as $a, ar, ar^2$.</note> whose sum is 8, and the **square** of the third is equal to the sum of the **squares** of the first and second. We will set them according to the first rule: 1, 1 **position**, 1 **square** <note>The three numbers are $1, x,$ and $x^2$.</note>. Their **squares** will therefore be 1, 1 **square**, 1 **square-square**. Thus, 1 **square-square** is equal to 1 **square** plus 1. Wherefore, from the 24th chapter on derivative [equations], we will have the **estimation of the thing** as the universal **root** of: the **root** of $1\frac{1}{4}$ plus $\frac{1}{2}$. And the third quantity is its **square**, namely the **root** of $1\frac{1}{4}$ plus $\frac{1}{2}$. And the first was 1; therefore the entire sum is $1\frac{1}{2}$ plus the **root**...

$1 \frac{1}{2}$ plus the **root** of $1 \frac{1}{4}$ plus the <term>universal root</term><note>Latin: "Rz v" (radix universalis). Multiply 8 by 1, which makes 8; divide 8 by $1 \frac{1}{2}$ plus the **root** of $1 \frac{1}{4}$ plus the **universal root** of the **root** of $1 \frac{1}{4}$ plus $\frac{1}{2}$, and the result is 4 plus the **universal root** of the **root** of 500 plus 10 minus the **universal root** of the **root** of 1920 plus 18, and this is the first quantity. Having obtained this, if you multiply it by the **root** of $1 \frac{1}{4}$ plus $\frac{1}{2}$, you will have the third quantity; if you then multiply this third quantity by the first of the quantities last found, the **universal root** of the product is the second quantity. And do not wonder that I place the third quantity before the second in the operation, because it is much simpler.
