## QUESTION XI.

If someone says: find a number which, when multiplied by its cubic **root** minus 3, makes 64. You will set that number as 1 **cube** <note>If the number is $x^3$, its cubic root is simply $x$.</note>; therefore, multiplied by its cubic **root** minus 3, it produces 1 **square-square** <note>The "square-square" is the fourth power, $x^4$.</note> minus 3 **cubes**, equal to 64. Therefore, 1 **square-square** minus 3 **cubes** is equated to 64. I say that we can solve this by the method of the seventh question, and also in another way, without <term>transmutation</term><note>Transmutation refers to transforming the equation into a different form through substitution.</note>, by which the seventh question can also be solved, and more easily; but I wanted to teach both ways so that you might better know how to operate. 

You ought to know two things. First, that just as the "things" <note>The "things" (res) refer to the unknown $x$.</note> must always remain on the other side from where the number and the **squares** are, and not on the side of the **square-square**, so the **cubes** (whether plus or minus) must remain with the **square-square**. Second, that just as the number of "things" must never be varied, so neither should the number of **cubes**. And we can add a third to these: namely, that where there are "things," we arrive at 1 **square-square** plus **squares** plus a number, equal to **squares**, "things" (plus or minus), and a number; so here we arrive at a **square-square** plus **squares** plus a number, equal to **square-squares**, **cubes** (plus or minus), and **squares**.

With this understood, the question is solved thus: you will add to the number 2 <term>positions</term><note>Cardano uses "positions" (positiones) here as auxiliary variables to complete the square, similar to the modern $y$ in Ferrari's method for quartics.</note> of **squares**; therefore, half of it multiplied by itself makes 1 **square** of the number of **square-squares**; therefore, dividing that by 64, you have $\frac{1}{64}$ of the **square** of the number of **square-squares**. Wherefore you see that you have added to have the root:

| $\frac{1}{64}$ **square** plus 1 | minus 3 **cubes** | plus 2 **positions** | $\frac{1}{64}$ **square** | 2 **positions** | 64 |
|:---|:---|:---|:---|:---|:---|
| **square-square** | | **square** | **square-square** | **square** | |

the **root** of $\frac{1}{8}$ **square** for the number of the **square-square**, and 2 **positions** for the number of the **square**. Therefore you will add the same to 1 **square-square** minus 3 **cubes**, and you will have $\frac{1}{64}$ of the **square** plus 1 for the number of the **square-square**, and minus 3 **cubes** and 2 **positions** for the number of the **square**. Therefore, for this to have a **root**, it is necessary that the extremes multiplied by each other produce as much as the square of half the middle quantity. Moreover, half is $1 \frac{1}{2}$ **cubes**, which multiplied by itself produces $2 \frac{1}{4}$ <term>cube-squares</term><note>Latin: "cu'quadrata", representing $x^6$.</note>, and $\frac{1}{64}$ of the **square** plus 1 of the number of the **square-square** times 2 **positions**...

-># GIROLAMO CARDANO<-

...of the **square number** produces $\frac{1}{32}$ of a **cube** plus 2 <term>positions</term><note>Latin: "positionibus." In Cardano's algebra, this refers to the unknown variable $x$.</note> of the **cubo-square number**<note>Latin: "cu'quadrati," meaning $x^6$, the square of a cube.</note>, for a **biquadrate**<note>Latin: "qd'qdratum," meaning $x^4$.</note> multiplied by a **square** produces a **cubo-square**; you have therefore $\frac{1}{32}$ of a **cube** plus 2 **positions** of the **cubo-square** number equal to $2 \frac{1}{4}$ **cubo-squares**. Therefore, just as a **cubo-square** is to a **cubo-square** in equality, so is a number to a number; wherefore $\frac{1}{32}$ of a **cube** plus 2 **positions** is equal to $2 \frac{1}{4}$. Thus, 1 **cube** plus 64 **positions** is equal to 72. Wherefore the <term>estimate of the thing</term><note>Latin: "rei aestimatio." This refers to the value of the unknown $x$.</note> is: the **universal cubic root** of the **root** of $11005 \frac{1}{27}$ plus 36, minus the **universal cubic root** of the **root** of $11005 \frac{1}{27}$ minus 36. And double this will be added to each part for the **squares**; therefore, the **root** on one side is 8 plus the **things** under the number of the estimate of the thing; on the other side, however, the **squares** under the number of the **universal root** of $\frac{64}{27}$ of the **square** of this estimate, with 1 added, minus the **positions** under the number of the **root** of the double of this estimate.

## QUESTION XII.

If someone says: 1 **biquadrate** plus 3 is equal to 12 **things** ($x^4 + 3 = 12x$), you add 2 **positions** of **squares** and 1 **square** of the number of **squares**<note>This is the "Ferrari Method," where one adds $2yx^2 + y^2$ to complete the square on the left side.</note>; wherefore it will thus have a **square root** without a number, as is clear. Therefore, we shall add from the other side 2 **positions** for the number of **squares**, and for the number 1 **square** minus 3; you will have the parts as you see:

| | | | |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 1 **biquadrate** plus 3 | | 12 **positions** | |
| 2 **pos. of squares** plus 1 **sq.** minus 3 | | | |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 **biquadrate** plus 2 **pos.** plus 1 **sq.** | 2 **pos.** | 12 | 1 **sq.** minus 3 |
| 1 **biquadrate** plus 6 **squares** plus 9 | 6 **squares** | plus 12 **pos.** | plus 6 |

Wherefore, the parts having been multiplied, you have 2 **cubes** equal to 6 **things** plus 36; and 1 **cube** equal to 3 **things** plus 18 ($y^3 = 3y + 18$), and the **thing** is worth 3. Therefore the parts are as you see, and 1 **square** plus 3 will be the **root** of the first part, equal to the **things** of the **root** of 6 plus the number of the **root** of 6. And the **thing** sought will be: the **universal root** of the **root** of 6 minus $1 \frac{1}{2}$, plus the **root** of $1 \frac{1}{2}$.

## QUESTION XIII.

Find a number whose **biquadrate** with double the **cube** is 1 plus the number itself ($x^4 + 2x^3 = x + 1$). Therefore you say: 1 **biquadrate** plus 2 **cubes** are equal to 1 **position** plus 1. Here, there is no place given for subtracting a **root**, nor for division. But you say from the first rule: find three proportional numbers whose sum has the same ratio to the sum of the second and third, as the sum of the second and third has to the first. You will therefore set them as 1, 1 **position**, 1 **square**. You will have therefore 1 **biquadrate** plus 2 **cubes** plus 1 **square** equal to 1 **square** plus 1 **position** plus 1. Wherefore, having cast out the common 1 **square**, we will have 1 **biquadrate** plus 2 **cubes** equal to 1 **position** plus 1. Therefore, we now know the ratio of the quantities; because indeed from the sum [multiplied] by the first, the **square** of the sum of the second and third is produced; therefore such a sum is divided according to the <term>proportion having a mean and two extremes</term><note>This refers to the "Golden Ratio." Cardano is recognizing that the structure of the equation mimics the geometric division of a line into extreme and mean ratio.</note>, and its smaller portion is 1; therefore the remainder (and it is the larger portion) is the **root** of $1 \frac{1}{4}$ plus $\frac{1}{2}$, and this is equal (as is supposed) to 1 **square** plus 1 **position**. Therefore the quantities are as you see.

<summary>Cardano demonstrates the solution to quartic equations (fourth-degree) using the method of completing the square, applying it to problems where $x^4$ is related to $x^3, x^2,$ and $x$. He utilizes the Golden Ratio (mean and extreme ratio) to solve Question 13.</summary>

# ON ARITHMETIC, BOOK X.

Therefore, of the middle quantity (which is the <term>thing</term><note>Latin: "res." This is the unknown variable, which we call $x$ today.</note>), 1 **biquadrate**<note>Latin: "qd'q'dratum," meaning $x^4$.</note> plus 2 **cubes** are equal to the quantity itself plus 1, that is:
| | |
|---|---|
| 1st | first |
| 2nd thing | The **root** of the **universal root** of $1 \frac{1}{4}$ plus $\frac{3}{4}$, minus $\frac{1}{2}$ |
| 3rd square | The **root** of $1 \frac{1}{4}$ plus 1, minus the **root** of the **universal root** of $1 \frac{1}{4}$ plus $\frac{3}{4}$ |

You will understand the methods of these rules if you diligently observe these examples along with their operations.

->On the modes of general suppositions pertaining to the Great Art, and on rules which are outside the common order, and on <term>estimates</term><note>Latin: "aestimationibus." In this context, an estimate is the numerical solution or value of the unknown variable.</note> of a different kind from those which have been mentioned. Chapter XL.<-

<margin>1st.</margin>
**W**hen a **cube** is equal to **squares** and a number ($x^3 = ax^2 + n$), if the number of **squares** is subtracted from that **estimate** [the solution], there will remain the **estimate** of a **cube** and the same number of **squares** ($y^3 + ay^2 = m$), which are equal to a number existing in the same proportion with the number of the first equation as the second **estimate** is to the first **estimate**. 

For example: a **cube** is equal to 2 **squares** plus $1 \frac{17}{64}$ ($x^3 = 2x^2 + 1 \frac{17}{64}$), and the **estimate** [the value of $x$] is $2 \frac{1}{4}$. I say that if you subtract 2 (the number of **squares**), there remains $\frac{1}{4}$. This is the **estimate** of a **cube** and 2 **squares** equal to $\frac{9}{64}$ ($y^3 + 2y^2 = \frac{9}{64}$). This number [$\frac{9}{64}$] is in the same proportion with $1 \frac{17}{64}$ (the number of the prior equation) as $\frac{1}{4}$ (the second **estimate**) is to $2 \frac{1}{4}$ (the first **estimate**). The demonstration of this is as follows.

### DEMONSTRATION.

Let $AB$ be the first **estimate**, and $AC$ the number of **squares**. Then $BC$ will be the **estimate** of some **cube** and **squares** according to the number $AC$, which are equal to some number, let it be $E$. Now let $D$ be the number which, with the **squares** of $AB$ according to the number $AC$, is equal to the **cube** of $AB$. 

Because, therefore, the **cube** of $AB$ is equal to the product of $AC$ and $CB$ by the **square** of $AB$ <note>This is a geometric way of saying $x^3 = ax^2 + n$.</note>, and also equal to the product of $AC$ by the **square** of $AB$ plus the number $D$, then $D$ will be equal to the product of $CB$ by the **square** of $AB$. 

In a similar way, the **cube** of $CB$ with the product of $AC$ by the **square** of $CB$ is equal to the number $E$, and is also equal to the product of $AB$ by the **square** of $BC$. Therefore, the product of $AB$ by the **square** of $BC$ is equal to $E$. 

However, the product of $BC$ by the **square** of $AB$ is to the product of $AB$ by the **square** of $BC$ as $AB$ is to $BC$, as demonstrated in the seventh book on Euclid. Therefore, the proportion of $D$ to $E$ is as $AB$ to $BC$, which was to be proved. Similarly, it follows by swapping the proportions that the ratios of the numbers of the equations to their **estimates** are the same when the difference of the **estimates** is the number of the **squares**.

<summary>Cardano explains a rule for transforming one type of cubic equation into another by subtracting the coefficient of the square from the solution. He demonstrates that the constants in these two equations maintain a specific proportion to their respective solutions.</summary>
<keywords>cubic equations, biquadratic, estimation, proportions, Euclid, algebraic transformation, Ars Magna</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Chapter XL of Book 10 of the Ars Magna. Having just discussed the relationship between equations of the form x³ = ax² + n and x³ + ax² = n, Cardano now provides a formal geometric demonstration and introduces rules for cases where squares are equal to a cube and a number (ax² = x³ + n).</meta>

# THE GREAT ART OF HIERONYMUS CARDANUS

<margin>2nd</margin> When there are a **cube** and **squares** equal to a **number** ($x^3 + ax^2 = n$), and likewise a **cube** equal to the same number of **squares** and the same **number** ($y^3 = ay^2 + n$), the ratio of the sum of the first <term>estimate</term><note>Latin: "aestimatio," meaning the numerical solution for the unknown variable.</note> and the number of **squares**, to the remainder produced by subtracting the number of **squares** from the second **estimate**, will be as the square of the ratio of the second **estimate** to the first. For example, if I say: a **cube** and 3 **squares** are equal to 20, and a **cube** is equal to 3 **squares** plus 20. In the first **estimate**, the <term>thing</term><note>Latin: "res," the unknown variable $x$.</note> is 2. In the second, it is the <term>universal cubic root of 11 plus the root of 120, plus the universal cubic root of 11 minus the root of 120, plus 1</term><note>In modern notation: $\sqrt[3]{11 + \sqrt{120}} + \sqrt[3]{11 - \sqrt{120}} + 1$. This is the solution derived from Cardano’s famous formula.</note>. I say that if you add 3 (the number of **squares**) to 2 (the first **estimate**), it becomes 5; and if you subtract the same 3 from the second **estimate**, it becomes the <term>universal cubic root of 11 plus the root of 120, plus the universal cubic root of 11 minus the root of 120, minus 2</term>. The ratio of 5 to this root is the square of the ratio of the second **estimate** to the first **estimate** (which is 2). The demonstration of this matter is as follows:

-> DEMONSTRATION <-

Let the first **estimate** be $BC$, the second $AB$, and the common number of **squares** $AD$. Therefore, because the **cube** of $AB$ is equal to the products of $AD$ and $DB$ into the **square** of $AB$, and $AB$ is the number of **squares**, the product of $DB$ and the **square** of $AB$ will be equal to the number of the equation. Consequently, this is also equal to the **cube** of $BC$ plus the product of $AD$ into the **square** of $BC$. Therefore, that which is produced from $BD$ into the **square** of $AB$ is equal to that which is produced from the sum of $AD$ and $CB$ into the **square** of $CB$. Therefore, from the 34th [proposition] of the 11th [book] and the 7th of the 6th of the *Elements*<note>Cardano is referencing Euclid's Elements to justify the geometric proportions of solids.</note>, the ratio of $AD$ and $CB$ joined together, to $BD$, is as the square of the ratio of $AB$ to $BC$.

<margin>3rd</margin> When **squares** are equal to a **cube** and a **number** ($ax^2 = x^3 + n$), the chapter is converted into the chapter of <term>things</term> equal to a **cube** and a **number** ($x^3 + n = cx$). The second **estimate** is always to be added to or subtracted from <term>one-third the number of squares</term><note>Latin: "tpq̄d." or "tertia pars quadratorum." This is a standard step in Cardano's method to eliminate the quadratic term from an equation.</note> so that the first may be obtained. The method is this: take the difference between the given number of the equation and double the **cube** of <term>one-third the number of squares</term>, and set that as the **number** which, with a **cube**, is equal to as many **things** as the number which is one-third of the **square** of the [original] number of **squares**. Thus, having found the second **estimate**, to obtain the first, you will add it to the <term>one-third the number of squares</term> if the number was greater than double the **cube** of <term>one-third the number of squares</term>; or you will subtract it, if the number was less than double the **cube** of <term>one-third the number of squares</term>. The result or the remainder is the first **estimate**.

Example: A **cube** and 80 are equal to 9 **squares**. Double the **cube** of 3 (which is <term>one-third the number of squares</term>) becomes 54. The difference between this and 80 is 26. Therefore, a **cube** plus 26 will be equal to 27 **things** (27 being one-third of the **square of 9**). Thus the second **estimate** is 1, which, added to 3 (<term>one-third the number of squares</term>), constitutes 4, the first **estimate**, because the number (which is 80) is greater than double the **cube** of <term>one-third the number of squares</term> (which is 54).

Another example: A **cube** plus 5 is equal to 6 **squares**. Multiply 6 by itself, which becomes 36...

<summary>This page outlines geometric proofs and algebraic rules for transforming cubic equations between different forms, specifically focusing on the relationship between solutions (estimates) when squares are involved. Cardano demonstrates how to convert an equation with a squared term into a simpler "depressed" cubic equation using one-third of the coefficient of the squares.</summary>
<keywords>cubic equations, Ars Magna, Girolamo Cardano, geometric demonstration, duplicate ratio, third part of squares, depressed cubic, algebra history</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Chapter 40 in Cardano's *Ars Magna*, where he explores complex cubic equations. # On Arithmetic, Book 10

...results in 36; the third part of this is 12, which is the number of <term>things</term><note>Latin: "rerum." In Renaissance algebra, "things" refers to the linear unknown ($x$).</note>. From this, subtract 5 (the number of the equation) from 16 (which is twice the cube of the second part), and 11 remains. Therefore, 1 cube plus 11 is equal to 12 things. The <term>estimate</term><note>Latin: "aestimatio," meaning the numerical solution or root of the equation.</note> is 1. Subtract 1 from the 2 of the second part, because the number is less than twice the cube of the second part; there remains the estimate for a cube plus 5 equals 6 squares.

-> DEMONSTRATION <-

The demonstration of this is as follows: let **AB** be the number of squares, 9, and let **AC** be the <term>estimate of the thing</term><note>The value of $x$.</note>, whose cube plus 80 is equal to **AB** multiplied by **AF** (the square of **AC**). Let **AD** be the third part of **AB**, and similarly let **DE** and **EB** be the same. Let **AG** be a surface of equidistant sides [a parallelogram], and let it be the third part of the square of **AB** from the first [part] of the 6th [book]. Because, therefore, from **BA** times **AC** there is produced the cube of **AF** plus 80, it will be that **BC** times **AF** equals 80. Therefore, **BD** times **AF** is 80 plus that which is produced from **CD** times **AF**. Having subtracted, therefore, that which is from **BD** times **AH** (which is twice the cube of **AD**), there will result **BD** times the <term>gnomon</term><note>A "gnomon" is the L-shaped area remaining when a smaller square is removed from a larger one; Cardano uses these geometric shapes to represent algebraic differences.</note>, which is 26 plus that which is from **CD** times **AF**. 

But that which is from **BD** in the gnomon is equal to four times **CD** in the square of **AH**, and twice **AD** in the square of **HF**, because the lines **BE**, **ED**, **DA**, **DH**, and the remaining supplements are equal to one another. Therefore, four times **CD** in the square of **AH** with twice **AD** in the square of **HF** is equal to 26 plus that which is from **CD** in **FA**. But from **CD** in **FA**, there is produced the cube of **CD**, and twice **AD** in the square of **FH**, and **CD** in the square of **AH** once. Therefore, by taking away that which is from **CD** in the square of **AH** once, and from **AD** in the square of **HF** twice from both sides, there will remain three times **CD** in **AH**, equal to the cube of **CD** plus 26. But three times that which is from **CD** in **AH** is equal to that which is from **CD** in **AG** once, since **DH** is the third part of **DG**. Therefore, that which is from **CD** in **AG** (the third part of the square of **AB**) is equal to the cube of **CD** itself plus 26.

<margin>4th [Rule]</margin>
When the solution of a question arrives at a multitude of <term>denominations</term><note>In this context, "denominations" refers to the different powers of the unknown, such as squares and cubes.</note>, a solution can frequently be hoped for; for this often happens from poor handling [of the problem]. Thus, once reduced to fewer and known denominations, it is solved, and generally so. But when the solution of a question arrives at a chapter of few but unequal denominations, the knowledge [of the solution] will never be reached generally, since it always falls into that category which does not have a universal rule for finding the estimate—for instance, if it should fall into the chapter of the <term>first root</term><note>The linear term $x$.</note>, squares, things, and a number ($ax^2 + bx + c = 0$).

<margin>5th [Rule]</margin>
While this is true in all matters, it is especially useful in Geometric questions, which are heavy. It is often very helpful to solve other preliminary and less difficult questions with the help of this book; finally, you will condense these solutions into rules regarding the method. Then, by their help, step by step...

<meta>This page continues Cardano’s discussion in Book 10 of the *Ars Magna*, where he transitions from specific geometric demonstrations of cubic equations to general rules for solving complex algebraic problems. He focuses here on how to reduce difficult problems to "known chapters" (standard equation types) and how to scale solutions using proportional logic.</meta>

# THE WORKS OF GIROLAMO CARDANO

<meta>catchword: part</meta>

...proceeding step by step through the precepts and rules of position <note>Latin: "positionis." This refers to the "rule of false position" or algebraic substitution, where one assumes a value for the unknown to test the equation.</note>, you will eventually arrive at one of these known chapters <note>Cardano refers to the "chapters" of his book, each of which provides a specific rule for a different type of equation, such as $x^3 + ax = b$.</note> from which a clear solution will appear.

<margin>6th Rule</margin> Beyond these <term>estimates</term><note>Latin: "æstimationes," the numerical solutions or roots of an equation.</note>, however, others emerge which are infinite in number. There is no general use for any single one of them, but those which are most frequent occur in three ways. First, by a particular rule, as was shown in the sixth book—mostly in all the chapters concerning quantities in continuous proportion, as is easily experienced. Second, others arise from the repeated operation of rules or chapters, or from a mixture of them, as when we require multiple chapters or rules for the solution of a single inquiry. You have an example of this, among others, in the fourth question of the 35th chapter of this book, when you have led that question to its conclusion, and even more clearly in the second question of the 31st chapter. In the third way, you will obtain various estimates when you exercise the chapters or rules not with simple numbers, but with already varied estimates. For instance, if I should say: "Divide the <term>fourth root</term><note>Latin: "℞ ultimi 8," likely referring to the fourth root (the "root of the root") of 8.</note> of 8 minus the square root of 2 into two parts, which, when multiplied by the roots of each other alternately, produce numbers that when joined together make 4," the operation will result in an irrational quantity <note>Latin: "absonam quantitatem," literally a "discordant" or "out of tune" quantity, used here to describe a complex or "surd" numerical result.</note>.

<margin>7th Rule</margin> The nature of the product of the parts of a number multiplied by the square root, cube root, or any other kind of root of the remaining part, belongs to the category of the cube or the <term>quad-quadrate</term><note>Latin: "qd̃'q̃drati," meaning the fourth power ($x^4$).</note>, except that the quantity must be taken closest to the greatest, not the lesser. For example: if someone were to say, "Divide 10 into two parts whose product of one times the square of the other makes 9," and afterward you wished to say, "Divide some number into two parts from whose product of one times the square of the other 18 is produced," then you see that such a production is of the cubic category. Therefore, if the proportion were the same, this would happen from 20 (which is double 10, just as 18 is double 9); but because it is of the cubic category, we will find two proportional terms between 10 and 20. These are the cube root of 2,000 and the cube root of 4,000. Therefore, the number sought is the cube root of 2,000. For one part is the cube root of 2 and the other is the cube root of 1,458; if the cube root of 1,458 is multiplied by the square of the cube root of 2, the result is the cube root of 5,832, which is 18. I say, therefore, that if he had said to divide 10 into two parts such that the mutual multiplication of the parts by the root of the other makes 12, this follows a cubic ratio. Thus, if we were to say, "find a number from whose parts' reciprocal multiplication into their roots 24 is produced," and you wished to find the others from the first parts, then between 10 and 20 (which relate as 9 and 18), you will take two proportional terms in a cubic ratio by the same reasoning. The greater of these, which is the cube root of 4,000, is the term sought; for one...

| 9 | 18 |
|---|---|
| 10 | 20 |
| cube root | cube root |
| 2,000 | 4,000 |

<summary>Cardano explains how complex algebraic problems can be reduced to standard equation types and demonstrates how to scale problems involving powers by using proportional "cubic" logic rather than simple linear ratios.</summary>
<keywords>rule of position, estimates, continuous proportion, cubic category, quad-quadrate, proportional terms, cube root, Girolamo Cardano</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes Book 10 of the *Ars Magna*. ### ON ARITHMETIC BOOK X.

...one part is the <term>cube root</term><note>Latin: "Rz cubica." Cardano uses "Rz" as an abbreviation for "radix" or root.</note> of 4, the other is the cube root of 2916. Multiply these in turn by the square root of the other, and they become the cube root of 5832 and the cube root of 216, which are 18 and 6, and these joined together make 24.

<margin>8th Rule</margin>
Any equation of a cube equal to things and a number <note>In modern notation, $x^3 = ax + b$. Cardano uses "things" (rebus) for the unknown $x$ and "number" (numero) for the constant term.</note> is converted into a similar one, whose number of things consists of the division of the prior number of things by the number of the equation, and the number of the [new] equation is the square root of unity divided by the number of the [original] equation. As in the example: let a cube be equal to 6 <term>positions</term><note>Latin: "positionibus." Another term for the unknown $x$.</note> plus 2. Divide 6 (the number of positions) by 2 (the number of the equation); the result is 3, the number of positions for the second equation. Then divide unity by 2 (the number of the equation); the result is 1/2, the square root of which is the number of the [new] equation. It is the same in the two remaining examples.

| | |
|---|---|
| 1 cube | equals 6 things plus 2 |
| 1 cube | equals 3 things plus the root of 1/2 |
| 1 cube | equals 4 things plus 4 |
| 1 cube | equals 1 thing plus 1/2 |
| 1 cube | equals 6 things plus 9 |
| 1 cube | equals 2/3 things plus 1/3 |

However, the discovery of one <term>estimate</term><note>Latin: "æstimationis," meaning the solution or root of the equation.</note> through the other is very difficult. Nevertheless, I say that once the second estimate is obtained, it will be the root of the number of things to be multiplied—together with unity—by 1 cube and by the positions and the prior number on the other side. Then, you add as many squares to each side as the number resulting from dividing unity by four times the square of that same second estimate. You will then have a <term>square-square</term><note>Latin: "qd'qdratum" or "quadrati-quadratum," meaning $x^4$.</note> plus a cube plus a square on one side, which have a root (which will be a square plus a thing); and on the other side, a square plus a thing plus a number, which similarly have a root (which will be a thing plus a number). Thus, through the chapter, you will have the estimate. As in the third example: you have the second estimate of the thing as 1. To obtain the first, multiply 1 thing plus 1...

<margin>(He takes 1 for the rule) but 1 thing is on account of the square of the estimate of the thing, which was also 1 in 1 cube...</margin>

| | |
|---|---|
| 1 cube | 6 things plus 9 |
| 1 thing plus 1 | 1 thing plus 1 |
| --- | --- |
| 1 square-square plus 1 cube | 6 squares plus 15 things plus 9 |
| 1 square-square plus 1 cube plus 1/4 square | 6 1/4 squares plus 15 things plus 9 |
| --- | --- |
| 1 square plus 1/2 thing | 2 1/2 things plus 3 |

...and 6 positions plus 9; you will have 1 square-square plus 1 cube equal to 6 squares plus 9 plus 15 positions. Then add to each side 1/4 of a square <note>Cardano is completing the square for a quartic equation, a technique developed by his student Lodovico Ferrari.</note>, which is what always results from dividing unity by four times the square of the number of the added things. You will then have parts possessing square roots, whereby the thing is found to be 3.
